  - `1`: the command line is wrong.
  - `2`: the workbook does not open, or has no report of that name.
  - `3`: the workbook is not trusted.
  - `4`: a parameter error.
  - `5`: written, but some controls show error values. They are listed on stderr, by band and control.
  - `6`: the timeout passed (default 600 seconds) or the row limit was reached; nothing is written.
- **Limits** are the workbook's settings and the user's options, as in the application (formula budget, detail-row limit).

**Disconnecting from the command line** *(owner, 2026-11-02)*. The second subcommand runs Disconnect from Data Sources (see Data sources) and **writes the result to a separate copy**; the input file is never changed.
```
bidwillii disconnect <workbook.gossia> --out <copy.gossia>
             [--report <Report> [--param <name>=<value>]...]
             [--trust-digest <sha256>] [--overwrite] [--timeout <seconds>]
```
- **Without `--report`** it opens the workbook, fully recalculates it, replaces the reads as the menu command does, recalculates again, and saves the copy.
- **With `--report`** the named report is run first with the parameters given, read as for `report` (a parameter not given takes its default), but **no PDF is written**. That run takes effect in the copy before the sources are disconnected:
  - the report's **remembered parameter values** are the ones given, and its **results cache** is this run (or, with *Keep report results in the file* off, the parameter values only);
  - the reads in the **report's own expressions** are replaced by what they gave **with these parameters**. The copy's report therefore shows this run: changing a parameter later in the copy redraws it, but the replaced reads keep the values of this run. This is the point of the option: the copy is a report for these parameters, sent to someone without the sources.
  - `--param` without `--report` is a command-line error.
- **The copy** is written to a temporary file in the folder of `--out` and renamed, as a PDF is. `--out` must not be the input file (compared after resolving the path), and an existing file is replaced only with `--overwrite`.
- **Trust.** As for `report`: the input must be trusted by this user's trust record or by `--trust-digest`. **The copy is not trusted automatically**: its text changed, so its trust digest is new. The digest of the copy goes to stdout after its path, so the person who receives it, or a later step of the script, can check and trust it.
- **Output and exit codes** as for `report`: stdout has the path of the copy and its trust digest. `5` means written, but some uses of a data source stay (each listed on stderr with where it is and why, as the dialog lists them) or the report shows error values. `4` is a parameter error and `6` the timeout; on any code but `0` and `5` nothing is written.

## Forms

A Data Tab can be shown in more than one way. Each way is a **view** of the tab. A view holds no data of its own: every view reads and edits the same tab, through the same entry path, so an edit made in one view is an ordinary tab edit that appears in the others.

**Views of a Data Tab**
- **Data tree** is the grid described under Core model and Formulas. Every Data Tab has it, and it cannot be renamed, deleted, or customised.
- **Forms** are named data-entry screens over the tab. Every Data Tab has a form called **Default**, which cannot be renamed or deleted and starts out as the **generated form** (below). The user can create more named forms (*New form...*, or *Duplicate* of an existing one). Form names follow the rules for tab names, and are unique within the tab ignoring case.
- A **view selector** beside the tab (Data tree, Default, and the other forms by name) chooses the view that the tab shows. The choice is remembered per tab in the workbook.
- **Views belong to the tab.** A form has no binding by name: it is a view of the tab it was made on, and has the tab's schema, so there is no separate output schema and no unbound-form error. Renaming the tab keeps its views, and deleting the tab deletes them.
- **What the views share.** The selection, the undo history, the pending markers, and the status bar. Switching view first enters any edit in progress (as selecting another cell does; a refused entry keeps the user where they were). Selecting a node and switching view moves to the control bound to that node, or the nearest control above it, and the other way round.
- A **Report Tab** is a different kind of tab, not a view (see Reports). Reports read Data Tabs; they do not add views to them.

**The generated form.** A form that has not been customised is not stored. It is drawn from the tab's schema each time, so it follows every change to the schema, including a field added, renamed, or deleted from a data tab (see Editing the schema from a data tab).
- An object becomes a group titled by its field name (or the schema's `title`), fields in declared order. A scalar becomes a labelled field whose editor comes from its `TypeSpec`: a toggle for booleans, a dropdown for enums, a numeric field, a date picker for dates, a text field otherwise (multi-line when the schema allows long strings). A schema `description` becomes the field's help text, and a required field is marked.
- An array becomes a **repeater**. Items of scalars, or of flat objects, are drawn as the table of the tabular view of arrays (sorting, filters, hiding, and row commands included; the filters and the hiding are the tabular view's own for that array, so they are the same in both places). Other items are stacked, with a stepper (previous, next, *New*, *Delete*, "3 of 17") so that records can be entered one at a time. A map becomes a list of key and value pairs.
- An optional node that is absent shows an empty control, and a value entered creates the node. Computed nodes are read-only with the computed styling.
- The generated form has no buttons: buttons are part of customising.

**Customising a form, in place.** Any form has an *Edit form* switch that puts the view into layout mode, where the form is edited on the form itself. The first change to a generated form **materialises** it: the generated layout is saved in the workbook as a design and then edited. From then on that form no longer follows schema changes by itself.
- **Layout.** A design is a tree of controls: *field*, *group*, *repeater*, *label*, *button*, *separator*. In layout mode a control is selected by clicking it, moved by dragging, resized by the handles on its edges, added from a palette, and deleted from its menu. *(Revised on 2026-10-30, when the owner found that the designer gave too little control over size and place: before, a group only stacked its controls or split them into equal columns, and there were no sizes or positions.)*
- **Each group chooses its layout**, so that exact placement is used where it is wanted and the rest keeps flowing with the window. The top level of a form is a flow.
  - **Flow** (the default, and what the generated form uses): the controls follow one another in rows of the group's number of **columns** (1 to 6). A control takes one column unless it **spans** more, and can **start a new row**; a row is as tall as its tallest control, so the controls of a row line up. With one column the controls are one under the other. The group's width follows the window.
  - **Grid**: rows and columns that the group defines, each with a size (automatic, points, or a share of what is left), and each control placed in a cell by its row and column, spanning rows and columns as it says. Cells can be left empty.
  - **Free**: each control at a position in points (`x`, `y`) inside the group, with a width and a height, and **anchors** that say which edges of the group it keeps its distance to when the group is resized (left and top by default; left and right stretches it). Controls may overlap; the later one in the document is drawn on top. The group is as tall as its lowest control unless it has a height.
  - **The order in the document is the tab order** in every layout. A Free or Grid group lists its controls in that order on its property page, where it can be changed, as dragging a control around does not change it.
- **Size and place of a control**, in every layout:
  - **Width**: automatic (what the control needs; a field's editor is at most 360 points wide), a number of points (24 to 2000), or **fill** (the rest of the width of its cell or row). **Height**: automatic or points (16 to 2000); it is the height of a several-lines text box, of a table or a stacked repeater (which then scroll), of a label or button, or the least height of a group.
  - **Alignment** of a control in its cell, when it is narrower than the cell: left (the default), centre or right.
  - **Label** of a field: on the left (the default), above, or none, and a **label width** (0 to 600 points; 170 unless set), which a group sets for the fields in it and a field can set for itself, so that the editors of a group line up.
  - **Spacing** of a group: the space between its controls and its **padding** inside its box, each 0 to 48 points.
- **The designer for sizes and places.** The selected control has handles on its right edge, bottom edge and bottom-right corner; dragging one sets the width or height in points, snapped to the grid of 4 points (Alt drags without snapping), and a double click on a handle puts the size back to automatic. In a Free group a control is moved by dragging it, snapped to the grid and to the edges and centres of the other controls (a guide line shows the edge it snapped to), and the arrow keys move it one point, or one grid step with Shift. With several controls selected (Ctrl and a click), the property page and the context menu offer **Align** left, right, top, bottom and centres, **Same width**, **Same height** and **Distribute** horizontally and vertically. The property page shows each number, so a size or a position can also be typed.
- **Property page.** Selecting a control shows its properties: its label and help text; for a field, the path it binds to (chosen from the tab's schema), the editor to use, read-only, **masked** (a password box: the text field of a string shows dots instead of what is typed; it only changes what is shown, the value is stored as typed, and clearing it after use is up to the procedure that uses it), and the format of a number or a date; for a repeater, table or stacked, and the order of the columns of its table and which of them are shown; for a button, its label and the procedure it runs. Any control can have a *visible when* and an *enabled when* expression, and a *text colour* and a *fill colour* expression that give its colours (bare Python expressions, without `=`). So can each column of a table: its *visible when* runs once and hides the whole column, and the other three run for each row, on that row's cell.
- **Fonts.** Any control but a separator has a **font**: a typeface (sans, serif, monospace, condensed, script or handwriting; see Look and feel) and a size in points, each *Inherited* unless chosen. A control inherits each of the two from the group it is in, and a control in no group from the interface font. The design stores them as `font` and `font_size` (6 to 72) and leaves out what is inherited. Text that is code (a formula) takes the typeface too, except under Sans, where it stays in the monospace font and takes only the size.
- **Table columns.** Each column of a repeater's table, the row numbers (`#`) among them, has its **own properties**: its title, help text on its title, a width in points (24 to 800, or automatic), the alignment of its title (left, centre or right; left unless set) and of its cells (or automatic: numbers on the right), the editor (automatic or one line of text) and read-only, a number or date format, and a font for its cells, which they otherwise inherit from the table. **The table sets the font of its own title and the font of the titles of its columns**, each a typeface and a size inherited from the table's font unless set, so every column title is in the one font. The row numbers have the title, help, width, the two alignments and the font of their cells, and a **numbering**: `[0]`, `[1]` (the index, as a path writes it; the default), 1 2 3, a b c, A B C, i ii iii, or I II III, from a **start** that is any integer (0 for the index, 1 for the others, unless set). A number a numbering cannot write (a letter or a Roman numeral below 1, a Roman numeral over 3999) is written in digits; letters go on past z as aa, ab. **Where each column is, and whether it is shown, are properties of the table**: the table's property page lists its columns in their order, each with a check box to show it, buttons that move it up and down, and a button that opens its properties. The last column of the items that is shown cannot be hidden; the row numbers can. In layout mode a click on a column's title selects the column (outlined over its whole width, from its title to the end of the table) and shows its properties; its menu moves it left or right, past columns that are hidden, or hides it. The design stores the columns as `table_columns`, a list in screen order of `{name, hidden, ...}` with the row numbers as `#`; a field of the items that the list does not name comes after the others, shown, so a field added to the schema appears, and a list that is the default is not stored.
- **Choices** *(step 7e, built 2026-10-30)*. A field, or a column of a table, can have a **choices** expression: a bare Python expression, like the other expressions of a control, that returns the acceptable values. Each item of what it returns is a value (it is both the code and what is shown), a pair `(code, text)`, or a dict with `value` and `label`; so `["NL", "BE"]`, `[("NL", "Netherlands"), ("BE", "Belgium")]` and `[{"value": c.code, "label": c.name} for c in Countries.list]` all work, and so does a query of a data source.
  - **The editor.** A field with choices is a **dropdown** (the default) or a **typeahead** (*Editor*): a text box that, as text is typed, lists the items whose text contains it (ignoring case), best matches first, chosen with the arrows and Enter or a click. Both store an item's **code**; the typeahead shows its **text**, and the dropdown shows **both**, `AMS — Amsterdam` (the text alone when it is the code; owner, 2026-11-01), entered through the ordinary entry path as the code typed would be, so it must fit the node's type. A stored value that is not in the list is shown as it is, marked *not in the list*, and is kept; it is not refused, since the list can change after the value was entered. *Allow other values (write-in codes)* lets the field take a code not in the list: a typeahead enters typed text that matches no item as the value itself, and a dropdown has an *Other...* item at the end that turns it into a text box for one code (owner, 2026-11-01). Without it, a typeahead refuses such text with the reason and a dropdown has no *Other...*.
  - **Evaluated like the other expressions of a control**: on the evaluation thread, with dependencies recorded, so a list that reads another field (a dependent dropdown: the cities of the chosen country) is worked out again when that field changes; the result is kept in memory, the pending marker shows while it is out of date, and it is part of the trust digest. An optional field also offers *(none)*. In a table, a column's choices run for each row, with `this` the cell and `parent` the row, as the column's other expressions do.
  - **Enumerations.** On a node whose schema has an enumeration, the schema's values stay the rule: items whose code is not one of them are left out of the list, and the property page says how many.
  - **When it cannot run.** In an untrusted workbook, or when the expression fails, the field is an ordinary text box of the code; a failure is shown under the expression on the property page, and in a tooltip on the field.
  - **Stored** as `choices` and `allow_other` on a control or a column, and the editor values `dropdown` and `typeahead`.
- **Number and date formats.** A field or a table column of a number has a **thousands separator** (commas) and a number of **decimal places** (0 to 10, or automatic: as many as the value has); one of a date or a date-time (an XSD date, or a JSON Schema string of `format: date` or `date-time`) has a **date format**, a pattern of `d`, `dd`, `ddd`, `dddd` (7, 07, Sat, Saturday), `m`, `mm`, `mmm`, `mmmm` (3, 03, Mar, March) and `yy`, `yyyy`, with any other character copied; the property page offers some patterns, and any can be typed. A date-time shows its time after the date as it is stored. A format only changes what is shown: while a field has the focus it shows the value as it is stored, and that is what is typed (the grid and formulas are not affected). The design stores `thousands`, `decimals` and `date_format`, on a control or a column.
- **Designed items.** A repeater shown stacked can have its items designed: *Design the items*, on its property page, puts in it the controls the generated form has for an item, which are then arranged as on the rest of the form (any control, in groups of any layout). Their paths are read from the item shown (`sku`, `address.city`; the empty path is the item itself), and their expressions run for each item, where the control's node is in that item: `this` is the node and `parent` the item, as for the cells of a table; a control that is not bound runs at the item. *Use the generated items* takes the design away. A repeater inside the items of another shows its items as generated.
- **The keyboard.** Tab and Shift+Tab go through the controls in the order of the document. The **arrow keys** go to the control above, below, left or right, the one most nearly in line, as in a spreadsheet: in a table from cell to cell. In a text box Left and Right move the caret first and leave the box at its start or its end; Up and Down leave a one-line box at once and move within a box of several lines, and in a typeahead they go through its list. What was typed is entered when the key leaves the box, as on Tab.
- **Formulas appear only here.** The form view has no formula bar. A computed field shows a small *fx* marker. The property page of a field shows the formula of the node it is bound to, which can be edited there; it is the same entry as in the formula bar of the Data tree view, an undoable edit of the tab.
- **Paths are literal**, like formula references. A control whose path no longer exists in the schema (after a rename or delete) is drawn as a placeholder naming the missing path, and the rest of the form works. Layout mode lists **fields not on the form**, each with an *Add* button, and **broken controls**, each with *Re-bind* and *Remove*. *Reset to default* (on Default) and *Delete* (on the others) discard a design; Default then goes back to being generated.
- **Edits to a design** are not in the workbook's undo history in version 1, like module edits and tab operations (see Undo and redo). Layout mode has its own Undo and Redo, which last for the session in layout mode.
- **Stored** as a JSON document validated against a built-in schema (`builtin:form`), so it is diff-friendly and versioned with the workbook (see File format). The code area offers the same document as raw text in an editor pane, for direct editing and for review. A design is valid only if its field paths exist in the tab's schema; one that is not is still opened, with the broken controls as above.

**Additions in 0.2.0, shared with reports** *(owner, 2026-11-02)*. The report designer shares the form designer's model (see Reports, The report designer), so what reports need is added to forms, where it is also useful. Every addition is a property of the shared model and has the same name in `builtin:form` and `builtin:report`; a design that does not use one is stored as before.
- **A field shows a node or an expression**: it has a path *or* a value (*Source* on its property page: *Path* or *Value*), never both. A value is a bare Python expression whose result the field shows. With a path the field is read-write; with a value it is read-only, unless it has a **write procedure** (owner, 2026-11-02): a function of a module, `name(form, ctx, value)`, which gets the text typed into the field when the field loses the focus, writes as a button procedure does (all or nothing, undoable, `ctx.button` the label of the field), and after which the value is worked out again. It keeps the label, the label position and width, the font and the number or date format of a field. It is not in the tab's data, and it is executable content, in the trust digest; in an untrusted workbook it shows `#UNTRUSTED!`.
- **A label can be a template**: its text is fixed, or a template in which each `{expression}` is filled in as in a Python f-string (`{total:,.2f}` included). A template is executable content; in an untrusted workbook its fixed text is shown and each expression part as `#UNTRUSTED!`.
- **A repeater's items can come from an expression** (*Items*) instead of a path, shown as a table or as designed items. Such a repeater is read-only: no row commands, no *New* or *Delete*, and its cells are not editable. On a form it shows related records, such as the orders of the customer shown.
- **A repeater can name its item** (`as order`) and its whole list (`as rows`), for the expressions of the controls inside it, at any depth. `this` and `parent` keep their meaning; in a repeater whose items come from an expression, an item has no node, so `this` in its controls is the item.
- **Designed items nest.** A repeater inside the designed items of another can have its own items designed (before, it always showed generated items), to any depth.
- **Fixed colours, bold and italic.** *Text colour* and *fill colour* take a fixed colour (chosen, or `#rrggbb`) or an expression. A fixed colour runs no Python, so it is shown in an untrusted workbook too. A font has **bold** and **italic** as well as typeface and size, inherited in the same way.
- **Borders and fill on groups**: a border width (0 to 8 points), a border colour and a fill colour, and a **separator** can be horizontal or vertical, with a **thickness** of 1 to 12 points (owner, 2026-11-03). Reports need boxes and lines; forms use them for the same.
- **More number formats**: **percent** (the value times 100, with `%`), and a **prefix** and **suffix**, such as `€ ` or ` kg`, beside thousands and decimals.
- **Not built** (owner, 2026-11-05): **charts on forms** (charts are on reports only) and **a separate totals row on tables**.

**Expressions on controls** (*visible when*, *enabled when*, *text colour*, *fill colour*) are executable content. A field whose node has a formula that was not run, because the workbook is not trusted, shows the `#UNTRUSTED!` mark of the grid beside it, and a value out of date in grey. A colour expression returns the colour to use: a name (`"red"`), `"#rrggbb"`, or an `(r, g, b)` tuple; `None` is the usual look. They are evaluated by the same machinery as formulas: dependencies are recorded, they run on the evaluation thread, never on the UI thread, and the result is kept in memory, not written to the data. While one is out of date the control keeps its last state, with the pending marker. An expression that is an error leaves the control visible and disabled, and says why in a tooltip. In an untrusted workbook they do not run (see Trust model): a control stays visible, and a control with an *enabled when* is disabled.

**Data flow: a form reads and writes the tab directly**
- **Retrieve.** When a form is shown, and whenever the tab's data changes, it shows the tab's current values. It always reflects the last calculated values (see Evaluation and scheduling).
- **Replace on change.** When the user commits a change in a field (on Enter, or when the field loses focus), the new value **replaces** the corresponding node's data in the tab. The edit is an ordinary tab edit: it is undoable and triggers normal recalculation. A form does not hold a draft or a submit step.
- **Validation.** A field validates against its `TypeSpec` as the user types. An invalid value stays in the field with its error and is **not** written; the tab keeps the previous value.
- **Computed nodes are read-only.** A field bound to a node carrying a formula, or inside a computed subtree, is shown read-only with the computed styling. Edits cannot overwrite a formula result.
- Arrays are edited through repeaters: adding, removing, and editing items change the array in the tab directly.

**Button procedures**

A button runs a Python function `def my_procedure(form, ctx): ...` defined in a workbook module.
- `form` is a view of the tab's data. Reading it gives the current values. Changes made to it are collected during the call.
- `ctx` offers read access to the whole workbook through the same sheet proxies that formulas use (`ctx.tab(name)`), and it reports which form and which button were used (`ctx.form`, `ctx.button`), so one function can serve several buttons, and inside the items of a repeater, which item (`ctx.index`, None outside one).
- Procedures **obtain and send data to and from wherever needed**: they can read other tabs and `form`, and they can write to the form's tab through `form` or to other tabs through `ctx`. They can also use the data sources defined on the computer, by name (see Data sources). Writes to tabs are collected during the call and applied **atomically** when it returns.
- Every written value is validated against the schema of the tab it targets. If any write is invalid, or the procedure raises an exception, **none** of the writes are applied, the message is shown to the user, and the data is unchanged. Writing into a computed (formula-driven) node is refused in the same way.
- The applied writes form one undoable edit and trigger normal recalculation.
- Return values are ignored.
- **A click first enters the field being edited**, so the procedure sees what is on the screen; a refused entry stops the click and shows why. The procedure then waits its turn on the evaluation thread and reads the tab as it is when it starts, after any evaluation ahead of it has been applied.
- **A conflicting edit refuses the writes.** If the user edits a node after the procedure started, and the procedure wrote that node, none of the writes are applied and the message names the node. This is the rule for an evaluation that an edit overtook. Fields stay editable while a procedure runs.
- Buttons run one at a time on the evaluation thread. While one runs, all buttons of the workbook are disabled and the status bar shows it.
- Procedures run under the same trust rules as formulas, with their own time limit, the **button procedure time limit** (see Settings and limits), because a procedure that loops over a large array or waits on a data source can reasonably run longer than a formula. In an untrusted workbook buttons are disabled; fields still edit the tab directly, since that runs no Python.

## Data sources

A **data source** is a named connection to something outside the workbook. v1 supports:
- **Databases**: SQLite (a file), PostgreSQL, and MariaDB; on Windows also **ODBC**, which reaches any database that has an ODBC driver (added at the owner's request, 2026-10-30).
- **HTTP / HTTPS endpoints**: a base URL.
- **Beancount journals** (plain text accounting): a file, read and added to (added at the owner's request, 2026-10-31; see Beancount journals).

Data sources are the only way Python code reaches outside the workbook. The sandbox still exposes no `socket`, `subprocess`, `os`, or file I/O modules; everything external goes through a named source.

*This section was rewritten for step 6b on 2026-10-29 from the direction agreed with the owner on 2026-10-28 and 2026-10-29. It replaces the first design, which stored the specification in the workbook, filed the secret in the operating system's credential store under the workbook id, granted each source to each workbook, and asked the user to confirm a target that came from a file.*

**Definitions belong to the user and the machine, not to the workbook.**
- They are kept in `data-sources.json` in the per-user configuration directory, beside `trust.json` (see Settings and limits, "Where preferences live"), passwords, tokens and API keys included. The file is as safe as that directory is: anything that runs as the user can read it. The operating system's credential store is not used.
- **A `.gossia` file refers to data sources by name only.** It carries no connection details and no credentials.
- **Another user or machine sets up the same names.** A workbook used elsewhere needs its data sources defined there under the names it uses; until then each missing one fails with `#SOURCE!` saying no data source of that name is defined on this computer, and the rest of the workbook works.
- **The same name may point at a different instance.** A definition on another machine can name another server or database (a test copy, a local replica); the workbook works as long as the definition and the formulas that use it fit it. This is intended, not a fault.
- **Definitions cannot be exported** or imported. Each machine has its own, set up by hand or by a module (see Defining sources from Python).
- **The kinds available depend on the target.** The desktop application has SQLite, PostgreSQL, MariaDB and HTTP/HTTPS, and on Windows ODBC too; the wasm version (0.3.0, in VS Code) has HTTP/HTTPS only, with the requests made by the host (see Web port, 0.3.0). A workbook that names a source of a kind its target lacks opens and works; calls to that source fail with `#SOURCE!` saying the kind is not available here.
- Another window may change the file while this one runs, so every read goes to the file and every change reads it again first. What a newer version wrote and this one does not know is kept, in the file and in each definition. A file that cannot be read is never overwritten (it holds passwords the user may still want); the panel says why and changes are refused until it is fixed or removed.

**No grants.** **A workbook may use any data source defined on the machine** (settled with the owner on 2026-10-29). There is no per-workbook grant and no allow step. What still guards a source:
- Python runs only in a trusted workbook (see Trust model), so an untrusted workbook reaches no source at all.
- Each definition says whether the source may be written to (*Allow writes*, below).
- The account in the definition is what the server enforces.

**A definition** has a name, a kind, the connection details, the credentials, optional pooling (see Connection pooling), and *Allow writes*.
- *PostgreSQL and MariaDB:* host, port (5432 and 3306 unless given), database, user name, password, and **TLS**: *Prefer* (the default: encrypted when the server offers it), *Require* (fail rather than send anything unencrypted), *Unchecked* (encrypted, but the certificate is not checked) or *Off*. With *Prefer* and *Require* the server's certificate is checked against the operating system's certificate store and the host name, so a self-signed certificate fails the Test; such a server needs *Unchecked* (added at the owner's request, 2026-10-30), which still keeps the traffic from being read on the way but would not notice a server pretending to be this one, or its certificate trusted on the machine.
- *HTTP/HTTPS:* base URL, default headers, and the kind of authentication: none, basic (user name and password), bearer token, or API key (in a header or a query parameter, with its name, and the key). An optional *test path* is what the Test fetches.
- *SQLite:* the path of the file. No credentials and no pooling.
- *Beancount journal:* the path of the journal's main file. No credentials and no pooling.
- *ODBC* (Windows only): the name of a DSN (set up in Windows' ODBC Data Source Administrator) or a connection string (`Driver={...};Server=...`), user name and password. The password has its own masked field: a connection string with `PWD=` or `Password=` in it is refused, so that the secret is never shown as part of the target.
- **Allow writes**, off unless ticked. Off: SQLite is opened read-only, database sessions use read-only transactions, HTTP may only `GET`, and nothing is added to a Beancount journal. This takes the place of the read and read-write levels of the old grants.
- **Several definitions may point at the same database or endpoint**, each with its own credentials: for example a read-only account for reports and another, with *Allow writes*, for a procedure that saves.
- **Names** are letters, digits and underscores, not starting with a digit, at most 64 characters. They are not Python names in the workbook's namespace: code reaches a source through `source("Sales")`, so a source name cannot clash with a tab, a module or a function, and which names exist may differ from machine to machine without changing what a formula means.

**The Data sources panel** (*Tools > Data sources...*) lists the definitions: name, kind, where it goes, and whether writes are allowed. A place longer than 56 characters loses its middle (`beancount:C:\Users…\books.beancount`), so the kind and the file name both show, and the whole place is shown on hover *(owner, 2026-11-04)*. *New...*, *Edit...* (or a double click), *Copy...*, *Delete* (after asking) and *Test* act on the one chosen.
- **The form** shows the fields of the kind chosen; what another kind uses is dropped on save, so a definition never carries a password typed for another kind.
- **Secret fields are masked.** When an existing definition is edited the stored secret is never shown: the field says *Stored*, with *Change* (a password box) and *Clear*; leaving it alone keeps the stored value. A copy has the settings but not the credentials.
- **The target is always shown in full** (for example `postgres://db.example.com:5432/sales as reporting_ro`), so the user can see where the secret is going.
- **Test** checks the settings as entered, before they are saved, in the background with a spinner; it can be cancelled and obeys the data source call timeout (see Settings and limits). A passed test is not required to save, and saving after a failed test says so but is allowed. It is a Rust-side check that does not run Python, so it works in an untrusted workbook.
  - Databases: it opens a single throwaway connection (never one from a pool), authenticates, and runs a trivial read-only query. It reports success with the server version (for ODBC, the name of the database the driver reaches) and the time taken, or the reason for failure (unreachable, authentication refused, TLS problem, unknown database).
  - HTTP: it sends a `GET` to the test path (or the base URL), with the authentication. A 2xx or 3xx response is success; 401 or 403 is reported as an authentication failure; other statuses are shown as information.
  - SQLite: it opens the file read-only and reads SQLite's version and the number of tables, which also shows that the file is a database.
  - Beancount: it reads the journal with its included files and reports how many transactions, accounts and files it has, the dates it covers, and how many problems it found.
  - It cannot show whether an account may write.

**Defining sources from Python** (asked for by the owner on 2026-10-28). Two functions are available to module code and the Immediate window:
- `sources.define(name, kind, *, replace=False, **settings)` **creates (or replaces) a definition** on this machine. `kind` is `"http"`, `"sqlite"`, `"postgres"`, `"mariadb"`, `"beancount"` or (on Windows) `"odbc"`; the settings are those of the form: `host`, `port`, `database`, `user`, `password`, `url`, `headers` (a dict), `auth` (`"none"`, `"basic"`, `"bearer"`, `"key-header"`, `"key-query"`), `token`, `key_name`, `test_path`, `path`, `connection` (ODBC: the DSN or connection string), `pool`, `writes` and `tls` (`"prefer"`, `"require"`, `"unchecked"` or `"off"`). An unknown setting is refused, so that a misspelt one does not go unnoticed, and so is a definition the form would refuse. A name that is already defined needs `replace=True`. This lets a workbook carry its own setup: a button that, on a new machine, defines the sources the workbook uses.
- `sources.check(name)` **says whether a named source exists on this machine and whether it passes the Test**, as an object with `.exists`, `.ok` and `.message`, which is true when it passed.
- Both run only where side effects and waiting are allowed (button procedures and the Immediate window), not in formulas, which re-run on their own; there they fail with `#SOURCE!`. Like all Python, they need a trusted workbook.
- **Where the password comes from is the module author's responsibility.** `sources.define` takes it as an argument and stores it in the definition; how it is kept before it reaches the call (written in the code, which is saved in the workbook, typed into a form field, which is tab data and saved too, or obtained some other way) is the author's choice, and Gossia does not police it. A procedure can erase a field's value after the call. For typing a password into a form, a field control has a **masked** property (see Forms, Property page).

**Using a source from Python**
- `source("Sales")` gives the source defined on this computer under that name, with `.name`, `.kind`, `.writes` and `.target`, never its secrets. A name that is not defined here raises `SourceError`, which a formula shows as `#SOURCE!`.
- Databases: `source("Sales").query(sql, params)` returns a list of dicts, one per row. `.execute(sql, params)` runs a statement that changes data and returns the number of rows affected. Parameters are always passed separately from the SQL text; there are no string-building helpers.
- HTTP: `source("Api").get(path, params=None, headers=None)` and `.head(...)` and, when writes are allowed, `.post`, `.put` and `.patch` (each with `json=` sent as JSON, or `data=` sent as text, or as a form when it is a dict) and `.delete`. **Any other method, standard or custom**, goes through `.request(method, path, json=, data=, params=, headers=)` (owner, 2026-11-06: testing an API as Postman does): `OPTIONS`, `PURGE`, `PROPFIND`, `QUERY` and the like. The method is put in upper case and must be an HTTP token (letters, digits and ``!#$%&'*+-.^_`|~``, at most 64 long); `CONNECT`, `TRACE` and `TRACK` are refused, as browsers refuse them. `GET`, `HEAD` and `OPTIONS` read; every other method writes and needs *Allow writes*. (`options()` without `request` is already a Beancount journal's.) A response has `.status`, `.reason`, `.headers` (a dict with lower-case names), `.text`, `.json()`, `.xml()` (the body read as an XML file is imported, its types inferred: the root element as a dict, child elements by name, attributes as `@name`, the text of an element with attributes as `#text`; owner, 2026-11-05) and `.raw()` (the body as bytes, as `RAW` gives; owner, 2026-11-05). A status of 400 or more is not a response but `SourceError`, `#SOURCE!` in a formula, with the status and the start of the body in the message.
  - **Only below the base URL.** The path is joined to the definition's base URL; a full URL, `//host` or `..` is refused, so the definition alone decides where a request goes and its credentials go nowhere else. Parameters are escaped and added to the query.
  - **Redirects are not followed**: a 3xx comes back as a response, so that a server cannot pass the credentials on to another host.
  - **Certificates** are checked against the operating system's certificate store (rustls with the platform verifier), so a company root certificate installed there is trusted as the browser trusts it.
  - The definition's default headers go first, then the request's own (which replace a default of the same name), then the authentication, which a request cannot replace.
- Values convert to Python types: integers, floats, strings, booleans, `date` / `datetime`, and `None` for SQL `NULL`. Result checking applies as usual when such a value reaches a node.
- `source`, `sources` and `SourceError` are reserved names.

**Read versus write is enforced by the connection, not by parsing SQL**
- Without *Allow writes*, SQLite is opened read-only and PostgreSQL and MariaDB sessions use read-only transactions; `execute` and non-read HTTP methods are refused before anything is sent.
- **ODBC is the exception: read-only is best effort** (owner, 2026-10-30). Gossia asks the driver for read-only access and runs each query in a transaction that is rolled back, but a driver may ignore both (Excel and text files have no transactions; some drivers ignore `ReadOnly`). For such a driver the protection is only the account in the definition, so a read-only account is advised for a source that formulas read.

**Where each kind of call may be used**
- **Reads** (`query`, `get`, `head`, and `request` with `GET`, `HEAD` or `OPTIONS`) may be used in formulas, button procedures, and report expressions.
- **HTTP writes may be sent from a formula** (owner, 2026-11-06, replacing the rule below for HTTP): a source that allows writes takes `post`, `put`, `patch`, `delete` and any other method from a formula, and the formula sends it **each time it runs**: when what it reads changes, on F9, when the workbook is opened (trusted), and on *Recalculate this formula* on its cell's menu (below). A write is never taken from the pass's cache, so two formulas sending the same write send it twice on the desktop; in the wasm version one pass sends a request once, as its rounds share the answers. This is what an API test wants: the request and its answer side by side, sent again when asked.
- **Other writes** (`execute`, a journal's `add`) are allowed **only in button procedures and the Immediate window**, because formulas and reports re-run automatically and must not have side effects. (The Immediate window was added on 2026-10-29: it runs once, when the user presses Enter.) Writes happen immediately during the call; they are not part of the atomic tab-write rule for procedures (see Forms), so a procedure that fails after a write does not undo it.
- Reads in formulas are slow and not deterministic, so their results are **cached for a recalculation pass**, keyed by (source, call, parameters): the same read in several formulas of one pass reaches the source once. They refresh on the next pass that runs the formula, and all of them on an explicit "Recalculate" (F9; see Evaluation and scheduling). Editing a source node does not by itself re-run a query, unless the query's parameters depend on it.

**Recalculate this formula** (owner, 2026-11-06). The menu of a cell that holds a formula, in the tree, in a table and on a form, has *Recalculate this formula*: that formula alone runs again (`Calc::recalc_one`), in a pass of its own, so its reads are made afresh and its writes sent again, and what reads its result follows when the result changes. Other formulas, and their sources, are left as they are. An untrusted workbook runs nothing, and says so, as F9 does.

**Convert formula to values** (owner, 2026-11-06). Beside it on the same menus: the formula is removed and what it last gave stays as literal data, everything inside it included (`Calc::formula_to_values`), so that a fetched or computed tree can be filled out or corrected by hand. Errored fields and items inside are left out, as an export leaves them out; a result that is itself an error is refused (*there is no value to keep*), and a formula that gave nothing leaves nothing. It is one entry to undo, and works in an untrusted workbook too, on the values last saved. On a cell of a column formula it ends the column formula, as typing a value there does. It is the way to keep what a formula fetched once, without fetching again: clearing a formula throws its result away.

**Limits.** Waiting for I/O does not count against the per-formula time limit (the watchdog is paused during the wait, and the evaluation thread does not count the wait when it decides a formula is stuck), but every call has its own timeout (default 30 seconds) and a maximum response size (default 16 MB), both workbook settings (see Settings and limits). A source failure (not defined here, connection, authentication, SQL error, HTTP error status, timeout, oversize response) produces `#SOURCE!`, with the details in the message.

**Connection pooling.** Pooling is a property of a definition, off unless it allows it.
- **Off (the default):** each call opens a connection, uses it, and closes it.
- **On:** the definition states a **maximum connection count** (a whole number of at least 1). Connections are kept after use and reused, up to that many at once.
- Calls that need a connection when all are in use wait for one to be returned, and fail with `#SOURCE!` if none is returned within the call timeout.
- A pool belongs to one definition only. Two definitions to the same server never share connections, because their credentials differ.
- A connection is reset when it goes back into the pool (open transactions rolled back, session state discarded), and the read-only enforcement is applied again each time it is handed out.
- Pools are closed when the definition is edited or deleted and when the application exits.
- **SQLite and Beancount definitions cannot enable pooling**, as each is a local file. For HTTP, pooling means keeping connections alive for reuse, up to the maximum.
- Evaluation is sequential in v1 (see Evaluation and scheduling), so a pool in practice holds about one connection in use. Its value is avoiding the cost of reconnecting. The maximum still limits the total number of connections to the server, including any held by abandoned evaluations (see Settings and limits): such a connection counts against the maximum until the abandoned call returns, and is then closed instead of being reused.

**Beancount journals** (asked for by the owner on 2026-10-31: "Read/ write", and "Query the journal file where it lives").
- **Why Beancount.** Of the plain text accounting formats (Ledger, hledger, Beancount) it has the strictest grammar: accounts must be opened, every transaction must balance, and dates and amounts have one spelling. That makes it the one Gossia can read and add to without guessing. The owner agreed ("Use beancount").
- **Read where it lives.** The definition names the journal's main file; Gossia reads it in place every time it has changed, and never copies it into the workbook. `include` lines are followed, with `*` and `?` in the name, but **only to files in the journal's folder or a folder below it**, so that a journal cannot make Gossia read a file elsewhere.
- **The parser** is the `beancount-parser` crate (Unlicense). `rustledger`, a fuller implementation, was not used because it is GPL-3.0 and Gossia is Apache-2.0. Gossia itself does what Beancount does after parsing: sorts by date, turns `pad` into a transaction, fills in the one posting without an amount, weighs costs and prices, checks that each transaction balances (within half a unit of the last digit written), that accounts are open on the date, and that `balance` assertions hold. The currencies an `open` allows are checked when Gossia adds a transaction, not when it reads one.
- **What is wrong is listed, not hidden.** Problems (a transaction that does not balance, an account that is not open, a failed assertion, a plugin that is not run) are listed with their file and line by `errors()`, and the rest of the journal is still read. **A line that is not Beancount fails the whole journal**: a syntax error, or a line the parser would silently pass over, gives `#SOURCE!` naming the file and line, because a journal read with a line missing would give wrong totals without anyone noticing.
- **Reads**, usable in formulas: `transactions(account=, since=, until=, payee=, tag=, link=)` (an account includes its sub-accounts; both dates are included), `balance(account, until=)` (a dict of currency to amount), `accounts()`, `prices(currency=)`, `errors()` and `options()`. **Amounts are exact `Decimal`s and dates are `date`s**; a `Decimal` stored in a node becomes a number.
- **Formulas follow the file.** A formula that reads a journal depends on it: the window looks at the journal's files (the main file and those it included) every two seconds, and when one has changed, **only the formulas that read that journal run again**. Reads are also cached for the pass, as for the other kinds.
- **Writing is adding, never editing.** `add(date, narration, postings, payee=, flag=, tags=, links=, meta=, kind='original')` appends one transaction to the end of the main file, from a button procedure or the Immediate window, when the definition allows writes. Nothing is written unless the postings balance, the accounts are open on that date and the currencies are allowed; the text added is in the style of `bean-format`. **Gossia never edits or deletes a transaction** (owner, 2026-10-31: "Add is sufficient"): a correction is a new transaction.
  - **Every transaction Gossia writes is marked as such** at the end of its narration: `[Gossia generated original transaction]`, `[Gossia generated adjustment transaction]` or `[Gossia generated reversal transaction]`, following the owner's examples. A narration that already contains the mark is refused, so the mark always means Gossia wrote it. `transactions()` gives each one's `generated` kind (`None` for the user's own).
  - **Corrections are worked out by formulas.** `reversal(transaction)` gives the postings that undo a transaction, and `adjustment(existing, wanted)` the postings that take what a set of transactions (an original and its adjustments, found by a link) adds up to, account by account, to what is wanted. Both only work postings out, so a formula may show the correction before a button adds it.
- **Account names.** Beancount requires each part of an account name to start with a capital letter or a digit (`Assets:Debtors:Bloggs`); the lower-case names of Ledger and hledger (`assets:debtors:bloggs`) are not Beancount.
- **Not done:** plugins are not run (each one in the journal is listed by `errors()`); lots are not booked (a sale at cost is weighed at the cost written, and which lot it reduces is not checked); `note`, `document`, `custom`, `query` and `event` directives are read past; transactions are never edited or deleted.
- **Tests:** `journal_tests.rs` (reading, includes and their folder rule, padding, filling and balancing, balance assertions, accounts not open, filters, exact balances, the test, adding with its refusals, the marks, re-reading after a change); `python/tests_sources.rs` (formulas reading `Decimal`s and dates, a refused `add` in a formula, a changed journal re-running only its readers, adding, adjusting and reversing from the Immediate window); `sources_ui_tests.rs` and `tests/sources.rs` (the form, and a formula following the file in the window).

**Disconnect from Data Sources** (*Tools* menu; asked for by the owner on 2026-11-02, who chose rewriting to literals over recording the answers). It makes a workbook that can be sent to someone who does not have the sources: **every read of a source written out in a formula or a form is replaced by a literal of what it gives now.**
- **What is a read.** A call `source('Name').m(...)` with the name written out, where `m` is `query`, `get`, `head`, `transactions`, `balance`, `accounts`, `prices`, `errors` or `options`. The literal takes the place of **the longest chain on the call that has one**: `source('Api').get('/x').json()['rows']` becomes the rows, though the response alone has no literal. Writes (`execute`, `post`, `put`, `patch`, `delete`, `add`), any `request`, even one that reads, and the corrections of a journal are never replaced.
- **Where.** Formulas, column formulas (the rule once, and the formula of every item), and the expressions of the forms: *Visible when*, *Enabled when*, the colours, *Choices*, *Value*, *Items*, the expressions of columns, and each expression of a template label.
- **How.** The workbook must be trusted. Each read is run once more, where its formula or expression runs, once for each row or item, as `_as_literal((read))`. Python gives the literal: `None`, booleans, numbers (`float('nan')` and the infinities too), text, bytes, lists, tuples and dicts, and calls of `_date('…')`, `_datetime('…')`, `_decimal('…')` and `_record({…})` for what has no literal of its own; those four names are in every formula's globals. A read that gives anything else (a response, an error) is tried with a shorter chain and is otherwise left.
- **What stays, and is listed in the dialog**: a read whose name is not written out (`source(name)`), a read that uses a name of a comprehension or lambda, one that gives something different for different rows or items, one that has not run (in a repeater with no items), a literal of more than 1,000,000 characters, and anything in a module (modules are never changed).
- **The dialog** says how many reads will be replaced and in how many formulas and expressions, and lists what stays. *Replace* makes the changes. **The formula changes are one step of the history** ("Disconnect from data sources"); **the forms have no history**, so the dialog suggests saving a copy first. The window waits for the sources (at most two minutes), as it does when saving.
- **Reports** *(0.2.0; owner, 2026-11-02)*. The expressions of reports are rewritten too, as those of forms are, including the arguments of charts. A read that uses a parameter is replaced by what it gives **with the report's current parameter values** (in the window, those in the parameter bar), and the dialog says so. The report's results cache is calculated again afterwards.
- **From the command line**, `bidwillii disconnect` does the same and writes a separate copy, optionally after running a named report with given parameters (see Batch export from the command line).

**Web port** *(0.3.0; see Web port, 0.3.0)*. The wasm version, first in VS Code, has HTTP/HTTPS only, with the requests made by the host so that CORS does not apply; SQLite, PostgreSQL, MariaDB and Beancount are unavailable there in 0.3.0.

## Trust model

**What trust covers.** Trust applies to all **executable content**: formulas, module sources, form button procedure names, the expressions of form controls, and report expressions. The trust hash is a digest of a canonical serialisation of exactly that content. Data edits do not affect it.

**Recording trust.** User config stores, per workbook, a random **workbook id** (also stored in the file) and the **content hash** at the time of trust.
- **Edits made inside the app** to executable content (typing a formula, editing a module, pasting) update the stored hash while the workbook is trusted. No re-prompt.
- **On open**, a matching id and hash opens trusted. A matching id with a different hash means the file changed outside the app, so it opens untrusted, and the banner lists what changed (which formulas, modules, buttons, report expressions) so the user can review before re-trusting.
- **Save As** gives the copy a new id and carries the trust over, since the user made it.
- Copying an id into a hostile file does nothing, because the hash must match as well.
- A new or unknown file is untrusted. The banner offers "Trust this version" or continue read-only. Trusted workbooks can be listed and revoked in settings.

**Untrusted mode**
- Data, stored values, and stored errors display, and data editing works. Formula cells show the stored value with an `#UNTRUSTED!` badge.
- Formulas can be typed or edited but are not evaluated. Dependents of anything the user edits are shown as **stale** (greyed), since they cannot recalculate. Entering a formula prompts for trust; refusing keeps the text unevaluated.
- Form buttons are disabled, since they all run Python. Form fields still edit the tab directly.
- Reports are drawn from their results cache with the `#UNTRUSTED!` mark, and fixed text and fixed colours as designed; what the cache does not have shows the `#UNTRUSTED!` placeholder (see Report storage and the results cache).

**What containment can and cannot do.** Trust is the real security boundary, and the trust banner says plainly that trusting a workbook allows it to run code. Containment is defence in depth against accidents and casual abuse, not a determined attacker, because sandboxing Python is notoriously leaky:
- The Python engine is built without any operating-system access: no `os`, `subprocess`, `socket`, or file I/O modules exist in it (see Python engine for what this rests on). External access is only through named data sources (see Data sources), which a trusted workbook may use when they are defined on the machine.
- `open`, `exec`, `eval`, `compile`, and `__import__` are removed from the builtins formulas see, and double-underscore attribute access is rejected when a formula is entered. These catch mistakes; they are not a boundary (see Python engine).

**Resource limits.** Evaluation takes a **budget**: a deadline and optionally a step count. Exceeding it aborts the evaluation with a new error kind, `#TIMEOUT!`, distinct from other Python errors. The limits are user settings (defaults: 1 second per formula and 10 seconds per recalculation; see Settings and limits). A memory limit is not promised in v1. How the budget is enforced is described under Python engine.

## Python engine

- **RustPython**, embedded behind a small `PythonEngine` trait: `eval(expr, globals) -> Result<PyValue>` and `load_module(src)`. The trait keeps the engine swappable (e.g. PyO3 + CPython on desktop later).
- Pure Rust, so the same code compiles to wasm for the web port, with no Python install required.
- Known limits: slower than CPython, incomplete stdlib, no C extensions (no numpy). RustPython describes itself as not totally production-ready, so the engine trait matters.

**Spike results.** A spike was run in October 2026 against RustPython 0.6.1 (GitHub head), on Windows with rustc 1.99.1. It built the interpreter with `--no-default-features --features freeze-stdlib`, which leaves out the `host_env` feature (the part that gives Python access to the operating system). The findings below replace the earlier assumptions in this section.

**Building and depending on it**
- RustPython 0.6.0 from crates.io **did not build** from a fresh dependency resolution: its `ruff` parser crates resolve to 0.16.10, which breaks the code generator, and pinning them to 0.16.5 breaks the parser. RustPython 0.5.0 fails on a `malachite-bigint` version clash. On Windows, `rustpython-host_env` also needed its `ctypes` feature.
- It built only from the repository's own `Cargo.lock`, which uses a git dependency on RustPython's `ruff` fork. On Windows that checkout failed with "path too long" and needed a clone with `core.longpaths` and a `[patch]` to a local copy.
- It needs **rustc 1.97 or newer** (the stable toolchain on the test machine had to be updated from 1.94).
- Consequence: depend on RustPython by pinned git revision, together with its lock file, and vendor or mirror the `ruff` fork. Do not rely on a plain `rustpython = "0.6"` dependency (see Build and dependencies for how it is vendored). Expect to re-check this on every upgrade, and keep the `PythonEngine` trait.

**Cancelling a running formula (the budget)**
- The mechanism works: a watchdog thread sends a closure through `user_signal_channel`, and the VM raises it inside the running code. A `while True: pass` loop and a loop of builtin calls were both stopped about 300 ms after a 300 ms deadline, with the exception surfacing as an ordinary Python error. The interpreter was **reusable afterwards**.
- **A single long builtin call cannot be interrupted.** `sum(range(10**10))` ignored the signal for as long as the test ran (it was killed externally after 20 seconds), because the signal is only checked between bytecode instructions. So the budget cannot catch everything.
- Mitigation: when a cancellation does not take effect within a grace period, abandon the evaluation thread and start a fresh interpreter, accepting that the old thread runs until the call finishes. Also guard the obvious cases (for example `range` above a size limit). The abandon path is the real backstop on desktop, and the number of abandoned threads is limited by a setting (default 20); see Settings and limits.
- On **wasm32** there is no second thread, so terminating the Web Worker is the only mechanism (see Web port, 0.3.0).

**What the sandbox really is**
- With `host_env` left out, the interpreter has **no file, socket, subprocess, or OS modules at all**: `os`, `posix`, `nt`, `socket`, `subprocess`, `ctypes`, `sqlite3`, `threading`, `pathlib`, `shutil`, `urllib`, and `http` all fail to import. `_io` imports but cannot open files ("Couldn't get FileIO").
- **Removing builtins is not a boundary.** With `open`, `__import__`, `eval`, `exec`, and `compile` removed from the builtins a formula sees, direct use fails with `NameError`. But `().__class__.__base__.__subclasses__()` still reaches `BuiltinImporter`, and `create_module(find_spec('sys'))` hands back `sys.modules`. Had `host_env` been compiled in, the same route would reach file I/O.
- So the guarantee comes from **what is compiled in**, not from what is hidden. Decisions that follow:
  - The Python engine is always built **without `host_env`** (and without `ssl`, sockets, and similar features). All access to files, databases, and the network happens in Rust, through the data source layer.
  - Trimming builtins is kept as a courtesy that catches mistakes, but it is not described as security.
  - As an extra layer, formulas are scanned when entered and rejected if they use double-underscore attribute access (`__class__`, `__subclasses__`, ...). The same scan applies to module source, with a short list of allowed dunder names (`__init__`, `__name__`, ...).

**Modules that work**
- Import fine without `host_env`: `math`, `datetime`, `re`, `json`, `itertools`, `decimal`, `fractions`, `collections`, `functools`, `string`, `textwrap`, `time`, `io`, `sys`.
- **`statistics` and `random` fail** (`no os specific module found`), because they import `os`. They are therefore **not available in v1**, and no replacement is planned. The built-in aggregates (`SUM`, `AVERAGE`, ...) cover the common cases, and a workbook module can supply anything else.
- Registering only an allowlist of native modules was not tested, because nothing dangerous is compiled in. It remains an option for trimming size. (Step 13g did it, for size: see the decisions for a smaller download.)
- **`datetime.strptime` fails** too: it imports `_strptime`, which imports `os`. Found in 13g; it was so from the start. `date.strftime` failed in formulas until 13g as well (the import hook refused the `time` it imports), and works since.

**Speed (release build, Windows)**
- One formula over a 10,000-item list of objects (`sum(o['price'] * o['qty'] for o in items)`): **about 3.6 ms** per evaluation.
- 10,000 separate formulas, each precompiled and run in a fresh scope: **about 20 ms in total** (about 2 microseconds each).
- Compiling each formula from source every time: **about 147 ms** for 10,000 (about 15 microseconds each). So **compiled code should be cached per formula**, but even uncached it is not a bottleneck.
- Not measured: the real application reads data through proxy objects with lazy member access and dependency recording, which will be slower than plain dicts. This is measured when step 3 is built, against these **budgets**:
  - a proxy attribute or index read: **5 microseconds or less** (the Python prototype took about 1.9);
  - 10,000 separate formulas, each reading a few nodes, including result checking: **250 milliseconds or less** in total (plain evaluation took 20);
  - one aggregate formula over 10,000 items read through proxies: **50 milliseconds or less** (plain evaluation took 3.6).

  The nightly performance job reports these figures and fails when a budget is exceeded. They are targets set before measurement and are revised, with the reason recorded here, if the first measurements show they were wrong. If a budget cannot be met, the mitigations are caching converted children, and native fast paths for common aggregations such as summing one field.

**Measured in step 3** (release build, Windows, `cargo test --release -p gossia-calc --test perf -- --ignored --nocapture`):

| What | Budget set before | Measured | Budget now |
|---|---|---|---|
| One proxy attribute or index read | 5 microseconds | 0.75 to 0.9 microseconds | 5 microseconds |
| 10,000 separate formulas, each reading two array items by index, recalculated with their code already compiled | 250 ms | about 150 ms to evaluate and 70 ms to apply and record dependencies, 225 ms together | 250 ms |
| The same, calculated for the first time (10,000 different formulas to compile, 25 microseconds each) | not set | 410 to 435 ms | 500 ms |
| One aggregate over 10,000 integers, `sum(S.nums)` | 50 ms | 20 ms | 50 ms |
| One aggregate over a projection, `sum(S.items.price)` | 50 ms | 43 to 52 ms | 75 ms |
| One aggregate that goes into 10,000 objects for two fields | 50 ms | 66 to 86 ms | 100 ms |

- **Why the last two changed.** The 50 ms was set for a sum over a plain list of 10,000 numbers read through proxies. Reading an object field makes a proxy for each item (an allocation) and a read for each field, so an item costs about 5 to 8 microseconds in place of 2. The mitigations above are still there if this is not good enough: a native `sum` of one field is the first.
- **The first calculation** is slower than a recalculation because each different formula is compiled once (the engine keeps the compiled code by text). The spike measured 15 microseconds for this; it is 25 here, with the parser and the checks on top.
- **What the first measurements found.** A proxy that remembers the node it read keeps that node shared with the data, so the next edit to the data copies the node first. With a tab's proxy remembering its root, writing 10,000 results into an array copied the array 10,000 times and took 1.4 seconds. The proxies that live as long as the engine now remember nothing; only the proxies made during one evaluation do, and they are not used after it.

**Web (wasm32)** *(spike results, for 0.3.0; see Web port, 0.3.0)*
- RustPython's own `rustpython_wasm` crate **builds** for `wasm32-unknown-unknown`, which needs the `getrandom` `wasm_js` feature (the crate sets it).
- The unoptimised output was **54.6 MB**. That build includes features we would not use (`ssl`, documentation strings) and was not passed through `wasm-opt` or compression. The achievable size is not yet known and must be measured with a minimal feature set.
- Python was not run in a browser. Terminating a Web Worker was tested in a VS Code web view with plain JavaScript (2026-11-05): a worker spinning forever was terminated and a new one answered 6 ms later (see Web port, 0.3.0).

**Not covered by the spike:** running under `wasm32` in a real browser, the proxy layer, memory use, and the effect of the `threading` feature. Step 13a ran the engine and the proxy layer in a browser's Web Worker and measured the size and the start-up time (see the decisions for the engine on wasm); memory use is still to be measured.

## Evaluation and scheduling

All Python evaluation (formulas, form button procedures, report expressions) runs on a **background evaluation thread**, never on the egui UI thread. On the web port (0.3.0) the same role is played by a Web Worker, using the same message protocol.

**The UI never waits.** Every cell is drawn from its **last calculated value**, the one stored in the tree (see Stored values). A cell whose evaluation is outstanding keeps showing that value, with a subtle pending marker, until a new result arrives. A cell that has a formula but has never been calculated shows empty with the pending marker. The same applies to reports: they render the last values and refresh as results arrive.

**Status bar.** A status bar along the bottom always shows evaluation state:
- `Ready` when nothing is outstanding.
- `Calculating... N pending` while evaluations are queued or running, optionally with a progress indicator.
- Counts of errors and invalid cells, and the workbook's trust state.

**Message flow**
1. An edit on the UI thread marks the affected formula nodes dirty and sends them to the evaluation thread.
2. The evaluation thread evaluates dirty nodes in dependency order and posts results (value or error value, plus the dependencies read) back.
3. The UI applies results at the start of a frame and requests a repaint. Dependents of a changed result are queued in turn.

**Consistency**
- Every result is tagged with the input version it was computed from. If an edit has since changed something the formula read, the result is discarded and the formula is queued again. The UI never shows a result computed from superseded inputs once it has been superseded.
- A formula never observes a half-applied edit: the evaluation thread works against a consistent snapshot of the data and picks up newer versions between evaluations.
- An in-flight evaluation whose inputs have changed may be cancelled through the same mechanism as the timeout budget (see Python engine), so a long-running formula does not delay the response to an edit.
- Per-formula and per-recalculation budgets apply as described in the trust model; a timeout is stored as `#TIMEOUT!` like any other error.

**Volatile functions.** `NOW`, `RAND` and similar are evaluated once per recalculation pass and keep that value until the next pass. A full recalculation happens when a trusted workbook is opened and when the user asks for it explicitly ("Recalculate", F9). There is no timer-driven refresh in v1.

**Saving while evaluating.** Saving waits for outstanding evaluations to finish so the file never holds half-updated values. The status bar shows that it is waiting, and the user can cancel the wait and save with the current last values, which are flagged as pending in the overlay.

**Forms.** Button procedures run on the same thread, one at a time (as already specified). While one runs, the form's buttons are disabled and the status bar shows it.

## Settings and limits

Limits are not hard-coded. The user sets them, with defaults, in two places.

- **Workbook settings** are stored in the workbook (`settings.json`) and edited in the *Gossia Options* dialog (see The Options dialog). They govern how that workbook's Python and data sources run.
- **Application preferences** belong to the user, not to any file. They hold the limits that apply *before* a workbook is open or trusted (reading a file, importing), and a **ceiling** for each workbook setting.

**A workbook cannot raise its own limits past the user's ceilings.** A file is untrusted input, so a hostile workbook that asks for a one-hour time limit gets the ceiling instead. The dialog shows each value with its default and ceiling, rejects out-of-range input, and shows the effective value when a ceiling applies. Workbook settings are part of the executable content covered by trust, since they govern execution (see Trust model); editing them inside the application keeps trust, and a change made outside it drops trust. Changes are undoable edits.

**Workbook settings**

| Setting | Default | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| Formula time limit | 1 s | Longest a single formula or module load step may run (CPU time, not I/O waits) before `#TIMEOUT!`. |
| Button procedure time limit | 10 s | Longest a form button procedure may run (CPU time, not I/O waits) before its writes are dropped and the message shown. |
| Recalculation time limit | 10 s | Longest one recalculation pass may run. |
| Cancel grace period | 500 ms | After a cancel signal, how long to wait for the evaluation to stop before abandoning it. |
| **Maximum abandoned threads** | **20** | How many abandoned evaluations (see below) may still be running at once. |
| Maximum range length | 1,000,000 | Largest sequence `range` may produce. |
| Data source call timeout | 30 s | Longest a data source call may take, including waiting for I/O. |
| Maximum response size | 16 MB | Largest data source response accepted. |

More settings can be added in the same way, with a default and a ceiling each.

**Application preferences** (not stored in workbooks): the ceiling for each workbook setting; and limits that apply while a file is being read or imported, namely the maximum number of zip entries, maximum total uncompressed size, maximum import file size, and maximum nesting depth; plus the undo history bounds (entry count and memory budget).

**Abandoned threads.** A formula that does not stop after a cancel signal and the grace period (for example one long builtin call, which cannot be interrupted; see Python engine) is **abandoned**: its result is discarded, its node gets `#TIMEOUT!`, and a fresh interpreter takes over so evaluation continues. The abandoned thread keeps running until its call returns, and then it is released. The number of such threads still running is limited by *Maximum abandoned threads*.
- The count appears in the status bar when it is above zero (for example `2 abandoned`).
- **At the limit, evaluation is suspended.** No new evaluation starts, pending cells keep their pending marker, and the status bar shows `Evaluation suspended: 20 abandoned threads still running`. It resumes by itself when the count drops below the limit.
- The Evaluation page of the Options dialog offers **Save and restart**, which saves the workbook (without waiting for evaluation, flagging pending cells as for any save) and restarts the application, since a thread that never returns can only be cleared that way.
- Raising the limit takes effect immediately, up to the user's ceiling.

**Guards on builtins.** The `range` builtin is replaced by a version that refuses a length above *Maximum range length*, raising `ValueError` (shown as `#VALUE!`) with a message naming the setting. This catches the accidental case such as `sum(range(10**10))` before it starts. Guards are **best effort**, not a security boundary: they cover the wrapped builtins and obvious literals, not every way to build a huge value. The abandoned-thread limit is the real backstop, and a memory limit is not promised in v1.

### The Options dialog

All settings are edited in one **Gossia Options** dialog, laid out like Excel's Options dialog. It opens from **Tools > Options**.
- A **list of categories** runs down the left, the chosen category's settings fill the right, and **OK** and **Cancel** sit at the bottom. Enter is OK and Esc is Cancel. Nothing takes effect until OK, and Cancel discards every change in the dialog.
- Each page says in its heading **what it applies to** (and is divided into sections, see below): *This workbook* (stored in the workbook) or *All workbooks* (stored for the user). If no workbook is open, the workbook pages are hidden.
- Each setting shows its default, rejects out-of-range input inline, and has a **Reset to default** button. A workbook value that a ceiling is holding down shows the effective value beside it and says why.
- **OK applies the workbook settings as one undoable entry.** Application preferences take effect on OK and are not part of the undo history, because they are not part of the workbook.
- Other parts of the application link straight to a page: for example the status bar's *Evaluation suspended* message opens the Evaluation page.

**Pages and sections.** Each page is divided into titled **sections** (group boxes), as in Excel, so related options sit together. The defaults are in brackets.

| Page | Applies to | Sections and their options |
|---|---|---|
| General | All workbooks | *Appearance*: theme (Light, Dark, or Follow system) [Follow system]; interface scale, 50% to 300% [100%]. (The font of the data views is not a preference: it belongs to the workbook, under *View > Font*.) |
| Display | All workbooks | *Tree grid*: indentation per level [16 px]; row density (Compact, Normal, Comfortable) [Normal]; show the Type column [on]; show the Formula column [on]. *Tabular view*: default column width [120 px]. *Comments*: hover delay before the popup appears [500 ms]; maximum popup width [360 px] and height [240 px]. |
| Editing | All workbooks | *Undo history*: maximum number of entries and the memory budget. *Formula bar*: the most lines it grows to before it scrolls [6]. |
| Code editor | All workbooks | *Editor*: font family (Geist Mono, the one monospace font bundled; see the 10c decisions) and size [13 pt]; indent width, 2 to 8 spaces [4]; show line numbers [on]; syntax highlighting [on]; wrap long lines [off]. *Immediate window*: history entries kept [100]; maximum output size of one result [64 KB]. |
| Evaluation | This workbook | *Time limits*: formula time limit, button procedure time limit, recalculation time limit. *Cancellation*: cancel grace period; maximum abandoned threads, the number currently abandoned, and the *Save and restart* button. *Guards*: maximum range length. |
| Data sources | This workbook | *Limits*: call timeout and maximum response size. The definitions themselves are not workbook settings: they belong to the user and the machine, and are edited in *Tools > Data sources...* (see Data sources). |
| Reports *(0.2.0)* | This workbook | *Limits*: most detail rows in a multipage report [100,000]; largest SVG a chart function may return [5 MB]. Both have ceilings, as other workbook limits do. *Storage*: keep report results in the file [on] (see Report storage and the results cache). *(Storage was built with 12f, the limits with charts.)* |
| Trust and security | All workbooks | *Trusted workbooks*: the list, each with *Revoke*. *Ceilings*: the maximum for each workbook setting (see below). |
| Files and import | All workbooks | *Reading files*: maximum zip entries, maximum total uncompressed size, maximum nesting depth. *Import*: maximum import file size. *COBOL records*: what *Copy* in the Raw view copies them as (a hex dump, hex digits, or Base64). |
| About | All workbooks | The usual About box: the name, version, a line on what Gossia is, its licence and repository; the main third-party libraries with their versions and licences (RustPython with its revision); the bundled fonts and their licences. |

- **Live preview.** Appearance options (theme, scale, fonts, density, indentation) preview immediately while the dialog is open, and revert if the dialog is cancelled.
- **Display of values.** There are no regional or number-format options. Values are shown in their canonical Python form (dates and datetimes in ISO 8601), the same as formulas see them.
- **Long comments.** A comment popup wraps its text at the maximum width and, past the maximum height, scrolls. This settles how long comments are shown.
- **Immediate window.** Its history is kept per user, not per workbook, in a separate file (`immediate-history.json`) in the same configuration directory, not in the preferences file, and holds at most the number of entries set on the Code editor page. The Code editor page has a *Clear history* button. A result larger than the output limit is cut off with a notice saying how much was left out.

**Ceilings.** The Trust and security page holds one ceiling for each workbook setting. A workbook's value is used only up to its ceiling. The defaults below are starting points and are all adjustable:

| Workbook setting | Default | Default ceiling |
|---|---|---|
| Formula time limit | 1 s | 60 s |
| Button procedure time limit | 10 s | 300 s |
| Recalculation time limit | 10 s | 600 s |
