| Cancel grace period | 500 ms | 10 s |
| Maximum abandoned threads | 20 | 100 |
| Maximum range length | 1,000,000 | 1,000,000 |
| Data source call timeout | 30 s | 300 s |
| Maximum response size | 16 MB | 256 MB |

**Where preferences live.** Application preferences are stored with the trust records and the data source definitions (`data-sources.json`, see Data sources) in one per-user configuration directory (the operating system's usual location for application settings), as a JSON file. The file carries a version number. Settings the application does not know (from a newer version) are kept unchanged when the file is saved, settings that are missing take their defaults, and a file from an older version is upgraded on load, so adding or removing a setting never loses the user's other settings.

**Changing a limit while something is running.** A new value applies to evaluations that start after OK. An evaluation already running keeps the deadline it started with. Lowering *Maximum abandoned threads* below the number currently abandoned simply suspends new evaluations until enough of them return.

## Undo and redo

The workbook keeps one **edit log** so that user edits can be undone and redone. The history is a single linear sequence for the whole workbook, like Excel's, not one per tab.

**What is logged.** Every edit the user makes:
- literal values, and structural changes (inserting, removing, moving, or sorting items and map entries);
- formulas, and comments (XML tabs);
- tabs: adding, removing, renaming, reordering, and rebinding schemas;
- Python modules, form designs, report designs, and the names of the data sources its code uses, inside that code (never their definitions or secrets);
- schema sheet edits, which are ordinary tree edits, and schema edits made from the data tab that uses the schema (see Editing the schema from a data tab), each together with the data changes it causes, as one entry;
- the table-wide edits made by paste, import, and button procedures.

**What is not logged.**
- Computed results, error values, and pending state. They are derived, not authored. After an undo, the formulas affected are recalculated and the new results appear as usual.
- View settings such as expanded nodes, column widths, and whether an array is shown as a table.
- Effects outside the workbook. A procedure that writes to a database or HTTP endpoint cannot be undone; its workbook edits can.
- Defining or deleting data sources, and trust decisions.

**Entries.**
- One user gesture is one entry: committing a field, a paste, running a button procedure (all of its writes together), an import, dragging items into a new order.
- Rapid consecutive edits to the same text cell during one editing session are merged.
- Each entry has a short description, shown in the Edit menu ("Undo Set Orders.items[2].price").
- An entry records enough to reverse itself: the old and new value, or the removed subtree. Removed subtrees are held by reference, so deleting a large array does not copy it, and restoring a subtree restores its original `NodeId`s.
- Entries refer to nodes by `NodeId`, not by path, so they stay valid when other edits shift paths. Formula entries refer to a location (a path), consistent with formulas belonging to a location.

**Behaviour**
- Undo and redo are ordinary edits as far as the rest of the system is concerned. They apply on the UI thread, bump the data version, and queue recalculation. Evaluation results computed from superseded inputs are discarded by the usual version rule.
- A new edit after an undo clears the redo history. There is no branching.
- Undoing or redoing a change to executable content (a formula, module, button, or report expression) counts as an edit made in the app, so it keeps the workbook's trust (see Trust model).
- Undo and redo are disabled while a button procedure is running.
- The log tracks the saved state, so undoing back to the saved point clears the "modified" mark.
- The history lives in memory only. It is not saved in the `.gossia` file and is cleared when the workbook is closed. It is bounded by an entry count and a memory budget (application preferences), and the oldest entries are dropped first.
- Keys: Ctrl+Z to undo, Ctrl+Y or Ctrl+Shift+Z to redo.

**Structure.** `gossia-core` defines the primitive edit operations on the data tree and how to apply and invert them. The workbook-level log, which also covers the other kinds of edit above, lives in the layer above (`gossia-calc`), and the UI only issues edits and requests undo and redo.

## Comments in the UI

Comments on XML tabs look and behave as they do in Excel.
- **Indicator.** A cell with a comment shows a small coloured **triangle in its top-right corner**.
- **Hover.** Resting the mouse pointer over the cell (after a short delay) shows the comment in a small popup beside the cell. The delay, and the popup's maximum size (long text wraps, then scrolls), are options on the Display page. It disappears when the pointer leaves. The comment can be read on any cell, including cells of computed nodes.
- **Commands.** Right-clicking a cell offers **Insert Comment**, or **Edit Comment** and **Delete Comment** when it already has one. Insert and Edit open a small text box for the comment. Enter adds a line break, and committing is done by clicking away or with Ctrl+Enter; Esc cancels. The commands are absent on JSON tabs.
- **Where.** The triangle is drawn on the node's cell in the tree grid and in the tabular view alike. For an object or array node it is on the node's own row (or on the array's header in the tabular view). A field on a **form** shows its node's comment the same way, with the same commands in its menu.
- **Comments inside an element.** A node read from a file can also have a comment inside its element, before its text or at its end (see Comments and structure fidelity). The popup shows each under the name of its place, the comment box has a box for each, and *Delete Comment* takes them all away.
- Inserting, editing, and deleting a comment are undoable entries in the edit log, and each is a change to the document, so it marks the workbook as modified.

## Editing the schema from a data tab

When the schema sheet's **auto-update** is on (see Schema binding), the single tab that uses the schema can change the schema from its own grid, instead of switching to the schema sheet. The commands are in the context menu of an object or array node (and, for fields, on a column header in the tabular view).

**Commands in v1**
- **Reorder fields** (dragging a column header).
- **Add field**: adds a property (JSON Schema) or element or attribute (XSD) to the object type, with a name and a type chosen in a small dialog, and whether it is required. The types are text, whole number, number, yes or no, date, date and time, **object** and **array** *(owner, 2026-11-04)*; the dialog has *Type* to the left of its list, which shows every type without scrolling. An object has no fields yet (`{"type": "object", "properties": {}}`, or in an XSD an element with an empty `complexType`), and an array is a list of such objects (`{"type": "array", "items": {...}}`, or an element of its own `complexType` with `maxOccurs="unbounded"`); their fields are added in the same way, on the object or on an item. An attribute cannot be an object or an array, and the dialog says so. A field that is not required is added to the schema only, so the data has no node for it yet: *Add item* on such an array makes it, holding its first item, as one entry to undo *(owner, 2026-11-04: it said "no node at" before)*. An array row's menu has no *Add field* *(owner, 2026-11-04)*: its fields are its items', added from an item's menu. It keeps *Rename field* and *Delete field*, since the array is itself a field of its record.
- **Force type definition** *(owner, 2026-11-04, as Force object definition; renamed on 2026-11-10)*: on a field whose type is a record with no fields, or an array of such records, or on the root when its type is a record with no fields (owner, 2026-11-05: the root's record takes the fields), whose formula returned a record (or an array of records) with fields, which the type refused (`#TYPE!`). The node's context menu offers it, and it gives the field the type inferred from that result, as an import infers one (closed records, the most common shape winning), in place of the empty record, except that **every field it finds is optional, at every depth** *(owner, 2026-11-06: one result does not show which fields every result has; randomuser.me sends another person on each request, some without an `id.value`)*; whether the field itself is required stays as it was, and on the root the record's required list is emptied. A field whose kind changes between answers (randomuser's `postcode`, a number for some countries and text for others) still needs its type edited by hand: deleting its `type` makes it any value. One entry to undo. The refused result is kept with the formula (`Formula::rejected`, carried from the evaluation thread in `Outcome::rejected`), so nothing is run again to find it. Like the other field commands it needs auto-update on the schema tab, and it is greyed, with the reason, otherwise; an XML element's type is defined on its XSD tab instead. It is the easy way to give `=source("db").query("select * from t")` its fields. **Arrays of simple values** *(owner's report, 2026-11-10: an array formula giving text had no way to its type)*: on an array of records with no fields whose formula returned a non-empty list of simple values (text, numbers, booleans, no nulls), it gives the items the inferred simple type (`{"type":"array","items":{"type":"string"}}`), hence the name.
- **Array item types** *(owner's report, 2026-11-10)*: *Add field* with type Array on a JSON tab has an **Items** choice (any type but Array; default Object, an object with no fields as before), giving `{"type":"array","items":<the item schema>}` (`Calc::add_array_field`, `FieldKind::ITEMS`); arrays of arrays are refused. On an XSD tab an array is a repeated element, as before. Changing a `type` on a JSON Schema tab also removes, in the same undo entry, the keywords beside it that the new type cannot have (`properties` and `required` once `object` becomes `string`, say; `jsonschema::keywords_not_for`), since otherwise the schema no longer lowers and the tab kept showing the old type.
- **Go to schema definition** *(owner, 2026-11-06)*: on the context menu of every node of a tab bound to a schema tab (not on a schema tab itself), in the tree and on its Value cells. It shows the schema tab with the node's definition selected and the nodes above it opened (`Calc::definition_of`). A field is its own entry: under its record's `properties` in JSON Schema, in the record's own place (the `$defs` entry of a shared type, not where it is referred to); its `element` or `attribute` in an XSD (the element, not the named type it has); its item in a copybook. An array item is the array's `items`, and the root is the top of the schema. A node the schema does not define goes to the nearest node above it that it does, and failing all, to the top of the schema tab.
- **A changed schema runs the formulas of its tabs again** *(2026-11-04)*: a result was checked against the old type, so a `#TYPE!` may fit now and a value may not. Every formula of each tab bound to the schema tab is out of date after the schema lowers again, by an edit, a field command or an undo.
- **Rename field.**
- **Delete field.**

More involved changes (changing a type, adding constraints, adding nested types) are made by editing the schema sheet directly, which is always possible.

**What each command does to the data**, so that the tab never ends up out of step with its schema:

| Command | Schema | Data |
|---|---|---|
| Reorder fields | Declared order changes. | Items are written in the new field order. No values change. |
| Add field | New optional or required field. | Existing items get no value for it: an optional field is simply absent, and a required one is shown as a missing required value (flagged, per the invalid-data rules). |
| Rename field | The field has the new name. | The key is renamed in every item, with its value, comment, and hidden or filter state. **Formulas are not rewritten**, as with any rename (see Renames do not rewrite formulas): formulas that use the old name show `#REF!`. A formula on the field stays at its location, which now has the new name. |
| Delete field | The field is removed. | The values are **removed from every item**. Because this is destructive, a confirmation states how many values will be removed. Formulas that use the field show `#REF!`. |

**One undo entry.** A command, together with its data changes, is **one entry** in the workbook's edit log, described in the Edit menu by what it did (for example *Undo Rename field "qty" to "quantity"*). Undo restores the schema, the field order, and any removed values together.
- Because the log is a single linear history, a later edit to the schema sheet (made directly on its tab) is a later entry. It must be undone first, so an entry can never be undone against a schema that has since been changed in some other way.
- Because auto-update allows only one user of the schema, a command never changes any tab other than the one it is issued from and the schema sheet.

**Details**
- **Attributes and elements (XML).** *Add field* on an XML tab asks whether the new field is an **element** or an **attribute** (attributes can only have simple types). Reordering is among elements and among attributes separately. An attribute cannot be dragged among the elements, because XSD keeps them apart. An element that holds text (`xs:simpleContent`) takes attributes only, and its text is not a field that can be renamed or deleted. See the decisions for the XML field commands.
- **Maps.** A `Map` has no declared fields (its keys are data, not schema), so the field commands are not offered on a map node. Map entries are added, renamed, and removed as data: in the data tree, *Add entry...* on the map's context menu and *Rename key...* on an entry's ask for the key in a small dialog, which refuses an empty key or one that is there already (owner, 2026-11-01). A new entry holds what a new value of the map's type holds, and an absent map is made. A renamed entry keeps its place among the others, and the formulas on it and inside it go with it (a column formula inside it ends for those items); each is one entry in the history (`map_keys.rs`, `Calc::rename_key`; tests `map_keys_tests.rs`).
- **Shared types.** If the object type is a named type (`$defs` with `$ref`, or a named `xs:complexType`) that the schema uses in several places, a command changes the type, and so **every place it is used**. The confirmation lists those places (for example *also used at Orders.billing and Orders.shipping*), and the data change applies at each. Renaming a global `xs:element` rewrites every `xs:element ref` to it in the same schema.
- **The item type itself.** Renaming or deleting a type is not a command of the data tab. It is done on the schema sheet, as ordinary tree editing, and references to it are not rewritten (an unresolved reference is a schema error shown on the sheet).
- **The schema sheet is also open.** The schema sheet and the data tab are two views of the same workbook state, so the schema sheet's grid simply updates. If the schema sheet has a cell edit in progress that is not yet committed, the command is refused with a message to finish or cancel that edit first.

## Tabular view of arrays

An array whose items are objects can be shown as a table instead of as indented tree rows.
- **Toggling.** The user right-clicks the array node's row. A context menu offers **Tabular view**, a toggle that switches it on or off. The menu item appears only on arrays of objects.
- **Persistence.** The setting is per array node and saved in the tab's overlay, as view state. It is not part of the data, not undoable, and not part of the trust hash.

**What the table shows**
- One **row** per item, with the item index in a row header.
- One **column** per scalar field of the item type, in the schema's declared order, with the field name as the header. Hidden columns and rows are not drawn (see Sorting, filtering, column order, and hiding).
- A field that is itself an object is **flattened** into dotted columns (`address.city`), to any depth.
- A field that is an array or a map has a **summary cell** (for example `[5 items]`). Opening it drills into that value as an ordinary tree below the table, so tables do not nest.
- Optional fields that are absent appear as empty cells that can be filled.

**Cells are the same cells as in the tree.** A table cell is the node's cell, with all the same behaviour: literal editing with the type-aware editors, formulas, error values, the pending marker, the invalid-data outline, and comments (on XML tabs). Selecting one shows its formula in the formula bar. Formulas refer to nodes by path as always (`Orders.items[2].price`); the table is a different way of drawing the tree, not different data.

**Editing in the table**
- Add a row at the end, insert above or below, delete rows, and move rows up or down. These are structural edits and are undoable.
- **Moving a row up or down swaps it with the next visible row.** The two rows exchange positions in the array, and any hidden rows in between stay where they are. Move up is unavailable on the first visible row, and move down on the last.
- A formula on the array node itself makes the whole array computed, so the table is read-only.
- Cell ranges copy and paste as tab-separated text. A paste validates each cell and is applied as one undoable entry; cells that don't validate are rejected with a message and nothing is written.

**Sorting, filtering, column order, and hiding**

*Filtering hides the rows that do not match.* A column header menu offers **Filter**, as in Excel. Rows that are filtered out are **hidden**, and hidden in exactly the sense defined under Hiding below: they still exist logically, keep their indices, and are read, validated, saved, and recalculated as usual. A filter changes what is drawn and nothing else, so unlike a sort it is not an edit of the data and is not undoable.
- **Conditions.** A column can be filtered in two ways, which combine: by a **list of values** (checkboxes of the distinct values in the column, including `(blank)` for absent values and `(errors)`), and by a **condition** chosen for the column's type: equals, does not equal, greater or less than, between, begins with, ends with, contains (strings, with an ignore-case option), and is blank. Comparisons use Python's ordering for the type.
- **Several columns** can be filtered together. A row is shown only if it passes every column's filter.
- **Indicator.** A filtered column shows a funnel on its header, and the table shows how many rows are shown, for example `12 of 340`. The marker where rows are hidden counts them separately from rows the user hid by hand.
- **Clearing.** The header menu has **Clear filter** for a column, and the table has **Clear all filters**. Clearing makes the rows visible again unless they were also hidden by hand.
- **Live, with one exception.** A filter is re-evaluated as the data changes, including when recalculation changes a computed value. The exception is rows the user has just added or edited: such a row stays visible even if it no longer matches, until the filter is next changed or the user chooses **Reapply filter**. This stops a row vanishing while it is being typed into.
- **Filtering a computed array** is allowed, since it changes only the view.
- **Where.** Filtering is available in the tabular view only.
- **State.** Filters are view state, saved with the tab in the overlay (by field name), not part of the data, and not part of the trust hash.

*Sorting changes the data.* **Sort ascending** and **Sort descending** in the context menu of a column header sort the array's items by that column (a click on the header selects the column, see Column formulas). The sort is a real reorder of the array, so it is logical and not just visual: after sorting `Data.items`, the reference `Data.items[2]` points at whatever item is now third. References are literal (see Formulas), so nothing is adjusted.
- **It is permanent.** A sort is an edit of the data, like any other. The table holds no sort state and shows no sort indicator, and the only way back is the **Undo** command (it is one undoable entry).
- **Python's ordering, in every case.** Items are compared with Python's own ordering of the column's values (numbers numerically, strings by code point, dates chronologically). There is no special handling of absent values or error values: if the ordering raises an exception, for example comparing a string with a number, or comparing an absent value with a number, or reading an error value, **the sort does not happen**. Nothing is changed, and a message gives Python's error and the rows involved. A column with absent or error cells therefore cannot be sorted until they are filled or fixed. To help, the message has a **Show these rows** button, which applies a filter that shows only the offending rows, so they can be fixed and the sort tried again.
- **Stable.** Items with equal values keep their existing relative order (a descending sort is also stable). This is what makes a multi-column sort work with no extra feature: sort by the least important column first, and then by each more important one, so that **each new sort becomes the first key and the earlier sorts remain as the tie-breakers** among equal values.
- **All items are sorted, hidden ones included.** Hidden rows, whether hidden by hand or by a filter, still exist logically, so a sort reorders the whole array. The visible rows come out in the new order and the hidden ones are placed by the same rule.
- Formulas belong to their location, not to an item. A sort therefore moves the literal values, comments, and children of each item, but a formula stays at its position and is recalculated against the item that is now there.
- A computed (formula-driven) array is read-only and cannot be sorted. The header click explains why, and a formula can use `SORT` instead.

*Reversing the order.* The array's context menu and the table's toolbar have **Reverse row order**, which reverses the whole array without naming any column. It is a permanent edit like a sort, applies to hidden items too, and is unavailable on a computed array. It can be reversed in either of two ways: with the **Undo** command, or by **executing Reverse row order again**, since reversing twice restores the original order exactly. Executing it again is a new edit of its own, so it adds another entry to the history rather than removing one, and Redo is not available afterwards.

*Column order changes the field order.* With auto-update on for the tab's schema, dragging a column header to a new position reorders the **fields** of the item type, and so the field order of every item (see Editing the schema from a data tab). Without auto-update the columns cannot be dragged, and the tooltip explains why.
- The field order is what Python sees when it iterates an object's fields, and what files are written with. For XSD it is also the order of elements in the XML.
- Dotted columns (fields of a nested object) can be reordered only among the fields of the same parent. A column cannot be dragged to a different parent.

*Hiding.* Columns, rows, and any other data value can be **hidden**, and any hidden value can be **unhidden**.
- **Hidden values still exist logically.** Hiding, whether by hand or by a filter, changes only what is drawn. A hidden row keeps its index (`Data.items[2]` is still the third item), formulas read hidden values as usual, they are validated, saved, exported, and recalculated like any other, and their errors still count in the status bar.
- **Where.** Hide a column from its header menu, a row from its row header menu, and any node from its context menu in the tree view. Hiding a node or row hides everything inside it.
- **Marker.** Where something is hidden, a thin marker sits between its visible neighbours (as in Excel). A hidden node's parent shows the count, for example `(3 hidden)`; in a filtered table the marker gives the two counts apart, for example `(3 hidden, 5 filtered)`.
- **Unhiding.** The marker, and the context menu of the parent or array node, open a list of the hidden items with **Show** for each and **Show all**, which cancels only manual hiding. Rows hidden by a filter come back by clearing the filter.
- **State.** Hidden state is view state. It is saved with the tab (in the overlay) but is not part of the data, is not undoable, and is not part of the trust hash. A hidden row or node stays hidden when it moves, for example after a sort, because the state is attached to the item and is saved by path. Hidden columns are recorded by field name.
- **Keyboard navigation skips hidden items.** The arrow keys, Tab, Home and End, and Page Up and Down move only among items that are shown, in both the table and the tree view. If the selected item becomes hidden, because the user hid it or a filter changed, the selection moves to the nearest shown item.
- **Copy and paste** include hidden items. A copied range is the whole logical rectangle between the selection's corners, so hidden rows (whether hidden by hand or by a filter) and hidden columns inside it are copied along with the visible ones. A paste is likewise cell for cell by logical position from the target cell, writing into hidden cells in the same way, so what is pasted matches what was copied. The same validation applies to every pasted cell, hidden or not.

**Performance.** Both the table and the tree view only draw the rows that are on screen, so arrays of many thousands of items stay responsive. The table can be built on `egui_extras`' virtualised table support.

## Import and export

**Importing a standalone file** creates new tabs in the workbook: a data tab, bound to a schema sheet. *File > Import...* chooses the file, and **dropping files on the window** imports each of them the same way (a dropped `.gossia` file is opened instead). **If the data fits a schema sheet already in the workbook, it is given that one; if it fits none, a schema is inferred from it** and becomes a new schema sheet, to which the data tab is bound by the normal sheet-reference rule.

| File | Data tab format | Inferred schema |
|---|---|---|
| `.json` | JSON | JSON Schema |
| `.xml` | XML | XSD |
| `.csv` (comma-separated), `.tsv` or `.tab` (tab-separated) | JSON | JSON Schema for an array of objects, one per row |
| `.xlsx`, `.xlsm`, `.xlsb`, `.xls`, `.ods` (0.4.0) | JSON, a tab for each sheet ticked | JSON Schema for an array of objects, one per row, for each |
| anything else (plain text) | JSON | JSON Schema for an array of strings |

- **CSV and TSV** *(required by the owner on 2026-10-28)* import as an array with an object for each row (`delimited.rs` in `gossia-core`). Cells are read as RFC 4180 has them: a cell in double quotes may hold the separator, line breaks and `""` for a quote; unquoted cells are trimmed of spaces, and blank lines are skipped.
  - **A header row is assumed**, and its cells name the fields. **Only when it cannot be a header, because one of its cells is a number or `true`/`false`, is it data**, and the columns are named `column_1`, `column_2`, `column_3`, ... An empty header cell is named the same way (`column_N` for its place), and a name that repeats gets `_2`, `_3`. Cells beyond the header's names are named from their place too. The report says which way the first row was read.
  - **Each column is typed as a whole**: whole numbers if every value in it is one, numbers if every value is a number, yes or no if every value is `true` or `false` (in any case), and text otherwise. So a column of codes keeps `007` as text, and a column with one word in it is all text. A quoted value is text. **An empty cell leaves the field out**, so a column with an empty cell is an optional field.
  - The fields of the schema keep the order of the columns.
- **Pasting** *(required by the owner on 2026-10-28)*. **Ctrl+V in the Raw view imports the clipboard as if it were a file** dropped on the window: the import dialog opens for it, titled *Import the pasted text*, and the tab is called `Pasted` (`Pasted2`, ...). With no file name, the format is told from the text: `{` or `[` first is JSON, `<` is XML, a tab in the first line is TSV (a range copied from a spreadsheet), a comma CSV, and anything else lines of text. When the paste is in the format of the tab shown (JSON on a JSON tab, XML on an XML tab), the dialog offers one more choice, **replacing that tab's data** instead of making a new tab, which is what a paste in the Raw view did before: the document must conform to the tab's schema, or it is refused with the reasons, and it is one undoable edit. A new tab stays the default.
- **Plain text** imports as though it were a JSON array of strings, one per line. Line endings are normalised, a final newline does not add an empty item, and blank lines in the middle are kept as empty strings. The text must be UTF-8 (a BOM is stripped); anything else is refused with a message.
- **Matching existing schemas.** Every schema sheet of the file's format (JSON Schema for JSON and text, XSD for XML) is tried: the data is read against it and validated. The dialog lists them with how many values would not fit each (an XSD whose root element is not the file's is left out), and starts on the first that the data fits completely, in tab order, or on the inferred schema when none does. The user may still choose any of them, or the inferred schema; data loaded against a schema it does not fit follows the normal invalid-data rules (kept, flagged, unrecognised content preserved).
- **Names.** The tabs are named from the file name, sanitised to a valid identifier (invalid characters become `_`; a leading digit, a leading underscore, a reserved word, or a clash with an existing name gets a prefix or numeric suffix). The schema sheet is `<Name>Schema`. The dialog lets the user change both before importing.
- **Import report.** A dialog shows the inferred schema and every warning (dropped nulls, type conflicts, oversized integers, a dropped DOCTYPE) before the user confirms. Nothing is added to the workbook until then. Several dropped files get one dialog each, in turn.
- **Undo.** An import is one entry in the edit log, *Import orders.json*, which takes out both tabs. An inferred schema sheet starts with auto-update off, so that the next file of the same shape can be given it as well.
- Parsing is bounded: limits on file size and nesting depth guard against hostile input (application preferences; see Settings and limits). Importing never runs Python.

**Schema inference** always produces a schema inside the v1 subset (closed objects, arrays, simple types, no unions or nulls). It is a starting point; the user can edit the schema sheet afterwards.

| Observed data | Inferred schema |
|---|---|
| Object | Object with properties in observed order. A property present in every observed instance is `required`; otherwise optional. |
| Array of objects | One item type; properties merged across all items, required only if present in every item. |
| Scalars | `string`, `integer`, `number`, or `boolean`. A mix of integers and non-integers is `number`. |
| Empty array | Items typed `string`, and flagged in the report as "inferred from no data". |
| `null` | Cannot be represented (no nulls in v1). Treated as absent: the property becomes optional; a null array item is dropped. Both are reported. This holds whichever schema the data is given. A property that is only ever null is an optional `string`, reported as inferred from no data. |
| JSON strings | Plain `string`. Date-like strings are not given a `format`. |

- **Conflicts.** If the same position holds different kinds of value, the **most common kind wins** (a tie goes to the more general: string over number over integer over boolean). Values of the other kinds are not converted or discarded: they stay in the tree as invalid literals, flagged. A conflict between structures (an object where most instances have a string, or vice versa) keeps the minority subtree as unrecognised content, flagged.
- **XML** specifics:
  - The root element becomes the single global element. Nested elements are anonymous types.
  - An element that repeats under its parent anywhere in the data becomes an array (`maxOccurs="unbounded"`); an attribute becomes `xs:attribute`, required if always present.
  - Element text is typed by its values: integers → `integer`, decimals → `decimal`, `true`/`false` → `boolean`, ISO 8601 dates and datetimes → `date` / `dateTime`, otherwise `string`. Values with leading zeros (other than `0`) are strings, so identifiers like `007` are not turned into numbers.
  - Child order is the merge of the orders seen across instances; if instances disagree irreconcilably, the first-seen order is used and the report says so.
  - **Refused with a clear message:** mixed content (non-whitespace text next to child elements), and more than one namespace. These are outside the v1 XSD subset.

**Other file formats** (chosen by the owner on 2026-10-31, before 0.1.0 is closed: YAML, TOML, JSON Lines and JSON5 now; Excel and ODS in 0.4.0).
- **Each format is a tab format of its own**, as XML is (owner: "Own tab format"). A tab keeps the format it came in: it is saved in that format inside the `.gossia` (`data.yaml`, `data.toml`, `data.json5`, `data.jsonc`, `data.jsonl`; the manifest records the tab's format), and exported in it by default, so a file goes in and comes out with its key order and, where the format has them, its comments.
- **All of them are JSON-shaped, so they use JSON Schema.** Schema sheets, inference, validation, formulas, forms and Python work exactly as on a JSON tab; Python never sees which format a tab is.
- **Comments on nodes extend to the formats that have comments**: YAML (`#`), TOML (`#`), JSON5 and JSONC (`//` and `/* */`). A comment on the line or lines above a key or item is that node's comment, as for XML; one at the end of the line it is on is kept with the node too, and is written back on the line above it. JSON and JSON Lines have none in the file, so a JSON or JSON Lines tab keeps its comments in the workbook only (owner, 2026-11-01): they are saved in the tab's overlay in the `.gossia`, the file stays strict JSON, and the Raw view and export leave them out (export says how many it leaves out). Only a tab of COBOL records has no comment commands.
- **Changing a tab's format.** A tab's menu has *Format*, which lists the formats its data can be written in: any JSON-shaped one for a JSON Schema tab, as long as the format can hold the data (TOML needs an object at the root and has no empty value for a missing key; JSON Lines needs an array at the root). Comments are dropped, after asking, only when the new format is one that keeps none at all; a change to JSON or JSON Lines keeps them in the workbook, so it never asks. It is one undoable entry. Export may write any of the formats the tab could change to.
- **Nulls** are treated as on JSON import: absent (see Schema inference). TOML has none.
- **YAML** (YAML 1.2, the core schema, so `yes`, `no`, `on` and `off` are text and only `true`/`false` are yes or no):
  - Read with `saphyr-parser` (MIT or Apache-2.0); comments, which YAML parsers drop, are gathered by Gossia from the lines between the parser's events.
  - **Anchors and aliases** (`&base`, `*base`, `<<: *base`) are expanded on reading, and the import report says so; the tab holds plain copies and writes them as such.
  - **Several documents** in one file (`---`) read as an array of the documents, and the tab remembers it, so that it is written back as documents.
  - **Tags**: the standard ones (`!!str`, `!!int`, `!!float`, `!!bool`, `!!null`) are honoured; any other tag (`!Ref` in a CloudFormation template) is dropped with a warning naming it and its line, and the value is read as it would be without it (owner, 2026-11-01); it is written back without the tag.
  - Written in block style with two spaces, keys and text unquoted where YAML allows it and double-quoted where it does not; flow style (`{a: 1}`) and the original quoting are not kept. An empty line before an entry (above its comment) is kept on its node and written back, except before the first entry of a table or list (owner: "blank lines only").
- **TOML** (TOML 1.0; `toml_edit` 0.25 reads TOML 1.1, which also takes what 1.0 refuses, such as line breaks inside inline tables), read with `toml_edit` (MIT or Apache-2.0) and written by Gossia:
  - The root is always an object, so a TOML tab's schema must have an object at its root.
  - **Dates map onto Gossia's**: an offset date-time is a zoned date-time, a local date-time a date-time without a zone, a local date a date; a local time (`07:30:00`) has no type of its own in Gossia and is read as text, with a warning.
  - Written with a `[table]` for each object that holds other objects, `[[array]]` for arrays of objects, and inline values otherwise; the original choice of inline tables and the spacing within lines are not kept. An empty line before a plain key is kept and written back, as in YAML.
- **JSON5 and JSONC**, one reader for both (Gossia's own, which already passes over comments and trailing commas):
  - JSON5 adds unquoted keys, single-quoted strings, hexadecimal numbers, a leading `+` or `.`, and line breaks escaped inside strings. `Infinity` and `NaN` are refused, as Gossia holds only finite numbers.
  - The tab remembers which it is, by the file's extension (`.json5` or `.jsonc`): JSONC is written as strict JSON with comments (what VS Code's settings and `tsconfig.json` expect), JSON5 with keys unquoted where they can be.
- **JSON Lines** (`.jsonl`, `.ndjson`): one JSON value per line, read as an array; blank lines are passed over; a line that is not JSON is refused naming its line. Written as one compact value per line, with a final newline.
- **Importing and pasting** tell the format from the extension. A paste with no file name is YAML when it starts with `---` or its first line is `key: value`, TOML when it starts with `[table]` or `key = value`, and JSON Lines when several lines each hold a JSON object; otherwise as before.
- **Built in 11a:** `DataFormat` and the JSON5/JSONC reader and writer (`json5.rs`) in `gossia-core`; the tab's format in `Calc` (`format`, `formats_for`, `format_problem`, `comments_lost`, `set_format` with its `Op::Format`), in the manifest (`format`, left out for JSON and XML) and as `data.<format>`; export in any format the tab could be written in; the Raw view in the tab's format; import of `.json5`, `.jsonc`, `.jsonl` and `.ndjson`, and a paste of JSON Lines; *Format* on the tab's menu, which asks before it takes comments off. A tab that cannot be rebound to a schema its format cannot hold (JSON Lines needs an array) says so. Tests: `json5_tests.rs`, `dataformat_tests.rs`, `formats_tests.rs`, and the import, paste and *Format* tests in `tab_commands_tests.rs`.
- **Built in 11b:** `toml_format.rs` in `gossia-core`, reading through `toml_edit`'s document (comments from the white space it keeps around keys, values, tables and array items) and Gossia's own writer: the plain values of a table first, then its `[table]` and `[[array]]` sections; an object inside an array of plain values is an inline table, which has no place for comments; a node holding nothing or an error is left out. `.toml` import and paste. Tests: `toml_format_tests.rs`, and `a_toml_file_becomes_a_toml_tab`.
- **Built in 11c:** `yaml.rs` in `gossia-core`, and two formats: *YAML* (one document) and *YAML documents* (a list, each item written after a `---` line); a `.yaml` or `.yml` file of several documents becomes the second. How comments are placed, since YAML has no brackets to put them inside:
  - comment lines above a key or item are its comment, and a comment after a value on its line is added to it;
  - comment lines at the top of the file are the document's when an empty line parts them from the first key, else the first key's;
  - comment lines after the last entry of a table or list, indented at least as far as its entries, are at its end; less indented ones go on to what follows.
  - Merge keys copy in the fields the table does not have itself (the first of several merged tables wins). Aliases copy at most 1,000,000 values in all, and tables and lists nest at most 512 deep, so that a crafted file cannot exhaust memory.
  - Text that YAML would read as something else is quoted, and so are the words other YAML readers (YAML 1.1) take for yes, no or null, and text that starts like a number; text of several lines is a literal block (`|`). A paste is YAML only when it reads as a table or a list, since any text at all is a YAML string. The Raw view's hint names YAML and TOML. Tests: `yaml_tests.rs`, `dataformat_tests.rs`, and `a_yaml_file_becomes_a_yaml_tab`.
- **Built for the leftovers** (2026-11-01): `Node::blank_before` in `gossia-core` (copied with the comments), set by the YAML and TOML readers and written by their writers; YAML's unknown tags dropped with a warning; `DataFormat::keeps_comments`, the overlay's `comments` in `package.rs`, and `comments_left_out` with the note in the export window. A form field's comment menu has an id of its own: egui gives a context menu the id a dropdown's list uses, so on a tab with comments a click on a dropdown closed its list at once (it showed on XML tabs before, and on every JSON form once JSON tabs had comments). Tests: `core_tags_are_read_and_others_dropped`, `empty_lines_between_entries_are_kept`, `empty_lines_between_keys_are_kept`, `a_json_tab_keeps_its_comments_in_the_workbook`, `a_json_tab_has_the_comment_commands`, `comments_follow_the_tab_through_a_change_of_format`.
- **Not done:** Excel and ODS (0.4.0). **Won't do** (owner, 2026-11-01, "blank lines only"): keeping the original quoting, flow style, inline tables and spacing within lines.

**COBOL copybooks** (asked for by the owner on 2026-10-31: a dropped `*.cpy` is imported as a schema, the data tabs that use it import and export `*.blk` files, and character content is EBCDIC).
- **A third schema language**, beside JSON Schema and XSD (owner: "Copybook schema tab"). A copybook schema tab shows the copybook as a tree under a built-in meta-schema (`builtin:copybook`): each item with its level, name, `PIC`, `USAGE`, `OCCURS` (with `DEPENDING ON`), `REDEFINES` and `VALUE`, nested by level. It is edited like any schema tab and exported back as `.cpy` text in the usual columns (7 to 72). Comment lines (`*` in column 7) are the comments of the items below them.
- **Lowering.** A group is an object; an elementary item is a string (`PIC X`, `PIC A`) with its length as the maximum, or a number (`PIC 9`, with `S` and `V`) with its digits and decimals as the range; `OCCURS` is an array with that many items at most; `FILLER` takes up its bytes but is not a field. The `01` level is the record; a copybook with several `01` records offers them as choices when a data tab is bound. A copybook meant to be copied in under an `01` of the program (IBM MQ's `CMQMDV` starts at level 10) has its records at its top level: whatever items are at the top are the records. Comment lines may start with several stars (`**`).
- **The data file, `.blk`**, holds **one record** (owner: "Fixed-length, no RDW"; one record, owner, 2026-11-01: "just have the object, single instance"): exactly the record's length, or a variable-length record after its record descriptor word (below), and a file of any other length is refused, files of several records included. A data tab's root is the record object itself. **The exception is a record whose top item has `OCCURS`** (owner, 2026-11-01): the root is then that table, an array of its items (of objects for a group, of values for an elementary item); its counter, if it has `DEPENDING ON`, would be outside the record, so that is refused. `.blk` is the format of data tabs bound to a copybook, as XML is for an XSD.
- **Character content is EBCDIC.** The code page is a setting of the copybook schema tab, **037 by default** (owner: "Per schema, 037 default"); also 1140 (037 with the euro), 500, 273, 1141, 285 and 297. On reading, trailing spaces of a `PIC X` field are kept as they are in the text; on writing, text is padded with spaces to the field's length, and text too long or with a character the code page lacks is refused naming the field.
- **Numbers** (owner: all four of these as well as `DISPLAY`):
  - `DISPLAY` (zoned decimal): a digit a byte, the sign in the zone of the last byte (`C` or `F` positive, `D` negative), or `SIGN LEADING`/`TRAILING SEPARATE`.
  - `COMP-3` / `PACKED-DECIMAL`: two digits a byte, the sign in the last nibble.
  - `COMP`, `BINARY`, `COMP-4` and `COMP-5`: big-endian binary of 2, 4 or 8 bytes by the number of digits; `COMP-5` uses the whole range of the bytes, the others are held to the digits of the `PIC`.
  - `COMP-1` and `COMP-2` (floating point, no `PIC`): 4 and 8 bytes, a number with no range. How they hold it is a setting of the copybook schema tab beside the code page, **IBM hexadecimal by default, or IEEE 754** (big-endian; owner, 2026-11-01), `floatFormat` in its tree (`hex` or `ieee`, left out for hex). A `COMP-1` is read with the fewest digits that are still its bytes (`0.1`, not `0.100000001`); a `COMP-2` in IBM hexadecimal has 56 bits, three more than a value holds, so its last bits may change when it is written back. A number too large for the bytes is refused naming the field; one too small is written as zero.
  - The implied decimal point (`V`) gives a number with that many decimals; a whole number is an integer. Bytes that are not a valid number (spaces in a `PIC 9`, a bad sign nibble) are read as an error value naming the field and kept, so the record can still be written back unchanged.
- **OCCURS DEPENDING ON.** The array has as many items as the counter field says. The table always takes its maximum size in the record; the bytes after the last item are passed over on reading and written as low-values (`X'00'`). The counter is set from the array's length when the record is written.
- **Variable-length records** (`RECFM=VB`). A file whose first two bytes are its length and whose next two are zero, and which is no longer than the record and its four bytes, is a variable-length record: its record descriptor word, then the record up to the end of its last item, a table with `DEPENDING ON` taking only the items its counter says. The rest of the record is read as low-values; a record whose length is not where its items end is refused, saying both. A file of exactly the record's length is a fixed-length record, unless its word gives that length and the record after it ends exactly where its items do. Importing such a file makes the tab a variable-length record, which is saved and exported with its record descriptor word (`recfm: "VB"` in the manifest); *Record* on the tab's menu switches it with *Variable length (VB, with a record descriptor word)*, an undoable entry. `RAW` of the root of such a tab is its file, with the word, and a root formula's bytes may have one or not.
- **SYNC** (`SYNCHRONIZED`, with or without `LEFT` or `RIGHT`). A binary item of up to 4 digits starts on a halfword (a multiple of 2 from the start of the record), a longer one and a `COMP-1` on a fullword (4), a `COMP-2` on a doubleword (8), with slack bytes before it, as IBM Enterprise COBOL does. On a group it is on each of its items. A table whose occurrences hold such items has each occurrence made a multiple of its largest boundary, so every occurrence is aligned as the first. Other items are not moved.
- **RENAMES** (level 66; owner, 2026-11-01: a layout choice like REDEFINES). `66 NAME RENAMES A THRU B.` names the run of items from `A` to `B`, and `66 NAME RENAMES A.` the one item. It comes after the last item of its record and belongs to that record (`renames` of the record in the tree, each with `name`, `from`, `thru` and its comment); a tree saved while they were comment lines (`66 X RENAMES A.`) has those lines read as RENAMES entries. **It is a group of layouts as a REDEFINES is**: *Layouts* offers the items renamed (`A THRU B`) or the renamed field, which is one text field over their bytes (a single elementary item renamed keeps what it is), and changing it converts the data in the same way. The items renamed must be beside each other at one level, with no `OCCURS`, not inside a table, and not in a REDEFINES or renamed already; each is refused with the reason.
- **REDEFINES** (owner, 2026-10-31: "the user picks what is displayed"). Of the layouts over the same bytes (the redefined item and each item that redefines it), **the tab shows one, which the user picks**; it starts on the first. The choice is made on the tab, for that group of layouts (in each item, when it is inside a table), and is an undoable entry saved with the tab.
  - **Changing it converts the data**: the record's bytes for that area are written from the layout shown and read again with the one picked, so the values are what those bytes mean in the new layout. Bytes the new layout cannot read (letters in a `PIC 9`) become error values naming the field, kept so the bytes can still be written back.
  - **Formulas in the area are deleted, after asking.** When the layout shown has formulas on its fields (or a column formula on them), the user is asked to agree to deleting them before the change; *No* leaves the layout and the formulas as they were. With no formulas there, the change is made without asking.
  - The record is always written from the layout shown.
- **Importing and exporting.** Dropping a `.cpy` makes a copybook schema tab (with the members of its `COPY` statements in it, below). A `.blk` is imported into a data tab bound to a copybook schema tab already in the workbook (a `.blk` alone says nothing about its layout, so no schema is inferred), and a data tab bound to a copybook exports as `.blk`. It is binary, so the Raw view shows a hexadecimal dump with the EBCDIC text beside it, read-only.
- **Condition names** (level 88; owner, 2026-11-01: a dropdown, other values allowed). They are entries of their own, not comments: each item of the schema tab's tree has `conditions`, a list of `name` and `values` (the literals as written, `'C' 'X'` or `1 THRU 9`), each with its own comment, and the `.cpy` text writes them back as `88` entries under their item. A tree saved while they were still comment lines (`88 ACTIVE VALUE 'A'.`) has those lines read as conditions. The values they stand for are the **choices** of the field: the Default form, and any form whose control has no *choices* of its own, shows the field as a dropdown of the codes and names (`O — STATE-OPEN`), storing the value (`'O'`). The type is not limited by them, as COBOL does not limit the field: any other value still reads, and is shown as `Z (not in the list)`. Whether a code not in the list can be typed in is the control's *Allow other values (write-in codes)*, which the generated form turns on for such a field; a table's column has it off until it is ticked. A name with several values (`VALUES 'C' 'X'`) is an item for each; **a range (`THRU`) is an item for each value in it**: whole numbers, or single characters in the order of their bytes in the code page (as COBOL compares them; `'A' THRU 'I'` in EBCDIC), printable ones only, and a range of more than 100 values is left out. `WHEN SET TO FALSE` is left out; `SPACES` and `ZERO` are the field filled with them. Text is padded with spaces to the field's length, as it is read.
- **COPY** statements (`COPY NAME`, with `OF` or `IN` a library, `SUPPRESS`, and `REPLACING`). A copybook file imported from disk has each member it copies in read from its own folder (`NAME.cpy`, `.cbl`, `.cob` or no extension, as written or in lower case), members inside members up to 10 deep. `REPLACING` takes pairs of pseudo-text (`==:P:== BY ==CUST==`) or words (`OLD-CODE BY KIND`), and `LEADING` or `TRAILING` for the start or end of a word; whole words are matched, and a tag in colons (`:P:`) is also replaced inside words, as IBM's compiler does. The schema tab holds the copybook with the members in it: its text has no `COPY` statements. A member that is not there is refused, naming it. Pasted text with `COPY` in it is not taken for a copybook, as there is no folder to read members from.
- **Changing a tab's record.** *Record* on the menu of a tab of records lists the records of its copybook, the one it holds checked; one of another length is greyed, saying both lengths. Picking one reads the tab's bytes with that record's layout, showing the first layout of each group of REDEFINES, as one undoable entry. The tab's formulas are for fields that are no longer there, so they are deleted, after a Yes/No question that counts them.
- **Field names.** A field's key is its COBOL name with `-` as `_` (`CUST-NAME` is `CUST_NAME`), so that formulas can name it without quotes; the copybook keeps the name as written. Two items of one table whose keys would be the same are refused.
- **Built in 11d-1:** `copybook/` in `gossia-core`:
  - `mod.rs` reads `.cpy` text, fixed form or free form, into `Copybook`. Comment lines go with the entry below them, and level 88 entries become the item's condition names. *(Level 66, `SYNC`, `COMP-1` and `COMP-2` were refused or kept as comments then: see Built for the COBOL gaps.)*
  - It also writes the text back (columns 8 to 72, with continuation lines for long literals) and turns a copybook into the schema tab's tree and back.
  - `ebcdic.rs` and `tables.rs` hold the seven code pages, taken from Windows' own and checked against Python's.
  - `lower.rs` makes the type and the byte `Layout`, which a `TypeSpec` carries as it carries `XmlInfo`; a tab holds one of its 01 records (the first unless 11d-3's import picks another).
  - `records.rs` reads and writes `.blk` files: zoned, packed, binary and `COMP-5` numbers, `OCCURS DEPENDING ON` counters, a choice among `REDEFINES` layouts, and unreadable bytes kept in the error value's message (`X'4040'`) and written back.
  - `metaschema::copybook_meta` is the meta-schema.
  - Tests: `copybook_tests.rs` and `records_tests.rs`.
- **Built in 11d-2:** in `gossia-calc`:
  - `SchemaFormat::Copybook`, with `add_copybook_sheet` and `create_copybook_schema_tab`. The schema tab holds the copybook's tree under `builtin:copybook` and is kept as JSON5, so its items' comments are saved. Its tabs' fields are changed on it, not from the tabs.
  - `DataFormat::Blk` for the tabs bound to a copybook. Like XML, it is read and written with its schema.
  - `copybooks.rs`: `records_bytes`, `read_records`, and the REDEFINES choice (`redefine_groups`, `redefine_formulas`, `set_redefine` with its `Op::Redefine`, one undoable entry that deletes the formulas in the layout shown). The choice is saved in the manifest as `redefines`.
  - Export gives `.blk` bytes (`export_bytes`) and `.cpy` text (`ExportFormat::Copybook`).
  - Tests: `copybooks_tests.rs`.
- **Built in 11d-3:** in the window:
  - **Importing.** A dropped or imported `.cpy` becomes a copybook schema tab (the dialog offers nothing else). A `.blk` is offered with each copybook schema tab, and each of its records, whose length is the file's, with how many values would not read (`Customer (SHORT-REC): the data fits it`); the first that reads cleanly is chosen. With none, the import is refused, saying to import the copybook first.
  - **The 01 record** a tab holds is kept on the tab (`record` in the manifest, left out for the first). Lowering takes it (`copybook::lower_record`), and a change to the copybook gives each tab the layout of its own record. *New tab* with an existing copybook, and *Change schema...*, use the tab's record, or the first.
  - **The Raw view** of a tab of records is a hexadecimal dump of its `.blk` bytes: sixteen bytes a line with the offset in the file, and the text in the code page beside them (`.` for a control character). Only the first 64 KiB are shown, with the rest counted. Its heading names the record, its length and the code page. A copybook schema tab's Raw view is its `.cpy` text.
  - **Layouts** on the menu of a tab of records: a submenu for each group of `REDEFINES` (`AREA-TEXT / AREA-NUMBER`), the layout shown checked, `FILLER` greyed. When the layout shown has formulas, a Yes/No question names them first.
  - **Code page** on the menu of a copybook schema tab sets its `codePage`. The values in the tabs stay as they are and are written in the new code page; to read a file again in another code page, import it again.
  - *New tab* offers *COBOL copybook (records)* beside JSON Schema and XSD: a copybook with a record named after the tab and one field, and auto-update off, since its fields are added on the copybook tab.
  - *Change schema...* lists the schema tabs of the tab's own language (a copybook for a tab of records).
  - Tests: `copybook_tests.rs` in `gossia-app`.
- **Built in 11d-4** (2026-11-01):
  - **Bytes are not a kind of value in the data.** `Engine::eval_answer` gives an `Answer`, a value or bytes, and only a formula on a node is run through it; `Engine::eval`, which control expressions and the Immediate window use, turns bytes into a `#TYPE!` error ("only a formula on a node can give" them). A formula of a column formula gives the same error, saying to give the field its own formula. Bytes inside a list or a dict cannot be stored, as any other unknown object.
  - **`node_bytes.rs`** in `gossia-calc` holds both directions. In a tab of records, `copybook::records::place` finds where a node's bytes are (a table not indexed is all of its occurrences); `RAW` writes the record and cuts them out, and bytes for a field are put into the record as the rest of it is now, without the field, and the record read again with the layouts the tab shows. Bytes of the wrong length are an error that says how many the field takes.
  - **In other tabs**, a node that holds other values is written and read in the tab's format: the root as the tab's file (the XML declaration and namespaces included), any other node as a piece of it (an element for XML; JSON for an item of JSON Lines and for what in a TOML tab is not a table; YAML for an item of YAML documents). **A single value is its text** as UTF-8: a string as it is, without quotes, a number, a boolean or a date as the cell shows it, and read back as typing it would be. Bytes that are not UTF-8 are an error.
  - **`RAW` of a single value.** A formula gets a single value as a plain Python value, which does not know its node. The proxy that hands one out keeps it, with where it is, until the next one; `RAW` of that same object uses that place, so `RAW(Orders[0].BALANCE)` works as written. `RAW` of anything else, such as `RAW(1 + 2)`, is a `TypeError` that says it takes a node.
  - The four conversion functions are in `gossia-std` (Text); `BASE64DECODE` and `HEXDECODE` of empty text give no bytes. `RAW` is there too (Data), and `raw` is beside `JSONPATH` in the prelude.
  - Tests: `node_bytes_tests.rs` in `gossia-calc`.
- **Built for the COBOL gaps** (2026-11-01):
  - In `gossia-core`'s `copybook/`: `Usage::Comp1` and `Comp2`, `Item.sync`, `Item.renames` (`Renames`), and `Copybook.float` (`FloatFormat`), each in the text, the tree and the meta-schema. `copies.rs` is `parse_with`, which expands `COPY` on the tokens before the entries are read; `parse` is it with no members. `lower.rs` has `Element::Float`, `Layout.float`, `alignment` for SYNC, `apply_renames`, and `RedefineSet.keys` as the keys of each layout (several for a run renamed, none for `FILLER`); condition names keep their ranges and `range_values` lists them. `records.rs` has `decode_float` and `encode_float`, and `has_rdw`, `used_length`, `write_variable` and `with_rdw`; `read` takes either kind of file.
  - In `gossia-calc`'s `copybooks.rs`: `set_variable` (`Op::Variable`) and `is_variable`, `record_problem` and `set_record` (`Op::Record`, with the formulas' and REDEFINES choices' own entries and an `Op::Replace`), and `set_redefine` swapping layouts of several fields. `records_bytes` writes the record descriptor word for a variable-length tab.
  - In the window: *Floating point* beside *Code page* on a copybook schema tab; *Record* on a tab of records, with the records and *Variable length (VB, with a record descriptor word)*, and its Yes/No window; `import::copied_in` reading members beside a dropped or imported `.cpy`; the VB flag set when the imported file has a record descriptor word; the Raw view's heading saying a record is variable length.
  - **Pasting a copybook.** Text that reads as a copybook is told from the other formats when pasted, before YAML, CSV and TSV. The import dialog makes it a copybook schema tab; pasted on a copybook schema tab, it also offers *replace the data of* that tab, which puts the text in place of the tab's and keeps its code page and floating point format, which text does not say.
  - Tests: `copybook_tests.rs` and `records_tests.rs` in `gossia-core`, `copybooks_tests.rs` in `gossia-calc`, and `copybook_tests.rs` in `gossia-app`.
- **Copying and pasting records** (asked for by the owner on 2026-11-01). *Copy* in the Raw view of a tab of records copies all of its bytes as the preference *Copy as* (Options, Files and import, *COBOL records*; `files.copy_records_as`) says: the hex dump as shown (default, `hex_dump`), hex digits (`hex`), or Base64 (`base64`). The dump copied is never cut short at 64 KiB as the view is. A paste in the Raw view of a tab of records is read as any of the three (a dump first, then hex digits, then Base64, since hex digits are Base64 too but Base64 of bytes is almost never only hex digits); a dump is recognised on any tab. The import dialog then offers the copybook records as long as the bytes, as for a `.blk` file, and, when the bytes are as long as the tab's own record, *replace the data of* that tab, read with its layout and the layouts it shows. The codecs are `gossia_core::binary`, kept for the bytes value of 11d-4.
- **Saving a copybook data tab** in a `.gossia` writes `data.blk`. If a value cannot be written, such as text too long for its field, that tab is saved as `data.json` instead, so saving never loses an edit.
- **Raw memory** (asked for by the owner on 2026-10-31):
  - A new value kind, **bytes**, which formulas pass between them; Python gets and gives `bytes`. A cell never shows or holds bytes (owner, 2026-11-01): bytes a node's formula gives are read by the node's schema (below), so they are never written to a file either.
  - Functions convert it: `BASE64DECODE(text)` and `HEXDECODE(text)` give bytes; `BASE64ENCODE(bytes)` and `HEXENCODE(bytes)` give text.
  - **A formula on a node that gives bytes has them read by that node's schema** (owner, 2026-10-31), the reverse of `RAW`.
    - In a copybook tab, a field's bytes are read as that field, a record's as a record, and the root's as a `.blk` file. For example, the root's formula could be `=BASE64DECODE(Inbox.payload)`.
    - In any other tab, the bytes are read as UTF-8 text of the tab's format, for that node's type.
    - Bytes that do not read as the node's type give an error value saying why.
    - What a formula on a container fills in is its detail: worked out from the bytes and read-only, as under any formula (owner: "Computed, read-only").
  - **Formulas that are not on a node give an error when they return bytes**: visible-when, enabled-when, colour and column expressions, and column formulas (owner, 2026-10-31).
  - **A reference gives the parsed value; `RAW(reference)` gives its bytes** as the tab's schema language holds them (owner, 2026-10-31).
    - In a copybook tab: the field's own bytes (`RAW(Orders[0].BALANCE)` is its packed decimal), a record's bytes, or, for the root, the whole `.blk` file. So `=BASE64ENCODE(RAW(Orders))` sends the records on.
    - In any other tab: the node written in the tab's format, as UTF-8 (an element for XML, a value for the JSON-shaped formats); a single value is its text, a string without quotes (see Built in 11d-4).
    - Python has the same, as `raw(x)`; a plain reference there is the parsed value too.
- **Won't do** (owner, 2026-11-01): `INDEXED BY` (kept but not used); files of several records (blocked, or `RECFM=V` without blocks); `RENAMES` of items under `OCCURS` or in a REDEFINES; `SYNC` boundaries other than IBM's, and the slack between records of a block; `COPY` from a library (`OF` and `IN` are read past) and `COPY` in pasted text. *(The rest of the list once here was built on 2026-11-01: see Built for the COBOL gaps.)*

**Exporting.** Any tree tab exports as a standalone file in its own format.
- Everything the user gave the tab is written: field order, array order, XML attributes and elements as the schema maps them, XML comments (the comments on nodes), namespace prefix and XML declaration, and **unrecognised content** that was preserved on import. The result is what the tab shows, including the latest computed values. Errored nodes are absent (see Stored values).
- Dates are written in their XML lexical forms, floats with their shortest round-trip spelling, and JSON/XML indentation is two spaces by default with a compact option.
- Formulas and tab settings are not part of a standalone export; the `.gossia` is the format that keeps them.
- A schema sheet exports as a standalone `.json` or `.xsd`/`.xml` file in the same way, and the export dialog offers to include the schema alongside the data: the schema tab the data tab is bound to, written beside the file and named after it, `<name>.schema.json` or `<name>.xsd`.
- A tab whose root is an array of strings can also be exported as a plain text file, one string per line, mirroring text import.

## File format

A workbook is saved as a **zip archive with the extension `.gossia`**. It is an ordinary zip, so any zip tool can open it, and the data inside is plain JSON, XML, and Python source.

**Layout**

```
manifest.json            first entry in the archive
sheets/<Tab>/data.json   (or data.xml)   the tab's data tree, with stored values
sheets/<Tab>/overlay.json                formulas and their status
sheets/<Tab>/forms/<Form>.json           a customised form design of the tab
reports/<Report>/design.json             a report's design (0.2.0)
reports/<Report>/cache.json              its results cache: the values of its last run and their parameters (0.2.0)
modules/<name>.py                        Python modules
datasources.json                         data source definitions, no secrets
settings.json                            workbook settings that differ from the defaults (see Settings and limits)
view.json                                view state of the whole workbook: the font of the data views
```

- **Tabs** are one directory each. Tab names are valid identifiers and unique ignoring case (see Names and reserved words), so they are safe as file names on every platform. A schema sheet is stored the same way as any tree sheet (JSON or XML data, according to its meta-schema).
- **Data files** hold the plain data, including the latest computed values, exactly as other tools would expect (see Stored values). Errored nodes are absent from them.
- **Overlay** holds, per node path: the formula text, and its status (ok, pending, or the error kind, message, and traceback). Entries are sorted by path. It also holds the tab's view state, such as which view is current and which arrays are shown as tables. Comments are not in the overlay: they are XML comments in `data.xml`.
- **Reports** are one directory each, named by the report: the design document described in their section, and the results cache (see Report storage and the results cache). **Forms** are stored under the tab they are views of: `sheets/<Tab>/forms/<Form>.json`. A form that has not been customised has no file, so the generated Default form is never stored.
- **Modules** are plain `.py` files, so they can be edited, diffed, and linted with ordinary tools.

**Manifest** (`manifest.json`) records:
- `format_version` (major.minor), and the application version that wrote the file.
- The **workbook id** used by the trust model.
- The **reserved-names version** (see Names and reserved words).
- The **Python engine and its version**, so a file written against one RustPython release can be flagged when opened with another.
- The list of tabs in tab order, each with its kind (`data`, or from 0.2.0 `report`) and, for tree sheets, its **schema binding**: `builtin:jsonschema`, `builtin:xsd`, or `sheet:<Tab>`.
- The list of modules in display order, and for each Data Tab the customised forms in display order.

**Writing**
- Text is UTF-8 with LF line endings. JSON is pretty-printed with a stable key order; data keeps the schema's declared field order.
- Archive entries are written in a fixed order, with a fixed timestamp and no per-save metadata. Saving a workbook whose content hasn't changed produces identical bytes, so a `.gossia` file behaves well in version control, and unzipped it diffs cleanly.
- Saves are atomic: write to a temporary file in the same directory, then rename over the original.
- Saving waits for outstanding evaluation (see Evaluation and scheduling).

**Reading**
- The zip is treated as untrusted input. Entry paths that are absolute or contain `..` are rejected, and limits apply to the number of entries and to total uncompressed size (to guard against zip bombs; these are application preferences, see Settings and limits). Opening a file never runs anything; Python only runs after the trust check.
- **Versioning.** The same major version opens. A newer minor version opens with a warning, and unknown entries are kept and written back unchanged on save. A newer major version is refused with a message saying which version is needed. Older formats are upgraded in memory on open, and the user is warned before the upgraded file overwrites the original.
- The trust hash is computed from the parsed executable content (see Trust model), not from the bytes of the archive. Reordering entries or changing whitespace does not change it.

**Plain data.** Because data files are ordinary JSON or XML, users can extract a tab's data from the archive directly. Importing and exporting standalone files is described in Import and export.

## Application structure

Cargo workspace:
- `gossia-core`: `Value`, `TypeSpec`, paths, schema lowering (JSON Schema, XSD), format adapters, validation. No UI, no Python.
- `gossia-calc`: formula storage, dependency graph, recalculation, error values, proxy objects, `PythonEngine` trait and RustPython implementation.
- `gossia-std`: built-in functions.
- `gossia-web` *(0.3.0; `gossia-worker` until 13b-2)*: the wasm version, one module for both sides: the application drawn in the page behind a JavaScript host, and the engine and the calculation layer run in a Web Worker (see Web port, 0.3.0). Built only for `wasm32-unknown-unknown` in use, though it compiles natively so that the workspace's checks cover it.
- `gossia-app`: egui frontend and file I/O. The only crate touching `eframe` or the filesystem, so that a later wasm port needs only a different `main` and storage layer. Keeping these crates free of UI and filesystem code is a v1 design constraint for that reason.

**Workbook file format**: a zip with the extension `.gossia` (see File format).

**UI**
- Sheet tabs along the bottom; formula bar above a tree grid. A report tab (0.2.0) shows its parameter bar above the rendered report instead, page by page for a multipage report (see Reports).
- Tree grid columns: `Name | Type | Value | Formula`; expandable nodes, array items as indexed rows.
- Type-aware editors: toggles for booleans, dropdowns for enums, numeric fields.
- Schema violations get a red outline; computed cells get a distinct fill.
- Side panels for the error list, and for modules and trust state.
- A view selector for each Data Tab (the Data tree and its forms), and a code area holding Python modules and the raw text of form designs (see Forms).

### Renaming a tab

A tab is renamed from the window, as in Excel.
- **Where.** Double-clicking a sheet tab, or *Rename tab...* in the tab's context menu (right-click on the sheet tab) or in the Edit menu (for the current tab). Each opens the same small dialog, *Rename tab Orders*, with the name field holding the current name, selected.
- **The name is checked live** as it is typed, by the rules of Names and reserved words: a valid Python identifier, not a reserved word, and unique ignoring case. The message under the field names the problem or the clash (for example *A tab called orders already exists*), and *Rename* is disabled until the name is valid. Changing only the case of the name (`orders` to `Orders`) is allowed. A name equal to the current one closes the dialog without an edit. Enter renames, Esc cancels.
- **What the dialog warns about.** Formulas are not rewritten (see Renames do not rewrite formulas), so the dialog says how many formulas and modules mention the old name as an identifier (a button names a module function, never a tab), for example *3 formulas and 1 module use Orders. They are not changed, and will show #NAME! until they are edited.* It is a count, not a list, and the rename goes ahead regardless. A find-and-replace over formulas may be offered later as a separate command, never as part of the rename.
- **What follows the tab.** Everything that belongs to the tab rather than naming it:
  - its data, formulas (each stays at its location), comments, forms and their designs, the current view and the rest of its view state, and its place in the tab order;
  - **schema bindings.** A binding is a reference held by the workbook, not text the user wrote, so it follows the tab: renaming the schema tab `Orders_schema` rebinds every tab bound to `sheet:Orders_schema` to the new name, and the schema bar's *Used by:* line shows the new name of a renamed data tab. Nothing is re-lowered or revalidated, because no schema changes;
  - on save, the tab's directory in the archive (`sheets/<Tab>/`) and its entry in the manifest are written under the new name. The old directory is not kept.
- **What does not follow.** Formula text, button procedures, module source and report expressions that mention the old name are left as they are. After the rename they are recalculated: references to the old name give `#NAME!`, naming it, and anything that already used the new name (and failed) now finds the tab.
- **A name that broke the rules.** A tab opened from a file with a name that breaks the rules (warned about on opening, as described under Where it is checked) is fixed by renaming it: the dialog accepts any valid name, and the next save writes it, so the warning does not come back.
- **Undo.** A rename is one entry in the edit log, *Undo Rename tab Orders_schema to OrderTypes*, and undoing it restores the old name, the old bindings and the old view state together. It is refused while a button procedure is running, like other edits.
- **Trust.** A rename changes no formula, module or setting. It is an edit made in the app, so it keeps the workbook's trust, and the next save records the digest again (the digest names each formula by its tab, so it does change).
- **The control channel** gets a `rename_tab` command (old name, new name) for the end-to-end tests, answering with the problem when the name is refused.

### Managing tabs

Tabs are added, deleted and given another schema from the window. The commands are in the Edit menu (for the current tab) and in a sheet tab's context menu, together with *Rename tab...* and *Import...*; a `+` after the last tab is *New tab...*.
- **New tab...** asks for the name and the schema: **its own schema**, a new empty JSON Schema sheet `<Name>Schema` with auto-update on, so that its fields are added from the data tab (as *File > New* makes), or **the schema of** a schema sheet already there, in which case the tab starts with what a new item of that type holds (required fields filled in). Both names are checked as a rename is. New tabs go at the end, and the new tab is shown.
  - **Data or report** *(0.2.0, owner 2026-11-02)*: the dialog starts with what kind of tab to make, **Data** (the default, with the choices of schema and data format described here) or **Report**. For a report it asks the name, the kind (single page or multipage) and, optionally, a tab and an array in it to start from (see Reports, The report designer, *A starting point*; greyed until multipage reports); the schema and format choices are not shown. The name is checked as any tab name is. *New report...* on the Edit menu and the tab menu opens the same dialog with Report chosen. The new report tab goes at the end and is shown, and, as *New tab Orders* is, it is one entry in the edit log (*New report Sales*).
  - **Data format** (asked for by the owner on 2026-11-01): for a JSON Schema, own or there already, the dialog also asks what the data is written in: JSON, JSON5, JSON with comments, JSON Lines, TOML, YAML or YAML documents. It is the tab's format from the start, as *Format* on the tab's menu would set it, so the Raw view, saving and export use it; it is set in the same history entry. Its own schema for JSON Lines or YAML documents is a list of objects, since those formats hold a list; a schema that is there must have a root the format can hold (TOML an object, the two list formats an array), or the dialog says why. An XSD or a copybook sets the format itself, and the choice is greyed. The control channel's `new_tab` takes `format` (`"yaml"`, `"toml"`, ...).
- **Delete tab...** asks first, and says how many formulas and modules use the tab's name (*3 formulas use Orders; they will show #NAME! until the tab is back*). It is refused for the only tab of a workbook, and for a schema sheet that tabs use (*Orders uses this schema; delete that tab or give it another schema first*). The tab after it is shown, or the one before for the last tab.
- **Change schema...** binds a data tab to another schema sheet of the same format: JSON data takes a JSON Schema and XML data an XSD, because a tab keeps its format. The data is not changed; the dialog says how many values would not fit, and they are flagged. A schema sheet with auto-update on that another tab uses is refused, as for any binding.
- **Report tabs** *(0.2.0, owner 2026-11-02)*. A report tab's menu has *Edit report*, *Export as PDF...*, *Copy batch command*, *Rename tab...*, *Duplicate* and *Delete tab...*.
  - **Rename** checks the name as for any tab. Nothing in the workbook names a report, so nothing changes elsewhere, and the dialog gives no warning (command lines that name the report are the user's to change).
  - **Duplicate** makes a copy of the design, named `<Report>_2` (or the next free number) and placed after it, with no results cache, so it is drawn as never calculated until it is shown.
  - **Delete** asks first and takes the design and the cache with it.
- **Undo.** Each is one entry in the edit log (*New tab Notes*, *Delete tab Orders*, *Bind Prices to Orders_schema*, *Duplicate report Sales*). Undoing a delete puts the tab back with its data, formulas, column formulas, forms and place; formulas that named it find it again. What the window kept for a deleted tab (open nodes, tables, the current view) is not kept.
