- **Trust.** Like a rename, these are edits made in the app and keep the workbook's trust.
- **The control channel** gets `new_tab`, `delete_tab`, `bind_schema` and `import` for the end-to-end tests.

### Look and feel

Gossia should look like a precise desktop utility, not a web page: flat fills, 1 px borders, small corner radii, and shadows only on menus, tooltips, popups and windows. The design started from a mockup and token list worked out with Canva AI and was then fitted to egui. Every colour, size and font is in one file, `gossia-app/src/style.rs`, which installs the fonts and the styles of both themes on the first frame.

- **Two themes, the same layout.** Light and dark differ only in colour. Each theme is a `Palette` of named colours: chrome (the bars), panel, field, border, row stripe, control, hover, selection and its outline, accent, primary button, text and weak text, computed tint, error and error fill, warning, success, pending, stale, trust banner fill and text, and the type badges. The window follows the system theme until the Options dialog lets the user choose.
- **Cell states** are told apart by more than colour: a formula's value has a translucent tint (so it still shows on a striped, hovered or selected row), an error value has red text on a red fill, invalid data has a 1 px red outline, a cell waiting to be calculated has a small amber dot, and a stale value under an untrusted workbook has grey text.
- **Type badges.** The Type column shows each type as a small rounded badge in the colour of its kind: string (and enum) violet, number blue, integer teal, boolean amber, object (and map) green, array purple, date and date-time pink.
- **Sizes.** Rows are 28 points high (more for a larger data or form font: the text height plus 11) and header rows 30. Body text is 14 points, small text and column titles 12, badges 11, headings 16, and formulas and paths 13 in a monospace font. Controls and badges have a corner radius of 4, panels and popups 6.
- **Fonts** are bundled, all under the SIL Open Font License and all variable fonts, so one file gives every weight (egui 0.37 sets the weight through variation coordinates: `FontTweak::coords` for a family, `RichText::variation` for one piece of text). Inter is the interface font, with a *medium* family at weight 560 for titles, badges and the current tab; Geist Mono is the font of formulas and paths (chosen to match Inter, and it has no coding ligatures, so a formula shows the characters typed), with egui's Hack after it. Source Serif 4 (serif), Oswald (condensed), Dancing Script (script) and Caveat (handwriting) are registered as named families. Behind each are egui's own fonts, for symbols and emoji, and the Phosphor icons. **The bundled text fonts are loaded without the characters of the private use area** (U+E000 to U+F8FF), where the icons are: Inter has 745 glyphs of its own there, which were drawn instead of the icons in text that is not in the icon font (the file dialog's, whose icons are plain text). Their character maps are rebuilt when the fonts are made (`style::without_private_use`, with `skrifa`, which egui reads fonts with); the files are unchanged, and the glyphs stay in the font. Putting the icon font first in each family would not do, since egui takes the line height of a family from its first font (the icons' is 14 points where Inter's is 16.9 at 14 points). Where they came from is in `assets/fonts/FONTS.md`.
- **The toolbar** sits under the menu bar, in the chrome colour, as in the mockup: icon buttons for *New*, *Open* and *Save*; *Undo* and *Redo* (the tip names what they undo); *Add an item* and *Insert a function*; buttons that show the Issues panel (its icon a tick, or a warning when there are errors or invalid data) and the node inspector; and, at the far end, a sun and a moon for the light and the dark theme. *View > Toolbar* hides it.
- **The node inspector** is a panel on the right of the data tree (*View > Node inspector*, or its toolbar button), under the Issues panel when both are shown. It says what the selected node is: its path, with a button that copies it; its type, with the schema's title and description, whether it is required, and what the schema allows; where its value comes from (a value, a formula, a column formula, computed by another formula, absent, or data the schema does not define) and the formula; the value, or the error with its message and traceback; whether it waits to be calculated or is not run because the workbook is not trusted; what is wrong with it; its comment; and how many nodes inside it are hidden.
- **Choosing a font.** The six typefaces can be chosen in two places. *View > Font* sets the typeface and size of the **data views** (the Data tree with its tabular pane, and the Raw view) for every tab of the workbook at once, from 10 to 28 points [Sans, 14 pt]; it is kept in the workbook (`view.json`), so the file opens in the font it was saved in. A **form** has no workbook font: each control has its own (see Forms). The type badges in the grid and the formulas take the chosen font too; under Sans, formulas and code stay in Geist Mono. The menus, bars, column titles and the formula bar stay in the interface font.

## Testing and CI

The project has automated tests at several levels, and **all of them run in a CI pipeline on Linux** with no manual steps. A developer on Windows runs the same default tests locally. Tests that need extra services (databases) or a display are opt-in locally and always on in CI.

**Principles**
- **Test the real thing.** Application-level tests drive the actual application code, including the evaluation thread and the RustPython engine, not mocks. The Python engine is real in every test above the unit level.
- **No sleeping.** The status bar's pending count is observable (see Evaluation and scheduling), and the application exposes "wait until idle" for tests. Tests wait for that condition with a timeout, never for a fixed delay.
- **Deterministic.** The clock and random numbers are injected, so `NOW` and `RAND` can be fixed. Fonts used in rendered tests are bundled. Tests use temporary directories and never touch the network except to local servers they start.
- **Reports** *(0.2.0, owner 2026-11-02)* are tested as the rest:
  - **End to end**, through the control channel, which gains commands to make a report, set its parameters, switch layout mode on and off, export a PDF, and report the pages and the error values a report shows.
  - **Snapshots** of report pages, single page and multipage, in both themes, like the form snapshots. The page view on screen is also compared with the PDF: the same lines on each page, broken at the same words.
  - **PDF output**: the text of each page, read back from the PDF, and where the page breaks fell, for fixed data, compared with expected files; and that the fonts are embedded.
  - **`bidwillii`**: each exit code, from a run that writes to each kind of failure (a wrong command line, a missing report, an untrusted workbook, a bad parameter, error values, the timeout), that a failed run leaves no file, that the workbook file is unchanged afterwards, and that the cache is not used.
- **Every rule in this document gets a test**, and every fixed bug gets a regression test. Test names refer to the section of this document that they check.

**Levels**

1. **Unit tests** (`cargo test`, per crate). `gossia-core`: `Value`, `TypeSpec`, JSON Schema and XSD lowering, validation, scalar-type checks, the XML mapping, inference, import and export. `gossia-calc`: the dependency graph, evaluation order, cycle detection, error values and propagation, version tagging of results, the edit log, the trust hash.
2. **Conformance and fixture tests.**
   - *JSON Schema:* a selected part of the official JSON Schema Test Suite, vendored at a pinned version. Only cases for keywords in the v1 subset are run as pass/fail checks. For every excluded keyword there is a test that checks it is **rejected at load with an error naming it**, which is what this document requires.
   - *XSD:* a curated corpus of schemas and instances covering the subset, and a test for each excluded construct. The built-in meta-schemas are validated by loading them through the normal path.
   - *Workbooks:* `.gossia` fixtures in the repository, one per file-format version, for upgrade tests, plus hostile files (zip paths with `..`, zip bombs, DTD entity expansion, very deep nesting, oversized input).
3. **Data-driven formula tests.** A case file (schema, data, formulas, expected values or error kinds) is loaded and checked, in a table-driven format that is easy to add to. This covers the Python semantics table, error mapping, `IFERROR` and `ISERROR` in both forms, `CONCAT`, `VALID`, syntax errors, and literal references.
4. **Property-based tests** (`proptest`).
   - Data round-trips through JSON and XML, and through save and load.
   - Saving twice gives identical bytes.
   - A random sequence of edits followed by undoing all of them restores the original state, and redo restores the final one.
   - A tree inferred from random data validates it, apart from the conflicts the inference reports.
5. **Python engine tests.** The error mapping table; the propagation rules for `ErrorValue`; module loading, including import cycles, failed modules, and `#NAME!` results; timeouts, with a tight loop that is cancelled and a long builtin call that is abandoned; and the **sandbox probes from the spike** kept as permanent regression tests (no `os`, `socket`, or file access reachable, including through `BuiltinImporter`).
6. **In-process UI tests.** The application's UI is written so that its top-level `update` function can be driven by `egui_kittest`, which runs egui headlessly and finds widgets through AccessKit, the way a screen reader would. This needs no display. Tests do what a user does: open a workbook, select a node, type `=...` into the formula bar and press Enter, wait until idle, and check the cell's text, error, and pending marker. Cases include the tabular view toggle from the context menu, undo and redo keys, the trust banner and untrusted mode, forms (field commits, button procedures), the code editor and the Immediate window, import and export dialogs, and the status bar. This is the main level for UI behaviour.
7. **Visual snapshot tests** (`egui_kittest` with its `snapshot` and `wgpu` features). Key screens are rendered and compared with stored images with a small tolerance. On Linux CI the rendering uses a software Vulkan driver (Mesa lavapipe), so no GPU is needed. They run as a separate job and fail only on a clear difference; differences are uploaded as artifacts. Snapshots start as advisory and become a gate once they are stable.
8. **End-to-end tests on the running application.** The real binary is started on Linux under a virtual display (`xvfb-run`) and driven through a **test control channel**: a loopback-only JSON command interface that exists only when the application is built with the `test-control` cargo feature, which release builds never enable. Commands include: open a workbook, select a node, enter text, press a key, read a cell's value, formula, error, and pending state, wait until idle, take a screenshot, and quit. Because the channel is compiled out of release builds, it adds no remote-control surface to the shipped product. A small number of tests, written as a Rust client against that channel, cover startup, open and save, edit-and-recalculate, and shutdown on the real windowing backend. Most behaviour is tested at level 6, which is faster and more stable.
9. **Data source tests.** SQLite runs everywhere. PostgreSQL and MariaDB run against test servers named by `GOSSIA_TEST_POSTGRES` and `GOSSIA_TEST_MARIADB` (in CI, secrets of the Gitea mirror, whose runner reaches the servers directly). HTTP uses a mock server started by the test on a local port. Cases include a name not defined here (`#SOURCE!`), *Allow writes* off versus on, read versus read-write enforcement, `#SOURCE!` errors, timeouts, oversize responses, and a check that a saved `.gossia` file contains no secrets. These are skipped locally unless the matching environment variable is set.
10. **Security and robustness.** The hostile-file fixtures above; the sandbox probes; trust-hash tests (a change outside the application drops trust, a change inside keeps it); and **fuzzing** with `cargo-fuzz` on the parsers: JSON and XML import, zip reading, schema lowering, and formula parsing. Fuzzing runs on a nightly schedule and does not block merges; any crash found becomes a regression test.
11. **Performance checks.** Benchmarks for the cases measured in the spike (a formula over 10,000 items, 10,000 separate formulas, loading and saving a large workbook) run on a schedule and report trends. They are not merge gates, because shared CI machines are noisy; a very generous threshold in an ordinary test catches only gross regressions.

**The test-control protocol.** The channel used by level 8 listens on a loopback port chosen by the operating system, which the application writes to a file named by `GOSSIA_TEST_PORT_FILE`, and it requires the token in `GOSSIA_TEST_TOKEN` in every request. It speaks **JSON lines**: one request per line, `{"id": 1, "cmd": "...", "args": {...}}`, and one response per request, `{"id": 1, "ok": true, "result": ...}` or `{"id": 1, "ok": false, "error": "..."}`. A first `hello` request returns the protocol version.

| Command | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `open_workbook`, `new_workbook`, `save`, `save_as`, `close` | File operations. In a host that keeps files (the browser page), `save_as` without a path, `open` and `open_recent` (`key`) ask the host, as the menu does. |
| `select` (a path) | Select a node, in the grid or the table. |
| `enter` (text), `key` (a chord), `menu` (a menu path), `context_menu` (a path and an item) | What a user types and clicks. |
| `view` (a name), `set_field` (a path and text), `click_button` (a label) | Switch the tab to the Data tree or a form, and do what a user does on a form. |
| `cell` (a path) | Returns the value, formula text, error, pending state, comment, hidden or filtered state, and validity. |
| `tree` (a path and a depth) | Returns a snapshot of a subtree. |
| `status` | Returns the status bar text, the pending count, and the abandoned-thread count, and whether the code editor is open. |
| `command` (a name), `open_module` (a name) | Runs one of Gossia's commands by name, as a host's command list does (`gossia_app::commands`), and opens a module as the code editor's explorer does. |
| `wait_idle` (a timeout) | Waits until nothing is pending. |
| `set_option`, `define_source`, `trust` | Preferences, data source definitions, and the trust decision, so tests can set up state without driving dialogs. |
| `screenshot` (a path) | Saves an image of the window. |
| `quit` | Exits cleanly. |

The same command set is implemented over the in-process harness used by level 6, so a scenario can be written once and run at either level.

**Selecting JSON Schema Test Suite cases.** The suite is vendored at a pinned commit, recorded in `vendor.lock` as a further item (a test-only one) when the suite is first added. A checked-in selection file lists the allowed keywords: `type`, `properties`, `required`, `items`, `minItems`, `maxItems`, `minimum`, `maximum`, `minLength`, `maxLength`, `pattern`, `enum`, local `$ref` and `$defs`, schema-valued `additionalProperties`, and `format` for `date` and `date-time`. A test group is run as pass or fail only if its schema, searched recursively, uses nothing outside that list. Every other group is run as a **must-reject** test: loading its schema has to fail with an error that names the excluded keyword. A group that is neither is listed in the selection file with the reason, so that nothing is skipped silently.

**Tests for auto-update and schema editing.** Switching on with zero, one, and two users; refusing a second binding while it is on; a file that breaks the rule opening with it treated as off; each of the four commands with its effect on the data and on formulas; shared-type confirmations; undo and redo of each as one entry; a later direct edit of the schema sheet having to be undone first; and the refusal while a schema-sheet cell edit is in progress.

**Pipeline** (GitHub Actions for lint, the tests and, from 0.6.0, the GitHub Pages site; Gitea Actions on the mirror for the rest)

Lint and Test run on GitHub's own Ubuntu runners (`.github/workflows/ci.yml`), for pushes and pull requests. Everything else runs on the owner's runner of the Gitea mirror (`.gitea/workflows/ci.yml` and `packages.yml`), for pushes to main only, since only the owner pushes there: no runner of the project's is registered with GitHub, so a pull request from a fork never runs on its machines (see the decisions for moving CI off GitHub's self-hosted runner). The Gitea runner's machine provides `git`, a C toolchain, `rustup`, `xvfb-run`, Mesa and Python, and no Node.js.

| Job | Runs | Blocks merge |
|---|---|---|
| Lint | `cargo fmt --check`, `cargo clippy -D warnings`, and `cargo deny` (licences, advisories) once its configuration is verified | yes |
| Vendor | `cargo xtask vendor fetch` and `verify` on Linux | yes |
| Test | `cargo test --workspace` on Linux: levels 1 to 6 | yes |
| Services | level 9 with the PostgreSQL and MariaDB test servers (part of Test while the secrets are set) | yes |
| E2E | level 8 under `xvfb-run` | yes |
| Snapshots | level 7 with software rendering | advisory at first |
| Windows installer | an unsigned x64 MSI, cross-built on Linux, on pushes to main | advisory |
| Sweep target | after the others, whatever they did: deletes build files in the kept `target/` not read for 3 days, and all of `target/` if it is still over 60 GB (it had reached 171 GB in a week by 2026-10-30) | no |
| Nightly | fuzzing, performance, `cargo audit` | no |

- The Rust toolchain is pinned in `rust-toolchain.toml` (1.97 or newer is required, as RustPython requires) and the dependency set is locked, including the RustPython git revision and the `ruff` fork (see Build and dependencies).
- Runs on the Gitea runner stay short because it is persistent: `target/` and `vendor/` stay in `~/ci/gossia` (the shell checkout does not clean them), and RustPython takes several minutes to build only the first time. `Swatinem/rust-cache` is not used, because its cleanup deletes `rustup` from `~/.cargo/bin` on a shared machine. A job on a throwaway runner would need a cache.
- On failure the snapshot diffs and the web harness's screenshots are kept in the Gitea package registry (`packaging/keep-ci-files.sh`, package `gossia-ci`, under the commit).
- **The Windows installer** is built on the Linux runner: `packaging/windows/build-msi.sh` cross-compiles the release application for `x86_64-pc-windows-msvc` with `cargo-xwin` (which downloads the Microsoft CRT and Windows SDK once, accepting their licence terms, into `~/.cache/gossia-msi`) and packages it with `wixl` from msitools, from `packaging/windows/gossia.wxs` (the WiX 3 dialect wixl reads). Nothing is installed system-wide, so the runner needs no root. The MSI installs `gossia.exe` and the licence under Program Files, adds a Start Menu shortcut, and upgrades in place (`UpgradeCode` never changes; the version is the workspace version). The release exe is a GUI program (no console window); debug builds and the test control build keep the console. Not yet: code signing (SmartScreen will warn), an icon, a version resource in the exe, and the `.gossia` file association (with the workbook layer). *(The icon, the version resource and the association were added later: see the decisions for the last items. Code signing is not planned.)* The job is advisory and uploads the MSI as the `windows-installer` artifact. *(Owner, 2026-11-06: build outputs go to Gitea, not GitHub, whose Actions storage the MSI artifacts had filled to 90%. The MSI is now built by the `msi` job of `.gitea/workflows/packages.yml` on the mirror's `selfhosted` runner and put in the Gitea generic package registry beside the MSIX and the `.vsix` by `packaging/windows/publish-msi.sh`; the GitHub job is gone, the `.vsix` is no longer also kept as a GitHub artifact, and the failure-only GitHub artifacts (`snapshot-differences`, `web-screenshots`) are kept 7 days.)*
  - **From 0.2.0 the MSI also installs `bidwillii.exe`** next to `gossia.exe` (owner, 2026-11-02). It is not put on the `PATH`; scripts name it by its full path.
- **An MSIX for the Microsoft Store** *(0.2.0, owner 2026-11-04, built with 12i-3)*. Gossia is listed on the Microsoft Store as an MSIX package, because the Store signs an MSIX itself, while an MSI or EXE listed there must carry our own code signing signature. The MSI stays for direct download. The package carries the identity reserved in Partner Center (publisher and package name), declares the `.gossia` file association, and gives `bidwillii` an app execution alias so that scripts can run it by name. The name Gossia was reserved on 2026-11-05 (Store ID `9P505WS779KW`), replacing the earlier reservation of ClioTree; a first submission is due within three months of that, or the reservation lapses. **The submission waits for 0.4.0** (owner, 2026-11-05): the package is built and tried with 0.2.0, but goes to the Store only once 0.4.0 is complete. The first submission (0.4.0) was completed and Gossia is on the Store at https://apps.microsoft.com/detail/9p505ws779kw (owner, 2026-11-09). The manifest's identity: `Identity/Name` `WardvanderVeer.Gossia`, `Identity/Publisher` `CN=929EF93A-E527-4C5D-8EB5-DF06E90F115A`, `PublisherDisplayName` `Ward van der Veer` (package family name `WardvanderVeer.Gossia_bd1w97kxbkr6y`). It is packed on Windows, not by CI: `packaging/windows/build-msix.ps1` builds the release executables, fills in `packaging/windows/AppxManifest.xml` and packs it with MakeAppx from the Windows SDK; `-Register` installs the packed folder to try it (Developer Mode), and `-Publish` puts the `.msix` in the Gitea package registry (owner 2026-11-04; see the decisions for the packages).
- **Linux packages** *(0.2.0, owner 2026-11-02)*: one package for each family of distributions, each built in a container of that distribution so that it links against that distribution's libraries: a **`.deb`** (Debian and Ubuntu, with `cargo-deb`), an **`.rpm`** (Fedora and openSUSE, with `cargo-generate-rpm`) and an **Arch package** (`.pkg.tar.zst`, from a `PKGBUILD` built with `makepkg` in an Arch container). Each installs `gossia` and `bidwillii` in `/usr/bin`, the licence, a `.desktop` file with the icon, and a MIME type for `.gossia` files that opens them in Gossia, and declares the system libraries the application needs (OpenGL, X11 and Wayland; ODBC is on Windows only, so unixODBC is not needed). The packaging files are under `packaging/linux/`. The jobs are advisory, like the MSI's, and upload the packages as artifacts. *(Owner, 2026-11-04.)* The Gitea mirror already has a runner for each family (act_runner), labelled `selfhosted` (Debian, without root), `arch` and `fedora`. Each package is built on its family's runner, so the job needs no container. These runners have no Node, so the jobs use plain shell instead of `actions/checkout` and `upload-artifact`. The packages go to Gitea's package registry (Debian, RPM and Arch) with a `PACKAGE_REGISTRY_TOKEN` secret.
- **The console window.** A release build on Windows is a GUI program with no console. A debug build keeps its console on purpose, so that panics and logs are seen while developing (owner, 2026-11-01).
- Coverage is reported with `cargo llvm-cov` for information, not as a gate.
- Because development happens on Windows, a Windows job running levels 1 to 3 is added once the basic pipeline is stable. It exists to catch path, case-sensitivity, and line-ending differences, which this design has several rules about (tab names unique ignoring case, per-tab files).

## Build and dependencies

**Required Rust version.** The project requires **Rust 1.97 or newer**. This is a project requirement for every developer and for CI, not just a CI detail. It comes from RustPython and its `ruff` fork (see Python engine), and it is re-checked whenever they are upgraded.
- The workspace `Cargo.toml` declares `rust-version = "1.97"`, so Cargo itself refuses to build with an older compiler and says why.
- `rust-toolchain.toml` pins the exact toolchain used by developers and CI. It starts at 1.99.1, the version the spike was run with, and is raised deliberately, not automatically.
- The README states the requirement, and a developer on an older toolchain is told to update with `rustup update stable`.

**The vendor folder.** Dependencies that cannot come from crates.io as published (RustPython, and the `ruff` fork it needs) live in a `vendor/` folder at the root of the repository.
- **Not committed.** `vendor/` is in `.gitignore`. Its contents are downloaded, never stored in the repository.
- **Not cleared between builds.** An ordinary build, rebuild, or `cargo clean` leaves it alone, so a build does not download anything again.
- **Cleared by the project's `clean`.** The project's own clean command (`cargo xtask clean`) removes `vendor/` as well as the build output. After it, the next build fetches everything again.
- **Fetched on demand, before Cargo starts.** The workspace declares which vendor items it needs (`[workspace.metadata.gossia] vendor = [...]` in the root `Cargo.toml`). Before `cargo xtask lint`, `test`, or `ci` runs Cargo, it checks that each of those items exists under `vendor/` and matches `vendor.lock`, and calls the **fetch script** for any that is missing or out of date.
- **Why not a build script.** The first design had a build script do the fetching. That cannot work: Cargo needs the manifest of every path dependency to exist before it runs any build script, so a build script can never create a path dependency of its own workspace. (This was confirmed by experiment: Cargo stops with `no matching package named ... found` before the build script starts.) The fetch therefore happens in `cargo xtask`, which is in a separate workspace and so runs on a fresh clone. A plain `cargo build` on a fresh clone, once the workspace depends on `vendor/`, needs one `cargo xtask vendor fetch` first, and the README says so.

**What is pinned.** A committed file, `vendor.lock`, lists each vendor item with its source URL and **exact commit hash**. The content is not committed, but its identity is, so every developer and CI build gets the same code.
- Items at first:
  - **RustPython** at commit `e5554e23abfbaee85508c32fc22e5074f88aebdd`, the revision the spike built and tested (version 0.6.1).
  - The **`ruff` fork** (`https://github.com/RustPython/ruff.git`) at commit `9290a8849542134d83f77e9360e7f0b93897fd93`, the revision that RustPython's own lock file names, with a sparse checkout of the crates it needs.
- The fetch script checks out that exact commit and verifies it against `vendor.lock`. A mismatch is an error.
- Each item gets a stamp file (`.complete`) holding the pinned hash. It is written only after the download and any build steps succeed, so an interrupted fetch is detected and redone. If `vendor.lock` changes, the stamp no longer matches and the item is fetched again.
- To upgrade, a developer runs `cargo xtask vendor update <item> <commit>`, which edits `vendor.lock` for review in the usual way.
- The URL in `vendor.lock` can point at a mirror if an upstream repository disappears.

**The format of `vendor.lock`.** A TOML file, one table per item:

```toml
version = 1

[[item]]
name   = "rustpython"
url    = "https://github.com/RustPython/RustPython"
rev    = "e5554e23abfbaee85508c32fc22e5074f88aebdd"   # full 40-character commit hash

[[item]]
name   = "ruff"
url    = "https://github.com/RustPython/ruff.git"
rev    = "9290a8849542134d83f77e9360e7f0b93897fd93"
sparse = ["crates/ruff_python_ast", "crates/ruff_python_parser", "crates/ruff_python_trivia",
          "crates/ruff_source_file", "crates/ruff_text_size"]
```

`name` is the directory under `vendor/`, `rev` must be a full commit hash (a branch or tag is rejected), and `sparse` is optional. The fetch script fails if the checked-out commit differs from `rev`.

**The `cargo xtask` commands.** The project's helper commands, all cross-platform:

| Command | Does |
|---|---|
| `cargo xtask clean` | Removes the build output and `vendor/`. |
| `cargo xtask vendor fetch` | Fetches any missing or out-of-date vendor item. |
| `cargo xtask vendor status` | Lists each item, its pinned revision, and whether `vendor/` matches it. |
| `cargo xtask vendor verify` | Checks every checked-out item against `vendor.lock`. |
| `cargo xtask vendor update <item> <rev>` | Changes a pin in `vendor.lock`, for review. |
| `cargo xtask lint` | Formatting and clippy (and `cargo deny` once its configuration is verified): the Lint job. |
| `cargo xtask test` | The default test levels: the Test job. |
| `cargo xtask e2e` | The end-to-end tests on the running application: under `xvfb-run` on Linux without a display, in a real window elsewhere. |
| `cargo xtask snapshots` | The visual snapshot tests (they need a graphics adapter). |
| `cargo xtask ci` | Everything the blocking CI jobs run, in order. |

**The fetch script is cross-platform.** It is a small Rust program in its own package (`tools/fetch-vendor`), so it runs the same on Windows, Linux, and in CI, with no shell scripting. It lives in its own workspace (`tools/`) with `cargo xtask`, which is what lets it run before `vendor/` exists, and the same code is available as a standalone `fetch-vendor` binary.

**Windows long paths.** The spike found that checking out the whole `ruff` repository fails on Windows because of very long test file names. The fetch script avoids the problem by **sparse checkout**: it fetches only the crates RustPython needs and none of the rest, and it sets git's `core.longpaths` as well. Nothing else about the layout depends on the platform.

**How Cargo uses it.** The workspace refers to the vendored sources by path (`vendor/rustpython/...`). All other dependencies come from crates.io in the usual way, locked by `Cargo.lock`.

**Patching a vendored item.** An item in `vendor.lock` may name a patch file (`patch = "vendor-patches/rustpython.patch"`, relative to the repository root). The fetch applies it with `git apply` after checking the commit, and the stamp records a fingerprint of the patch (and of the sparse list) together with the commit, so changing either fetches the item again. `vendor verify` checks that the patch is applied. The fetch also sets `core.autocrlf false`, so the files are the same bytes on every platform and the patch applies the same way. A patch file is kept with LF line endings by `.gitattributes`.
- **Why RustPython needs one.** RustPython names the `ruff` fork as a git dependency. Cargo checks such a dependency out itself, and on Windows the checkout fails on very long file names in the full repository (`path too long`, unless the operating system's long-path setting is on). A `[patch]` section does not help, because Cargo still reads the original source to resolve. The patch (4 lines in `Cargo.toml`) turns those four git dependencies into path dependencies on `vendor/ruff`, which is the sparse checkout. Cargo then never touches the ruff repository, and a build needs no network for it.
- **What the sparse list holds** for `ruff`: `ruff_python_ast`, `ruff_python_parser`, `ruff_python_trivia`, `ruff_source_file`, `ruff_text_size`, `ruff_unicode_names2` and `ruff_unicode_names2_generator` (the parser needs the last two).
- **Other pieces of the build.** `pymath`, a small crate RustPython takes from git, is still fetched by Cargo. `gossia-calc` depends on `rustpython-vm` as well as on `rustpython`, because RustPython's derive macros write `rustpython_vm::` into the code they generate. Dependencies are built with optimisation in every profile (`[profile.dev.package."*"]`), as RustPython does in its own workspace: unoptimised, its evaluator keeps oversized native frames and the interpreter overflows the stack while it starts. `cargo fmt` is run without `--all` (that would also format the vendored crates).

**Offline and failure.** If a fetch fails, for example without network access, the build stops with a message that names the item, the URL, and the directory. The environment variable `GOSSIA_VENDOR_DIR` can point at a copy of the vendor folder prepared elsewhere.

**CI.** The pipeline uses the same `cargo xtask` commands, so there is no separate CI procedure, and a separate *Vendor* job fetches and verifies every pinned item on Linux. On the persistent runner the `vendor/` folder simply stays in the workspace between runs, and a change to `vendor.lock` makes the next run fetch the item again (see Testing and CI).

**Security.** Fetching does not run anything from the downloaded repositories. Cargo does run the build scripts of dependencies when it builds them, as it does for any crate, which is why the pins are exact commits and changes to `vendor.lock` are reviewed.

## Build order

0. **CI skeleton** *(done)*: workspace, `rust-version` and pinned toolchain, the `vendor/` folder with its fetch script and `vendor.lock`, lint and test jobs running on Linux (see Build and dependencies, and Testing and CI). Every later step adds its tests together with its code.
1. **core** *(done)*: `Value`, `TypeSpec`, JSON Schema lowering, validation, the JSON adapter, tests. The bundled meta-schemas come with the schema sheets in step 4, and XSD with step 6.
2. **calc** *(done)*: formula store, dependency graph, evaluator, error values, result checking, snapshots, run against a small fake engine (`FakeEngine`). Trust, time budgets, the evaluation thread and its message protocol, and volatile functions come later (steps 3 to 5).
3. **RustPython** *(done)*: the Python engine, proxies, the error values formulas see, `IFERROR`, `ISERROR`, `VALID`, `PLAIN`, `JSONPATH`, the time limit, and the measurement of the proxy layer against the budgets (revised below). The Excel-named functions, workbook modules and trust come in step 5; the evaluation thread, with abandoning a formula that cannot be interrupted, in step 4.
4. **app**, in five parts:
   - **4a. Headless foundations** *(done)*: the edit log with undo and redo, the evaluation thread with its limits and abandoning, and the session that joins them to the workbook (all in `gossia-calc`, tested without a window).
   - **4b. The shell** *(done; the trust banner came with step 5c and opening files with 8b)*: the egui window, tabs along the bottom, the tree grid, the formula bar with Insert Function and name completion, the status bar, undo and redo keys. The window opens a sample workbook for now.
   - **4c. The tabular view of arrays** *(done; saving the view state came with 8c, and copy and paste of ranges and hiding of tree nodes on 2026-10-30)*: the table, filters, sorting, hiding, and the row commands.
   - **4d. Schema auto-update** and the schema-editing commands *(done; the XML element and attribute forms came after step 6, and the manifest with 8a)*.
   - **4e. The in-process UI tests** with `egui_kittest`, the visual snapshot tests, and the end-to-end test control channel *(done; the commands for files came with 8b; the two new CI jobs are advisory until the runner is known to suit them, as listed in the decisions below)*.
5. **std**, **modules**, and **trust**, in three parts:
   - **5a. The Excel-named functions** *(done, apart from `XMLPATH`, which waits for the XML adapter of step 6)*: `SUM`, `AVERAGE`, `MIN`, `MAX`, `COUNT`, `IF`, `LOOKUP`, `FILTER`, `SORT`, `UNIQUE`, `CONCAT`, `TEXTJOIN`, `TEXT` and `NA`, in `gossia-std`.
   - **5b. Workbook modules** *(done; trust came with 5c, the file format with 8a and the code editor with step 10)*: loading, the namespaces, publishing, reloading, generations, the reserved names and the clash checks.
   - **5c. Trust** *(done; reading and writing the record and the workbook id came with 8b and 8c)*: the trust hash, untrusted mode, the banner, and the recorded decisions.
6. **XSD** subset and XML adapter *(done as the XML reader and writer, the lowering, the data adapter, the built-in XSD meta-schema, XSD schema tabs and `XMLPATH`; comments in the window came later, and inference with import (8e); the XML forms of the field commands came later too)*.
6b. **Data sources**, in five parts (see Data sources; definitions per user and machine, referred to by name, no grants):
   - **6b-1. Definitions** *(done)*: `data-sources.json`, the Data sources panel with its form and Test, `source(name)`, `sources.define` and `sources.check`. The Test checks only that a server answers or a file opens until each kind is built.
   - **6b-2. HTTP/HTTPS** *(done)*: `get`, `post`, `put`, `patch`, `delete` and the response, the full Test, waits that do not count against the time limit, and *Tools > Recalculate* (F9).
   - **6b-3. SQLite** *(done)*: `query` and `execute`, the full Test, read-only unless writes are allowed.
   - **6b-4. PostgreSQL and MariaDB** *(done)*: `query` and `execute`, the TLS setting, the full Test, and connection pooling.
   - **6b-5. ODBC, Windows only** *(done; asked for by the owner on 2026-10-30)*: a DSN or a connection string, `query` and `execute`, the Test, pooling, best-effort read-only.
7. **Views and forms**, in five parts:
   - **7a. The view selector and the generated Default form** *(done; saving the view of each tab came with 8c)*: the view selector, the remembered view of each tab, the generated Default form with live read and write to the tab.
   - **7b. Named forms and customising** *(done; saving designs in the workbook came with 8a, visible-when and enabled-when are evaluated since 2026-10-28, and drag and drop came on 2026-10-30)*: the `builtin:form` schema, *New form...* and *Duplicate*, layout mode and the property page.
   - **7c. Button procedures** *(done; the evaluation of visible-when and enabled-when came later, with the colour expressions, and so did choosing a procedure from a list of module functions, both 2026-10-28)*: the procedure call on the evaluation thread, `form` and `ctx`, atomic writes, the conflict rule, trust, the time limit, and the button in the form.
   - **7d. Size and place of controls** (asked for by the owner on 2026-10-30; see Forms, Layout), in three parts: **7d-1** *(done, 2026-10-30)*: width, height, alignment, label position and width, spacing and padding, the Flow layout with spans and new rows in aligned rows, and the resize handles. **7d-2** *(done, 2026-10-30)*: the Free layout with dragging, snapping, guides, the arrow keys, anchors, multiple selection and the align tools, and the tab order list. **7d-3** *(done, 2026-10-30)*: the Grid layout.
   - **7e. Lists of choices for dropdowns and typeahead fields** (asked for by the owner on 2026-10-30; see Forms, "Choices"; done, 2026-10-30).

8. **The workbook layer**, in parts:
   - **8a. The archive in memory** *(done)*: `Calc::to_package` and `Calc::from_package` in `gossia-calc`: the manifest, the data and overlay of each tab, the form designs, the modules, format versioning, and what a newer version added.
   - **8b. Files and the File menu** *(done; the recent-files list came with 8c)*: the zip with its limits, atomic saves, *Open...*, *Save*, *Save As...* through the `egui-file-dialog` browser, the question about unsaved changes, the window title, the trust decision on opening, and the control channel commands. A native file picker is not planned: the browser needs no GTK, portal or operating-system dialog, and works the same on every platform and on the runner.
   - **8c. What a file carries besides the tabs** *(done; editing the settings and the preferences waits for the Options dialog, as listed in the decisions below)*: the state of the view of each tab in the overlay, the trust record and the preferences on disk, `settings.json`, *New*, recent files.
   - **8d. Renaming a tab** *(done)*: see Renaming a tab.
   - **8e. Managing tabs and importing** *(done; export came with 8f)*: new, delete and change schema (see Managing tabs), import with schema inference, and dropping files on the window (see Import and export).
   - **8f. Export** *(done)*: *File > Export...* and a tab's *Export...*, with the schema beside the file (see Import and export).
   - **8g. The Options dialog** *(done; the Code editor page came with 10c, and the Display page, with its comment popup options, later, as listed in the decisions below)*: see The Options dialog.
9. **Look and feel** *(done: the style file, the fonts, the grid, bars and tabs, the font of the data views and the font of form controls; the toolbar and the node inspector on 2026-10-30)*: see Look and feel.

10. **The code editor** for modules, in three parts (see Python modules):

The editor window** *(done)*: a second window opened with *Tools > Code editor* or Alt+F11, the project explorer with its *Modules* branch, a tab for each open module with line numbers, Python highlighting and automatic indentation, the procedure box, *Insert > Module*, and rename and remove in the explorer's context menu.
   - **10b. Checking and files** *(done)*: *Compile* with its clickable list of problems, export and import of a module as a `.py` file, and the *Forms* branch with the raw text of the customised form designs.
   - **10c. The Immediate window** *(done)*: an expression evaluated against the live workbook on the evaluation thread, its history in `immediate-history.json` and its output limit, and the *Code editor* page of the Options dialog.

11. **Other file formats**, before 0.1.0 is closed (see Import and export, Other file formats), in four parts:
   - **11a. Tab formats, JSON Lines and JSON5/JSONC** *(done, 2026-10-31)*: the format of a tab in the manifest and the archive, *Format* on the tab's menu, comments on the tabs whose format has them, and the two JSON relatives.
   - **11b. TOML** *(done, 2026-10-31)*.
   - **11c. YAML** *(done, 2026-10-31)*.
   - **11d. COBOL copybooks** (asked for by the owner on 2026-10-31; see Import and export, COBOL copybooks): the copybook schema tab and its meta-schema, lowering, `.blk` records in EBCDIC with zoned, packed and binary numbers, `OCCURS DEPENDING ON` and `REDEFINES`. Built in parts: 11d-1 the copybook, EBCDIC, lowering and `.blk` reading and writing in `gossia-core`; 11d-2 copybook schema tabs, binding and saving in `gossia-calc`; 11d-3 import, export, the Raw view's dump, the code page and the REDEFINES choice in the app; 11d-4 the bytes value, the four conversion functions, `RAW` and a root formula that gives bytes. *(All four built; 11d-4 on 2026-11-01, with condition names as choices.)*

12. **Reports**, version 0.2.0 (see Reports), in parts:
   - **12a. The report layout crate** *(done, 2026-11-02)*: `gossia-layout`, text measured as egui measures it, with the bundled fonts.
   - **12b. The form additions shared with reports** (see Forms, *Additions in 0.2.0, shared with reports*): **12b-1** *(done, 2026-11-02)* fields that show an expression, label templates, repeaters over expressions with item and list names, and nested designed items; **12b-2** *(done, 2026-11-02)* the look: fixed colours, bold and italic, group borders and fill, vertical separators, percent, prefix and suffix.
   - **12c. The report model, `builtin:report` and report tabs** *(done, 2026-11-03)*. **12d.** Single page reports, in two parts: **12d-1** *(done, 2026-11-03)* evaluating a report's expressions, placing its controls in `gossia-layout`, and drawing it in its tab; **12d-2** *(done, 2026-11-03)* *Edit report* on a canvas *(redone: a first version drawn by the form view was replaced, owner 2026-11-03)*: the single page canvas at the paper width with fixed boxes, turning existing designs into canvases, the designer drawn by `gossia-layout` with selection, dragging, handles, snapping and the property page over it, *Show expressions*, and zoom. **12e.** Charts *(done, 2026-11-03)*. **12f.** Parameters and the results cache *(done, 2026-11-03)*. **12g.** Multipage reports *(done, 2026-11-03, before 12e and 12f at the owner's choice)*: sections and the stack of band canvases, in the designer and the page view, paging, page values, the page setup on the property page, and starting from an array. **12h.** PDF export *(done, 2026-11-04)*. **12i.** `bidwillii` and the packages, in three parts: **12i-1** *(done, 2026-11-04)* `bidwillii report` and *Copy batch command*; **12i-2** *(done, 2026-11-04)* `bidwillii disconnect`, with the reads in the expressions of reports replaced by Disconnect from Data Sources as well; **12i-3** *(done, 2026-11-05: the Linux packages built and published by the Gitea runners, the MSIX installed and tried with `-Register` and published to the Gitea registry; the Store submission waits for 0.4.0)* the packages (the MSI, an MSIX for the Microsoft Store, and the Linux packages, built on the Gitea runners).

13. **The wasm version, HTML export and printing**, version 0.3.0 (see Later versions, Web port, 0.3.0; order agreed with the owner on 2026-11-05, after the spike on how the worker waits):
   - **13a. The engine on wasm** *(done, 2026-11-05; see the decisions for the engine on wasm)*: `gossia-core`, `gossia-std` and `gossia-calc` built for `wasm32-unknown-unknown`, with the data source kinds the wasm version lacks left out of that build; Python running in a Web Worker in a plain browser page; the size of the wasm measured. Making it smaller (freezing only the Python modules formulas can reach) became step 13g.
   - **13b. The host interface and the tests**: the interface, a test host behind the plain browser page, the application drawn by egui in that page, stop and run again for HTTP reads, the harness page with the `test-control` build, and the Playwright script for CI. In four parts: **13b-1** *(done, 2026-11-05; see the decisions for the worker protocol)* the worker protocol: the jobs and results of the evaluation thread encoded as bytes (serde), an `Evaluator` on wasm that posts them to the Web Worker, a clock that works in the browser, and native tests that an encoded round trip gives what direct evaluation gives; **13b-2** `gossia-app` built for wasm and drawn in the plain browser page, with the host interface and the test host, in three parts: **13b-2a** *(done, 2026-11-05; see the decisions for the application in the page)* the build, the host interface, the page's entry point and the test host, with the workbook saved and the preferences kept through the host; **13b-2b** *(done, 2026-11-05; see the decisions for files in a host)* opening, importing and exporting files through the host's dialogs; **13b-2c** *(done, 2026-11-05; see the decisions for data sources in a host)* the data source definitions and secrets in the host; **13b-3** *(done, 2026-11-05; see the decisions for HTTP through the host)* stop and run again for HTTP reads, through the host; **13b-4** *(done, 2026-11-05; see the decisions for the harness page)* the harness page, the `test-control` build's JavaScript function, and the Playwright script.
   - **13c. The VS Code extension**: the custom editor for `.gossia`, storage and secrets, HTTP through the extension host, the theme, the Command Palette, the code editor as a second panel, VS Code's test runner on GitHub CI, and the `.vsix` in the Gitea registry. In three parts: **13c-1** *(done, 2026-11-05; see the decisions for the VS Code extension)* the extension and its custom editor, with the file, storage, secrets, the dialogs, HTTP and the theme through the extension host, and its tests in VS Code's test runner on this machine; **13c-2** *(done, 2026-11-06; see the decisions for modules and commands in VS Code)* Gossia's commands in the Command Palette, and modules in VS Code's own editor (owner, 2026-11-06: in place of the code editor as a second panel); **13c-3** *(done, 2026-11-06; see the decisions for packing and CI of the extension)* the tests on GitHub CI, and the `.vsix` built and put in the Gitea registry.
   - **13d. Export of reports as HTML** *(done, 2026-11-06; see the decisions for HTML export)*: one self-contained file with no scripts.
   - **13e. Printing** *(done, 2026-11-06; see the decisions for printing)* of reports on the desktop: the PDF to a temporary file, opened in the system's PDF program to print from.
   - **13f. The interface in six languages** *(done, 2026-11-06; owner; see the decisions for the interface's languages)*: English, French, Spanish, Portuguese, Japanese and Mandarin (Simplified Chinese). In four parts: **13f-1** *(built, 2026-11-06)* the texts' machinery (`gossia-i18n`), the language in Options and the preferences, the system's font for Japanese and Chinese on the desktop, and the menu bar and the Options dialog translated; **13f-2** *(built, 2026-11-06)* the rest of the window's texts (dialogs, panels, the status bar, notices, tooltips, the designers, the code editor), with the names of choices that `gossia-core`, `gossia-calc` and `gossia-user` give the window; **13f-3** *(built, 2026-11-06)* the engine's messages (the explanations after error codes, from `gossia-calc` and `gossia-core`), with the language given to the evaluation thread and the worker; **13f-4** *(built, 2026-11-06)* VS Code: the extension's own texts and its commands' titles (`package.nls.*.json`), VS Code's display language as the system's, and the font for Japanese and Chinese loaded on demand beside the wasm.
   - **13g. A smaller download** *(done, 2026-11-06; owner: to offset what 13f's fonts add; see the decisions for a smaller download)*: freeze into the build only the Python standard library modules that formulas and modules can reach (the allowed modules of Names and what they import) instead of all of `Lib`, which is most of the 31.2 MB of data in the 55.5 MB wasm (see the decisions for the engine on wasm); the desktop executable shrinks with it. Also to try: leaving out the function names (`--remove-name-section`, 7.6 MB) and `wasm-opt`. Measured before and after.

Reports and the wasm port are **not part of v1**; see Later versions.

**The last items, and what is not planned** (decided by the owner on 2026-10-31). Of what the decisions below list as not done, only these were still to be built *(all done, 2026-10-31: see the decisions for the last items)*:
- the `.gossia` file association, in the installer;
- an icon, and a version resource, for the Windows executable and the installer;
- resizing the columns of a table on a form by dragging their edge (the tree grid and the tabular view have it);
- importing a `.xsd` or a JSON Schema file as a schema tab;
- a new XML tab with a blank XSD;
- reordering tabs by dragging them.

The rest are **not planned**: cut (Ctrl+X), dragging out a range with the mouse, Shift with Home, End and the page keys, selecting rows from the row header and pasting a range into the tree view; dragging a field from *Fields not on the form*, dragging between the columns of a group, scrolling while dragging, moving several selected controls by dragging one, snapping while resizing, and a separate height for one-line text boxes; formats in the data tree, by locale, and typing a value in its format; dragging a column to move it; the mark on the cells of a column formula, column formulas from forms and on nested arrays; exporting several tabs at once and remembering the export folder; name completion, a search box and a pending mark in the code editor; renaming an element written as `ref`; code signing. A "Not done" note below that names one of them is kept as the record of what was built when.

## Later versions

Deferred features, with what v1 does to keep them possible. Version 1 is release **0.1.0**; **Reports are 0.2.0**, **the web port is 0.3.0**, and **Excel and ODS files are 0.4.0** (owner, 2026-10-31); **XML Schemas with namespaces are 0.5.0** and **the web version on GitHub Pages is 0.6.0** (owner, 2026-11-12; browser extensions, phones and tablets, planned for 0.6.0 on 2026-11-09, were dropped for it). Version 1 was closed on 2026-11-01, and the version is now **0.2.0**: the work on Reports (owner, 2026-11-01). 0.2.0 was closed and tagged `v0.2.0` on 2026-11-05, without the optional form additions (charts on forms, totals rows), which will not be built (owner, 2026-11-05). The version is now **0.3.0** (owner, 2026-11-05). 0.3.0 was closed and tagged `v0.3.0` on 2026-11-06, after step 13g. The version was then **0.4.0** (owner, 2026-11-06), with calendars and contacts added to it (owner, 2026-11-07); 0.4.0 was closed and tagged `v0.4.0` on 2026-11-07, after step 14g. The version was then **0.5.0** (owner, 2026-11-07): XML Schemas, chiefly namespaces (owner, 2026-11-09); 0.5.0 was closed and tagged `v0.5.0` on 2026-11-10, after a fix to arrays in the data tree (owner's report, 2026-11-10), merged from `develop` into `main`. The version is now **0.6.0** (owner, 2026-11-10). **Builds are made from `main` only** (owner, 2026-11-10): CI (Gitea and GitHub) and the packages run on pushes to `main`, and work goes on `develop`, which is kept.

- **Reports, 0.2.0** (see Reports): the report layout calculation (`gossia-layout`; forms stay on egui); the form designer additions shared with reports (see Forms); the `builtin:report` schema, from the definitions of `builtin:form`; report tabs and single page reports, edited in layout mode; charts (chart functions returning SVG, user-defined with `@chart`, and the built-in bar, line, pie and scatter); report parameters and the parameter bar; multipage reports (bands, nested sections, page setup, page view); export as PDF; batch export with `bidwillii`, in the MSI and the Linux packages (.deb, .rpm, Arch); then the optional form additions (charts on forms, totals rows).
  - The manifest's tab `kind` and the `reports/` directory name are reserved.
  - A v1 build that opens a file containing reports keeps those entries unchanged on save (the unknown-entries rule in File format) and shows no report tabs.
- **Wasm version, 0.3.0**: the same core in another application's web view, first as a Visual Studio Code extension, together with **export of reports as HTML** and **printing** on the desktop (owner, 2026-11-05; see Web port, 0.3.0, below, and Reports, Export).
  - `gossia-core` and `gossia-calc` stay free of UI and filesystem code.
- **Excel and ODS files, 0.4.0**: importing `.xlsx`, `.xls` and `.ods` (each sheet a tab with an array of rows, as CSV) and exporting `.xlsx`, `.ods`, CSV and TSV (see Excel and ODS files, 0.4.0, below).
- **XML Schemas, 0.5.0** (owner, 2026-11-09): wider XSD support, chiefly namespaces (more than one namespace, `xs:import`, attributes in a namespace). See XML Schemas and namespaces, 0.5.0, below.
- **The web version on GitHub Pages, 0.6.0** (owner, 2026-11-12): the wasm version as a public web page, through a browser host behind the host interface of 0.3.0, installable and usable offline. It replaces the browser extensions, phones and tablets planned before, which are dropped. See The web version on GitHub Pages, 0.6.0, below.
- **Gossia pyplot, 0.6.0** (owner, 2026-11-10): a built-in module, `gossia.pyplot` (available as `plt`), whose names follow matplotlib's `pyplot` without claiming to be it, as a second way of writing a custom chart. See Gossia pyplot, 0.6.0, below.

### Web port, 0.3.0

*(Requirements settled with the owner on 2026-11-05. The same day the owner moved the target from a public web page to applications that host web content: the first is a Visual Studio Code extension. This section replaces both the open points recorded before and the first, browser-page version of these requirements.)*

**What it is.** The application compiled to wasm and shown inside **another application's web view**: the same tabs, formulas, forms and reports, drawn by egui on a canvas. It has none of what `bidwillii` does (no batch report runs, no `disconnect`), and it does not print.

**Targets, in order.**
1. **A Visual Studio Code extension** (0.3.0): Gossia opens `.gossia` files in VS Code, in a web view, with the extension's Node.js side (the *extension host*) doing what a web page cannot.
2. **0.6.0: a public web page on GitHub Pages** (owner, 2026-11-12), so that people can use Gossia without installing anything. See The web version on GitHub Pages, 0.6.0, below.
- *Dropped (owner, 2026-11-12):* browser extensions and phone and tablet apps, planned for 0.6.0 on 2026-11-09. The web page reaches phones and tablets through their browsers instead.
- **A public web page was not a goal of 0.3.0.** In 0.3.0 the plain browser page served only development and the test harness (see Testing).

**The host interface.** Everything the surroundings provide goes through one interface in the application, with an implementation for each host: VS Code, the plain browser page of the tests, and later others. It covers:
- **the workbook file**: reading it, saving it, and telling the host that there are unsaved changes;
- **opening and saving other files**: import, export, PDF and HTML, through the host's own dialogs;
- **storage**: preferences, the trust record and the data source definitions;
- **secrets**: data source passwords, tokens and keys, kept apart from the rest;
- **HTTP requests** for data sources;
- **opening a file or address outside**, with the system's program for it;
- **the host's theme** (light or dark), which the application follows;
- **messages between panels** (the code editor's, below).
The desktop application does not use the interface; it keeps its own files and settings as now.

**In VS Code.**
- **A custom editor for `.gossia` files.** VS Code opens, saves, reverts and backs up the file (Save, Save As, the unsaved-changes dot, hot exit), and the application tells it when the workbook changes. Undo and redo stay the application's own, inside the panel. The application's own file browser, recent files and *Save and restart* are not there.
- **Import and export** use VS Code's open and save dialogs. Dropping files on the panel imports them where VS Code passes the drop to a web view.
- **Modules open in VS Code's own editor** *(owner, 2026-11-06, in place of a second Gossia panel for the code editor)*, as files of a `gossia:` file system, with VS Code's highlighting, search and Python extensions; saving such a file puts the module in the workbook. The code editor stays in the workbook's panel, drawn inside it as egui draws a second window on the web, for the project explorer, the designs of forms and reports, *Compile* and the Immediate window. The workbook, its modules and the evaluation stay with the workbook's panel. VS Code's **Python debugger does not work** on them: it attaches to a CPython process through debugpy, and Gossia's Python is RustPython in the panel's worker (a debug adapter of our own, through the worker, would be a project of its own; not planned).
- **Storage** is VS Code's extension storage (`globalState`), and **secrets** are VS Code's `SecretStorage`, which keeps them in the operating system's keychain. Passwords are therefore better protected than in `data-sources.json` on the desktop.
- **Keyboard.** VS Code's key bindings stay VS Code's: Save is VS Code's Save, and the application does not take over its shortcuts. Gossia's own commands are also offered in VS Code's Command Palette. Keys VS Code passes to the focused panel work as on the desktop.
- **The theme** follows VS Code's light or dark theme.
- **Distribution** is a `.vsix` file. During development CI puts it in the Gitea package registry, as the MSIX is, to install from a file. A public listing (the Visual Studio Marketplace and Open VSX each need a publisher account) is the owner's choice later. Size is not a concern, since the extension is installed once rather than downloaded per visit.
- Only VS Code on the desktop is the target; the web edition (vscode.dev) is not, because it would bring back the limits of a web page.

**Python runs in a Web Worker** in the panel. The page itself never runs Python, and the worker uses the same message protocol as the desktop's evaluation thread.
- **A formula that overruns its budget** and does not stop at the signal is ended by **terminating the worker** and starting a fresh one, which loads the modules again and recalculates what was outstanding. This takes the place of abandoning the thread on the desktop, so the *abandoned threads* limit and *Save and restart* are left out of the Evaluation page.
- **HTTP data sources** ask the host to make the request. In VS Code the extension host makes it, so **CORS does not apply**. The worker does not wait for the answer: it **stops and runs again** (owner, 2026-11-05, after the spike below; chosen over stack switching because the evaluator already throws a run away and runs it again when it read a node that another formula had still to compute, see the step 2 and step 3 decisions). The difference is that a formula without an answer stops at the call, since there is no old value to give it, and is set aside until the host answers while the evaluator carries on with other formulas.
  - A call to a source whose answer the worker does not have yet ends the formula (or procedure, or report expression) with an internal *needs answer* signal, which is not an error value and is not shown; the cell keeps its old value with the pending mark. The worker asks the page, the page asks the host, and the answer goes into the pass's read cache (see Data sources). The formula is then run again from the start, and this time the call finds its answer in the cache. A formula that makes three calls one after another runs four times; calls are not made in parallel.
  - Formulas are expected to be free of side effects, so running one again is safe. **Procedures and other calls that write** (`post`, `put`, `patch`, `delete`, `execute`) are kept in a log for the run, in the order they were made, with their answers: on a run again, each call made before is matched by its place in that order and gets its logged answer without being sent again, so a write is made once. A run that then makes a different call at a place already logged (the procedure took another path) fails with `#SOURCE!` saying so, and nothing further is sent.
  - The time a formula spends waiting is not counted against its time limit, as on the desktop; the Python time of all its runs is.
  - *Spike (2026-11-05, VS Code 1.146 Insiders, Chromium 150).* A web view is not cross-origin isolated, so `SharedArrayBuffer` does not exist and the worker cannot block on shared memory. A synchronous `XMLHttpRequest` from the worker to a relay the extension host runs on 127.0.0.1 works but is held about 30 seconds per request by Chromium (the page's own asynchronous `fetch` to the same relay takes under 0.2 s), so the relay is not usable. A synchronous request from the worker straight to the site works only where the site allows cross-origin requests. WebAssembly stack switching (JSPI) is available and was the other candidate, but works only in Chromium-based web views; stopping and running again works in every host. Terminating a runaway worker and starting a new one took 6 ms.
- **The other kinds** (SQLite, PostgreSQL, MariaDB, ODBC, Beancount) are not available in 0.3.0. The extension host could open files and network connections, so some of them may come later through the host interface.

**Testing.**
- **The application in a plain browser page**, with a test host behind the host interface, is driven as in the owner's other web projects (keyscarf's `tools/web-test-harness`): a plain HTML and JavaScript **harness page** with no npm and no build step, served locally from the same origin, opens the application in another tab with `window.open`, runs tests against it, and logs `ok` and `FAIL` lines and a summary; a small Playwright script runs it headless for CI. The application draws on a canvas and has no page elements to query, so a **test build** (the `test-control` feature, as on the desktop) gives the window a JavaScript function that calls `control::run` (see the end-to-end tests), and the harness drives it with the same commands as the desktop end-to-end tests. Release builds do not have it.
- **The extension itself** (opening a `.gossia` file in the custom editor, saving it, secrets, the second panel) is tested with VS Code's own test runner. *(Since 2026-11-07 no Node.js is used: VS Code is started for the tests by a Python script and the `.vsix` is packed by another, so the job runs on the Gitea runner.)*

### Excel and ODS files, 0.4.0

*(Scope reviewed with the owner on 2026-11-06. The points marked "default" are Claude's proposals, kept unless the owner changes them.)*

**Importing a workbook** (`.xlsx`, `.xlsm`, `.xls`, `.ods`; `.xlsb` comes free with the reader):
- **Into JSON tabs, as CSV** (owner): there is no Excel tab format. Each sheet becomes a JSON data tab holding an array with an object for each row, read by the CSV rules: a header row unless its cells hold numbers or yes and no, each column typed as a whole, an empty cell leaves the field out, a schema inferred unless a schema tab already fits, one undoable import. Export as `.xlsx` is a separate choice open to any suitable tab, so nothing remembers that a tab came from a workbook.
- **Several sheets: chosen in the dialog** (owner). The import dialog lists the file's sheets with a tick each, all ticked except empty ones; each ticked sheet becomes a data tab and a schema (or is given an existing schema), named `<File>_<Sheet>` and `<File>_<Sheet>Schema`, changeable as now. All of them are one entry in the edit log. A paste is unaffected (a range copied from a spreadsheet is already TSV).
- **Cell types** (default): numbers, text and yes or no as they are; a cell whose number format is a date is read as a date, so that a column of dates becomes text with `"format": "date"`; a column of dates with times stays text (`2026-01-31T14:30:00`), since a JSON Schema `date-time` needs a time zone, which a cell does not have, and the report names those columns; a time of day alone is text (`07:30:00`); an error cell (`#N/A`, `#DIV/0!`) is left out, with a line in the report naming how many.
- **Formulas** (default): the last value the program that saved the file calculated is read; the Excel formula itself is not (it could not run here). The report says how many cells held formulas. A cell with a formula and no saved value counts as empty.
- **Where the table is** (default): the sheet's used range, from its first non-empty row and column, so a table that starts at B3 imports as it is; a merged cell gives its value to the first cell of the merge only; comments, styles, charts, pictures and hidden rows are not read (hidden rows are read as data, with nothing hidden).
- **Reader:** `calamine` (MIT), pure Rust, so the wasm version and the VS Code extension import workbooks too.

**Exporting a tab** as `.xlsx`, `.ods`, CSV or TSV (owner, all four):
- **Arrays of objects only, flattened** (owner): a tab, or an array within it, whose value is an array of objects is written one row per item, with a header row of field names; nested objects become dotted columns (`address.city`), as the table view shows them; nested arrays and maps are left out, and the export window says which columns it left out. Other tabs are not offered these formats.
- **Values only** (owner): a node computed by a formula is written as its value, an error value as its text (`#TYPE!`), never as a formula.
- **Types** (default): numbers as numbers, yes or no as booleans (CSV: `true` and `false`), dates and date-times as dates with a date format in `.xlsx` and `.ods` and in ISO 8601 in CSV and TSV; text that starts with `=`, `+`, `-` or `@` is written as text (CSV quoted), so a spreadsheet does not take it for a formula.
- **CSV and TSV** are written as their reader reads them (RFC 4180, UTF-8 without a BOM, `\r\n` line ends), so a tab exported and imported again gives the same data and schema.
- **Writers:** `rust_xlsxwriter` (MIT or Apache-2.0) for `.xlsx`. There is no mature Rust writer for `.ods`, so Gossia writes it itself: a zip of a `mimetype`, a manifest and one `content.xml` of a single table, with `zip` and `quick-xml`, which the workspace already has.

**Build order** (proposed): **14a** *(built, 2026-11-06; see the decisions for importing workbooks)* importing workbooks (reader, sheet choice in the dialog, dates and the report); **14b** *(built, 2026-11-06; see the decisions for exporting rows)* export of arrays of objects as CSV and TSV (the flattening and the export window, shared by the next); **14c** *(built, 2026-11-06; see the decisions for exporting workbooks)* `.xlsx` export; **14d** *(built, 2026-11-07; see the decisions for exporting ODS)* `.ods` export. Each with tests in `gossia-core` and the window, and the wasm build checked to keep building.

**Decisions made for importing workbooks** (step 14a, 2026-11-06).
- **`gossia-core::spreadsheet`** reads a workbook with `calamine` 0.38 (MIT; its `chrono` feature for dates, which takes the 1904 date system into account) into one `SheetRead` per worksheet: its name, its rows and a `JsonInference`, as `delimited` gives for CSV. Chart and dialog sheets are passed over. Its own typing, not `delimited`'s: the cells come typed, so a column is whole numbers when every number in it is whole (and below 2^53), numbers when they are all numbers, yes or no, dates, dates with times, or text; a mix of kinds is text, each cell written as it shows (`2`, `2.5`, `true`, `2026-01-31`). Text that reads as a number stays text (`007`). The header row is taken unless a cell of the first row is a number, a date or a yes or no. Rows with nothing in them are passed over, and so are columns past the last one holding something. Formulas are counted with `worksheet_formula`, where the reader has them, for the report.
- **The dialog** (`ImportPlan::sheets`): a workbook's plan holds a `SheetPlan` for each worksheet (its title, whether it is ticked, its rows, and a plan of its own, made as a CSV file's is, with its own schema candidates and inferred schema). The list at the top of the dialog has a tick for each sheet that has rows, named `Import sheet <name>` for the tests, and the sheet's name with its rows (*Costs: 2 rows*, *Notes: empty*); clicking a name shows that sheet's settings below. An empty sheet cannot be ticked. Names are `<File>_<Sheet>` and `<File>_<Sheet>Schema`, kept apart from each other as well as from the workbook's tabs; a slash in a sheet's name (ODS allows one) becomes `_`.
- **One entry**, *Import Budget.xlsx*, holds the tabs of every sheet ticked (`import_sheets`, with the tab making of one plan as `create_tabs`, shared with every other import); the first becomes the current tab. Refused before anything is made: no sheet ticked, a sheet's own problem (named with the sheet), or two new tabs of one name.
- *File > Import...* lists Excel and ODS beside JSON, XML, CSV and TSV. The wasm version and the VS Code extension import workbooks too: `calamine` builds for wasm, and the host hands over the bytes.
- **Known limit of the reader:** `calamine` refuses an `.ods` whose XML has white space between the cells, which no spreadsheet program writes.
- **Tests:** `spreadsheet_tests.rs` (a sheet that starts at B3 with typed columns, dates, dates with times, a formula, an error and `007`; a first row of numbers, a blank row and a mixed column; an `.ods` with a date; a file that is not a workbook), `the_ticked_sheets_of_a_workbook_are_imported_in_one_entry` (names, refusals, one undo) and the window test `the_import_dialog_of_a_workbook_lists_its_sheets_to_tick`. The workbooks are made by the tests, `.xlsx` with `rust_xlsxwriter` (a development dependency until 14c) and `.ods` by zipping its XML, since the repository keeps no binary files.

**Decisions made for exporting rows** (step 14b, 2026-11-06).
- **`gossia-core::tabular`** lays an array of objects out as a `Table` (column names, rows of typed cells, the fields left out), for CSV and TSV now and for `.xlsx` and `.ods` in 14c and 14d. The columns come from the items' type, in its declared order, not from the data, so every row has the same columns and an item without a field has an empty cell. A field that is an object becomes dotted columns to any depth; a field that is a list or a map is left out and named. An item that is itself an error is passed over; an error value inside an item is written as its code (`#TYPE!`); an `any` field holding a list or a record is written as its JSON.
- **Which arrays** (`row_arrays`): the root if it is an array of objects, and the arrays of objects reached through the fields of objects (`items`, `meta.notes`), in the order of the schema. Arrays inside arrays are not offered, since which item's would be meant. A tab with none is not offered CSV or TSV, nor is a schema tab.
- **The text** (`to_delimited`): a header row, a comma or a tab between fields, `\r\n` line ends, UTF-8 without a byte order mark. Quoting is as little as reads back the same: text is quoted when it is empty, holds the separator, a quote or a line break, starts or ends with a space, starts with `=`, `+`, `-` or `@`, or would be read as a number or a yes or no (`"007"`, `"true"`); a header name is quoted on the same rule, so a header of numbers stays a header. A whole number of a number field keeps its `.0` (`19.0`), so the column reads back as numbers, not whole numbers. Dates are ISO (`2026-01-31`), dates with times ISO 8601 with their zone if they have one. An empty value is nothing between the separators, which the reader leaves out.
- **The export window:** *CSV, a row for each item* and *TSV, a row for each item* follow the tab's other formats. With more than one array, a *Rows from* list picks it (the tab's name for the root). The window says which fields are left out and that fields of records become columns such as `address.city`. Indented or compact and the schema tick are greyed: a schema is never written beside rows, since the CSV reader infers one.
- **Control and wasm:** the `export` command takes `"format": "csv"` or `"tsv"` and an optional `"rows"` path, refused when it is not one of the tab's arrays of records; without it, the first. The wasm version and the VS Code extension export rows too, as the bytes go through the host as every export does.
- **Tests:** `tabular_tests.rs` (arrays found through objects; dotted columns and what is left out; CSV written and read back by the CSV reader to the same JSON; quoting in TSV of text that reads as a number; a header of numbers), the calc test of export formats, and the window test `a_list_of_records_is_exported_as_csv_and_tsv_rows` (values not formulas, no schema file, imported back, TSV, a field that is not a list refused).

**Decisions made for exporting workbooks** (step 14c, 2026-11-06).
- **`tabular::to_xlsx`** writes the same `Table` as CSV with `rust_xlsxwriter` 0.102, now a dependency of `gossia-core` (no `chrono` feature, which would turn on chrono's clock and time zones for the whole workspace; dates are made with `ExcelDateTime::from_ymd`). On wasm its `wasm` feature is on, since a new workbook reads the clock, which panics there without JavaScript's; `js-sys` and `wasm-bindgen` were already in the wasm build, at the pinned versions.
- **One worksheet**, named after the tab, or after the array when it is not the tab's root (`items`, `meta.notes`), made into a name a spreadsheet accepts (`sheet_name`: `[ ] : * ? / \` become `_`, at most 31 characters, `Sheet1` if nothing is left). A bold header row, frozen so it stays in view, and the columns fitted to their contents.
- **Types:** whole numbers and numbers as numbers, yes or no as booleans, dates with the format `yyyy-mm-dd`, dates with times with `yyyy-mm-dd hh:mm:ss`; text is always written as text, so `=SUM(A1)` and `007` stay text. What a cell cannot hold as it is becomes text, so nothing changes on the way: a whole number beyond 2^53, a number that is not finite, a date before 1900 or after 9999, and a date and time with a zone (a cell has none). Refused, naming the limit: more than 1,048,575 rows, more than 16,384 columns, or text longer than the 32,767 characters a cell holds (naming the row and column).
- **Read back** by the workbook import of 14a, the data is the same, with one known difference: a number column whose values are all whole (totals of `19.0`, `24.0`) comes back as whole numbers, since a cell does not tell a whole number from a number.
- *Excel workbook, a row for each item* follows CSV and TSV in the export window, with the same *Rows from* list and notes; the control `export` command takes `"format": "xlsx"`. An Excel workbook is bytes: `export_bytes` writes it, `export_text` refuses it.
- **Tests:** `an_xlsx_workbook_is_written_as_the_workbook_reader_reads_it_back`, `dates_are_dates_in_a_workbook_and_what_a_cell_cannot_hold_is_text`, `a_sheet_name_is_one_a_spreadsheet_accepts`, and the window test `a_list_of_records_is_exported_as_an_excel_workbook` (the extension added, the sheet named `items`, imported back).

**Decisions made for exporting ODS** (step 14d, 2026-11-07).
- **`gossia-core::ods`** writes the same `Table` itself, as planned: a zip (`zip`, now a dependency of `gossia-core`, deflate only, timestamps fixed at 1980 so the same table gives the same bytes) of the `mimetype` first and stored, as the format requires, `META-INF/manifest.xml`, `content.xml` with the one table, and `settings.xml` with the header row frozen (LibreOffice's view settings: `VerticalSplitMode` 2 at row 1). No `styles.xml` or `meta.xml`: the format does not need them. The XML has no white space between elements, which the workbook reader needs to read it back.
- **The sheet** is named as for `.xlsx` (`tabular::sheet_name`). A bold header style, date formats `yyyy-mm-dd` and `yyyy-mm-dd hh:mm:ss`, and each column about as wide as its longest value (4 to 60 characters).
- **Types** as for `.xlsx`: numbers as floats with `office:value`, yes or no as booleans, dates and dates with times as dates (any year from 1 to 9999, since the format has no 1900 limit); a whole number beyond 2^53, a number that is not finite, a date outside those years and a date and time with a zone as text. Text keeps every space: runs, and spaces at the ends, which a spreadsheet would collapse or drop, are `<text:s/>`, a tab is `<text:tab/>`, a line break `<text:line-break/>`; a control character XML cannot hold becomes U+FFFD. There is no limit on the length of text. The limits on rows and columns are those of `.xlsx`, which LibreOffice has too.
- **Read back** by the workbook import, the data is the same, with the same difference as `.xlsx`: a number column of whole values comes back as whole numbers.
- *ODS spreadsheet, a row for each item* comes last in the export window; the control `export` command takes `"format": "ods"`. Like a workbook, it is bytes: `export_text` refuses both with *as a spreadsheet file is bytes, not text*.
- **Not checked by the tests:** that LibreOffice and Excel open the file as intended (the frozen row, the widths), since neither is on the build machines; the tests check the bytes and read them back with `calamine`.
- **Tests:** `ods_tests.rs` (read back with dates, spaces, tabs, line breaks and markup characters; the `mimetype` first and stored; the paragraph rules), and the window test `a_list_of_records_is_exported_as_an_ods_spreadsheet`, which shares its steps with the `.xlsx` one.

### Calendars and contacts, 0.4.0
