*(Asked for by the owner on 2026-11-07. The points marked "owner" are the owner's choices; those marked "default" are Claude's proposals, kept unless the owner changes them.)*

**What is imported.**
- **An `.ics` file** (iCalendar, RFC 5545) gives **its events and its tasks, each into a tab of its own** (owner): the `VEVENT`s into an array with an object for each event, the `VTODO`s into another. Journal entries (`VJOURNAL`), free/busy blocks and alarms are not read; the report says how many there were.
- **A `.vcf` file** (vCard 2.1, 3.0 and 4.0) gives its contacts, an object for each, into one tab.
- Both come in by *File > Import...*, by dropping the file on the window, and by pasting the text (a paste that starts with `BEGIN:VCALENDAR` or `BEGIN:VCARD`).

**Fixed schemas** (default). The schema is not inferred: Gossia writes one of three schemas it knows, *Calendar events*, *Calendar tasks* and *Contacts* (the schema's root `title`), so that every import of the same kind has the same fields, a later file can be added to the tab, and the tab can be exported again as `.ics` or `.vcf`. The schema tab is an ordinary one and can be edited; a field added to it is kept, and export writes the fields it knows. A tab is taken to be one of these kinds when its schema's root title is the kind's.

**Adding to a tab that is already there** (owner).
- When the workbook has a tab of the kind (bound to a schema with that title), the import dialog **asks whether to add the entries to it or make a new tab**, and starts on adding to the first such tab. With events and tasks, each is asked about on its own, as the sheets of a workbook are, each with a tick.
- **An entry whose UID is already in the tab replaces it** (owner), in its place; new entries are added at the end in the file's order; entries in the tab that are not in the file stay. An event or task that changes one occurrence of a repeating one has the same UID and a `recurrence_id`, so the two together are what is matched. A contact without a UID (common in vCard 2.1 and 3.0) is matched by its full name. Two entries in the file with the same key: the later wins, and the report says so.
- Adding is one entry in the edit log, *Import calendar.ics* (with the tabs of a calendar's other part, if any), and undo takes the whole import back. An entry replaced loses any formulas inside it; formulas elsewhere in the tab are untouched.

**The fields** (default; names as Gossia's other fields, in English).
- **Events:** `uid` (required), `recurrence_id`, `summary`, `start` and `end` (date-times), or `start_date` and `end_date` (dates) for an all-day event, where `end_date` is the last day (the file's `DTEND` is the day after; a `DURATION` is turned into an end), `time_zone`, `location`, `description`, `status` (`tentative`, `confirmed` or `cancelled`), `categories` (a list), `organizer` (`name`, `email`), `attendees` (a list of `name`, `email`, `role`, `status`), `url`, `recurrence` (the `RRULE` as written, `FREQ=WEEKLY;BYDAY=MO`, not expanded into occurrences), `excluded` (the `EXDATE`s, as text), `sequence`, `created`, `last_modified`.
- **Tasks:** `uid` (required), `recurrence_id`, `summary`, `start` or `start_date`, `due` or `due_date`, `completed`, `percent_complete` (0 to 100), `priority` (0 to 9), `status` (`needs-action`, `in-process`, `completed`, `cancelled`), `time_zone`, `location`, `description`, `categories`, `url`, `recurrence`, `sequence`, `created`, `last_modified`.
- **Contacts:** `uid`, `full_name` (required), `name` (`family`, `given`, `additional`, `prefix`, `suffix`), `nickname`, `organization`, `department`, `title`, `role`, `birthday` and `anniversary` (dates), `emails` (a list of `address`, `type`), `phones` (`number`, `type`), `addresses` (`type`, `po_box`, `extended`, `street`, `locality`, `region`, `postal_code`, `country`), `urls` (a list), `note`, `categories`, `revision`. `type` holds the vCard types joined with commas (`work,voice`).
- **Left out, and counted in the report:** pictures, logos, sounds and keys in contacts; attachments and alarms in events; `X-` properties and any property not listed. A birthday without a year (`--0415`) cannot be a date and is left out, with a line in the report.

**Times** (owner: converted with a time zone database).
- A time with a zone (`DTSTART;TZID=Australia/Brisbane:20261107T090000`) is converted with the **IANA time zone database bundled with Gossia** (`jiff`'s `tzdb-bundle-always`, so the wasm version and the VS Code extension have it too) into a date-time with its offset, `2026-11-07T09:00:00+10:00`; the zone's name is kept in `time_zone`, so that export can write the times in it again. A UTC time (`...Z`) is a date-time in UTC.
- Zone names that are not IANA names: Windows names (`AUS Eastern Standard Time`, written by Outlook) through the table of Windows zones in Unicode CLDR, kept in the source; names with a prefix such as `/mozilla.org/20050126_1/Europe/Berlin` by their IANA part. A name still unknown is converted with the offsets of the file's own `VTIMEZONE` for it (its standard and daylight rules), and the report names it.
- A floating time (no zone and no `Z`, which RFC 5545 means as the same clock time everywhere) is taken in the computer's time zone, and the report says so.

**Reading.** Gossia's own reader for both formats, which share the line syntax (RFC 5545 §3.1, RFC 6350 §3.3): folded lines joined, `NAME;PARAM=value:value`, quoted parameter values, the escapes `\n`, `\,`, `\;` and `\\`, quoted-printable and character sets in vCard 2.1. UTF-8; a BOM is stripped. Bounded as other imports are (file size, number of entries). No crate is added for the formats themselves.

**Exporting** (owner: export too). A tab of one of the three kinds can be exported as **`.ics`** (events or tasks: a `VCALENDAR` of `VEVENT`s or `VTODO`s) or **`.vcf`** (vCard 3.0, which Apple, Google and Outlook all read).
- Times are written in the entry's `time_zone` with a `VTIMEZONE` for each zone used, made from the bundled database (its changes over the years the entries span), so that a repeating event keeps its clock time across daylight saving; an entry without a `time_zone` is written in UTC. All-day entries are written as dates, `DTEND` the day after `end_date`.
- Lines are folded at 75 bytes and end in `\r\n`, as the formats require; text is escaped. `PRODID` is `-//Gossia//Gossia//EN`. An event or task without a `uid`, and a contact without a `full_name`, is left out of the file, since the formats require them (the schema marks them required, so the tab shows the gap).
- An entry's fields that the schema has gained beyond the known ones are not written. What a file had that Gossia does not keep (alarms, pictures, `X-` properties) is not there to write back: import and export are a round trip for the listed fields only.

**Build order:** **14e** reading `.ics` (the shared line reader, events and tasks, the time zone database and the Windows zone table) with the dialog's choice of adding to a tab; **14f** reading `.vcf`; **14g** exporting `.ics` and `.vcf`. Each with tests in `gossia-core` and the window, translations of the new texts in the five languages, and the wasm build checked. All three built on 2026-11-07.

**Decisions made building 14e–14g** (default unless marked).
- **Where the code is.** `gossia-core`: `contentline` (the shared line reader, escaping and folding), `timezone` (IANA, Windows and prefixed names, `VTIMEZONE` rules), `windows_zones` (CLDR release 47, generated, sorted for a binary search), `calendar` and `vcard` (reading, the schemas, the keys), `entries_write` (writing). `gossia-calc`: `Calc::entry_kind`, `tabs_of_kind` and `add_entries` (`entries.rs`). The window: `ImportFormat::Calendar` and `Contacts`, `SchemaChoice::AddTo` in the import dialog, `ExportFormat::Calendar` and `Contacts` in the export dialog.
- **The dialog.** A calendar's two parts are shown as a workbook's sheets are (*Parts to import*, each with its number of entries, an empty part not ticked); a contacts file is one plan. Above the schema choices come *Add to the tab …* radios, one for each tab of the kind, the first chosen; below them *Or as a new tab, with the schema*, where a schema tab of the kind that is there already is chosen before a new one. The inferred-schema radio reads *A new schema for calendar events, called* and has no schema to show, since it is the fixed one. New tabs are named `<file>_Events` and `<file>_Tasks`, or after the `.vcf` file.
- **A paste** of calendar or contacts text is never offered as replacing the tab shown, even on a JSON tab: it is added or made a tab like a file.
- **Floating times** take the zone of the computer (`jiff`'s system zone; on wasm, the browser's).
- **Times on export.** A time is written in its `time_zone` only when the database knows that name; any other (no zone, or one only the imported file described) is written in UTC, the same instant, so such a time comes back as `…Z` rather than with its old offset. The `VTIMEZONE` lists the zone's changes from the last one before the earliest time written to ten years after the latest, each kind of change one `STANDARD` or `DAYLIGHT` with its dates as `RDATE`s, so readers without the database (and Gossia, given the file under a made-up zone name) convert the same way.
- **vCard 3.0 has no anniversary**: it is written as `X-ANNIVERSARY`, which Apple and others read, and reading takes `X-ANNIVERSARY` when there is no `ANNIVERSARY`.
- **Opening a calendar from Windows.** The MSIX registers `.ics` (as `text/calendar`) besides `.gossia` (owner, 2026-11-07); Windows lets the user choose Gossia as the default program for it, but no program can make itself the default. Started with a file that is not a workbook, Gossia opens a blank workbook with that file's import dialog, as dropping it would.

**Decisions made for moving CI off GitHub's self-hosted runner** (owner, 2026-11-07, before the GitHub repository is made public).
- **Why.** On a public repository, a pull request from a fork can run its own workflow on any self-hosted runner registered with it, so a runner on the owner's machines must not be registered with GitHub. The Gitea mirror is pushed to only by the owner.
- **GitHub** keeps lint and the tests, on its own Ubuntu runners (free for public repositories), for pushes and pull requests, with read-only permissions and no secrets. The build goes to `/mnt/target`; the runners no longer have a second disk there, so `.github/prepare-runner.sh` also deletes the toolkits the build never uses (.NET, Android, Haskell, CodeQL, Boost) to make room, and installs the libraries. There is no cache: each run builds everything.
- **Gitea** (`.gitea/workflows/ci.yml`, runner `selfhosted`) runs the rest, for pushes to main: lint, the tests with the live databases, Vendor, E2E, Snapshots, Web and VS Code, the `.vsix`, and the sweep of `target/`, in two jobs of steps rather than a job each, as every job would repeat the shell checkout and the runner takes one job at a time anyway. Each step runs whatever the ones before did (`!cancelled()`); the advisory ones are `continue-on-error`. The checkout is the same `~/ci/gossia` as the packages', one job after another.
- **No Node.js.** The Gitea runners have none, and the owner preferred Python to installing it: `vscode/test/run.py` replaces `run.js` and `@vscode/test-electron`, and `packaging/vscode/pack-vsix.py` replaces `vsce`, so the extension has no `package-lock.json` or development packages. The files of a failed step, which `upload-artifact` (a Node.js action) kept, go to the generic package `gossia-ci` under the commit's hash (`packaging/keep-ci-files.sh`).
- **No machine names in the repository.** The Gitea server is the job's own (`gitea.server_url`) in CI and the Git remote `gitea` by hand (`publish-generic.sh`, `build-msix.ps1`); the comments no longer name the hosts. Older commits still do; they are left as they are, since without a registered runner the names give nothing to run code on.
- **Left to the owner on GitHub:** remove the self-hosted runner and its secrets. On Gitea: the secrets `GOSSIA_TEST_POSTGRES` and `GOSSIA_TEST_MARIADB`.

### XML Schemas and namespaces, 0.5.0

*(Scope settled with the owner on 2026-11-09. The points marked "default" are Claude's proposals, kept unless the owner changes them.)*

**The goal.** Dropping a common XML document that uses namespaces onto the window (or importing it) just works: Gossia infers **as many XSD tabs as the document needs**, one per namespace, and the data tab reads and writes the file back unchanged, apart from what "Not preserved (accepted)" lists. XSD is widened only as far as that needs (owner: "I need common XML documents to work when imported; enable as many of these as would achieve that"). Type extension, `nillable` and `xs:any` are not needed by inference, so they stay out. They return only if a document in the corpus (below) needs them.

**Namespaces in the XSD subset.**
- **One XSD tab per namespace** (the no-namespace part counts as one). A tab's schema imports the others with `xs:import namespace="…" schemaLocation="<Tab>"`, where the location names the XSD tab. On export the location becomes the exported file's name (`<Tab>.xsd`). `xs:include` is not needed by inference and stays out (default).
- **More than one global `xs:element`**, and `ref` to a global element of this namespace or an imported one. An element from another namespace is always a global element of that namespace's tab, used by `ref`, as XSD requires.
- **Global `xs:attribute`** and `ref` to it, for attributes in a namespace. `attributeFormDefault` and `form` on a local element or attribute may be `qualified`.
- **The data tab's root** is one of the global elements of the schema it is bound to. Which one is part of its binding (default: the element the document started with).
- **Built in, never inferred**: the `xml:` namespace (`xml:lang`, `xml:space`, `xml:base`, `xml:id`) as a built-in schema `builtin:xml`, imported like a tab. The `xsi:` attributes (`type`, `nil`, `schemaLocation`, `noNamespaceSchemaLocation`) are fields like any other (`@xsi:type`), because `xsi:type` is often the data itself: in an Archi model it says what each `<element>` is (owner, 2026-11-09). Standard XSD forbids declaring them, so inside Gossia an XSD tab may list them with `xs:attribute ref="xsi:type"`, no import needed. Then they are table columns, form fields and in *Add field* like any attribute, and inference adds them where it sees them. **Exporting the `.xsd` leaves those lines out**, and the export says so, so the exported file stays standard XSD. An imported `.xsd` never has them. An `xsi:type` value is kept as text, and its prefix stays declared. Gossia does not switch the element's type by it.

**Names in the tree, forms and Python** (owner: prefix outside the main namespace). Names in the tab's own namespace stay bare (`title`, `@id`). Names from any other namespace carry the prefix: `dc:title`, `@xml:lang`, `atom:link`. The prefix is the one the document used. If a document uses two prefixes for one namespace, the first is used. The same prefixes appear in `XMLPATH` queries (`//dc:creator`), and Python reaches such fields by key (`item["dc:creator"]`). Two names with the same local part never clash, because only one of them is bare.

**Children in a varying order** (owner: ordered list). Inference checks each element's children. When their order varies (between instances, or within one, as in `rect, path, rect`), the element's children become **one ordered list**: the field `#children`, an array whose items are each one element, `{"rect": …}` or `{"path": …}`. In XSD this is `xs:choice maxOccurs="unbounded"`, the first union in the subset, lowered to a new `Choice` kind. It is written back in the list's order. Elements whose children keep one order keep today's fields by name. Attributes stay fields beside `#children`. *Add field* on such an element adds a choice to the list. *Add item* on the list asks which element.

**Mixed content** (owner: kept as raw XML). An element that holds text beside child elements (XHTML paragraphs, DocBook, SVG `<text>` with `<tspan>`) is inferred as `mixed="true"` and holds **one string of XML**: its content exactly as written, inline elements included. It is shown read-only in the tree and on forms, edited in the Raw view, and written back as it was. Formulas see the string. It is never parsed into fields.

**DOCTYPE and processing instructions** (owner: keep both). The DOCTYPE declaration and processing instructions outside the root (`<?xml-stylesheet?>`, `<?mso-application?>`) are kept verbatim and written back. DTDs are still never expanded and external entities never fetched. Processing instructions inside the root stay dropped with a warning (default).

**Namespace declarations** are kept on the element where they were written, not only on the root, so a SOAP body or an inline SVG keeps its own `xmlns`.

**Import and inference.**
- The import dialog lists every XSD tab it will add (one per namespace, named after the prefix: `Feed`, `Feed_dc`, `Feed_atom`; default) and, as today, offers existing schema tabs that fit. Existing tabs for a namespace are reused when the data fits them (default).
- The dialog's report says which elements became ordered lists and which became mixed content.
- An `.xsd` file imported on its own may now use the widened subset, and an imported `.xsd` whose `xs:import` points at a file next to it brings that file in as a tab too (default).

**The corpus** (default). A test corpus of common documents, each of which must import by drag and drop, infer, and write back unchanged: an RSS 2.0 feed with `dc:`, `content:` and `atom:` (and `<?xml-stylesheet?>`), an Atom feed, `sitemap.xml`, a Maven `pom.xml` (default namespace and `xsi:schemaLocation`), GPX, KML, SVG (with `xlink:`), XHTML, an Android manifest and layout (`android:` attributes), an SDK-style `.csproj`, a plist (DOCTYPE, key and value pairs), a SOAP envelope, XAML, and a Word `document.xml`, and an Archi model (root in a namespace, unqualified elements below it, `xsi:type` on most elements; the test uses a made-up model).

**Build order** (default):
- **15a.** *(done, 2026-11-10)* Reading and writing: declarations on any element, DOCTYPE and processing instructions, mixed content as raw XML; the corpus read and written back.
- **15b.** The XSD subset: several namespaces, `xs:import` between tabs, global elements and attributes with `ref`, `xsi:` attributes (declared inside Gossia, left out of an exported `.xsd`), `form` and `attributeFormDefault`, `builtin:xml`, prefixed names, `XMLPATH` with prefixes; the meta-schema widened with it. Split in two:
  - **15b-1.** *(done, 2026-11-10)* The engine: lowering with imports, refs, `form`, the built-in `xml` and `xsi` schemas; reading and writing by namespace; inference of `xml:` and `xsi:` attributes beside one namespace; `XMLPATH` prefixes; `xsi:` refs left out of an exported `.xsd`; the meta-schema widened where no tab path changes.
  - **15b-2.** *(done, 2026-11-10)* The workbook: `xs:import` resolved to schema tabs, the choice of root element when binding, several global elements in a schema tab (the meta-schema's `element` becomes a list, which changes XSD-tab paths and the field commands), `schemaLocation` rewritten on export, field commands across namespaces.
- **15c.** `Choice` and `#children`: lowering, reading, writing, validation, the tree, forms and tables, the field commands. Split in two:
  - **15c-1.** *(done, 2026-11-10)* The engine: the `Choice` kind, lowering a repeating `xs:choice`, reading and writing `#children` in order, validation, inference (with attributes in the document's own namespace, which Word needs), the meta-schema, the corpus.
  - **15c-2.** *(done, 2026-11-10)* The window: `#children` in the tree, on forms and in tables; *Add item* asking which element; *Add field* on such an element adding a choice; the field commands on the elements of a choice.
- **15d.** Inference of several XSD tabs, the import dialog, `.xsd` files with their imports, drag and drop of the whole corpus. Split in three:
  - **15d-1.** *(done, 2026-11-10)* The engine: one inferred XSD per namespace, global elements and attributes referred to across them, the whole corpus importing and written back.
  - **15d-2.** *(done, 2026-11-10)* The workbook and the import dialog: the XSD tabs a drop needs added together with the data tab, existing tabs for a namespace reused, the report.
  - **15d-3.** *(done, 2026-11-10)* An `.xsd` file whose `xs:import` points at a file beside it brings that file in as a tab too.

**Decisions made for reading and writing** (step 15a, 2026-11-10; default unless marked).
- **What stands outside the root** (`xmltree::Misc`): comments, processing instructions (`<?…?>`, kept as their text) and the DOCTYPE (kept as its text, never read). In a data tab the comments before the root are still the root's comment, and the rest is the document's `prolog`, written after the XML declaration and before that comment, so a comment written between a DOCTYPE and a processing instruction moves after both (accepted). An internal subset is kept with a warning that its entities are not expanded, and a reference to such an entity is still refused, as before. `XmlDocument.after` keeps comments and processing instructions; it is saved untagged, so the plain strings of older workbooks read back as comments.
- **No declaration stays none** (`XmlDocument::without_declaration`): a file that had no XML declaration (a `.csproj`) is written back without one. A new document still gets the default declaration.
- **Declarations below the root are on their node** (`Node::xml_declarations`), boxed like the comments inside an element, and they go where the node goes (copied with its comments). The root's stay the document's. Writing puts them before the element's attributes. Where a declaration changes the meaning of names below it is 15b's business.
- **Markup is a string format** (`StringFormat::Markup`, `Kind::is_markup`), not a kind of its own, so everything that handles strings handles it. The XSD form is exactly `mixed="true"` with a `sequence` of one `xs:any processContents="skip" minOccurs="0" maxOccurs="unbounded"` (with no `namespace`, or `##any`); any other mixed form is refused with that form in the message, and `xs:any` anywhere else is refused as before. With no attributes the element's value is the markup; beside attributes the markup is `#text`. The XSD meta-schema has `mixed` on a complex type and `any` in a sequence, so a schema tab holds it.
- **Reading and writing markup.** The inside of the element (text, elements, comments) is written out as one string, `xmltree::write_fragment`, so its comments are part of it, not comments on the node, and entity spelling follows the rule above. Writing reads it back with `xmltree::parse_fragment`, which does not require the prefixes to be declared (they are, in the document). Markup that is not well-formed is flagged by validation ("is not well-formed XML") and written as text, escaped, so nothing is lost. The pretty writer never lays out anything inside an element that holds text, at any depth, since that would change the text.
- **Markup is read-only** (owner): `GossiaApp::can_edit` refuses it, which also makes it read-only on forms, with the message that it is edited in the Raw view. The node inspector describes the type as XML markup.
- **Inference.** An element that holds text next to child elements in any instance makes its position a mixed type, and what is inside it is not looked at further, including namespaces (inside markup they do not matter). The report says which elements became markup.
- **Typed values keep their spelling** (`Node::lexical`; found by the corpus, in the spirit of the owner's "written back unchanged"). When text read as a number, a yes or no, a date or a date-time would be written differently (`1.0`, `+7`, ` 2.5 `, `…Z`, `1` for true), the text is kept on the node, and the writer uses it as long as it still reads as the node's value; a changed value is written as before. JSON tabs are not changed.
- **The corpus** is `gossia-core/tests/xml-corpus/` with `tests/xml_corpus.rs`: all 16 documents read and write back as the same tree; the documents of the steps done (`DONE`) must also infer, read, validate and write back as the same text. After 15a that is the sitemap, Atom, KML, the `.csproj` and DocBook.
- **Known limit.** Entities defined only in an external DTD (`&nbsp;` in XHTML) are still refused as undefined, as before; the corpus avoids them.
- **Tests.** In `xmltree.rs`: the DOCTYPE and processing instructions kept, an internal subset, fragments. In `xml_tests.rs`: the DOCTYPE and processing instructions written back, declarations below the root, markup that is not well-formed, spellings kept until changed. In `xsd_tests.rs`: mixed content lowered and its other forms refused. In `infer_tests.rs`: mixed content inferred and written back. In the app, `xml_fields.rs`: markup is read-only.

**Decisions made for the engine** (step 15b-1, 2026-11-10; default unless marked).
- **Names in the model.** A field keeps its namespace (`Field::namespace`, saved with a default of none). A name is bare when it is in the main schema's target namespace or in none, and otherwise `prefix:local`, `@prefix:local` for an attribute. The prefix is the one the schemas declare for that namespace; `xml` is always `xml`; one never declared gets `xsi` for the instance namespace and `ns1`, `ns2`, … otherwise. The prefixes used are kept on the schema (`XmlInfo::prefixes`) for writing.
- **Attributes have namespaces.** `xmltree::Attribute::namespace` is resolved when the document is read, and an undeclared attribute prefix is an error, like an element's. An attribute without a prefix is in no namespace.
- **Lowering** (`xsd::lower_with(root, imports, root_element)`): `imports` finds the schema a `schemaLocation` names (in 15b-2, a tab), and the imports' imports are followed, each namespace once. An import with no `schemaLocation`, one that cannot be found, or one whose `targetNamespace` is another is an error naming it. The `xml` and `xsi` namespaces are built in (`builtin:xml/schema`, `builtin:xsi/schema`) and never looked up, so the standard `xs:import` of `xml.xsd` works offline. `xs:include`, `xs:redefine` and `xs:override` stay refused, with a message pointing at `xs:import`. `xsd::lower` imports nothing but the built-in namespaces.
- **Global declarations.** A schema may have several global elements and global attributes; the root is the global element asked for, or the first. All of the main schema's global types, elements and attributes are lowered, so a mistake anywhere in it is reported, not only in what the root reaches. `ref` reaches any global element or attribute, of this schema or an imported one; a type in a namespace no schema is imported for is an error saying so.
- **`form`.** `elementFormDefault`, `attributeFormDefault` (now `qualified` too) and `form` on a local element or attribute decide whether the name is in the target namespace.
- **Reading and writing** match elements and attributes to fields by namespace and local name, so any prefix in the file works. Undefined content keeps its document prefix when in another namespace. The writer uses the document's declarations, declares on the root a prefix it needs that is not there yet, and writes `xmlns=""` on an unqualified element below a default target namespace.
- **Inference beside one namespace.** `xml:` and `xsi:` attributes no longer make a document "another namespace": the inferred schema refers to them (`<xs:attribute ref="xsi:type"/>`), declares their prefix, and imports `xml.xsd` when `xml:` is used. Attributes are ordered the way children already were, merged from every instance (a new attribute goes after the one before it), since an instance that leaves one out would otherwise put it last (found with Archi files).
- **`xsi:` on export** (owner): an exported `.xsd` leaves out every `xs:attribute ref` into the instance namespace, and that namespace's declarations (`xsd::without_xsi`); the schema tab keeps them.
- **`XMLPATH`** reads one `prefix:` in a name step (`dc:creator`, `@xml:lang`), the field name; prefixes are not resolved against namespaces.
- **Meta-schema.** Widened where no XSD-tab path changes: `import` and global `attribute` lists on the schema, `form` on elements and attributes, `ref` on attributes (their `name` now optional), `attributeFormDefault` with both values. Several global elements in a tab wait for 15b-2.
- **The corpus** after 15b-1: XHTML, the `pom.xml`, GPX and the Archi model also import and write back unchanged. RSS, the Android manifest, SOAP and XAML need several XSD tabs and move to 15d. Namespace declarations are written before an element's other attributes (accepted above), so the made-up GPX file is written that way.
- **Tests.** In `xsd_tests.rs`: imports and refs named by namespace, another root, missing and wrong imports, `xsi:` left out. In `gossia-calc`: an exported XSD tab without `xsi:` (`xsd_fields_tests.rs`), prefixed names in `XMLPATH` (`xmlpath.rs`).

**Decisions made for the workbook** (step 15b-2, 2026-11-10; default unless marked).
- **An import names a tab** (`Calc::xsd_import_tab`): the XSD tab whose name is the `schemaLocation`, or the file name in it without `.xsd` (`dc.xsd` and `schemas/dc.xsd` both name the tab `Dc`); tab names ignore case. A location that names no tab is the import error of 15b-1 on the importing tab, until the tab is there.
- **Imported tabs are lowered with their importers.** When an XSD tab changes, is added, or is renamed, every XSD tab that imports it, directly or not, is lowered again and gives its tabs the new type (`relower_schema`, once each, so a cycle ends). Opening a workbook needs no order among schema tabs.
- **Renaming a tab renames its imports**: the `schemaLocation` of each import that named it becomes the new name, in the same history entry (undoing names it back; a `dc.xsd` spelling becomes the tab name). **Deleting a tab another imports is refused**, naming the importers, as deleting a schema tab in use already was.
- **Export.** An exported `.xsd` writes each `schemaLocation` that names a tab as `<tab>.xsd`, the file that tab exports to, so the files work side by side; the tab keeps its own spelling.
- **Several global elements in a tab.** The meta-schema's global `element` is a list, so an XSD tab's paths are `element[0]…`; saved tabs are kept as XSD text and read back unchanged. Writing a schema tab puts its global elements together, then its attributes, then its types (as the meta-schema orders them), whatever order the file had; 15c's ordered lists may lift that.
- **The root a tab holds** is its global element, kept in the tab's `record`, which copybooks already used for their 01 record and the workbook already saved (none for the first element). It is chosen by name, never asked: importing an XML file into an XSD tab with several global elements offers one choice per element that reads the document (in practice the one named like its root, shown as `Books (shelf)`), and binding or rebinding a tab to an XSD tab keeps the global element of the tab's current root's name, or the first. `Calc::xsd_roots`, `Calc::create_xml_tab`, and `Calc::record_spec` for an XSD.
- **Field commands across namespaces.** A field that is a `ref` (`dc:title`, `@dc:lang`) is found by its `ref` and can be deleted or reordered; renaming it is refused, since it takes the global declaration's name. A command on a record whose type is written in an imported tab is refused with the tab to change it in. *Go to definition* finds a `ref` field's declaration in the importing tab.
- **Still for 15d:** an `.xsd` file imported from disk whose `xs:import` points at a file beside it (it is added with the import error for now), and dropping documents that need several tabs.
- **Tests.** `gossia-calc/src/xsd_imports_tests.rs`: imports named by tab or file, the importing tab retyped by an edit of the imported one and by undo, rename and delete, export, a tab holding the second global element, saving and reading back, rebinding, and field commands on refs. In the app, `tab_commands_tests.rs`: an XML file is offered the global element of its root.

**Decisions made for the engine** (step 15c-1, 2026-11-10; default unless marked).
- **The XSD form.** A complex type whose content is one `xs:choice` with `maxOccurs` above 1 (inference writes `minOccurs="0" maxOccurs="unbounded"`) in place of a `sequence`, its elements each occurring once (the choice repeats, not they), with the attributes after it. It lowers to the field `#children`, after the attributes: an array (its bounds are the choice's) of the new kind `Choice`, whose fields are the elements, none required. A choice that does not repeat, one inside a `sequence`, an element in a choice with its own `minOccurs` or `maxOccurs`, and a type with both a `sequence` and a `choice` are refused with what is supported. Mixed content stays markup, never a choice.
- **The data.** Each item of `#children` is an object with one key, the element's field name: `{"rect": {"@x": 0}}`, so Python reaches it as `item["rect"]` and `XMLPATH` and the tree need nothing new. An element with no children has no `#children` (unless the choice requires one). An element the choice does not name stays in its place in the list, read as content the schema lacks, and is flagged. A comment before an element goes with the element's value, as elsewhere.
- **Writing** follows the list's order, and the order of keys within an item; an item that is not an object is left out.
- **Validation.** An item must hold exactly one key ("holds 2 elements, but an item holds one of: rect, title"); a key the choice does not name is flagged where it is, as an undeclared field.
- **Inference.** Wherever the children's order varies (between instances, or one name apart from itself within one, as in `key, string, key`), the element gets the choice, its elements each once, in the order merged from every instance; the report says so. Elements that keep one order keep their `sequence`.
- **Attributes in the document's own namespace** (found with the Word document: `w:id`). An attribute whose prefix is the root's namespace is qualified. When every such attribute is, the schema says `attributeFormDefault="qualified"`; otherwise each has `form="qualified"`. Its field name is bare, being in the main namespace. One name seen both with and without the prefix is refused. An attribute of any other namespace is still for 15d.
- **The meta-schema** has `choice` (with `minOccurs`, `maxOccurs`, `annotation`, and `element` list) on a complex type, between `sequence` and `simpleContent`, so a schema tab holds it. Its own global declarations stay grouped (15b-2).
- **The corpus** after 15c-1: the plist and the Word `document.xml` import and write back unchanged. The SVG has an `xlink:` attribute, a second namespace, so it moves to 15d.
- **Tests.** In `xsd_tests.rs`: a repeating choice lowered, and the forms refused. In `xml_tests.rs`: the list read in order with its comments and an unknown element, written back; an item of two elements or none flagged. In `infer_tests.rs`: a plist-like list inferred and written back; qualified attributes, by default and one by one.

**Decisions made for the window** (step 15c-2, 2026-11-10; default unless marked).
- **The tree.** An item of `#children` that holds one element is one row, the element's, named `[2] rect`: its cell is the element's value, and it opens onto the element's fields, so a plist's `[0] key` shows `"CFBundleName"` in one row. The row stands for the item as well: *Insert item above*, *Delete item* and *Hide* act on the item. An item that does not hold exactly one element shows what it holds, the keys that are no element of it greyed, as elsewhere.
- **Adding asks which element.** *Add item* on `#children`, and *Insert item above* on one of its items, open a list of the elements; the new item holds that element with its default value, and the list is made when it is absent.
- **Forms.** An ordered list is a repeater shown one item at a time (never a table), each item drawn as the control of the element it holds, named after it. *New* opens the list of elements. An item that does not hold one element says so in red. A designed stacked repeater's item design is bound to the item, which draws the same way.
- **Tables.** `#children` is never a table, in the tree (*Tabular view* is not offered) or on a form, since its items differ. In a table of records that have a list, the list is a column like any other array.
- **Field commands.** *Add field* adding an element to an element whose children are an ordered list adds it to the list's `choice` (an attribute stays the element's own); an element of a choice occurs once, so an array is refused. The elements of a list are the fields of its items: *Rename field*, *Delete field* and the moves work on them from an item's row (`FieldPlaces::choice`). Renaming renames the key in every item; deleting deletes the items that held the element, the last first, in one history entry, and the last element of a list cannot be deleted. `#children` itself cannot be renamed or deleted, and moving fields keeps it after the attributes.
- **Tests.** `gossia-calc/src/xsd_children_tests.rs`: adding into the choice (from the element and from an item) and undo, renaming in every item, deleting with the items and undo, reordering, and what is refused. In the app, `tests/xml_children.rs`: an item is its element's row, *Add item* and *Insert item above* ask which element, *Delete item*, the fields of a list's items, and the form.

**Decisions made for the engine** (step 15d-1, 2026-11-10; default unless marked).
- **One schema per namespace** (`infer::infer_xsds`, giving `XsdTab`s; `infer_xsd` stays for a document that needs one and refuses others with "more than one namespace"). The first is the root's namespace (none counts as one); the others follow in the order first seen. Each `xs:import`s those it refers to, by a `schemaLocation` the caller gives from the namespace's prefix (15d-2: the tab's name).
- **What is global.** An element in another namespace than its parent's is a global element of its own namespace's schema, with one type merged from wherever it is met, and the parent's schema refers to it with `ref`; so is an attribute in another namespace than its element's schema (Android's `android:name`, XAML's `x:Class`, SVG's `xlink:href`), its type merged from all its values. A child in the same namespace, or in none below a namespace, is local, as before; a schema's local elements are all in its namespace or all in none, and a mix is refused naming the element. The built-in `xml:` and `xsi:` attributes stay refs that need no tab.
- **Prefixes.** Each namespace is named by the prefix the document first used for it; one only ever the default namespace (an inline `<svg xmlns="…">`), or one whose prefix another namespace took first, gets `ns1`, `ns2`, …. Field names use these prefixes (15b-1).
- **Writing uses the document's bindings.** An element or attribute of another namespace is written with the prefix the document binds to that namespace where it stands, or, for an element, the default namespace when that is it; only a namespace bound nowhere is declared on the root (found with the inline SVG). An element's own declarations count for its own name (found with SOAP's `m:`).
- **The corpus** after 15d-1: all sixteen documents infer, import, validate and write back unchanged, the RSS feed through four schemas. The made-up RSS and XAML files write their declarations first and the RSS its CDATA as text, both accepted under "Not preserved".
- **Tests.** In `infer_tests.rs`: several namespaces as several schemas with refs and imports (elements and attributes, a schema referring on to another), a default namespace given a prefix, local elements partly in a namespace refused, and `infer_xsd` refusing what needs more than one.

**Decisions made for the workbook and the import dialog** (step 15d-2, 2026-11-10; default unless marked).
- **Names.** The main schema tab is named as before (`feedSchema` for `feed.rss`, changeable in the dialog). Each other namespace's tab is `<data tab>_<prefix>` (`feed_dc`, `feed_atom`, `AndroidManifest_android`), fixed when the file is read and listed under the inferred schema, each with its namespace and whether it is new or there already. A name that clashes with the two tabs named in the dialog is refused, as two new tabs with one name are.
- **Reuse.** For each other namespace in turn, an XSD tab of the workbook with that `targetNamespace` is used instead of a new one when the document still reads and validates with it as well as with a new one (nothing more dropped in reading, nothing more invalid); the first that does is taken. So a second document with `dc:creator` in it uses `book_dc`, and one whose `dc:creator` has an attribute that tab does not declare gets a tab of its own. A whole schema there already that fits is offered as before, and chosen first.
- **Adding.** The schemas are inferred again on Import with the main tab's name as it is then (another namespace may import it back). The new tabs of other namespaces are added first, each after the new ones it imports, then the main schema tab, then the data tab, all as one entry in the history, *Import feed.rss*.
- **A schema tab of attributes only** (the `android:` namespace holds global attributes and no element) has no root to lower, so it is not marked as an error; it cannot be bound, and its problems show on the tabs that import it.
- **XML by its name or its text.** Besides `.xml` and `.xsd`, the names of the common documents are read as XML: `.rss`, `.atom`, `.svg`, `.xhtml`, `.gpx`, `.kml`, `.csproj`, `.vbproj`, `.fsproj`, `.props`, `.targets`, `.xaml`, `.plist`, `.archimate`, `.resx`, `.wsdl`, `.xsl`, `.xslt`; and a file of a name not known whose text is well-formed XML starting with `<` is read as XML rather than as lines of text.
- **`response.xml()`** in a data source procedure infers one schema per namespace too (`XsdInferences::lower`), so a namespaced body reads.
- **Tests.** `xml_import_tests.rs` in the window crate: every document of the corpus dropped on a blank workbook (one tab per namespace, no schema errors, valid, exported back as the same text, and dropped again onto the schema it made); the RSS feed's tabs and their undo as one; a namespace's tab reused and not reused; the names of the other tabs refused when they clash, and a main schema renamed in the dialog, which still imports the others.

**Decisions made for `.xsd` files with their imports** (step 15d-3, 2026-11-10; default unless marked).
- **Found beside it.** An `.xsd` imported from a folder (File > Import, or dropped) has each `xs:import`'s `schemaLocation` looked for relative to its own folder (`schemas/dc.xsd` too), and the files found are imported with it, theirs in turn, each file once. Left out: the built-in `xml:` and `xsi:` namespaces, a location with `://` (a web address is never fetched), an import the workbook already has a tab for (`Calc::xsd_import_tab`), and a file that is not there, too large, or not an XSD; such an import stays the import error it was, shown in the dialog and on the tab. A file read through the host or pasted has no folder, so it brings nothing.
- **Names.** Each file becomes a schema tab named after it as any imported file is (`dc-terms.xsd` is `dc_terms`); where that is not the name the import reaches (`xsd_import_tab` reads the file name without `.xsd`), the importing schema's `schemaLocation` is changed to the tab's name, which export writes back as `<tab>.xsd`. The dialog lists them under *import it as a schema tab*, each as *file, as the tab name*; a clash with the tab name typed is refused.
- **Adding.** The imported tabs are added first, the deepest first, then the file's own tab, as one entry in the history, *Import book.xsd*. The import report's problem is the file's schema lowered with them.
- **Tests.** In `xml_import_tests.rs`: an XSD importing one in a subfolder under a name that is no tab name, which imports another in turn (three tabs, no errors, importers right, data reads with them, one undo); an import the workbook has a tab for left to it; a missing file left as the error; a file without a folder bringing nothing.

### The web version on GitHub Pages, 0.6.0

*(Owner, 2026-11-12, in place of browser extensions, phones and tablets. The owner accepted the four points marked below; the rest are Claude's proposals, kept unless the owner changes them.)*

**What it is.** The wasm version of 0.3.0 as a public web page on the repository's GitHub Pages site. Anyone can open it in a browser and use it without installing anything. Everything runs in the visitor's browser: GitHub only serves the files, and nothing of a workbook leaves the machine except the data source requests the user makes. It is the plain browser page of 13b with a **browser host** behind the host interface in place of the test host. What the wasm version lacks against the desktop (no `bidwillii`, no printing, no database sources, Python in a Web Worker) stays as in Web port, 0.3.0.

**No cross-origin isolation is needed.** GitHub Pages cannot set the `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy` and `Cross-Origin-Embedder-Policy` headers, so `SharedArrayBuffer` is not available. The wasm version does not need it: HTTP reads stop and run again, and a runaway worker is terminated (see Web port, 0.3.0, and its spike).

**The browser host.**
- **Workbook files.** Where the browser has the File System Access API (Chrome and Edge), a `.gossia` file is opened, saved and saved again in place. Elsewhere (Firefox, Safari) it is opened by choosing or dropping a file and saved by downloading it. In both cases the page keeps a **list of recent workbooks** with a copy of each in the browser's storage (IndexedDB), so a workbook comes back after the tab is closed, and leaving the page with unsaved changes asks first.
- **Cloud storage** (OneDrive, Google Drive and the like) is reached only through the providers' own sync folders, where the File System Access API opens and saves those files in place. **No provider's API is built in** (owner, 2026-11-12): the app registrations need accounts that ask for a credit card, under terms the provider can change on its own. On Firefox, Safari, phones and tablets a workbook in such a folder is opened from it and saved by downloading.
- **Import and export** use the same: the browser's file choosers, drops on the page, and downloads.
- **Storage.** Preferences, the trust record and the data source definitions are kept in the browser's storage for the site.
- **Secrets** *(owner, 2026-11-12)*: kept **for the session only** by default, in the page's memory, and asked for again in a new session. A secret can be **stored in `localStorage`** instead (owner, 2026-11-12), chosen per secret where it is entered (*Remember on this browser*), with a warning that it is kept unencrypted and that anything running on the site could read it. A stored secret stays until it is removed in the data source panel or the browser's site data is cleared. The browser has no keychain like VS Code's `SecretStorage`.
- **HTTP** *(owner, 2026-11-12)*: the page makes data source requests itself with `fetch`, so **CORS applies**. Only sites that allow cross-origin requests can be used, and the browser's rules for private network addresses (`localhost`, the local network) apply too. A request refused for CORS gives a clear error that names the cause and says that the desktop application and VS Code do not have this limit. It is never shown as a vague network failure.
- **Opening outside** opens an address in a new browser tab, and the **theme** follows the browser's `prefers-color-scheme`.
- **The code editor** is drawn inside the page as a second egui window, as on the web in 13b.

**Installable and offline** *(owner, 2026-11-12, in 0.6.0)*. The page is a progressive web app: a web app manifest with the icons, and a service worker that keeps the page, the wasm and the fonts in a cache. The application can then be installed from the browser, on the desktop and on phones and tablets, and opened without a connection, except for HTTP data sources. A new version is fetched in the background and used on the next start, and the page says when one is ready.

**Phones and tablets** reach the site through their browsers. 0.6.0 makes it usable there with touch and on a small screen (larger touch targets where egui allows, panels that fold away on a narrow window), without a separate layout. The designers and the code editor are not reworked for phones.

**Deployment** *(owner, 2026-11-12)*. A GitHub Actions workflow builds the release wasm and the page and publishes them with `actions/deploy-pages`, on pushes to `main`. It uploads the site and commits nothing, so no binary goes into the repository. It is the one build job on GitHub, an exception to Gitea doing everything beyond lint and the tests (see Pipeline), since the site is GitHub's.
- The site's size is the release wasm (45.3 MB, 15.5 MB gzipped) and the fonts. Whether GitHub Pages compresses `.wasm` is checked during the build; the page shows progress while loading either way.

**Share files** *(owner, 2026-11-12)*. A way to send a workbook to someone who does not have Gossia: one `.html` file that holds the workbook and opens it in the web version.
- **Saving one.** *Save the workbook as* offers **Gossia share file (`.html`)** beside **Gossia workbook (`.gossia`)**, on the desktop and on the web. It is a copy: the open workbook keeps its own file and name, and the next *Save* writes the `.gossia` as before. In VS Code, whose Save As is VS Code's own, it is the command *Gossia: Save as share file*.
- **What is in it.** A small page, in the sender's interface language: the workbook's name, a line saying what Gossia is, a button **Open in Gossia**, and the workbook itself as base64 inside the page. It is the `.gossia` unchanged (already a zip), so the file is about a third larger than the workbook. No scripts or styles are loaded from elsewhere, and without scripts the page still shows the site's address and says to drop the file on it.
- **Opening.** The button opens the Pages site in a new window (a click is needed, since browsers block windows opened without one). The site tells the page it is ready with `postMessage`, and the page sends the workbook back the same way. Nothing passes through a server: the site's files come from GitHub, and the workbook goes from one window to the other in the browser. The app cannot run from the share file itself, because a page opened from a file may not start the Web Worker from another site.
- **In the recipient's Gossia** the workbook opens as a new, unsaved workbook named after the original, **untrusted** (formulas not run until the recipient trusts it, as for any workbook from elsewhere), with a note that it came from a share file. Saving makes it the recipient's own.
- **Gossia reads share files too.** Opening or dropping a share file, in any version, opens the workbook in it. This is the way in where the button does not work: mail programs that show attachments without running scripts (on phones especially), or a recipient who already has the desktop application.
- **The site's address** is fixed when Gossia is built, as the address of the official Pages site.
- **Limits.** Some mail systems refuse `.html` attachments, as they are used for phishing; the `.gossia` file and the site's address are then the way. Above 20 MB, saving a share file warns that many mail systems will not take it.
- **Not done:** a link with the workbook in its address (too long for most chat programs beyond small workbooks), and a share file that works without the site or offline.

**Build order.**
- **17a.** The browser host: workbooks (File System Access API, or choose and download), recent workbooks, storage, secrets for the session, HTTP with the CORS error.
- **17b.** The Pages site: the workflow, a short landing text on what runs where and what the web version lacks, compression and loading progress.
- **17c.** Installable and offline: manifest, service worker, update notice.
- **17d.** Touch and small screens.
- **17e.** Share files: the choice in *Save the workbook as* (desktop and web) and the VS Code command, the share file's page, the hand-over to the site, and reading share files on open and drop.
- **Tests:** the Playwright harness of 13b-4 drives the browser host too (files through the fallback path, storage, the CORS error against a local server that refuses, the service worker's cache, and a share file opened from disk handing its workbook to the site served locally).

**Decisions made while building 17a** (2026-11-13; Claude's, kept unless the owner changes them).
- **One page, one host.** `index.html` is the browser host's page (`web/host.js`), not a separate test host; the harness drives the same page, answering the file dialogs itself (`answerPicks`), so the files go through the fallback path there. `?open=` and `?sample` stay for the tests.
- **Gossia's own File menu.** A host that says it keeps files (`keepsFiles()`) gets *New*, *Open...*, *Open Recent* and *Save As...* in Gossia's File menu, which VS Code (whose files are its own) does not; there is no *Quit* in a page. *Open* asks the host for a workbook (`pickFile('open', ['gossia'])`), and the application tells it when one was opened (`workbookOpened`, with the bytes for the copy) or a new one started (`newWorkbook`), so that the page knows which file a save goes to. A workbook that is not one is not opened and the page is not told.
- **A save counts when it is written.** The page answers each save (`workbook_saved(name)` or `workbook_not_saved(why)`, `null` when the user cancelled the browser's dialog), and the workbook counts as saved as it was when the save was asked for (the history's place then, `Calc::mark_saved_at`), so an edit made while the dialog was open stays unsaved. The name the user chose in the dialog becomes the workbook's. *Save* before *Open*, *Open Recent* or *New* waits for that answer, and a save not written opens nothing. With the File System Access API, *Save* writes the file the workbook came from when the browser already lets the page write it, and otherwise shows the save dialog in that file's folder with its name (after which saving it again needs nothing more); *Save As* always asks; without the API, both download. The page never asks the browser for permission (`requestPermission`, which shows the browser's own prompt; the owner does not want it, 2026-11-13).
- **Recent workbooks.** At most ten, in IndexedDB (`gossia` database: `recent` with each one's key, name, file handle and time, `copies` with its file), the copy replaced at each open and save. *Open Recent* reads the file itself where the browser still lets the page read it (the handle, permission already granted) and the copy otherwise, without asking; a workbook opened from its copy keeps its handle, so that its first save shows the save dialog in that file's folder. A file chosen again that is already on the list keeps its place (`isSameEntry`). *Clear the list* removes the copies too. Unsaved changes are not kept: leaving the page with them asks first (`beforeunload`).
- **A workbook dropped on the page** opens, after the question about unsaved changes, as one without a file of its own (VS Code still says to open it from outside).
- **Secrets.** In the page's memory for the session; *Remember on this browser* in the data source's form (under the password or token, only where the host keeps secrets per session) moves the secret to `localStorage` (`gossia-secret:` and the secret's name) and back, with the warning on hover. A source renamed keeps the choice under its new name.
- **The CORS error.** A browser's `fetch` fails the same way for a site that does not allow the page's origin and one that is not there, so after a failure the page sends a `no-cors` `HEAD` to the same address: if that gets through, the site is there and refused the origin. The page says what went wrong by its kind (`fetch_problem`: `cors`, `private` for a private address from a public page, `unreachable`, `timeout`, `redirect`, `too-large`) and Gossia words it in the interface's language, saying that the desktop application and VS Code have no such limit.
- **Tests.** In `host_tests.rs`, the browser page's files with a fake host (a save counted when written, cancelled and refused, edits during a save, Save As, Open and a file that is no workbook, a dropped workbook after the question, saving before opening another, New, remembering a secret). In the harness, `tests-browser.js`: a save in the recent list, a recent workbook opened from its copy, Open with a chosen file, Save As, session and remembered secrets, and the CORS error against a second origin that `run.py` serves (another port), beside a site that is not there.

**Decisions made while building 17b** (2026-11-13; Claude's, kept unless the owner changes them).
- **The workflow** is `.github/workflows/pages.yml`: on pushes to `main` (and by hand), `cargo xtask web` on GitHub's Ubuntu runner, `actions/upload-pages-artifact` of `target/web`, then `actions/deploy-pages` to the `github-pages` environment. wasm-bindgen's command line is kept between runs (`actions/cache` of `target/wasm-tools`, keyed by `Cargo.lock`). The build stays in the checkout's `target/` (no `CARGO_TARGET_DIR`), where `cargo xtask web` looks for the module. The repository's Pages source has to be set to *GitHub Actions* once, in its settings. The site's address is https://wvdveer.github.io/gossia/; the README links it.
- **Compression** is checked after each deployment: the job asks the site for the module with `Accept-Encoding: br, gzip` and writes the `Content-Encoding` it got, with the module's size and its gzipped size, in the run's summary.
- **Progress.** The page reads the module itself (`fetch` with a reader) and hands the bytes to wasm-bindgen's `init`, filling a bar. The total comes from `build.json`, which `cargo xtask web` writes beside the page (`version`, `wasmBytes`), because a server that compresses gives the compressed length while the reader counts the bytes as they arrive uncompressed; without `build.json` the bar moves without a total. The worker reads the module again, from the browser's cache.
- **The landing text is the loading screen**: the name, the bar, two short paragraphs (everything runs in the browser and only the data source requests leave it; the web version cannot print, has no database sources and no `bidwillii`, and reads only sites that allow it, which the desktop application and VS Code do not limit), and links to the Microsoft Store and the repository. It is in the browser's language where Gossia has it (the five translations are in the page, since the module is not loaded yet), English otherwise, and gives way to the application once it has started. A failure to start is shown there in red. No separate landing page: a visitor comes to use the application, and a page before it would be one more click every time.
- **Tests.** The harness checks that `build.json` gives the module's size and the version and that the loading screen is gone once the application has started. The workflow itself is tried on its first run from `main`.

**Decisions made while building 17c** (2026-11-13; Claude's, kept unless the owner changes them).
- **The manifest** (`manifest.webmanifest`): name Gossia, `start_url` and `scope` the site's folder, `standalone`, the dark background of the page, and icons of 192 and 512 pixels. `cargo xtask web` draws the icons (`icons/` 32, 180, 192 and 512) with the window's icon code, so the repository keeps no image files. No file handlers for `.gossia`: the desktop application is the one the system opens them with.
- **The service worker** (`sw.js`) keeps one cache per build, `gossia-<build>`. `cargo xtask web` writes the build, a hash of the page's files (FNV-1a over their names and contents), and the list of files into `sw.js` and the build into `build.json`, so every change of a file is a new build, without a version change. The list is the page's own files, the module and its glue, the icons, `build.json` and `./`; not the harness. A new build's files are fetched past the browser's HTTP cache (`no-cache`), so a cached file of the last build cannot get into the new cache. The site's own files are served from the cache (a page's address with a query is the page, `ignoreSearch`), anything else from the network; data source requests go to other origins and are never touched. It is registered once the application has started, so that it does not compete with the module's download, and looks for a new build every hour as well as at each start.
- **Updates.** A new build installs in the background and waits; the build in use keeps running until Gossia starts again. At the next start, before the module is read, the page asks the waiting build to take over if no other window of the site is open (a window still open keeps the build it started with, since its worker may be restarted and must load the same module), and reloads. While Gossia runs, a new build ready shows a notice at the bottom right, in the browser's language: *A new version of Gossia is ready. It is used the next time Gossia is opened.*, with **Later** and **Restart now** (which takes the new build at once and reloads; unsaved changes are asked about first, by the page's `beforeunload`). The notice is HTML beside the canvas, like the loading screen, not a Gossia notice, which is for errors and is cleared by the next edit.
- **Developing.** On a local server the service worker is registered too, so after `cargo xtask web` a plain reload starts the last build and shows the notice; Ctrl+Shift+R loads past the service worker.
- **Tests.** The harness checks that `build.json` names the build, the service worker becomes active, the cache of that build holds the page, the module and the icons, the manifest makes the site installable, and the notice shows with its buttons and goes with *Later*. Starting offline and taking a waiting build at the next start are tried by hand.

### Gossia pyplot, 0.6.0

*(Added to 0.6.0 by the owner on 2026-11-10. The naming, the import fix, the functions, the start and end of a chart function and the ignored arguments were settled with the owner on 2026-11-12; the other points are Claude's proposals, kept unless the owner changes them.)*

**Why not matplotlib itself.** matplotlib needs numpy, and its own core (font rendering, path clipping, images, the `kiwisolver` layout solver) is compiled C and C++. RustPython cannot load compiled extensions, on the desktop or in the wasm version, so neither can run in Gossia.

**What instead.** A built-in, read-only module, **`gossia.pyplot`**, written in Python as the built-in charts are, whose function names and arguments follow the most used parts of matplotlib's `pyplot` where they overlap, drawing to SVG. It is available without `import` as **`plt`**, as the standard library modules are, and `import gossia.pyplot as plt` (or `from gossia import pyplot`) works in modules too. It is another way of writing the body of a chart function (see Reports, Chart functions); nothing about `@chart`, the parameters, the size, trust or export changes. The figure is the size of the control: `plt.figure()` takes `width` and `height` from the chart call, and `figsize` is read as a ratio only.

```python
@chart("Sales and target")
def sales(months, totals, targets, width=400, height=300):
    plt.clear()
    plt.figure(width=width, height=height)
    plt.bar(months, totals, color="tab:blue", label="Sales")
    plt.plot(months, targets, "r--", label="Target")
    plt.title("Sales")
    plt.xlabel("Month")
    plt.legend()
    return plt.svg()
```

**Gossia's own, and said so** *(owner, 2026-11-12)*. It is never presented as matplotlib, since claiming to be what it is not would mislead users:
- **No `matplotlib` import.** `import matplotlib` and every part of it (`matplotlib.pyplot`, `from matplotlib import pyplot`) is an `ImportError`: *matplotlib is not available in Gossia (it needs compiled code). Gossia's own module `gossia.pyplot` covers common charts in a similar style.* `matplotlib` is not a reserved name.
- **The fix offered.** Where that error is shown (the code editor's problem list after *Compile*, and the module's load error), it has a button **Change to `import gossia.pyplot as plt`**, which rewrites the import line in the module: `import matplotlib.pyplot as X` and `from matplotlib import pyplot as X` become `import gossia.pyplot as X`, keeping the user's own name (`plt` in almost all code). It is one step in the editor's undo. Imports it cannot rewrite safely (`import matplotlib as mpl`, `import matplotlib.pyplot` without `as`, `from matplotlib.pyplot import bar`) get the error without the button. In VS Code the same fix is a quick fix (code action) on the line.
- **The words used.** The help and the release notes say that names and arguments follow matplotlib's `pyplot` where they overlap, list what is covered, and say it is not matplotlib; they never say *compatible*. Refusals name Gossia: *`gossia.pyplot` does not support `imshow`*.
- **Reserved names.** `plt` becomes reserved (with the exposed standard library module names); names starting with `gossia` already are. A workbook made before 0.6.0 with a tab, report, data source or module function named `plt` still opens, with a notice asking to rename it; until then its formulas see the tab, and the module is reached with `import gossia.pyplot`.

**First version.**
- **Plots:** `plot` (with format strings such as `"r--"` and `"o-"`, and several series in one call), `bar` and `barh` (with `bottom` for stacking), `scatter` (sizes and colours per point), `hist`, `pie`, `fill_between`, `errorbar`, `step`, `boxplot` (owner, 2026-11-12), `axhline` and `axvline`, `text` and `annotate`.
- **Axes:** `title`, `xlabel`, `ylabel`, `xlim`, `ylim`, `xticks` and `yticks` (with labels and rotation), `grid`, `legend` (location by name), `xscale` and `yscale` (`linear`, `log`), dates on an axis.
- **Colours and styles:** matplotlib's default cycle (`tab:blue`, `tab:orange`, ...), the named and `C0`-style colours, hex, line styles, markers, `alpha`, `linewidth`.
- **Figures and axes objects:** `figure`, `subplots(nrows, ncols)` giving `fig, ax` or an array of axes, with the same methods on `ax` (`ax.plot`, `ax.set_title`, `ax.set_xlabel`, ...), `twinx`, and `tight_layout` as a no-op since layout is always tight.
- **A chart function starts with `plt.clear()` and ends with `return plt.svg()`** *(owner, 2026-11-12)*. `plt.clear()` discards every figure left over (from an earlier chart that failed before its `svg()`, say), so a chart never draws into another's figure. `plt.svg()` gives the SVG text of the current figure and closes it. A chart function that returns anything else (a figure, or nothing) is an error: *a chart function using `plt` must finish with `return plt.svg()`*. There is **no `plt.show()`**: calling it is refused by name with the same advice, and `savefig` is refused too, since a chart function cannot write files.
- The built-in text and colours of the window's theme apply as in the built-in charts (`currentColor`, the `.background` and `.surface` classes), so a pyplot chart follows light and dark.

**Not covered** (default): 3D, images and colour maps of arrays (`imshow`, `contour`, `pcolormesh`), LaTeX maths in text, style sheets and `rcParams` beyond a few keys, animation, interactive backends, `plt.show()` (refused, see above), and the long tail of rarely used options, which are refused by name rather than ignored, so a user sees what does not work. **The exception** *(owner, 2026-11-12)*: keyword arguments that make no difference to an SVG at the control's size are accepted and ignored, so that code copied from elsewhere does not fail on them: `dpi`, `zorder`, `antialiased`, `rasterized`, `picker`, `clip_on` and `snap`. Code copied from elsewhere often runs as it is, and otherwise needs small changes.

**Build order** *(owner, 2026-11-12: before the web version)*.
- **16a.** Figures and axes, `plt.clear()` and `plt.svg()`, tick placement, text measurement, dates on an axis, the theme classes.
- **16b.** The plots, colours, styles and markers, the legend, `subplots` and `twinx`, the refusals and the ignored arguments.
- **16c.** The `matplotlib` import error and its fix button (and the VS Code quick fix), `plt` as a reserved name, the gallery-style examples as snapshots, a chart exported to PDF, the world outline (below), and the help.

**The world outline** *(owner, 2026-11-12: in 16c; the details are Claude's proposals)*. matplotlib draws no maps (Cartopy and Basemap are compiled add-ons), so this is Gossia's own and named so. It draws points at their coordinates on an outline of the world's land and countries, offline, in the theme's colours, into PDF as any chart.
- **The data.** Natural Earth at 1:110m (land polygons and country borders), which is in the public domain, stored compactly as `gossia-calc/src/python/world.txt` (whole hundredths of a degree, each point as the difference from the last; 128 rings of land and 333 border lines, about 8,100 points, 60 KB), given to the module as text and read the first time a map is drawn. Named on the About page although no notice is required.
- **`ax.world(borders=True, land=None, edgecolor=None, linewidth=0.6, alpha=None)`** (and `plt.world()`): draws the land, and the country borders, behind what the Axes has, in longitude (x) and latitude (y) degrees, so that `scatter(lons, lats)`, `plot`, `text` and `annotate` put things in place. It sets an equal aspect and shows the whole world (−180 to 180, −90 to 90), or, when the Axes has other data, that data with room around it (a fifth of its spread and at least ten degrees each way, within the world); limits that are set win. Only the shapes inside the view are written into the SVG. Land is filled faintly in the text colour (8 %) and outlined at 45 %, the borders at 30 %, so it follows light and dark; `land` (`'none'` for no fill) and `edgecolor` change the colours.
- **Mercator** as an axis scale, `plt.yscale('mercator')`, as matplotlib's scales are named: latitudes are placed by Mercator's formula, cut at ±85°, with the ticks still in degrees, so the points and the outline stay in step without converting anything.
- **A built-in chart type *Map*** (`map_chart(longitudes, latitudes, labels=None, title=None)`), for a map without writing Python: a dot at each place with its label, on the outline, without axes. A longitude outside ±180 or a latitude outside ±90 is an error.
- **Not covered:** roads, towns, rivers, map tiles from the web (they would need HTTP from a chart, images in the SVG and PDF, and the tile providers' terms), other projections, and countries by name (colouring a country by its value).

**Questions for the build.**
- Workbook modules may not define classes (`__build_class__` is removed; see Python engine), but figures and axes are objects. The module is Gossia's own, compiled before the builtins are trimmed, as the prelude is, so it can define them; users only call them.
- Tick placement (matplotlib's "nice numbers"), text measurement for labels and legends (the fonts the charts already use, through the layout crate's measurer or a table of widths), and dates on an axis are most of the work.
- **Tests:** each function's SVG checked for its parts, a set of matplotlib gallery-style examples rendered and compared as snapshots, the refusals by name, the `matplotlib` import error and its fix, and a chart in a report exported to PDF.

**Decisions made while building 16a** (2026-11-12).
- **Where it lives.** `gossia-calc/src/python/pyplot.py`, run in a namespace of its own after the built-in charts and before the builtins are trimmed (so it defines classes), which becomes the module `gossia.pyplot` inside a package `gossia`. Both are kept with the standard library modules the replacement `__import__` serves: `import gossia.pyplot as x` binds the package and takes `pyplot` from it, and `from gossia.pyplot import plot` is given the module, as Python's own import does. *Compile* knows `gossia` as a module that can be imported. `plt` and `_chart_size` are in the globals.
- **The chart's size.** The expression of a chart call is `_chart_svg(_chart_size(width, height) or chart(...))`, so pyplot knows the size before the chart function runs, and `plt.figure()` without a size takes it. Outside a chart call it is 400 by 300 points.
- **Measuring text.** build.rs reads the advance width of each character of Latin, Greek, punctuation, super- and subscripts, currency, arrows and the mathematical operators from Inter and writes them into the engine; `_gossia.text_width(text)` adds them up in ems. There is no shaping or kerning. A character past those ranges is a whole em for East Asian scripts and 0.6 of one otherwise; bold is taken as 6 % wider.
- **Layout.** The cells of `subplots` are equal. Each Axes is laid out in three rounds, since the ticks depend on the plot box's size and the margins on the tick labels: room for the title, the y tick labels (measured), the y label, the x tick labels (measured, rotated or not), half of the last x label where it overhangs, and the x label. The plot box has a frame on four sides and ticks 3.5 points long outside it, in the text colour. Tick labels are 9 points, axis labels 10, titles 12 and a figure's title 13. What is drawn is clipped to the plot box, by a clip path whose id is `gp` and a hash of the figure's SVG, so two charts on one page never share one.
- **Ticks.** Steps of 1, 2, 2.5, 5 and 10 times a power of ten, about one tick per 60 points along x and per 36 points up y (3 to 10), and never fewer than three shown: on a short axis, as in a grid of subplots, the step is made smaller until three fit. The limits are the data's with a margin of 5 % each side and are not rounded out to the ticks, as in matplotlib. Labels have as many decimals as the step needs, and a minus sign (U+2212), not a hyphen; at a million or more, or below a thousandth, the labels are scaled and the axis shows `×10ⁿ`. A logarithmic axis has a tick per decade (`10³`), thinned to fit, and 1, 2 and 5 times the decades when fewer than two are shown.
- **Dates** are days since 1 January of the year 1, with the time as a fraction of a day. The interval is the shortest of 1 to 30 seconds, minutes, 1 to 12 hours, 1, 2, 3, 7 or 14 days (days 1, 8, 15 ... of each month), 1 to 6 months or 1 to 1,000 years that gives no more ticks than fit. Labels: `2026` for years, `Mar` for months (with the year on the first tick and on January), `3 Mar` for days, `14:30` for times (with the date on the first and at midnight). Month names are in English for now.
- **Text on an axis** is categories, placed 0, 1, 2 ... in the order first seen, with a tick each, thinned when the labels would overlap. An axis takes one kind: numbers, dates or text.
- **`plot` came forward from 16b,** with format strings and markers, as the drawing function needed to test the axes. The other plots stay in 16b.
- **`subplots`** gives one Axes, a list of them, or rows of them, indexed `axs[1]`, `axs[0, 1]` or `axs[0][1]`, with `axs.flat`, `ravel()` and `shape`. `subplot_kw` and `gridspec_kw` are refused, as is a numbered `figure(num)`; `sharex` and `sharey` came in 16b.
- **Refusals by name** come from `__getattr__` on the module, on `Axes` and on `Figure`. `show` has its own message, pointing at `return plt.svg()`.
- **Colours.** The cycle is matplotlib's ten (`tab:blue` first); the letters have matplotlib's values (`'g'` is `#008000`), and CSS names go to the SVG as they are. A colour the user names is drawn as named in either theme, so `'k'` stays black on a dark window; only the text, the frame and the grid follow the theme.

**Decisions made while building 16b** (2026-11-12).
- **Plots added:** `bar` and `barh` (stacking with `bottom` or `left`, colours per bar, `tick_label`, `align`, `yerr` and `xerr`), `scatter` (a size in square points and a colour per point), `hist` (bins as a count or edges, `range`, `weights`, `density`, `cumulative`, several data sets side by side or `stacked`, `histtype` `bar`, `step` and `stepfilled`, `orientation`), `pie` (labels, `autopct` as a format or a function, `explode`, `startangle`, `counterclock`, donuts through `wedgeprops={'width': ...}`), `fill_between` and `fill_betweenx` (with `where` and `step`), `errorbar` (symmetric or `[below, above]` errors, `capsize`, `errorevery`), `step`, and `plot(drawstyle='steps-...')`, `boxplot` (matplotlib's quartiles and whiskers at 1.5 times the spread, fliers, `showmeans`, `patch_artist`, the `*props` dictionaries, horizontal boxes), `axhline`, `axvline`, `axhspan`, `axvspan`, `text` and `annotate`.
- **Each plot follows matplotlib's details** where they show: bars and histograms stand on their base (the margin does not go past 0), the default bar width is 0.8, histograms of several data sets share 80 % of each bin, a pie has no frame or ticks and limits of ±1.25 with an equal aspect, `axhline` takes the first colour without moving the cycle, error bars and box plot lines take the line colour. **What matplotlib draws in black** (box plot lines, fliers, error bars of bars) is drawn in the text colour instead, so that it shows on a dark window; colours the user names are kept as named.
- **Handles.** Plots give back objects that can be changed afterwards, as in matplotlib: a line from `plot`, the bars from `bar` (a list, with `get_height()` and the like), wedges and texts from `pie`, a dictionary of parts from `boxplot`. Their `set_color`, `set_facecolor`, `set_linewidth`, `set_label`, `set(...)` and similar change what is drawn; anything else is refused by name (*`gossia.pyplot` does not support Line.set_dash_capstyle*).
- **The legend** takes no arguments (the labelled plots), the labels alone, or handles and labels, and follows matplotlib's order: lines, areas and points as drawn, then bars and error bars. Labels starting with `_` are left out. `loc` takes the names and codes, and `best` picks the first place, in matplotlib's order, that covers the fewest points of what is drawn. Also `ncol`, `title`, `fontsize`, `frameon`, `framealpha`, `facecolor`, `edgecolor`, `markerscale`, `labelcolor`. **A legend outside the plot box** (`bbox_to_anchor`) is refused for now, since the layout would have to make room for it.
- **`twinx`** gives a second Axes on the same plot box that shares the x axis (one axis object, so the limits cover both), with its ticks, labels and offset on the right; the layout makes room for them. Its own colour cycle starts again at the first colour, as in matplotlib. A twin of a twin, and `twiny`, are refused.
- **`sharex` and `sharey`** (`True`, `'all'`, `'row'`, `'col'`) make the Axes use one axis object, so they have one range, and show tick labels only along the bottom row and the left column, as matplotlib does.
- **Aspect.** `axis('equal')`, `set_aspect` and a pie make the plot box smaller, centred in its cell, so that a unit is as long on both axes (matplotlib's `adjustable='box'`); the limits are not changed. `axis('off')` hides the frame, ticks and labels, and `axis('tight')` drops the margins. `ax.set(title=..., xlabel=..., ylim=...)` sets several things at once.
