A format is shown while the field does not have the focus, and the stored value while it does; the field's editor keeps its own id, so that the change of text when the focus arrives does not make it another widget. A string that is not a date (in a field that has a date format) is shown as it is.
- **Not done:** formats in the data tree and its tabular pane, a format by locale (the separator is always a comma and the decimal point a point), typing a value in its format (`1,234.50`), resizing columns by dragging their edge, and dragging a column to move it. *(Resizing by dragging was done later: see the decisions for the last items.)*
- **Tests.** In `gossia-core`, the formatting of numbers, dates, date-times and date strings; the columns and formats in the design document and their bounds; the arrangement from the list, from the old `show`, and by default; and a default list stored as nothing. In `gossia-app` without a window, moving (by one, and past hidden columns), showing and hiding, the last column of the items kept shown, a column's properties, and undo. In `tests/forms.rs`, selecting a column by its title and setting its title and the alignment of the row numbers; hiding and ordering columns from the table's page; a number shown in its format and typed as stored; the number format on a field's page, and none for a text field; and dates in a field and a column of a JSON tab. Row numbers in each numbering and from a start, in `gossia-core`; in the window, the numbering chosen on the page of `#` and a start of 100, and the title of a column in layout mode as wide as its cells. The sample's schema tabs are named `Orders_schema` and `Prices_schema` (they were `Orders schema` and `Prices schema`, which a file flags as invalid tab names when it opens), and a test checks that the sample opens with no warning.

**Decisions made while building the renaming of a tab** (step 8d)
- **One operation in `gossia-calc`**, `Calc::rename_sheet`, recorded as `Op::RenameSheet`. The history needs nothing rewritten: its entries name tabs, but they are undone in order, so an entry made before a rename is only reached after the rename has been undone, when the tab has its old name again.
- **What moves with the tab** is moved by name: its place in the order, the bindings (`sheet:<Tab>`) of the tabs that use it, its forms (`FormStore::rename_tab`), and what the dependency index knows of its formulas (their places include the tab's name). The formulas that read the old name, those that looked for the new one, and the tab's own formulas are made out of date; nothing is rewritten. An evaluation that was running is dropped as overtaken, as after any edit.
- **The window follows renames, not the rename.** `Calc::take_renames` gives the renames since it was last called, undoing and redoing included, and the window moves what it keeps for a tab (the current tab, open nodes, views, tables and their filters, form drafts, layout mode, the view state read from the file) at the start of each frame and after its own undo, redo and rename.
- **The name check** is `Calc::tab_name_problem`: the rules of a tab name, unique among the tabs ignoring case (the tab's own name in other letters is allowed), and not the name of a module. Adding a tab still does not use it (see step 5b's decisions): no command adds tabs yet.
- **The warning counts identifiers** (`Calc::mentions`, with the same tokenizer that finds what a module uses), so a field or a word in a string with the tab's name is counted too. A column formula counts once.
- **The dialog selects the name** when it opens, so typing replaces it, and *Rename* is disabled while the name has a problem. A double-click on a sheet tab, its context menu, and *Edit > Rename tab...* open it; typing in progress is entered first, and nothing opens while a button procedure runs. The control channel has `rename_tab` (`from`, `to`).
- **Tests.** In `gossia-calc`, the place, data and formulas kept; the old name missing and the new one found; one entry to undo and redo; earlier edits undone after the rename; refused names; a change of case; bindings and forms following. In `gossia-app` without a window, the window's state following a rename and its undo, a renamed tab saved under its new name with its binding and view and opened again, the dialog's checks and wording, and the control command; in the window, renaming from the context menu with a refused name on the way.

**Decisions made while building tab management and import** (step 8e)
- **Three recorded operations in `gossia-calc`**: `Op::AddSheet` and `Op::RemoveSheet` hold the whole tab as it was (`SavedTab`: its data, formulas, column formulas, schema role, XML document, forms and index), so adding and deleting are each other's reverse; `Op::Bind` holds the schema before and after. Nodes are shared, not copied, so a tab put back has the same node ids. Putting a tab back registers what its formulas read again and calculates them again, since what they read may have changed meanwhile.
- **New tabs are made by `create_data_tab`, `create_json_schema_tab` and `create_xsd_schema_tab`**, which check the name as a rename does (the check that step 5b left for when tabs could be made) and record the entry. Loading a file still uses the unrecorded `add_sheet` family, since opening a file is not an edit.
- **Deleting a schema sheet that tabs use is refused** rather than unbinding them, so a delete never changes another tab and undoing one never has to rebind.
- **The window follows tabs as it follows renames.** `Calc::take_removed` gives the tabs taken out since it was last called, undoing and redoing included; the window forgets what it kept for them, opens the root of any new tab, and shows the last tab if the current one is gone. `follow_renames` became `follow_tabs`.
- **Inference is in `gossia-core` (`infer.rs`)**: `infer_json` gives a JSON Schema document and the data without its nulls, `infer_xsd` gives XSD text, and `text_lines` the array of a text file's lines. The XSD text is read with the XSD meta-schema to become the schema sheet's tree, and lowered to read the data, so an inferred schema goes through the same path as one written by hand. Ties between kinds go string, number, integer, boolean, object, array. XML text is typed by a type that every value fits (integer, then decimal, boolean, date, dateTime, else string), so no value of an inferred XML schema is invalid; an empty value makes the type a string. Child order is merged across instances: a name not seen before goes after the name before it, and instances that disagree, or elements of one name that are apart, are reported.
- **Matching tries every schema sheet** by reading and validating the whole file against it, when the file is read; the dialog keeps the counts. The data that is matched is the data without nulls (see the inference table).
- **An inferred schema starts with auto-update off.** With it on, no second tab could use it, and the next file of the same shape would get another schema of its own.
- **JSON written by hand is read leniently on import** (`json::parse_lenient`): a comma before a closing `]` or `}`, and `//` and `/* */` comments, are passed over and listed in the report, since files like the randomuser.me sample have them. They are blanked rather than removed, so a real error is still reported at its line and column in the file. Opening a workbook stays strict.
- **The import is one history entry** built with `begin_entry`; if its second tab fails (which the checks before it should prevent), the first is undone.
- **Dropping files.** egui gives the paths of dropped files; each is read at once and its dialog waits its turn (`GossiaApp::imports`). While files are dragged over the window, a layer says what dropping them does. The web port will need the bytes instead of the path.
- **Scroll bars are solid** (`ScrollStyle::solid`): egui's floating ones are hidden until the pointer is near, so a wide imported table did not look as if it scrolled. A form scrolls both ways, and a table in a form has its own horizontal scroll bar, as the tabular pane does. All the Windows snapshot images changed with this; the Linux ones must be made again on the runner.
- **The file dialog's `save` flag became a purpose** (`Browsing::Open`, `SaveAs`, `Import`), shared by the file browser and the text dialog that the tests use.
- **Not done, and why.** Export, which is a step of its own; importing a `.xsd` or a JSON Schema file as a schema sheet (it imports as data, with a schema inferred for it); a new tab with its own XSD (there is no blank XSD to start from yet; an XML tab comes from importing or from an XSD sheet already there); reordering tabs by dragging; reading a dropped file's bytes on the web. *(The first three were done later: see the decisions for the last items.)*
- **Tests.** In `gossia-core`, 11 for inference: objects, arrays and required fields; nulls; the most common kind and ties; mixed numbers; empty arrays; text lines; XML elements, attributes, repeats and order; namespaces; disagreeing orders; what is refused; typing of text; and 1 for lenient JSON. Each inferred schema is lowered and the data validated against it. In `gossia-calc`, 6 more in `tabs_tests.rs`: new tabs and their entry, an import as one entry, names checked, a deleted tab coming back with its formulas, forms and place, what cannot be deleted, rebinding and its undo. In `gossia-app`, 14 in `tab_commands_tests.rs` (both kinds of new tab, deleting the current tab, the delete warning, rebinding, names from file names, data fitting a schema, data fitting none and the next file fitting the inferred one, XML with an inferred XSD and the next file fitting it, refused files, JSON with a trailing comma, text, dropped files and a dropped workbook, the control commands), and one window test: `+` makes a tab and its context menu deletes it.

**Decisions made while building the Options dialog** (step 8g)
- **Seven pages, not nine.** General, Editing, Evaluation, Data sources, Trust and security, Files and import, and About are built, with what exists to set. The Display page (indentation, density, the Type and Formula columns, the table's column width, comment popups) and the Code editor page wait for the work that makes those options mean something; the formula bar's line count waits with Display. On the Data sources page only the limits are there, with a line saying data sources are not in this version yet; *Manage data sources...* and the grants come with 6b. There is always a workbook, so the workbook pages are never hidden.
- **The dialog holds text** (`options.rs`, `OptionsDialog`): each number is kept as typed, so a value out of range is shown under its field with the reason, and the first problem is repeated above OK, which is disabled. A number whose text was not changed keeps its exact stored value, so opening the dialog and pressing OK never changes a value by rounding. Times are shown in seconds and sizes in megabytes (of 1,048,576 bytes), with decimals where needed (`0.5 s`); counts and the scale are whole numbers. Each number shows its default and has *Reset*, which is disabled at the default.
- **OK** applies everything: the workbook settings with `Calc::set_settings`, one undoable entry, or none if they did not change; the preferences in one write of `preferences.json`; the history bounds to the workbook at once; and the revoked workbooks. Cancel, Esc and the close button drop all of it. The dialog opens again on the page it was left on, and the status bar's *Evaluation suspended* message is a link that opens the Evaluation page.
- **A workbook setting above its ceiling** is kept as typed, and the field says what is used (*60 s is used: your ceiling (Trust and security).*), measured against the ceilings as they are typed in the same dialog.
- **New preferences:** `appearance` (`theme`: `system`, `light` or `dark`; `scale_percent`, 50 to 300) and `files.max_import_bytes` (512 MB, as before, when import used the entry limit). A value that cannot be used is the default, with a warning, as for the others. Nesting depth is not a preference: both readers refuse more than 128 levels, and the Files page says so.
- **Theme and scale** are given to egui (`set_theme`, `set_zoom_factor`) when they change, which includes every change while the dialog is open, so they preview at once and go back on Cancel. The first frame changes only what differs from the defaults, so a window made with a theme of its own (the tests) keeps it. The title bars, which Windows draws, are given the theme too (`ViewportCommand::SetTheme`), the main window's when the theme changes and the code editor's when it opens or the theme changes: without it a dark window kept a white title bar under a light system theme (reported by the owner, 2026-10-30). *Follow system* gives the title bar back to the system. **winit is patched for this** (owner report, 2026-11-03: the title bar opened white in dark mode until the theme was switched to light and back). On Windows, winit 0.30.13's `set_theme` set the title bar but did not remember the theme, so the next `WM_SETTINGCHANGE`, which Windows sends at start-up and whenever any setting changes, put the system's theme back. `vendor-patches/winit.patch` makes it remember the theme, and (owner report, 2026-11-04: still white on the first start) repaints the title bar after setting it, by posting `WM_NCACTIVATE` inactive and then active again if the window has the focus, as losing and regaining the focus does, since Windows does not repaint the title bar of a shown window otherwise; winit is a vendor item at the 0.30.13 commit, used through `[patch.crates-io]`, until a release has the fix. A Windows API hook of our own would have needed `unsafe`, which the workspace forbids.
- **The trusted workbooks** are listed by the file each was last seen in. `trust.json` now keeps a `file` with each record, noted whenever the trust record changes while the workbook has a file. A workbook trusted before it had one is listed by its id. *Revoke* strikes a row through (and *Keep* puts it back) until OK. Revoking the open workbook applies when it is next opened; it does not stop what is running.
- **Save and restart** saves and starts Gossia again with the file, then closes the window. The application now opens a workbook named on its command line. A workbook with no file cannot use it: the button is disabled and says to use *Save As* first.
- **The dialog has a fixed size**, centred. It first grew by a row every frame, because a vertical separator takes all the height it is offered; and a dialog that changed size from page to page moved under the pointer. The page scrolls inside a fixed area, the line for a problem is always there, and the labels are in a column of the same width in every section. The width given to the window includes its frame, so the page takes the width left inside it: a page wider than that widened the frame but not the title bar. No page may be wider than the dialog, or the window grows past its title bar (found by hand): notes wrap under their section, a trusted workbook's path is cut to the page with all of it on hover, and the engine's revision is shortened; a window test checks that every page has the dialog's width.
- **While the dialog is open the grid takes no keys**, so arrows, Delete and typing never reach the selected cell behind it.
- **Numbers are right-aligned** in their fields, as in a spreadsheet; the unit follows the field.
- **The maximum range length is 1,000,000 by default, as a ceiling (Trust and security) and as a workbook setting**, down from 100,000,000 and 10,000,000. The workbook default is not above the ceiling, so that a new workbook is not held down by it from the start; a workbook may still ask for more, up to a ceiling raised on this machine.
- **About, not About the engine.** The last page is the usual About box. The library versions are read from Cargo.lock by the application's build script (`build.rs`), so they follow every update; the build fails if a listed library leaves the lock. Where the lock has two versions of one library, the one a Gossia crate names is shown. The licences are written in the build script, taken from each library's Cargo.toml.
- **Control channel:** `set_option` (`name`: a workbook setting's key, `ceiling.` and a key, `scale_percent`, `undo.max_entries`, `undo.max_weight`, `files.max_zip_entries`, `files.max_total_bytes`, `files.max_entry_bytes`, `files.max_import_bytes`, or `theme`; `value` in the dialog's unit) does what typing it and pressing OK does.
- **Not done, and why.** The Display and Code editor pages (above); the data source grants (6b); the Immediate window's history and its *Clear history* (with the code editor).
- **Tests.** In `gossia-app`, 12 in `options_tests.rs`: seconds and megabytes shown and read, and every key found again; OK with nothing changed changing nothing; a workbook setting as one undoable entry reaching the evaluation thread, and the page remembered; a number out of range refused with nothing changed, and a grace period of 0 allowed where a time limit is not; a ceiling holding a setting down; the preferences written and read by the next session; Cancel dropping everything and the preview; the import limit as its own preference; a revoked workbook forgotten on OK, with its file in `trust.json`, and not trusted the next time; *Save and restart* needing a file and saving it; `set_option`; and the About page's libraries, each with a version from Cargo.lock, quick-xml at the version gossia-core uses. Two window tests: *File > Options...*, typing on the Evaluation page and OK; and every page as wide as the dialog, with a long trusted path; two snapshots: `options_evaluation`, a setting held down by its ceiling, and `options_about`.

**Decisions made while building export** (step 8f)
- **`Calc::export_text`** (`gossia-calc/src/export.rs`) gives the text of a tab's file with the same writers a save uses, so an exported file is the tab's `data.json` or `data.xml` as the workbook holds it, indented (two spaces) or compact. `export_formats` says what a tab can be written as: its own format, and text too when its root is a JSON array of strings. `export_schema` names the schema tab that can go beside it: the one the tab is bound to. A schema tab has none, since its schema is built in.
- **The format follows the schema**: XML for a tab whose schema is an XSD, an XSD schema tab included (written as `.xsd`), and JSON for the rest. Writing one as the other is refused.
- **Text** is one line for each string with a newline after each, so text import reads back the same array. Errored items are left out, as they are from JSON. A string with a line break in it would come back as two lines, so text is not offered for such a tab, and asking for it is refused with the item's index.
- **The dialog comes first, then the file.** *Export...* in the dialog opens the file browser to save, starting on `<Tab>.<ext>` in the workbook's folder. A name without an extension is given the format's. The choices are kept while the file is chosen, so a write that fails shows its reason in the file-name dialog and the user can try another name.
- **The schema file is named after the data file** (`orders.json` gives `orders.schema.json`, `orders.xml` gives `orders.xsd`) and replaces a file of that name, which the dialog says. The browser asks only about the data file. If the two names would be the same (exporting XML data as `a.xsd` with its schema), the export is refused. The schema is not offered with text. Both files are written atomically, the data first.
- **Nothing is recorded**: an export changes nothing in the workbook, so it is not an undo entry and does not make the workbook dirty. Success shows no message, as a save shows none.
- **The control channel** gets `export` (`tab`, `path`, `format`, `compact`, `schema`), which answers with the path of the schema file, if one was written.
- **Not done, and why.** A recent-folder memory for exports (the browser starts where the workbook is); exporting several tabs at once.
- **Tests.** In `gossia-calc`, 3 in `package_tests.rs`: JSON with computed values and no formula or errored node, indented and compact, and its schema tab lowered again; XML and its XSD read back, compact, and refusing JSON; an array of strings as text, errored items left out, and a line break refused. In `gossia-app`, 6 in `export_tests.rs`: what the dialog starts with; the dialog's way through the file-name dialog, the extension added, the schema beside the file, and importing the file again choosing the schema it was exported with; an imported XML file exported with its XSD and read back to the same text, comments included; a text file out again as the same lines; a schema file that would be the data file; a failed write keeping the dialog and the choices.

**Decisions made while building the code editor window** (step 10a)
- **A second operating-system window** (`code.rs`), shown with egui's immediate viewports, so it can sit on another monitor as Excel's Visual Basic editor can. It opens at 900 by 600 points. Where a backend cannot open windows (the headless tests), egui draws it as a window inside the main one, which is how the tests drive it. Closing it, or *File > Close and return to Gossia*, hides it; Alt+F11 in it brings the main window to the front. Opening another workbook keeps it open, with no module in it.
- **Typing goes to the workbook after a pause.** Each open tab holds its text as typed. It goes to the workbook (`Calc::set_module_source`, one undoable *Edit module X*) once typing has paused for a second, and at once before undo, redo, saving, opening, a new workbook, quitting, closing a tab or the window, and the module commands. This is how "rapid typing is merged": a burst is one entry, and a module is not reloaded, nor its formulas recalculated, at every key. The history is not changed for it. The status line says *loading...* while text waits to go in.
- **One history.** Undo and redo in the editor (Ctrl+Z, Ctrl+Y, Ctrl+Shift+Z and the *Edit* menu) are the workbook's, not the text box's own, so there is one history for both windows. What was typed and has not gone in yet is undone first, as one entry (*Undo typing*). After an undo or redo the tab shows the source the workbook now has.
- **Inserting, removing and renaming a module are undoable now** (`Op::AddModule`, `Op::RemoveModule`, `Op::RenameModule`), as tabs became in 8e; step 5b had them outside the history. Being in the history also makes them changes to save, which they were not: a new module could be lost without the question about unsaved changes. Removing puts the module back in its place on undo. Opening a file clears the history, so loading modules is not an edit.
- **Insert > Module** makes an empty module named `Module1`, `Module2`, ... (the first free number; names ignore case), as Excel does, opens it and puts the cursor in it. Rename asks for the name in a dialog that says why a name cannot be used as it is typed (`Calc::module_name_problem`); remove asks first and says that *Edit > Undo* puts it back. Renames reach the open tabs through `Calc::take_module_renames`, undone and redone ones included.
- **The explorer** lists the modules in their order, the one shown selected and a failed one in red with the reason on hover. A click opens a module (a double click too). The context menu of a module has *Open*, *Rename...*, *Remove...* and *Insert module*, and the *Modules* branch has *Insert module*.
- **The text** is egui's text box with egui_extras' Python highlighting (the simple highlighter, no new dependency) in the monospace font at 13 points, with the line numbers beside it, and long lines not wrapped: the pane scrolls both ways. Enter keeps the indentation of the line and adds a level after a line whose code ends with a colon (a colon in a comment or a string does not count); Tab inserts spaces to the next stop of four, or indents every selected line; Shift+Tab takes up to four spaces off each line touched (`code_text.rs`). Indentation is always spaces.
- **The procedure box** lists the module's top-level functions (`def` and `async def` at the start of a line, read from the text as typed) and shows the one the cursor is in, or *(General)* above the first, as the VBE does. Choosing one puts the cursor on its line and scrolls to it.
- **The status line** says what loading the module shown came to: *loaded*, *loading...*, *failed to load:* with the reason in red, or *not loaded, because the workbook is not trusted*. In an untrusted workbook modules are shown and edited as usual and nothing is loaded, as 5c decided.
- **A jump waits for a frame without a click**, and for the window's second frame: egui takes the focus from every text box a click is not on, and a window's first frame only measures it, so asking for the focus then is lost.
- **Not done here, and why.** *Compile*, export and import of `.py` files and the *Forms* branch are 10b; the Immediate window and the *Code editor* page of the Options dialog (font, indent width, line numbers, highlighting, wrapping) are 10c, so the editor uses the defaults that page will have. Name completion in the editor, a search box, and a mark on a tab whose text has not gone in yet are not built.
- **Tests.** In `gossia-calc`, 2 more in `python/tests_modules.rs` (inserting, removing and renaming undone and redone, with the order and the renames the window follows; the first free `ModuleN` and the name check). In `gossia-app`, 5 in `code_text_tests.rs` (Enter, Tab, Shift+Tab, the procedure box's functions, line numbers) and 6 window tests in `tests/code.rs` (Alt+F11 and *Insert > Module*, a burst of typing as one entry with undo and redo, Tab and Shift+Tab, the procedure box, renaming and removing from the explorer with undo, the status line for a failed module and in an untrusted workbook). One snapshot, `code_editor`; four others changed only because *Code editor* moved the items below it in the *View* menu, where the tests click. Tests set a long pause, because the test clock runs a quarter of a second a step.

**The Tools menu**
- The main window's menu bar is *File*, *Edit*, *View* and *Tools*. *Tools* holds *Code editor* (Alt+F11) and *Options...*, which were in *View* and *File*: both are tools for the workbook rather than ways of looking at it or of handling its file, and this is where Office and most editors put them. Every snapshot shows the menu bar, so all of them changed with it.

**Decisions made for a formula over what is inside**
- **The question is the window's; the refusal stays in the calculation layer.** `Calc::set_formula` still refuses a formula over formulas (`EditError::ContainsFormulas`), so a script or a caller that did not ask cannot erase them by accident. The window asks before entering, from `Calc::detail_inside` (or `detail_inside_column`), which counts the values no formula computes and lists the formulas, stopping at each formula; it then enters with `set_formula_replacing` or `set_column_formula_replacing`.
- **Values count, not only formulas.** Typed and imported values are detail as much as formulas are (the owner's correction): a formula over an array of items asks, though the calculation layer has always allowed it. Nulls do not count. Changing a formula already on the node does not ask, since what is inside it is its own result.
- **One entry.** The formulas removed and the formula entered are one undoable *Set formula at X*; the old values come back with undo because the formula's entry keeps the node as it was.
- **Where it is asked.** Entering in the cell or the formula bar, the formula of a column, and the formula box of the form designer. While the question is open the text being typed waits: Enter does nothing more, and Escape cancels the edit as No does. *No* ends the edit, as Escape does. The control channel gets the question as its error.
- **Tests.** In `gossia-calc`, 2 in `calc_tests.rs` (replacing the formulas inside, with undo and redo; what `detail_inside` counts) and 3 in `columns_tests.rs` (a formula on the array replacing its column formula, with undo; one item's formulas; what a column formula would erase). In `gossia-app`, 1 window test in `tests/ui.rs` (No keeps everything, Yes enters and erases, undo brings it back, and a formula on a leaf does not ask); the test of typing a column formula now answers the question, since the column held typed quantities.

**Decisions made for checking and files in the code editor** (step 10b, 2026-10-28)
- **Compile is *Debug > Compile Module1*,** as in the Visual Basic editor, and in a module's context menu. There is no debugger, so it is the only item of *Debug*. It puts what was typed in the workbook first, then shows a list under the code: *Compile Tax: 2 problems*, one line each, *Line 2, column 8: ...*. A click opens the module and puts the cursor at that line and column. The list is closed with its x.
- **The list is live, not a picture taken at the click.** It is worked out each frame from `Calc::module_problems`, so a fix makes its problem go, and a failure found when the module loads comes in when loading ends (*loading...* until then). In a workbook that is not trusted nothing is loaded, and the list says that it holds only what can be found without running the module.
- **What it finds** (`Calc::module_problems`): a syntax error, alone, since nothing else can be read then (the double-underscore check is one); in a module on a cycle of imports, the import that closes the cycle, with the cycle named; an import of something that is neither a workbook module nor one of the standard library modules formulas may use (`Syntax::standard_modules`, so it is found before loading, and in an untrusted workbook); and a failure while loading (an exception, a name that clashes), with the line Python names in it. An unknown import is not listed a second time as the load failure it causes. The places of imports come from the parser (`ModuleInfo::import_at`).
- **Export and Import** are in the editor's *File* menu, as in the Visual Basic editor, and *Export...* in a module's context menu. They use the same file browser as the workbook's files, drawn in the editor's own window. Export writes the source as it is, UTF-8, `.py` added to a name without an extension. Import reads a UTF-8 file of at most the import size limit, drops a byte order mark, turns CRLF line ends into LF, and adds a module after the others, named after the file: characters that cannot be in a name become `_`, a name taken or reserved gets a number (`Tax2`), and a name kept for internal use (`_x`, `gossia...`) gets `Module_` before it. It is one entry in the history (*Insert module Tax2*) and the module opens.
- **The *Forms* branch** lists the customised forms under their tabs; a generated form has no design to show. A form opens in a tab of its own, titled `Orders: Entry`, as the JSON it is stored as, highlighted as JSON and indented by two spaces. Typing goes in after the same pause as a module's, but **only a text that is a usable design replaces the design** (`FormDesign::from_json`: JSON that conforms to `builtin:form`). Otherwise the form keeps its design, the status line says why in red, and the text stays as typed until it is fixed. A change made in layout mode is shown in the tab. Forms are not in the workbook's history (step 7b), so Ctrl+Z in a form's tab is the text box's own undo.
- **Tests.** In `gossia-calc`, 1 in `python/tests_modules.rs` (each kind of problem with its line and column). In `gossia-app`, 3 window tests in `tests/code.rs` (*Compile* and a click on a problem, then fixing it; export and import, with the numbered name and a missing file; a form's text that is not a design and one that is).

**Decisions made for the Immediate window and the Code editor page** (step 10c, 2026-10-28)
- **The Immediate window is a pane at the bottom of the editor,** shown at first, hidden and shown with *View > Immediate window* or Ctrl+G, as in the Visual Basic editor. It shows each expression (`> Tax.half(7)`) and its result below it, an error in red; *Clear* empties it, and the line to type in is under it.
- **It runs as a formula on the node selected in the workbook's window would** (`this` is that node, or the root of the tab shown), and the pane says where: *this is Orders.items[0]*. It goes to the evaluation thread as a job of its own (`Session::run_immediate`, `Immediate`), waits its turn as a button procedure does, so it reads the workbook up to date, and runs under the formula time limit; one that does not stop is abandoned as a formula is. The text is checked as a formula's is before it runs (syntax, double underscores). Nothing it reads becomes a dependency, and nothing is stored. It needs a trusted workbook; untrusted, the line is not shown and the pane says why.
- **Results are shown in Python's own form** (`repr`), after `PLAIN`, so a node shows as the lists, dicts and values it holds; anything else, a function say, shows as Python shows it. An error shows its code and message, with the traceback under it. This is the "canonical Python form" of the Options section.
- **Only expressions,** as the design says, not statements: `x = 1` is a syntax error. A walrus assignment does not outlive the expression, as in formulas.
- **The history** is `immediate-history.json` in the configuration directory: `{"version": 1, "entries": [...]}`, oldest first. An expression run goes to the end, and the same text typed again moves to the end instead of being kept twice. The file is read again before each change, as the other files are, so two windows keep each other's entries. Up and Down in the line go through it, and Down past the newest gives back what was being typed.
- **The output limit** cuts a result longer than *Largest result shown* at a character boundary, with *... 1234 more bytes left out*. The pane keeps the last 200 expressions.
- **The Code editor page** comes after Editing. *Editor*: the font, the size (8 to 32 points), the indent width (2 to 8 spaces), and the check boxes *Show line numbers*, *Syntax highlighting* and *Wrap long lines*. *Immediate window*: *History entries kept* (0 to 10,000) and *Largest result shown* (1 KB to 16 MB), and *Clear history*, which, like *Revoke*, takes effect on OK and can be taken back with *Keep history* before then. Lowering the number of entries drops the oldest on OK. They are stored under `code_editor` in `preferences.json`.
- **The font is Geist Mono, the only monospace font bundled.** Any other monospace font would have to be found and loaded from the operating system, which nothing else does yet; the page shows the font so that the choice has its place when that comes.
- **Line numbers are painted beside the rows of the text,** a number on the first row of each line, so that a wrapped line takes its rows without a number on each. A wrapped pane scrolls only up and down.
- **Tests.** In `gossia-calc`, 2 in `session_tests.rs` (results in Python's form, `this`, modules, errors, refusals before running, nothing pending afterwards; the time limit, one at a time, and trust). In `gossia-app`, 1 in `options_tests.rs` (the page's options and history on disk, *Clear history*, fewer entries kept), 2 more checks in `code_text_tests.rs`, and 1 window test in `tests/code.rs` (a result, an error, the history file, Up and Down, untrusted). The `code_editor` snapshot shows the Immediate window and the *Debug* menu now.

**Decisions made for the Display page** (a leftover of step 8g, 2026-10-28)
- **The page comes after General,** as in the table above, with *Tree grid* (indentation per level, 0 to 64 px [16]; row density; *Show the Type column*; *Show the Formula column*) and *Tabular view* (*Default column width*, 40 to 1,000 px [120]). They are stored under `display` in `preferences.json`: `tree_indent_px`, `row_density` (`compact`, `normal` or `comfortable`), `show_type_column`, `show_formula_column`, `table_column_width_px`.
- **Row density applies to the tabular view too,** so that a table opened inside the tree has rows of the same height as the tree around it. A row is its text's height plus 5, 11 or 17 points, and at least 22, 28 or 34 (`Density::padding`, `style::grid_row_height`); Normal is the height rows had before. Forms keep their own rows: their sizes are part of the form's design.
- **A hidden column is left out of the grid,** not drawn at no width; with the Formula column hidden the Value column takes the rest of the width. The grid keeps the widths it was dragged to for each set of columns, so hiding one column does not give its width to another.
- **The default column width is where a column starts.** A column that was dragged keeps its width; a new table, or a column first shown, takes the default.
- **The formula bar's lines** (Editing, *Formula bar*, 1 to 40 [6]) are stored as `editing.formula_bar_lines`. While editing, the bar grows with the text to that many lines and then scrolls.
- **The comment popup options** (hover delay, popup width and height) came with comments in the window; see the decisions for comments.
- **Control channel:** `set_option` takes `display.tree_indent_px`, `display.table_column_width_px`, `editing.formula_bar_lines`, and `display.row_density` with `compact`, `normal` or `comfortable`.
- **Tests.** In `gossia-app`, 1 in `options_tests.rs` (the page's values and their limits, what is written to `preferences.json` and read by the next session, `set_option`), and 1 window test in `tests/ui.rs` (a deeper indentation, the three row heights in order, and the grid without its Type and Formula columns).

**Decisions made for comments in the window** (a leftover of step 6, 2026-10-28)
- **What can have a comment** (`Calc::comment_refusal`): on an XML tab, the root and every element, including each item of a repeated element. Not an attribute (XML has no place for one), not an element's `#text`, not an array as a whole (its items are the elements), not a node that is absent, and not a node inside the result of a formula, which the formula replaces on every run. The node that holds a formula can have one: a result keeps the node's comment.
- **An edit of its own** (`Calc::set_comment`, `Op::Comment`): inserting, editing and deleting are each one entry in the history (*Insert comment on Order.qty*), which marks the workbook as modified. Formulas cannot see comments, so nothing is recalculated, and undoing a comment on a formula's node leaves its result alone (an `Op::Replace` of the node would have put back the old value). Text that is only white space is no comment; Windows line ends become `\n`; the same text again is no edit. Text with `--` or ending with `-` is refused with the reason (`comment_text_problem`).
- **The triangle** is drawn in the top-right corner of the Value cell, in the tree and in the tabular view alike, in the palette's warning colour (amber): red already marks invalid data and errors.
- **The popup** opens beside the cell when the pointer has rested on it for the hover delay, wraps at the largest width and scrolls past the largest height. It stays while the pointer is on it, so that a long comment can be scrolled, and it is not shown while a menu is open, which it would cover, or while the comment's box is open.
- **The commands** are in the menu of the Value cell (a right click anywhere on it; a click elsewhere on the cell selects it) and in the tree's row menu: *Insert Comment*, or *Edit Comment* and *Delete Comment*. On a node that cannot have one, *Insert Comment* is shown disabled, with the reason on hover. On a JSON tab there are none.
- **The comment box** opens beside the cell with the comment's text. Enter adds a line break; Ctrl+Enter, or a click outside the box, saves; Esc cancels. Text that is refused keeps the box open with the reason under it. While it is open the grid takes no keys.
- **Display options** (Options, Display, *Comments*): *Hover delay* (0 to 5 s [0.5 s]), *Largest popup width* (120 to 1,200 px [360]) and *Largest popup height* (60 to 1,200 px [240]), stored as `display.comment_hover_delay_ms`, `comment_popup_width_px` and `comment_popup_height_px`.
- **Control channel:** `cell` returns `comment`; `set_comment` (`path`, `text`, absent or null to delete) does what the box does.
- **Not done, and why.** A comment at the end of an element and a comment next to text are still dropped with a warning when a file is read (step 6): giving them a place needs a node that is not an element, which nothing shows yet. An errored or null node is absent from the XML written, and so is its comment, as the design says of errored nodes. Forms show no comments. *(Both done later: see the decisions for comments inside elements.)*
- **Tests.** In `gossia-calc`, 3 in `comments_tests.rs` (insert, edit, delete, undo and redo, and the history's words; what is refused and why, and a JSON tab; a comment through a formula's result, no recalculation, and the XML written). In `gossia-app`, the Display test in `options_tests.rs` covers the three options, and 3 window tests in `tests/comments.rs` (the menu, the box, line breaks, a refused text, Esc, delete and undo; the popup on hover, an attribute's disabled item, a click outside saving; no commands on a JSON tab). One snapshot, `comments`: the triangle on two cells and one popup.

**Decisions made for comments inside elements** (2026-10-31)
- **Three places on a node** (`CommentPlace`): *before* its element (the node's comment, as before), at the *start* inside it (before its text) and at the *end* inside it (after the rest). The two inside are rare, so a node keeps them in one boxed pair that is absent when both are, and nodes stay as small as they were. They are part of the node, so they move with it, as the one before does, and formulas cannot see them. No new kind of node was needed: the comments belong to the enclosing element, as Comments and structure fidelity says.
- **Reading** (`xml.rs`). A comment followed by a child element is that element's, as before. Of the rest, those before the element's text, with nothing before them, are at the start; the others are at the end, each place joined with line breaks. A comment inside the text (`Ac<!--x-->me`), or between a child element and text, cannot keep its place, because the text is one value: it goes to the end, with the warning *a comment inside the text was moved to the end of the element*. Elements the schema lacks keep their comments too, which before were lost without a warning when they stood before one of their children.
- **Writing.** The one at the start is written first inside the element when the element begins with text; with no text to stand before, it is written at the end, before the one there, so that reading it again does not give it to a child element.
- **The window.** The triangle shows when a node has a comment at any place. The popup shows one comment as before, and several each under the name of its place (*Comment*, *Comment at the start of the element*, *Comment at the end of the element*). *Edit Comment* opens a box for each place the node has, named the same, and saves them as one entry in the history (`Calc::set_comments`); an empty box takes that comment away. *Insert Comment* makes only the one before: the places inside come from files. *Delete Comment* takes every place away, as one entry.
- **Forms.** A field shows its node's comments as its cell does (the triangle at the field's corner and the popup on hover), and outside layout mode its menu has the comment commands, on an XML tab. A computed field shows the triangle and the popup but has no menu, as it takes no clicks.
- **Control channel:** `cell` returns `comment_start` and `comment_end` too; `set_comment` takes `place` (`before`, `start` or `end`; before when absent).
- **Tests.** In `gossia-core`, 3 in `xml_tests.rs` (comments at the start and the end read and written back in their places, one only comment in an element, a comment inside the text moved with a warning, the comments of elements the schema lacks). In `gossia-calc`, 1 in `comments_tests.rs` (read, set together in one entry, refused text changing nothing, written back, undone). In `gossia-app`, 2 window tests in `tests/comments.rs` (the popup and the boxes of the places, editing and deleting them together, undo; a form field's popup and *Insert Comment* from its menu).

**Decisions made for the last items** (2026-10-31; see Build order for what is not planned)
- **Importing a schema file.** A file is a schema when it is an XSD (an XML document whose root is `schema` in the XML Schema namespace; `.xsd` files are read as XML now) or JSON that names the JSON Schema meta-schema in `$schema`, or a file called `*.schema.json`. The import dialog then starts on *The file is an XSD (a JSON Schema): import it as a schema tab*, with the choices of data below it, so the file can still be imported as data. The schema tab is named from the file and is one entry, *New tab order*. A schema that does not lower is still imported, as one written by hand would be, with the reason in the import report and on its tab. The control channel's `import` takes `schema: "self"`.
- **A new tab with its own XSD.** *Its own schema* in the *New tab* dialog has a choice of JSON Schema or XSD. The XSD (`tab_commands::blank_xsd`) has one root element named after the tab, with an empty sequence, and auto-update on as for JSON, so fields added from the data tab go into it; the tab is an XML tab from the start. The control channel's `new_tab` takes `xml: true`.
- **Reordering tabs** (`Calc::move_sheet`, `Op::MoveSheet`): a tab dragged along the bar goes before the first tab whose middle is right of the pointer, with a line where it will go. It is one entry, *Move tab C*, which marks the workbook as modified; formulas name tabs, not places, so nothing is recalculated. A click still selects a tab and a double click renames it. The control channel has `move_tab` (`name`, `to`).
- **Resizing the columns of a form's table.** Every column can be dragged wider or narrower by the edge of its title. Out of layout mode the width is the viewer's, kept while the window is open, as in the tabular view; the table starts again from the design's widths when those change. In layout mode the table is drawn disabled, so its own handles cannot be used: the right edge of each column title is a handle of its own, a line shows where the edge goes, and letting go sets the column's *width* in the design, one step of layout undo (`ColumnEdit::Width`). A column with a width of its own can still be dragged out of layout mode; before, it was fixed. A resizable column draws a faint line at its edge, so four Windows snapshots of forms changed (`default_form_orders`, `layout_mode_orders`, `table_column_expressions`, `two_column_form_orders`); the Linux ones must be made again on the runner.
- **The icon** (`app_icon.rs`) is drawn in code, so that no image file is kept in the repository: the *bracket canopy*, chosen from four concepts in the Canva design thread (2026-10-31), a tree whose crown is a pair of bold green JSON braces on a white trunk with a flared base, on a near-black rounded square with a thin white outline. It replaced a first, plainer icon (a white tree outline on the accent blue). It stays readable at 16 pixels and on light and dark taskbars. The braces are strokes along a few points, the trunk a curve, all anti-aliased by sampling 4 by 4 points per pixel. The window uses it at 64 pixels (eframe's own icon was the egui logo).
- **The executable's resources** are written by `build.rs` as a `.res` file (icons at 16, 24, 32, 48, 64 and 256 pixels as 32-bit bitmaps, the icon group, and the version resource: the numbers and *Gossia*, `gossia.exe` and the version as texts) and given to the linker as it is, which both `link.exe` and `lld-link` (cargo-xwin) take. No resource compiler is needed, so the Linux build of the installer works as before. Only for MSVC targets.
- **The `.gossia` association** is four registry values under `HKLM\Software\Classes` in the installer, in a component of their own: `.gossia` is `Gossia.Workbook`, whose icon is the executable's and whose *open* command runs it with the file, which it opens (as *Save and restart* does). They are removed with the application.
- **Tests.** In `gossia-calc`, 1 in `tabs_tests.rs` (a move, past the end, the same place, undo and redo). In `gossia-app`, 3 in `tab_commands_tests.rs` (a new XML tab from a blank XSD with a field added to it, and the control command; an XSD file as a schema tab, a data file then given it, and an XSD as data; a JSON Schema by `$schema` and by its name, plain JSON not taken for one, and one that does not lower), 2 in `app_icon.rs` (the icon's square, tree and corners; the resources), and window tests: `tests/ui.rs` (a tab dragged along the bar, and undo) and `tests/forms.rs` (a column dragged wider out of layout mode, and in layout mode into the design, and layout undo). The installer's registry values are checked only by building it on the runner.

**Decisions made for picking a place, and `LOOKUP`** (2026-10-31)
- **`VLOOKUP` is called `LOOKUP`.** It never was Excel's vertical lookup (it takes a list of objects and field names), so the *V* only misled. There is no alias: workbooks made before have to be changed by hand.
- **The pick button** (`pick.rs`) is at the right end of the formula bar, a crosshair, *Pick a place*. It works while text is being typed for the tab shown, in the bar or in a cell. Pressing it sets the text aside (the bar shows it, greyed, and says under it what to do) and the user goes to the place: opening and closing nodes, the arrow keys, the tabular view and other tabs all work as usual, and the name box shows the place under the selection. A click on a node, in the tree, a table or a form, puts its address (`Prices.list`, the name a formula uses, always with its tab) into the text where the cursor was, goes back to the tab of the formula and its selection, and typing goes on in the formula bar with the cursor after the address. Nothing is entered until Enter, as before.
- **While picking**, a click only picks: it does not start editing, enter a value, or run a command of the grid, and the second click of a double click on the place (within 0.6 s) is ignored too. Editing cannot start. Esc, or the button again, stops picking and goes back to the text as it was. If the tab of the formula is deleted or renamed meanwhile, the text is dropped with a message.
- **Not done**: picking a range or a whole column (`Orders.items[].qty` is not a formula), and picking into the code editor or a form's expressions.
- **Tests.** In `tests/ui.rs`, 2: a place on another tab picked into the middle of a formula that is then entered, and a place on the same tab, with Esc stopping a second pick.
- **A click anywhere in a row of the tree selects it** (and, while picking, picks it), not only a click on the name, the badge, the value or the formula: the grid's table senses clicks on the whole row, under what its cells show, so those keep their own clicks (the triangle, the editors, the comment menu). Tested in `tests/ui.rs` by a click in the empty Formula column of a row.

**Decisions made for the XML field commands** (a leftover of step 6, 2026-10-28)
- **The type is found from its lowering source** (`xsd_fields::xsd_type_path`): the XSD lowering records where each type came from (`schema/element[order]/complexType`, `schema/type[Item]`), and that is turned into the path of the `complexType` node in the XSD tab's tree, by the `@name` of each item on the way. The commands of 4d are the same commands: `FieldPlaces` says `xsd`, and only the edit of the type differs (`Calc::xsd_edited_type`); the data is changed exactly as on a JSON tab, since an attribute is a field named `@name` there too.
- **What a new field is written as.** An element goes at the end of the type's `xs:sequence` (made if there is none), with `minOccurs="0"` unless it is required; an attribute goes after the other attributes, with `use="required"` if it is required. Its type is the built-in type of the choice: `string`, `integer`, `decimal`, `boolean`, `date`, `dateTime`, with the prefix the XSD tab binds to the XML Schema namespace (`xs:` usually, none if it is the default namespace). Keys are put in the order the meta-schema declares, so the XSD is written in the order XSD requires.
- **Names are XML names.** A name must start with a letter or `_`, hold only letters, digits, `_`, `-` and `.`, and not start with `xml`; a prefix (`a:b`) is refused, since a new field has no namespace of its own.
- **An attribute is typed without its `@`.** *Add field* on an XML tab has *Element* and *Attribute* choices above the name, and the name is typed bare; *Rename field* on an attribute shows its name without the `@` and keeps it an attribute. In the calculation layer, a field whose name starts with `@` is the attribute, and renaming an element to an attribute or back is refused.
- **Text content.** On an element that holds text (`xs:simpleContent`), the attributes are those of its `xs:extension`; adding an element is refused with a hint to add an attribute, and `#text` cannot be renamed or deleted (its type is changed on the XSD tab).
- **Reordering keeps the kinds in place.** A new order that moves an attribute into the elements' places, or the reverse, is refused, so dragging an attribute among the elements leaves the dialog open with the reason.
- **Not done, and why.** An element written as `ref` to a global element cannot be renamed from the data tab (the name is the global element's, and rewriting every `ref` is an edit of the XSD tab's own structure); the refusal says so. Choice and `xs:all` groups are not in the XSD subset, so a type has one sequence.
- **Tests.** In `gossia-calc`, 8 in `xsd_fields_tests.rs` (the fields of an XML record; adding an element and an attribute, what the XSD says and that the data written reads back against it, undo; text content; XML names; renaming and its refusals; deleting with undo; reordering and its refusal; a named type used by every item) and the old refusal test now checks that the commands are available. In `gossia-app`, 1 in `schema_tests.rs` (an imported XML file with an inferred XSD: add an element and an attribute, rename an attribute) and 2 window tests in `tests/xml_fields.rs` (the *Attribute* choice in the dialog; renaming an attribute without its `@`).

**Decisions made for the procedure list** (a leftover of step 7c, 2026-10-28)
- **A drop-down, not a text box.** The property page of a button offers *(none)* and the public functions of the workbook's modules (`Calc::procedure_names`), by bare name, in module order. It is read from the source, so it works in an untrusted workbook and before the modules are loaded.
- **What is left out.** Private functions (a leading underscore), and every function of a module that does not parse or that failed to load. Two modules cannot define the same name (the second fails to load with a clash), so a bare name is never ambiguous and `module.name` is not offered; a design that names one by hand still runs it, and the list shows it as chosen.
- **All functions, not only those that take `(form, ctx)`.** The list is read from the source, which gives names, not parameters; a function with the wrong parameters fails when clicked, with Python's message.
- **A name that no module has** (a function renamed or removed, or a module that fails) stays chosen and is shown as *name (not in any module)*, so fixing the module brings the button back; choosing another, or *(none)*, replaces it.
- **No functions yet**: the page says to write one, `def name(form, ctx):`, in the code editor (Alt+F11).
- **Tests.** In `gossia-calc`, 1 in `procedure_tests.rs` (what is listed before and after the modules load, and that each runs). In `gossia-app`, 1 window test in `tests/buttons.rs` (the missing name marked, the list, choosing one, and the button running it). No new snapshot.

**Decisions made for expressions on table columns** (2026-10-28)
- **The same four expressions on a column** of a repeater's table (`ColumnDesign::visible_when`, `enabled_when`, `text_colour`, `fill_colour`), edited on the column's property page. The row numbers column has none.
- **Visible when is for the whole column** (the owner's choice): it runs once, at the array the table is bound to (`this` is the array), and when it is false the column is left out, title and all. A column cannot be hidden in some rows only.
- **The others run for each row, at the cell** (the owner's choice): `this` is the cell, `parent` the item, as a formula in that cell would see them. *Enabled when* disables the cell's editor, and the colours colour the cell's text and fill the whole cell.
- **`this` is a value for an expression.** For the expressions of controls and of columns, `this` at a node that holds one value is that value (`this > 5` works), `None` for an optional field that is absent, and the node for an object or an array (`Context::this_by_value`). Formulas and the Immediate window keep the node: a formula reading its own value would depend on itself.
- **Which cells.** A column of items that are objects is a field (dotted for a field of a field); the one column of items that are not objects is the item (`value`). A column the items' type does not have, a hidden column, and every column of a repeater shown stacked have none run.
- **The list follows the rows.** Each row's expression is its own (`ExprKey::column`, `row`), so a new item runs its own and a removed one's are forgotten; an edit, or a formula's result, rebuilds the list when some column has expressions per row. An edit inside an item runs that item's again, as for any read.
- **Errors, pending, trust and layout mode** are as for controls, per cell: an error in a column's *visible when* keeps the column and disables its cells with the reason on hover; a cell waiting to run again keeps its last look and shows a small pending marker; untrusted, nothing runs, every column is shown and a column with *enabled when* has its cells disabled; layout mode draws the table as designed.
- **Cost.** Three expressions on a table of a thousand rows are three thousand runs on the first pass; after that only the rows whose reads changed run again.
- **Tests.** In `gossia-calc`, 3 in `control_exprs_tests.rs` against the real interpreter (a colour and an *enabled when* per row following edits, new and removed rows; *visible when* once at the array; a dotted column, a column of plain items, and columns that run nothing). In `gossia-app`, 3 window tests in `tests/form_exprs.rs` (cells coloured and disabled per row and following edits; a hidden column; an untrusted workbook) and a new snapshot, `table_column_expressions`.

**Decisions made for expressions on controls** (a leftover of steps 7b and 7c, 2026-10-28)
- **Four expressions per control:** *visible when*, *enabled when*, *text colour* and *fill colour*, stored in the design as `visible_when`, `enabled_when`, `text_colour` and `fill_colour`. The owner asked for colouring by condition as a Python expression that returns the colour, so a colour is the expression's value, not a rule with a fixed colour: `"red" if this < 0 else None`.
- **Where they run.** At the node the control is bound to, as a formula there would (`this` is that node); a control that is not bound runs at the root of the tab. Controls are designed at the top level of a form, not inside the items of a repeater, so each expression runs once, not once per item. *(Since the items of a stacked repeater can be designed, the expressions of the controls there run once per item: see the decisions for the gaps in forms.)*
- **Run with the formulas, after them** (`Snapshot::exprs`, `Run::expression`): the expressions that are out of date go with the next evaluation and run once its formulas are done, so they read fresh results. What each reads is recorded; an edit there, a new result there, a change to its text, or a reload of the modules marks it out of date again. A result is dropped, and the expression run again, under the rule for formulas: an edit after the snapshot of what it read, or a read of a formula that is still out of date. The results live in `Calc` (`control_expression`), not in the data, are not saved and are not in the history.
- **What the form makes of a result** (`form_exprs::gate`): Python's truth for *visible when* and *enabled when*; for a colour, a CSS name (the 16 basic names and a few more), `#rgb`, `#rrggbb`, `#rrggbbaa`, a list or tuple of three or four numbers from 0 to 255, or `None` or `""` for the usual look. A hidden control takes no room. A disabled one is drawn as a disabled egui area, so its editors and buttons cannot be used.
- **Colours.** The text colour is the colour of every text in the control, its titles, a check box's tick and a drop-down's arrow included. A fill is drawn behind the control, and its text boxes, check boxes, drop-downs and buttons at rest take it too, with an outline; with a fill and no text colour, the text is black or white, whichever reads on the fill.
- **Errors.** A *visible when* or *enabled when* that fails, or does not parse, leaves the control in view and disabled; a colour expression that fails, or gives something that is not a colour, leaves the usual colour and the control usable. Either way, hovering over the control says which expression and why. A syntax error is found when the design is read and the expression is not run. The property page also shows it, in red under the expression's field: the first line of the message, with the whole of it on hover; for a column's expression that runs per row, the first row that failed (*Row [0]: ...*). A colour typed without quotes (`red`) is a Python name and fails this way (added 2026-10-28, after a colour without quotes looked as if it did nothing).
- **While out of date** the control keeps its last state and shows the pending marker in its top-right corner. Before an *enabled when* has first run, its control is disabled.
- **Not trusted.** Nothing runs: every control is in view, a control with an *enabled when* is disabled and says that the workbook has to be trusted, and no colour is applied.
- **A runaway expression.** One that does not stop within the formula time limit fails with `#TIMEOUT!`, as a formula does. If the thread has to be given up, the expressions of that control fail with the reason instead of being run again and again.
- **Layout mode** draws every control as designed, hidden or not, without its colours.
- **Not done, and why.** Expressions on the columns of a repeater's table (per row) and on stacked items: they would run once per item, which wants the per-item form designs that 7b left out. *(Columns were done next: see the decisions for expressions on table columns. Stacked items followed: see the decisions for the gaps in forms.)*
- **Tests.** In `gossia-calc`, 6 in `control_exprs_tests.rs` (running and running again after an edit of what was read, the last result kept meanwhile; reading the results of formulas in the same pass; errors and syntax errors; a changed and a removed expression; an untrusted workbook; a control in a group and a renamed tab). In `gossia-app`, 2 unit tests in `form_exprs.rs` (colours, truth), 4 window tests in `tests/form_exprs.rs` (hiding, disabling, an error and its reason, the colours, an untrusted workbook) a new `form_expressions` snapshot, and `layout_mode_orders` updated for the two colour fields of the property page.

**Decisions made for CSV, TSV and pasting** (2026-10-28)
- **Our own reader, no new dependency.** RFC 4180 is small; `delimited::rows` reads it, and the rows become JSON-shaped data that the JSON inference types and checks as it does any other (`delimited::infer_delimited`). A quote left open makes the rest of the text one cell, with a line in the report.
- **Columns are typed before inference, not cell by cell.** Inference would take each cell's own kind and flag the odd ones; a column of postcodes or codes with a leading zero must stay text, and a column of mostly numbers with one note in it is text rather than numbers with an invalid value. The data is then all valid under the inferred schema.
- **What makes the first row data:** a number or a yes or no in it, as the owner put it. An empty or repeated header name does not; the column gets `column_N` or a suffix instead, so the other names are kept.
- **The paste is a plan like a file's** (`GossiaApp::plan_paste`, `begin_paste`): the same dialog, limits, matching against the schema tabs and report. Its format is told from the text, since there is no extension. Replacing the tab's data stayed as a choice of that dialog (`SchemaChoice::Replace`) so that nothing the Raw view could do is lost; the control channel's `paste` still replaces directly. While another text box has the keyboard (the dialog's tab name), the Raw view leaves Ctrl+V to it.
- *File > Import...* lists CSV and TSV beside JSON and XML.
- **Tests.** In `gossia-core`, 5 in `delimited_tests.rs` (the header and the typing of columns, a first row of data, quotes, empty cells and ragged rows with the column order, blank and repeated names). In `gossia-app`, 3 more in `tab_commands_tests.rs` (the format of pasted text, CSV and TSV imported, a paste as a new tab and as a replace), and the two Raw view tests in `tests/forms.rs` now go through the dialog.

**Decisions made for masked fields** (a leftover of step 6b's notes, 2026-10-29)
- **A property of a field control, `masked`**, shown on the property page as *Masked* when the field is bound to a string (and kept visible while set, so it can be cleared after the schema changes). Table columns do not have it: a password belongs in a single field, not in a column of rows.
- **Only the text box changes.** It is egui's password box: one line of dots, whatever the editor (*Multiline* is ignored while masked). The value is stored and saved as typed, shows in the data tree and the Raw view as it is, and is not kept out of undo. Clearing it after use is the procedure's job, as recorded under Data sources.
- **Tests.** In `gossia-core`, the design round-trip covers `masked`; in `gossia-app`, the property-page unit test sets it, and 2 window tests in `tests/forms.rs` (dots shown and the typed value stored; the property offered for a string and not for a number). No snapshot.

**Decisions made for data source definitions** (step 6b, part 1, 2026-10-29)
- **The direction of 2026-10-28 is now the Data sources section**, rewritten: definitions per user and machine in `data-sources.json` beside `trust.json`, secrets in that file, referred to by name, not exportable. The keyring, the per-workbook grants and the target confirmation are gone.
- **No grants** (owner, 2026-10-29): a workbook may use any source defined on the machine. **Writes are a tick box on the definition**, *Allow writes*, off by default (owner, same day), in place of the read and read-write grant levels.
- **`source("Sales")`, not a bare global** (owner, same day): which names exist depends on the machine, so a global would turn a missing definition into a `NameError` and could clash with tabs and modules. `source`, `sources` and `SourceError` are reserved.
- **Where it lives.** `gossia-calc::sources` holds the definition, its checks and target, the store, and the Test; the store is shared by the window and the evaluation thread (`PythonSettings::sources`, from `User::python_settings`). Python reaches it through one native callable, `_source_access(op, payload)`, with JSON text both ways, and the prelude turns an error reply into `SourceError`, which `convert::failure` maps to `#SOURCE!`. `Shared::side_effects` is on only in procedures and the Immediate window, which is what keeps `define` and `check` out of formulas.
- **The file.** Read on every use and read again before every change (another window may have changed it); written beside itself and moved over; unknown fields kept; an unreadable file never overwritten.
- **The Test, at first,** opened a TCP connection to the server's port (the URL's for HTTP) or opened the SQLite file for reading, on a background thread, within the call timeout. Each kind has had its full Test since its own part (HTTP 2, SQLite 3, PostgreSQL and MariaDB 4).
- **The form keeps only what the kind uses** on save. A stored secret is *Keep*, *Change* or *Clear*; a copy has no credentials. The fields are named "... of the data source" for the screen reader and the tests, since "File" is also a menu.
- **Tests.** In `gossia-calc`, 5 in `sources_tests.rs` (checks and target, URLs, the file with unknown fields, renames and an unreadable file, Python settings, the Test against a file and a local listener) and 4 in `python/tests_sources.rs` (a formula finds a source and not its secret, `#SOURCE!` for a missing one, `define` and `check` refused in formulas, the Immediate window defining and checking, reserved names). In `gossia-app`, 4 unit tests in `sources_ui_tests.rs` (secrets kept, changed and cleared; a copy; the kind's fields and the numbers; saving and renaming) and 3 window tests in `tests/sources.rs` (made and tested, a stored password never shown, delete after asking).

**Decisions made for HTTP data sources** (step 6b, part 2, 2026-10-29)
- **ureq 3.5**, blocking, with rustls and the platform verifier (`gossia-calc::http`): the evaluation thread already waits for one formula at a time, so an async client would add a runtime for nothing; the platform verifier uses the Windows certificate store, where a company proxy's root certificate lives (the owner's machine has one: git needs schannel there).
- **Requests stay below the base URL and redirects are not followed**, so the credentials of a definition are never sent anywhere it does not name. A URL in an error message is shown without its query, which may hold an API key.
- **A status of 400 or more raises `SourceError`** (the design's "HTTP error status" is `#SOURCE!`), with the status and the first 200 characters of the body. 2xx and 3xx are responses.
- **Pooling** keeps one agent per definition (as it is, with its limits) so its connections are reused, at most the pool size; without pooling each call has a fresh agent and keeps no connection. An edited definition gets a new agent.
- **Waits do not count against the limit.** The native call pauses the formula's watchdog for the request and starts it again with what was left; a thread-local `IoClock`, which the evaluation thread gives each worker, records the wait as it happens, and `abandon_if_stuck` subtracts it. The call timeout still bounds every wait.
- **Writes in the Immediate window too**, not only in procedures: it runs once, when the user asks, which is what the rule protects. Formulas are refused, with *Allow writes* checked first.
- **No cache per recalculation pass yet** (added later: see "Decisions made for the cache of source reads"). Each formula that calls a source fetches; two formulas with the same call fetch twice. A formula runs again only when what it read in the workbook changes, or on F9. **Tools > Recalculate (F9)** is new: it marks every formula out of date (`Calc::recalc_all`), so the sources are read afresh; in an untrusted workbook it says why nothing runs. The expressions of form controls are not run again by it.
- **The sample window's engine has the user's data sources.** `GossiaApp::new` now makes its engine from the in-memory user it keeps, rather than from settings with a store of their own.
- **Tests.** In `gossia-calc`, 5 in `http_tests.rs` against a small HTTP server on this machine (the URL and its escaping, the authentication in headers and query, sending and reading with a method and a body, a status and a redirect returned as they are, the size limit and the timeout, the Test and a refused sign-in) and 4 more in `python/tests_sources.rs` (a formula reading JSON and parameters, `#SOURCE!` for a 404 and for a write without *Allow writes*, writes refused in formulas and made from the Immediate window as JSON and as a form, a 1.5 s wait under a 0.5 s formula limit, and the evaluation thread not abandoning it). In `gossia-app`, 1 more window test in `tests/sources.rs` (F9 and *Recalculate* fetch again).
- **The owner's live test case: https://randomuser.me/api/** (2026-10-29). Two tests use it, `http_tests::randomuser_me_answers_over_https` (a `GET` with `results=3` over HTTPS through the platform verifier, three users with names, and the Test) and `tests_sources::a_formula_reads_randomuser_me` (formulas reading it). They need the internet, so they are `#[ignore]`d and run only when asked: `cargo test -p gossia-calc -- --ignored randomuser`. CI and `cargo xtask test` stay offline. Without a path, the base URL is used exactly as typed, trailing slash included.

**Decisions made for SQLite data sources** (step 6b, part 3, 2026-10-29)
- **rusqlite 0.41 with SQLite built in** (`bundled`), so the version is the same on every machine and nothing has to be installed. `gossia-calc::sqlite` holds `query`, `execute` and the Test; the prelude's `Source.query` and `Source.execute` reach them through `_source_access` like the HTTP methods.
- **One connection per call**, opened and closed again (SQLite does not pool). **The file is never created**: a mistyped path is an error, not a new empty database. The path is used as typed; a relative one is relative to the folder Gossia was started in.
- **Read-only by the connection.** Without *Allow writes*, and **always in a formula** (even from a source that allows writes), the file is opened read-only, so SQLite refuses a change itself. `query` also refuses a statement that SQLite says changes data (`sqlite3_stmt_readonly`), with "use execute()", so a procedure's mistake is not a silent write. `execute` needs *Allow writes* and a button procedure or the Immediate window, as HTTP writes do, and gives the number of rows changed.
- **One statement per call**; a second one after `;` is refused rather than ignored.
- **Parameters**: a list for `?` (or `?1`) or a dict for `:name` (the colon may be left out); a node is read first. The count must match. Dates go in as ISO text (`2026-10-29`, `2026-10-29 09:30:00`), as SQLite keeps them; `bytes` as a blob; `True` and `False` as 1 and 0.
- **Values**: integers, reals, text, `None` and `bytes`. SQLite has no date type, so **a column declared `DATE` gives a `date`, and one declared `DATETIME` or `TIMESTAMP` a `datetime`**, when the text is in ISO form; other text in such a column stays text. A real that is NaN comes back as `None`.
- **Limits**: a locked database is waited for, and a long statement stopped (by SQLite's progress handler), within the call timeout; the rows read may not exceed the maximum response size. The wait does not count against the formula's limit, as for HTTP.
- **The Test** opens the file read-only and reads `sqlite_version()` and the number of tables: "SQLite 3.x: <path> opened read-only, 4 tables, in 2 ms". A file that is not a database fails it.
- **Tests.** In `gossia-calc`, 5 in `sqlite_tests.rs` (columns, declared types and rows with positional, named and byte parameters, and the errors for counts, names, two statements and bad SQL; read-only refusing a change and `execute` counting rows; a missing file not created and the size limit; a runaway statement stopped at the timeout; the Test, a text file and a folder) and 2 more in `python/tests_sources.rs` (formulas reading rows, dates, bytes and NULL, a node as a parameter, `execute` without *Allow writes*; writes refused in formulas, `query` refusing a change, HTTP methods on a SQLite source, and the Immediate window writing and reading back).


**Decisions made for PostgreSQL and MariaDB data sources** (step 6b, part 4, 2026-10-29)
- **The `postgres` crate (synchronous, over tokio-postgres) and the `mysql` crate**, both with **native-tls**, in `gossia-calc::database` with a file for each server (`database_postgres.rs`, `database_mariadb.rs`). native-tls uses the operating system's TLS and certificate store (SChannel on Windows, OpenSSL on Linux), as HTTP does with the platform verifier; the `mysql` crate's rustls option knows only the built-in web roots, so a company root certificate would not be trusted. The `mysql` crate speaks the MySQL protocol, so a MySQL server works too.
- **A `query` always runs in a read-only transaction that is rolled back**, whatever the definition allows, so a formula never changes data and a statement that tries is refused by the server ("cannot execute ... in a read-only transaction"). `execute` commits on its own (autocommit) and needs *Allow writes* and a button procedure or the Immediate window, as for SQLite and HTTP.
- **Parameters**: PostgreSQL takes `$1`, `$2` ... and a list; MariaDB takes `?` and a list, or `:name` and a dict (the colon may be left out). The count must match. Values are sent as text and the server converts them to the column's type, so `"2026-10-01"` goes into a `date`; `bytes` go as `bytea` or binary, `True` and `False` as booleans (1 and 0 for MariaDB).
- **Values**: integers, floats, text and `None`; `numeric`/`DECIMAL` as an integer when whole and small enough, otherwise a float; `bytea` and binary columns as `bytes`; `json`/`jsonb`/`JSON` parsed into Python values; `date`, `timestamp`/`DATETIME`/`TIMESTAMP` as `date` and `datetime` (a `timestamptz` is given in UTC); `time`, `uuid`, enums and `citext` as text. A PostgreSQL type not listed is refused with the advice to cast it with `::text`.
- **Limits**: the connect, and every read and write on the connection, give up at the call timeout; PostgreSQL also gets `statement_timeout` so the server stops a long statement itself. The PostgreSQL driver's own connect timeout covers only the TCP connect, so the sign-in runs on a thread that is given up at the deadline (a server that accepts and then says nothing would otherwise hold the call forever). The rows may not exceed the maximum response size.
- **TLS** is a setting per definition, *Prefer*, *Require*, *Unchecked* or *Off* (see the definition). *Prefer* tries TLS and falls back to an unencrypted connection only when the server says it has none; a certificate that cannot be checked is an error, not a reason to fall back. *Unchecked* is *Require* with native-tls told to accept any certificate for any host name; the Test then says "encrypted (certificate not checked)". The target says ", TLS required", ", TLS without certificate check" or ", no TLS" when the setting is not *Prefer*. Both test servers have self-signed certificates, so *Prefer* fails on them, as it should.
- **Pooling** keeps up to the definition's maximum of open connections per definition (keyed by its name and its settings, so an edited definition starts a new pool). A connection goes back reset (`DISCARD ALL`, or MariaDB's reset), and one that failed is closed. A call that finds every connection in use waits for one within the call timeout, then fails with "all N connections of its pool stayed in use".
- **The Test** opens a throwaway connection and reports the server's version and whether the connection is encrypted: "PostgreSQL 15.19 at host:5432/sales as reporting, encrypted, in 183 ms" A MariaDB source whose server is MySQL (no "MariaDB" in its version) is reported as MySQL.
