- **Tests.** In `gossia-calc`, `database_tests.rs` has 2 that run everywhere (the value conversions; an unreachable server named in the error, with and without a pool) and 3 that run against live servers when `GOSSIA_TEST_POSTGRES` or `GOSSIA_TEST_MARIADB` holds a URL such as `postgres://user:password@host:5432/database`, ending in `?tls=off`, `?tls=require` or `?tls=unchecked` to set TLS (the table of every value type written and read back, parameters and their count, a query refusing a change, a pool of one reused and surviving a failed statement, and the size limit; a wrong password refused by the Test). `database_postgres.rs` tests its `numeric` decoder. The `sources_tests.rs` Test case now also checks that a port that accepts and then says nothing fails within the timeout. PostgreSQL was tested against the owner's `postgresdb` machine (PostgreSQL 15, a separate `cliotree_test` role and database), and the MariaDB source against the MySQL 8.0 server on the owner's build host (a separate `cliotree_test` database and user); both with TLS *Unchecked* (encrypted) and *Off*, as both servers have self-signed certificates. CI runs them with *Unchecked*.


**Decisions made for ODBC data sources** (step 6b, part 5, 2026-10-30)
- **Windows only**, with the `odbc-api` crate as a Windows-only dependency: the driver manager (`odbc32.dll`) is part of Windows, so nothing has to be installed besides the database's own driver. The kind is in every build's definitions (`data-sources.json` is the same everywhere), but the form offers it only on Windows (`SourceKind::available`), and elsewhere a call fails with "available on Windows only". `gossia-calc::database` serves it beside PostgreSQL and MariaDB, from `database_odbc.rs`.
- **Where it connects**: one field, *DSN or connection string*. Text with `=` in it is a connection string; anything else is a DSN name, used as `DSN=<name>`. The user name and password are added as `UID` and `PWD`, in braces only when a `;`, a brace or spaces at either end would otherwise change them (the PostgreSQL driver keeps braces around a plain user name as part of it).
- **Read-only, best effort** (owner's choice): without *Allow writes* the connection string gets `ReadOnly=1`, which the Access, Excel, text and PostgreSQL drivers honour and others ignore; and every `query` runs with autocommit off and is rolled back, where the driver has transactions (one that has not refuses manual commit, and the query runs as it is). The driver's read-only connection attribute is not set: `odbc-api` does not give the handle of a connection it has opened. Pooled connections go back rolled back with autocommit on.
- **Parameters** are `?` with a list (a dict is refused). The count is checked when the driver can say how many the SQL takes. Whole numbers go as 32-bit integers when they fit, as the Access and text drivers cannot take 64-bit ones; text as text, `bytes` as binary, `True` and `False` as bits, `None` as a null.
- **Values** by the column's ODBC type: integers, floats and decimals as numbers, bits as booleans, binary columns as `bytes`, `DATE` and `TIMESTAMP` columns as `date` and `datetime` (a fraction of zeros is dropped: `09:30:00.000` is `09:30:00`), everything else as text, read as UTF-16 so that no code page is involved.
- **Limits**: the login timeout and the query timeout are given to the driver, and the sign-in also runs on a thread that is given up at the call timeout, for a driver that does not honour its login timeout.
- **The Test** reports the database the driver names and where it went: "PostgreSQL through ODBC, odbc:Driver={...};Server=... as reporting, read-only asked of the driver, in 39 ms".
- **Tests.** In `gossia-calc`, on Windows: `database_odbc.rs` checks the connection string (braces, `ReadOnly=1`); `database_tests.rs` reads a CSV folder through the Access text driver (skipped where it is not installed), and runs the whole round (types, parameters and their count, a query's change rolled back, read-only refusing `execute`, a pool of one, a wrong password) against the server named by `GOSSIA_TEST_ODBC`, a connection string with `UID` and `PWD` in it; it was run against the owner's `postgresdb` through the PostgreSQL ODBC driver. `sources_tests.rs` checks the definition everywhere (a password in the connection string refused, the target, `sources.define`). CI runs on Linux, so the ODBC tests run only on a developer's Windows machine.


**Decisions made for the cache of source reads** (after step 6b, 2026-10-30)
- **A pass** is one evaluation (`Snapshot::evaluate_with`): the formulas that are out of date and the expressions of form controls. At its start the evaluation calls the new `Engine::begin_pass` (a no-op by default), and the Python engine forgets what the previous pass read.
- **What is cached**: reads made by formulas and control expressions only, that is `query` and HTTP `GET`. The key is the request as the prelude sends it (the operation and its JSON: source name, path or SQL, parameters, headers), so other parameters are another read. A failure is cached too, so that two formulas with the same failing read show the same `#SOURCE!` and the source is not asked twice.
- **Not cached**: button procedures and the Immediate window always read afresh (they wait for a source on purpose, and may have just written to it), and do not fill the cache either. `execute`, `sources.check` and HTTP methods other than `GET`, `HEAD` and `OPTIONS` are never cached.
- **No volatility**: a formula that reads a source is not run again on every pass, only when what it read in the workbook changes, or on F9 (`Calc::recalc_all`), which makes every formula out of date and so reads every source once.
- **Tests.** `python/tests_sources.rs`: two formulas with the same `GET` and two with the same failing one make two requests; other parameters make another; F9 reads everything again; the Immediate window reads twice when asked twice.

**A fix to the Immediate window** (2026-10-29): it shrank a little each time it was redrawn, so moving the pointer over it made it smaller. The resizable panel takes the height of what it draws, and the output's height rested on an estimate of the input line's that was too large. The pane now claims its whole height first (`immediate_pane`); `tests/code.rs` hovers over it 30 times and checks that its top does not move.

**Decisions made for the report layout crate** (step 12a, 2026-11-02)
- **`gossia-layout`** depends on `gossia-core`, `skrifa` 0.44, `harfrust` 0.12 (MIT, the shaper epaint uses) and `epaint_default_fonts` 0.37, and not on egui. The bundled fonts moved from the app into it (`assets/fonts`), and the app's style takes them from there, so there is one copy.
- **Text is shaped with harfrust** and measured as egui measures it: the ascent, descent and line gap of the font, the row height rounded to 1/32 of a point, and the same fallback chain (the typeface, then Inter, then Ubuntu Light; monospace is Geist Mono, Hack, Inter, Ubuntu).
- **Lines break** after a space and after a hyphen between letters; a word wider than the line breaks between clusters; a tab is four spaces.
- **Tests.** `text_tests.rs` (every font reads, row heights, widths by size and weight, kerning, fallback, tabs, line breaks, hyphens, a long word); `style.rs` `reports_measure_text_as_egui_does` lays out the same strings in egui and checks widths within 0.25 pt and equal row heights.

**Decisions made for the form additions, part 1** (step 12b-1, 2026-11-02): fields that show an expression, label templates, repeaters over expressions and nested designed items. The look (fixed colours, bold and italic, borders, vertical separators, the new number formats) is part 2.
- **New properties**: `value` on a field and on a column of a table, `template` on a label, and `items`, `item_name` and `list_name` on a repeater. Item and list names are Python names (`^[A-Za-z_][A-Za-z0-9_]*$`).
- **A template becomes one Python expression**, `''.join((format((expr), 'spec'), ...))`, with `!r`, `!s` and `!a` as in an f-string. A template without an expression runs nothing and is not in the trust digest; one that cannot be read shows `#ERROR!`, with the reason on hover and on the property page.
- **An expression is kept per item**: its key holds the item of each repeater around, outermost first (before, one row). Items of an expression reach Python as names bound for the expression: `this`, the item name and the list name; a record is a `Record`, a dict whose fields are also attributes (`order.no`).
- **When the items change**, the expressions inside are made again for the new items. Items that are not a list put nothing inside, and the repeater says what it got.
- **Paths inside items** are read from the item of each bound repeater around (`orders[0].lines[3].sku`); inside the items of an expression they start again from the top of the tab. A field that shows an expression and a repeater over one have no path to check.
- **In the window**: a field that shows an expression is a read-only box in its format; in an untrusted workbook it shows `#UNTRUSTED!`, and a template its fixed text with `#UNTRUSTED!` for each expression. A table over an expression is a read-only grid with the columns of its design, or with none the fields of its first item, and the row numbers if shown; its columns take *Value*, *Visible when* and the two colours. Stacked items of an expression have a stepper, whose position is the window's own (not saved, not undone).
- **On the property page**: *Value*, *Items*, *Item name*, *List name*, *Text is a template*, and *Add a column with an expression* (a column `columnN` showing `this`). An error inside items names the item of each repeater, as `Item [0][1]: ...`. Every expression box on the property page (and the text of a label that is a template) keeps what is typed aside until it loses the focus, by a click elsewhere, Tab or Enter: only then is the expression stored and run, so a half-typed one shows no syntax error (owner, 2026-11-02).
- **A field has a path or a value** (owner, 2026-11-02). *Source* on the property page chooses, and clears the other (the write procedure goes with the value). A design with both, or a write procedure without a value, is a problem of the field, as a missing path is. A write procedure gets the text as typed, a `str`, since an expression has no type to read it by; the box shows the value unformatted while it has the focus. It is not offered in an untrusted workbook or while a procedure runs, Inside the items of a repeater, `ctx.index` is the index of the item the field (or a button) is in: the array index for a repeater bound to an array, the position in the list for one over an expression, the innermost when repeaters nest, and `None` outside one (owner, 2026-11-02). The whole chain is kept in the call (`Call::rows`), for outer indexes if they are wanted later.
- **A column can show an expression, with a write procedure** (owner, 2026-11-02), in a table bound to an array as in one over an expression. In a bound table it is a column beside the fields of the items, kept in the design (a column of the design with a value is one of the table's columns), with `this` the item. *Add a column with an expression* adds one (`columnN`, showing `this`) to either kind of table, and its page has *Value*, *Write procedure*, *Visible when* and the two colours. Its cells are read-only boxes, or with a write procedure boxes whose text goes to `name(form, ctx, value)` when a cell loses the focus with a change, with `ctx.index` the row and `ctx.button` the title of the column and the row (`Twice [1]`).
- **Tests.** `form/template.rs` (splitting, the Python expression, the marks); `control_exprs_additions_tests.rs` (a value that follows the data, templates, nested repeaters over expressions with item and list names, nested designed items, a table over an expression, items that are not a list, the trust digest); `form.rs` `item_paths_chain_through_the_repeaters_around`; `tests/form_shown.rs` (the value and the template follow the data, the untrusted marks, a table over an expression with a computed column, stepping through stacked items).

**Decisions made for the form additions, part 2** (step 12b-2, 2026-11-02): the look.
- **A fixed colour is `#rrggbb` in the same property** (`text_colour`, `fill_colour`, of a control or a column) as an expression. `#` begins a comment in Python, so no expression is ever a fixed colour and the two cannot be confused. A fixed colour is not an expression of the control, is not in the trust digest, and is drawn in an untrusted workbook and in layout mode. On the property page each colour has *Expression* or *Fixed*: *Fixed* shows a colour picker (first black for text, `#fff2cc` for a fill), *Expression* the box as before, empty for the usual look.
- **Bold and italic** are `bold` and `italic` on a control and on a column, each absent (inherited from the group, as the font is), `true` or `false` (so a control can undo its group's). On the property page: *Inherited*, *Yes* or *No*.
  - **Bold is the bundled font at weight 700**, registered as its own egui family per typeface (`sans-bold`, ...), as reports lay it out (`gossia-layout`); a test checks that the two measure bold text the same. Under Sans, code in a bold control is Geist Mono bold.
  - **Italic is drawn slanted** (the bundled fonts have no italic files, as for reports): egui slants text drawn with an italic format, so labels, titles, label templates, field labels, field boxes and the boxes of fields that show an expression slant; the typeahead box and buttons do not.
- **A group's border**: `border_width`, 0 to 8 points (absent: the usual look, 0: none), and `border_colour`, a fixed colour only. A group with a border is drawn in a frame even without a title. Its fill is its fill colour, fixed or an expression.
- **A vertical separator** (`vertical` on a separator) is a line as tall as the control's height, or a row when it has none; the height handle and *Height* are offered for it.
- **The thickness of a separator** *(owner, 2026-11-03)* is `thickness` on a separator, 1 to 12 whole points, *Thickness* on the property page (ticked gives 2 points; each drag of the number is one step of Undo layout). Absent, it is the thin line it always was (egui's separator on a form, 0.75 points in a report). A thicker line takes its thickness plus 4 points across (at least the usual room), with the line in the middle, in forms and reports alike.
- **Percent, prefix and suffix** (`percent`, `prefix`, `suffix`, up to 20 characters each) dress numbers only: `prefix`, the number (times 100 for percent, then the thousands and decimals), `%`, `suffix`. A whole percentage with no decimal places set is `25%`, not `25.0%`. While a field is typed into it shows the plain value, as with thousands (0.25, not 25%).
- **Tests.** `form.rs` (the formats, the fixed colour, the schema); `control_exprs_additions_tests.rs` (a fixed colour runs nothing and is not in the digest); `style.rs` (every typeface has a bold family; bold measures as reports measure it); `tests/form_look.rs` (the formats in the window, picking a fixed colour, the look in an untrusted workbook); the `form_look` snapshot.

**Decisions made for the report model and report tabs** (step 12c, 2026-11-03). Reports are not drawn yet (12d); this step makes them tabs that can be made, kept, saved and edited as text.
- **The model** (`gossia_core::report`): `ReportDesign` is `version`, `kind` (`single_page` or `multipage`), `page` (paper `a4`, `a3`, `letter` or `legal`; `portrait` or `landscape`; margins in whole points, half an inch by default) and `layout`, a form design (`FormDesign`) as is. `builtin:report` is the schema of forms with `Report`, `PageSetup` and `Margins` added and `Report` as its root, so the controls have one schema. The bands and sections of multipage reports, and the parameters, are added to it in their own steps (12g, 12f).
- **What a report cannot have** is found by `ReportDesign::problems`, by the control's address: a path, a button, an editor, choices, a masked box, *enabled when*, a procedure or write procedure, and the same on table columns. The schema allows them, since it is the forms' schema, so the check is separate; the code editor refuses a design with a problem and says which control, and the report page lists them.
- **The starting point** (`report::starting_design`) makes the repeater the generated form would have for the array: a table when the items are scalars or flat objects (objects inside are flattened to `a.b` columns), designed items otherwise, with nested arrays as nested repeaters. The item is named after the array in the singular (`lines` gives `line`; for `items` or the root, the tab's name: `Orders.items as order`), `item` when that is not a free Python name, and `_2` and on inside a repeater whose outer item has the name. Field names that are not Python names are written `order["first-name"]`. *(Not used since 2026-11-03: starting from an array waits for multipage reports, see No repeaters in reports.)*
- **Report tabs in the workbook** (`gossia-calc`, `reports.rs`). Reports are kept apart from data tabs, and one list holds the order of all tabs on the bar (`Calc::tab_names`); data tabs keep that order among themselves, so code that lists only data tabs is unchanged. A report's name is checked as any tab's, against data tabs and reports together. *New report*, *Duplicate report* and *Delete tab* are history entries (`AddReport`, `RemoveReport`); a rename and a move are the tab entries there were. Nothing uses a report, so it can always be deleted, and a rename changes no formula. A new design (`Calc::set_report`) is not in the history, as for forms, but is an unsaved change.
- **Files.** A report is written to `reports/<Report>/design.json` and listed in the manifest's tabs, in its place, with kind `report`. A workbook with reports is written as format 1.1, one without as 1.0, so files without reports still open in 0.1.0; 0.1.0 opens a 1.1 file with its warning and keeps the reports as tabs it cannot show. A report whose design cannot be read is left out with a warning, and its file is kept.
- **Trust.** The expressions of a report's controls are in the trust digest, as `report <Name> control <address> <what>`, exactly as a form's are (the code that lists them is shared).
- **In the window.** A report's tab has a chart before its name. A report tab shows no formula bar, data tree, views or inspector; until 12d its page says what kind of report it is, how many controls it has and any problems, with a button that opens the design in the code editor. *File > Export...*, *View > Show as*, *Forms* and *Collapse all* are greyed on a report.
  - **New tab...** starts with *Make a: Data tab / Report*; for a report it asks the name, the kind and *Start from*, either nothing or any array of a data tab (schema tabs are left out), shown as a formula names it (`Orders.items`); greyed since 2026-11-03 until 12g, see *No repeaters in reports*. Choosing the other kind gives that kind's free name (`Data`, `Report`, `Report2`, ...) unless a name was typed. *New report...* is on the Edit menu and every tab's menu.
  - **The report tab's menu** has *New tab...*, *New report...*, *Import...*, then *Edit report*, *Export as PDF...* and *Copy batch command* (built with 12i), then *Rename tab...*, *Duplicate* and *Delete tab...*. A duplicate is shown when made. *Delete tab* asks *Delete the report Sales with its design? Undo puts it back.*, and *Rename tab* gives no warning about formulas.
  - **The code editor** has a *Reports* branch under *Forms*; a report's design opens as its JSON and is taken after the typing pause when it is a design a report can have, as a form's is. Its tab follows a rename of the report.
- **The control channel**: `new_tab` with `report: true`, `kind` and `start_tab`/`start_path`; `duplicate_tab`; `tab` takes a report; `status` gives `view: "Report"` on one; `open_workbook` and `new_workbook` list every tab.
- **Tests.** `report_tests.rs` in core (the schema, reading and writing, problems, the starting point); `reports_tests.rs` in calc (order, names, duplicate, rename, moves, delete and undo, the design not in the history, the trust digest, saving and reading back, an unreadable report); `report_tab_tests.rs` in the app (the dialog, the starting point, duplicate, delete, rename with the code editor, the code editor's check, the control channel); `tests/ui.rs` (making, duplicating and deleting a report with the mouse); the `new_report_dialog` and `report_tab` snapshots.

**Decisions made for single page reports, part 1** (step 12d-1, 2026-11-03): a report's expressions run, its controls are placed by `gossia-layout`, and its tab draws it. Editing it in layout mode is part 2.
- **The expressions** of a report's controls are a form's (`control_exprs.rs`), keyed by the report's name and a form name no form can have (`REPORT_FORM`). They run at the root of a tab that does not exist, so `this`, `parent` and `root` are bound to `None` outside the items of a repeater; inside, `this` and the item and list names are the item's, as in a form. **Only the report being shown runs** (`Calc::show_report`, set by the window each frame for the current tab); leaving its tab drops its results. Disconnect from Data Sources does not yet rewrite reports.
- **Placing** (`gossia_layout::report`, `single_page`): the layout is a list of paints (lines of text already broken, fills, borders, lines, pending markers), hovers with the reason for an error, and the area of each control with its address and items, in points from the top left. The values come through a `Source`, so the layout runs no Python and the results cache (12f) can stand in for the workbook. Groups use the forms' placement rules (`flow_rows`, `grid_cells`, `track_sizes`, `free_place`).
- **The look** (my defaults): text 12 points; 6 points between controls; 8 points of padding in a group with a border or a fill; a title (of a group or a repeater) bold at 1.2 times the size, or its title font. A field is its label (on the left, 170 points or the group's label width, at most 60% of the width; or above, in bold) and its value as text, numbers on the right; no box. A table has a title row in bold on the header fill, a line under each row, cells padded 6 by 3 points; a column is as wide as its widest text unless it has a width, and a table wider than the report shrinks its automatic columns (none under 24 points, or its own width if less) so it fits. Designed items are all shown, one under the other, each laid out as a Flow group of the repeater's columns. The row-number column counts from 1. Text wraps in its control; a long word breaks between letters.
- **What shows instead of a value**: `#ERROR!` in the error colour, with the reason on hover; `#UNTRUSTED!` in a workbook that is not trusted (a template shows its text with `#UNTRUSTED!` for each expression); nothing while waiting, with the pending marker; items that are not a list say what they are. A control whose *visible when* is false takes no room; a column whose *visible when* is false is left out.
- **The starting point** now gives a table columns without expressions, which show the item's field of their name (blank when it is absent), and designed items read a field that may be absent with `.get("name")` (a nested array with `.get("lines", [])`), so a missing optional field is blank, not an error.
- **In the window** the report fills the width of the tab, less 16 points each side, and scrolls. It is laid out again only when the data, the results, the designs, trust or what is waiting change. An empty report says so, with the button to open its design in the code editor; problems of the design are listed above it.
- **Colours and truth** of values (`colour_of`, `truthy`) moved from the app to `gossia_core::form`, so the layout and the forms share them.
- **Tests.** `report_tests.rs` in `gossia-layout` (order, visibility, errors, untrusted, pending, templates, wrapping, tables with and without columns, shrinking, designed items, items that are not a list, Flow columns, borders, Grid, Free, colours, alignment); `report_exprs_tests.rs` in calc (only while shown, following the data, `None` names, items and table columns, a rename); `tests/reports.rs` (a report from an array follows the data, a field, only while shown, untrusted, an error beside a value); the `report_drawn` snapshot.

**Decisions made for single page reports, part 2** (step 12d-2, 2026-11-03): the canvas, and *Edit report* on it. A first version that drew layout mode with the form view was replaced the same day (owner); what it had that stays is noted below.
- **The model.** `ReportDesign` has `canvas` (true once the top level is a canvas) and `height` (the canvas dragged taller than its lowest control). The top level of a canvas is laid out as a Free group at the paper width less the margins (`gossia_layout::report::canvas`), so the forms' placement rules (`free_place`) place it.
- **Fixed boxes** in the layout: a control with a height keeps it; what is taller is cut (text below the box goes, the last line that stays ends with `…`, fills and borders are shortened), the box is listed in `Laid::cuts` and its hover says *Cut: what it shows is taller than its box*. A control with an automatic height is as tall as its content and pushes nothing.
- **Turning old designs into canvases** (`to_canvas`): when a report that is not a canvas is shown, the window waits until nothing is pending and every expression has a result, then places each top-level control where the Flow layout put it, with its width and height rounded up to whole points, and stores the design (not in the workbook's history; it is an unsaved change, as an edit of a design is). In a workbook that is not trusted there are no results, so the heights are left automatic. A new report is turned the same way, the first time it is shown.
- **The designer** (`report_design.rs`) draws the canvas with `gossia-layout`, exactly as the report view, on the page colour with a faint line every 16 points (four steps of the grid) and a border; selection outlines, eight resize handles on the control the others follow, guide lines while moving, and a red mark at the bottom of a cut control are drawn over it. Places are kept in report points, so zoom changes nothing, and they are given to the property page and the align tools as a form's are (`FormRects`, with the canvas as the inside of the empty address).
- **What the mouse does.** A click selects the innermost control under it (Ctrl adds or takes out; the empty page selects nothing). Dragging a control in a Free group or on the canvas moves it, snapped to the 4-point grid and to the edges and centres of the others (Alt for no grid); a handle resizes, snapped to the grid, no smaller than 24 by 16 points; the bottom edge of the canvas sets its height, no higher than its lowest control. A drag starts where the button went down. Each drag is one step of Undo layout, except the canvas height, which is not part of the controls and is not undone by it. Delete takes the selected controls away.
- **Adding.** *Add ...* on the strip puts the control at the left, under the lowest control; dragging it from the strip puts it where it is let go, in the innermost Free group there or on the canvas. A new control has a box (my defaults, in points): label 160 by 20, field 320 by 20 (labelled *Field*), group 240 by 80, separator 240 by 16. A control is not dragged from one group into another on the canvas; *Move into the group above* and *Move out of the group* on the property page do that.
- **The strip** has *Edit report*, the zoom (*Fit width*, which fits the tab between 50% and 200%, or 50% to 200%) and, in layout mode, *Show expressions*, then the palette and Undo layout and Redo layout. *Show expressions* shows each field as `[its expression]` and a template label its text with the braces.
- **What stays from the first version**: the design read and written under `REPORT_FORM` (`stored_design`, `put_design` to `set_report`), *Edit report* on the tab menu, the palette without *Add button*, the property page without what only editing needs (path, help, editor, read only, masked, enabled when, choices, write procedure, the node's formula) and with number (or date) formats for report fields; the top level of the canvas counts as a Free group there, so X and Y can be typed. Numbers in boxes that show an expression's value are on the right unless aligned, in forms too, and a field's *Alignment* has *Automatic*.
- **A column's width** on the property page of a form's table is set as a control's is: *Width* ticked gives it 80 points, then a number from 24 to 800 points that can be dragged or typed, each drag one step of Undo layout. (The box had started at 0 for automatic, and the first 24 points of a drag disappeared under the narrowest width, so it seemed stuck at 24.)
- **Tests.** `report_tests.rs` in `gossia-layout` (the canvas width and places, its own height, a cut box with the ellipsis and nothing moved, an old design turned into a canvas showing the same); `tests/reports.rs` (an old report turned into a canvas, moving by dragging with Undo layout, resizing to the grid with a handle, adding under the lowest control and Delete, clicking to select and on the empty page, *Show expressions*, the palette without buttons or repeaters, the property page, the tab menu, no start from an array yet); `tests/forms.rs` (a column's width ticked, typed, and dragged as one step of Undo layout); the snapshots `report_drawn`, `report_layout` and `report_tab`.

- **No repeaters in reports** *(owner, 2026-11-03, after the canvas)*. A list of rows belongs in the detail band of a multipage report, so reports have no repeaters and no tables: the report palette has no *Add repeater*, `builtin:report` designs with one show the problem *a report has no repeaters: a list of rows is the detail band of a multipage report*, and `gossia-layout` draws nothing for one. The table and repeater code of `gossia-layout` (rows, header, *and N more rows*) went with them. *Start from* an array in *New tab...* is greyed until 12g, where it makes a multipage report with a detail band; `starting_design` stays in `gossia-core` for that.

**Decisions made for PDF export** (step 12h, 2026-11-04).
- **The crate.** The PDF is written by `gossia-report` (no egui), from what `gossia-layout` laid out, so the application and `bidwillii` write the same file: `single_page(&Laid, title)`, `pages(&Paged, title)` and `write(title, &[Page])`. Paging stays in `gossia-core` (`report::paginate`), where calc and the layout already share it, so the crate holds the PDF writing only.
- **krilla 0.8.2 and krilla-svg 0.8.1**, krilla without its default features (its own text shaping and raster images are not needed; krilla-svg turns raster images on for `data:` images in SVG). krilla reads fonts with skrifa 0.42 while the layout uses 0.44, so both are built; that is accepted rather than holding the layout back.
- **Text is placed glyph by glyph.** Each line the layout painted is shaped again with `Fonts::shape`, the same call that measured it, and its glyphs go to krilla in runs of one font with the measured advances and offsets (normalised by the size; the layout's offsets go down, the font's up), so the PDF breaks no line and moves no word. Each glyph carries the bytes of its cluster, so the text can be selected, searched and copied. The baseline is the row's top plus the ascent, as on screen.
- **Fonts.** The fonts of the chain the glyph came from (`fonts::FILES`), embedded as subsets by krilla. The bundled fonts are variable, so a font is the instance at weight 400 or, for bold, 700 (`Font::new_variable`), as the window draws them; a font without the axis is read as it is. **Italic** is slanted by a quarter of the height, as egui slants it, around the baseline.
- **Colours on paper** are the light theme's (`paper_colour`): text, weak text, errors and rules as the window has them in light mode, whatever the window's theme, on white paper. A colour of the design is as it is, its alpha as opacity. Borders are drawn inside their rectangle, as on screen. The pending marker is never drawn, and hovers are left out.
- **Charts** go in as vector graphics with krilla-svg: parsed with the options and the chart's style sheet the window renders with (`chart::svg_options_styled`), fitted into the chart's box keeping their proportions and centred. Text in a chart is embedded as text with the chart's fonts.
- **Pages.** A multipage report is a page for each page of `report::pages`, on its paper; one with no pages is one empty page. A single page report is one page as wide and as tall as its canvas (no margins), as the design says. The PDF's title is the report's name and its creator `Gossia <version>`; no date is written, so the same report gives the same bytes.
- **In the application**, *Export as PDF...* on the report tab's menu, and in the File menu after *Export...* (owner, 2026-11-04; greyed unless a report is shown), shows the report (so it is calculated) and opens a dialog: what the PDF will hold (pages on the paper, or one page), *With the parameter values in the parameter bar* when the report has parameters, in an untrusted workbook that the PDF has the results calculated on that date (or `#UNTRUSTED!` with no cache), and a required parameter left empty, which disables *Export...*. *Export...* asks for the file (`Browsing::ExportPdf`, `<report>.pdf` in the start folder; a name without an extension is given `.pdf`). In a trusted workbook the file is written when the workbook is idle, the dialog meanwhile saying the report is being calculated (and showing the report again if another tab was chosen); it is written atomically. A failure is shown in the dialog. The PDF is laid out afresh from the results, not taken from what the tab last drew, with the same functions the tab uses.
- **The control channel** has `export_pdf` (`path`, and `report`, the tab shown if left out): the report is shown, waited for until the workbook is idle (a minute at most), and written as the dialog writes it. *(`export_html` came with 13d, the same for HTML.)*
- **Licences** (checked 2026-11-04): krilla, krilla-svg, pdf-writer, subsetter, xmp-writer, skrifa 0.42, read-fonts, write-fonts, font-types, gif and image-webp are MIT OR Apache-2.0; fontconfig-parser (Linux) is MIT. In the tests only: pdf-extract, lopdf, adobe-cmap-parser, pom, type1-encoding-parser and ecb are MIT, encoding_rs (Apache-2.0 OR MIT) AND BSD-3-Clause, the rest MIT OR Apache-2.0.
- **Tests.** In `gossia-report`: the page sizes, the text of each page read back with pdf-extract (bold, italic, a serif and a monospace line with a tab), the fonts embedded, the title, a report without pages, and a chart drawn as vectors with its text. In `tests/report_pdf.rs`: a single page report exported through the dialog and read back, a multipage report whose every page has the words its page in the page view has, an untrusted workbook (the dialog's note and `#UNTRUSTED!` in the PDF), a required parameter left empty, the menu showing the report, and `export_pdf` on the control channel. The page view drawn by egui is compared with the layout by the existing width tests in `style.rs`.

**Decisions made for the interface's languages** (step 13f, 2026-11-06).
- **The owner's choices** (2026-11-06): Claude drafts all five translations, marked as drafted so that a native speaker can correct them; Japanese and Chinese use the system's fonts on the desktop, and in VS Code a font shipped beside the wasm and loaded only when one of them is chosen, so the wasm does not grow; the interface and the engine's messages are translated, while error codes (`#REF!`), function and keyword names, Python's own exception text, the files Gossia writes and the texts on reports stay as they are.
- **`gossia-i18n`**, a crate with no egui, below `gossia-user` and `gossia-app` (and `gossia-calc` in 13f-3). **The English text is the key**: code keeps its texts and wraps them in `tr!`, `tr!("Save")` (a `&'static str`) or, with named places, `tr!("Export {tab} as {kind}", tab = name, kind = "PDF")` (a `String`); a translation may put the places in another order. Each other language is a flat TOML file in `crates/gossia-i18n/locales/` (`fr`, `es`, `pt`, `ja`, `zh-Hans`), from the English text to its own, read once when first used. A text a file lacks is shown in English, never as a key. Fluent and gettext were not used: a flat table keyed by the English keeps the code as readable as before and needs nothing but `toml`, which the workspace has.
- **One language for the program**, an atomic set at the start and when the Options change it; English until then, so tests see English. A thread may have a language of its own (`set_thread_language`), and then a change made on it stays on it: the tests that switch languages do so on their own thread, beside the others.
- **Which language.** *Options > General > Language* lists *Follow system (Français)*, with the system's language named, and the six by their own names (English, Français, Español, Português, 日本語, 简体中文). It is previewed while the dialog is open, as the theme is, and kept as `appearance.language` in the preferences: `"system"` or a tag; a tag not known follows the system, with a warning. The system's language is `sys-locale`'s on the desktop (the first of the user's languages that is one of the six, else English); in a host, the host's (`Host::language`, VS Code's display language in 13f-4). Every Chinese tag is Simplified Chinese.
- **Fonts for Japanese and Chinese.** When the language is Japanese or Chinese, the window adds the system's font behind Inter and Ubuntu in every family (so Latin text keeps the bundled fonts, and data in those scripts shows too): Yu Gothic, Meiryo or MS Gothic for Japanese and Microsoft YaHei or SimSun for Chinese on Windows (from `%WINDIR%\Fonts`, face 0 of the collection), Hiragino and PingFang on macOS, and on Linux the font `fc-match :lang=ja` (or `zh-cn`) gives if it has the language. It is lowered by 0.15 of the size (`FontTweak::y_offset_factor`), so that kana and Han characters sit on Inter's baseline. With none found, the notice says their characters show as boxes. Back in a Latin language the font is taken out again. Reports keep their bundled fonts.
- **Whole sentences** (13f-2). A text is translated whole, never put together from translated pieces, since the pieces' order and agreement differ between languages: a message with a count has a form for one and one for more, through `counted(n, tr!("1 value"), tr!("{n} values"))`; one that differed by a word in English (*it is* or *they are*, *quitting* or *opening another workbook*) is two or three whole texts; a list is joined by `and_list`, whose `"{list} and {last}"` and `", "` are texts too (`、` in Japanese and Chinese). The Python a text shows (`params.<name>`, `def name(form, ctx):`, `source("Sales")`), key names, file names, data formats' names and fonts' names stay as they are, and so do the names the window gives new things (a tab called *Data* or *Report*, a file *Untitled.gossia*).
- **Names of choices from the other crates.** The `label()` of an enum the window lists (the kinds of controls, editors, alignments, bands, parameters and periods in `gossia-core`; settings, field kinds, data source kinds, authentication and TLS modes in `gossia-calc`; theme and density in `gossia-user`) is a `tr!` text, so `gossia-core` and `gossia-calc` depend on `gossia-i18n` from 13f-2. Where a label also served as a name the program reads, it no longer does: the command line's period names (`last-month`) are spelled out in `PeriodPreset::key`, and the control server finds views by their English name (`View::name`), not their label.
- **Completeness is a test** (`crates/gossia-i18n/tests/catalogues.rs`): it reads every `.rs` file of the workspace, comment lines aside, for `tr!` texts (a string literal, with the escapes texts use) and fails for a text a language's file lacks, a translation whose named places differ from the English's, and an entry no source has. Adding a text therefore means adding its five translations in the same change. Names (Gossia, the fonts' names, key names such as Alt+F11) are not wrapped.
- **The engine's messages** (13f-3). What `gossia-calc`, `gossia-core`, `gossia-std`, `gossia-layout`, `gossia-report` and `gossia-user` say is a `tr!` text too: the explanation after an error code, a refused edit, a warning when a file is read, a data source's answer to Test, the descriptions of the built-in functions and the allowed modules. About 1,000 texts, drafted in the five languages like the rest. What stays English: error codes, Python's own exception text, keywords and names a formula writes (`execute()`, `RAW`, `maxOccurs`, `COMP-3`), the JSON and XSD type names a message names (`array`, `dateTime`, so the French says *une valeur de type array*), and the keys of the trust digest, which are data. A text in a constant table that is shown later is marked with `tr_noop!` (the English itself) and translated with `tr` where it is shown; the completeness test reads `tr_noop!` as it reads `tr!`.
- **Whole sentences in the engine too.** Where a message was put together from an English word (*a {noun} name*, *the {which} of*, *{took}* as " in 12 ms", "a boolean"), it became whole texts or a translated phrase: *tab name*, *module name* and so on as one phrase, *the cost of…* and *the price of…* as separate texts, *answered {status}* with and without *in {ms} ms*. The English wording did not change, so the English tests stand.
- **Which language the engine speaks.** On the desktop the evaluation thread reads the program's language, so a change in Options reaches the next message at once. The worker of the web version is a thread of its own: its settings (`EngineConfig`) carry the language's tag, the worker sets it on `Configure`, and a language changed in Options sends the settings again.
- **VS Code** (13f-4). **The language** is VS Code's display language (`vscode.env.language`, given to the panel and answered by the host's optional `language()`), which the interface follows unless the Options choose another; the web build now sets the language at the start as the desktop's `main` does. **The manifest's texts** (the description, the editor's name and the commands' titles) are `%keys%` in `package.json`, with `package.nls.json` in English and `package.nls.<locale>.json` for VS Code's locales `fr`, `es`, `pt-br`, `ja` and `zh-cn`; the titles are the window's own words for the same commands. **The texts of `extension.js`** (its messages, the dialogs' filters, the module picker, and the panel's *Loading Gossia...* and *Gossia did not start*) go through `vscode.l10n.t`, in `l10n/bundle.l10n.<locale>.json`; the three HTTP failures use the engine's wording. The refusals of `run_command` (`commands.rs`), which the Command Palette shows, became `tr!` texts too, with a few the window had missed.
- **The font for Japanese and Chinese in VS Code** (13f-4). A web view cannot read the system's fonts, so the extension ships **Noto Sans JP** (9.6 MB) and **Noto Sans SC** (17.8 MB), variable fonts under the OFL, from the Google Fonts repository at the bundled fonts' commit, in `media/fonts` beside the wasm, not in it. `cargo xtask vscode` fetches them with `curl` into `target/fonts` the first time, checks each against its SHA-256, and copies them in (they are not in git). The host's `loadFont(tag)` fetches the one wanted when the language first needs it and hands it to `font_loaded` (or why not to `font_failed`); until it comes the window keeps its fonts, and one that fails says so in the notice. The window asks the host before the system (`Host::load_font`; a host without fonts leaves the system's), keeps what came, so choosing the language again loads nothing, and sets the weight axis to 400, since these fonts default to Thin. The `.vsix` grows by the fonts; step 13g makes the wasm smaller.
- **Tests.** `gossia-i18n`: tags, places, counts and lists, the files parsing, and the completeness test. `gossia-calc`: the worker taking the language from its settings, and the reasons a name is refused in French and Japanese. `gossia-user`: the language kept and a tag not known. `tests/languages.rs`: French chosen in Options shown at once in the menus and the dialog, kept on OK, and a previewed Spanish taken back by Cancel; Japanese and Chinese given the system's font after Inter (checked on Windows), and English taking it away. The Options dialog in Japanese was looked at by eye with Yu Gothic. 13f-4: with the fake host, the host's font for Japanese asked for once, waited for, used, kept for a second time, and one that fails named in the notice in Chinese; a host without fonts leaving the system's; the extension's texts in every language (the `%keys%` of `package.json` against `package.nls.json` and each locale's, every `vscode.l10n.t` text in each bundle, the same `{0}` places), and its commands' titles, through `package.nls.json`, against `COMMANDS`. In VS Code (`test/suite.js`, now 22 checks): the language VS Code's, and Japanese chosen through `set_option` (`appearance.language`, a tag or `system`) loading its font from beside the wasm, as `status` says (`language`, `cjk_font`), and going again with the system's language.

**Decisions made for printing** (step 13e, 2026-11-06).
- **Opened, not printed blind** (owner, 2026-11-06). On the owner's laptop PDFs open in Chrome, which registers no *print* action for them, so Windows has nothing to hand a file to print; the default printer there is also *Microsoft Print to PDF*, which would ask where to save. So *Print...* opens the PDF in the program the system opens PDFs with, and the printer, copies and pages are chosen in that program's print dialog (Ctrl+P). It keeps the PDF's vector quality and works with any PDF program. Gossia's own print dialog (printing each page drawn at 300 dpi through Windows' printing, `lp` on Linux) and the *print* action where a program offers one were the choices not taken.
- **The file** is `<report>.pdf`, so the PDF program shows the report's name, in a folder of its own (`gossia-print/<milliseconds>-<process>`) under the system's temporary folder; folders there older than a day are deleted at the next print. It is opened with `explorer` on Windows (a GUI program, so no console window flashes, as `cmd /c start` would make), `open` on macOS and `xdg-open` elsewhere, collected by a thread when it ends.
- **In the application**, *Print...* is after *Export as HTML...* on the report tab's menu and in the File menu (greyed unless a report is shown). Its dialog is the export's, titled *Print <report>*, saying that the PDF opens in the PDF program to print from, with a *Print...* button and no file to choose; it waits for the calculation and the parameters as an export does, and a failure to write or open the file is shown in it. In a host (VS Code, the web page) the item is not there, and *Print...* reached otherwise says to export and print from the program that opens the file. VS Code's Command Palette has no print command.
- **The control channel** has `print` (`report`, the tab shown if left out; `open`, true by default): the report is shown, waited for, written and opened as the dialog does, and the answer is the file. With `open` false the file is only noted (`Outside::Record`), as the tests do.
- **Tests.** A print through the dialog writing the report's PDF, named after it, noted as opened and read back; `print` on the control channel with `open` false, in a new folder, putting the opener back; a print path of its own each time under `gossia-print`, and old folders deleted; and *Print...* refused in a host.

**Decisions made for HTML export** (step 13d, 2026-11-06).
- **The crate.** The HTML is written by `gossia-report` too (`html::single_page(&Laid, title)`, `html::pages(&Paged, title)`, and `workbook::html(calc, name)`), from the same layout as the PDF; the PDF and the HTML share the laying out (`workbook::lay_out`). The colours are the PDF's (`paper_colour`): the light theme on white paper.
- **Each page is an inline SVG in points** (`viewBox` in points, `width` and `height` in `pt`), not positioned HTML elements: SVG puts text on the baseline the layout measured, where CSS would place it by its line box. Fills and borders are `rect`s (a border inside its rectangle, as the window draws it), lines are `line`s, and the pending marker is left out, as in the PDF. On screen the pages are white sheets with a shadow on grey, at most as wide as the window; printed, each page of a multipage report goes on a sheet of its own (`@page` with the report's paper and no margin, `break-after: page`). A single page report has no `@page`: it is as tall as it is, as its PDF is.
- **Text stays text** (`<text>`, with `white-space: pre`), so it can be selected, searched and read aloud, and each page has an `aria-label` (the title, and "page 2 of 3"). Each line of more than one character is given the width the layout measured (`textLength`, `lengthAdjust="spacing"`), so the browser's shaping and kerning cannot move where a line ends or a right-aligned number starts. Bold is weight 700 of the variable font; italic is slanted by the PDF's transform (a quarter of the height), not the browser's oblique.
- **Fonts.** The bundled fonts that a line of the report was shaped with are embedded, whole, as `data:` URLs (base64, the `base64` crate the workspace already has), under names of their own (`gossia-0` for Inter, in the order of `fonts::FILES`) so that a font of the reader's system is never taken for them; a line's `font-family` is its typeface's chain, as far as those are embedded. Fonts are not subset: the PDF's subsetter (krilla's `subsetter`) writes fonts for PDF, without the tables a browser needs. So a report in Inter is about 1.2 MB (Inter is 0.9 MB, a third more as base64); subsetting is left for later, if the size matters.
- **Charts** are parsed with usvg, with the options and the chart's style sheet the window renders with, and **written again by usvg** (`Tree::to_string`), placed in their box by a nested `svg` with the chart's `viewBox` (fitted keeping its proportions and centred, as on screen and in the PDF). What usvg writes has only shapes, gradients, clip paths, filters and embedded images, with text as paths in the application's fonts: whatever the chart function returned, there are no scripts, event handlers, links, `foreignObject` or outside files. Each chart's ids are given a prefix of its own (`chart1-`), so that two charts' gradients cannot be mixed up. SVG that does not parse draws nothing: the layout has already put its error there.
- **No scripts.** Besides what it holds, the page has a content security policy that allows only its own styles, `data:` fonts and `data:` images (`default-src 'none'`), and a `generator` meta tag with Gossia's version.
- **In the application**, *Export as HTML...* is beside *Export as PDF...* on the report tab's menu and in the File menu (greyed unless a report is shown), and is *Gossia: Export as HTML...* in VS Code's Command Palette. It is the PDF's dialog and flow with the other format (`ReportFormat` on the export: `Browsing::ExportHtml`, the `.html` extension added to a name without one, the dialog's text saying what the HTML holds); in a host the file is saved through the host, as the PDF is.
- **The control channel** has `export_html`, as `export_pdf`.
- **Tests.** In `gossia-report` (`html_tests.rs`): a single page with its text as text (escaped), the measured widths, a half-transparent fill, a line and a border, only Inter embedded, no `@page` and no script; a multipage report with a page each, `@page` and the page labels, and one with no pages; each typeface embedding only its own font, and italic slanted; a chart with a script, an event handler, a link, an outside image and text written again without any of them, fitted, with ids of its own; and a chart that does not parse. In `tests/report_pdf.rs`: a report exported through the dialog (without an extension, given `.html`) and read back, and `export_html` on the control channel writing the same file. The charts report was also looked at in Chrome by eye against the window.

**Decisions made for `bidwillii report`** (step 12i-1, 2026-11-04).
- **The crates.** `bidwillii` is a crate of its own with the program and a library (`run(args, stdout, stderr)`), so the tests can run either. What it shares with the application without egui moved out of `gossia-app`: the user's preferences, trust record and data sources and the `.gossia` zip (`prefs.rs` and `file.rs`) are the crate `gossia-user`, which the application re-exports as `crate::prefs` and `crate::file` (the theme of the title bar, the one egui type there, became `style::window_theme`); the values of a report read from the workbook (`CalcSource`) and the whole report as a PDF are `gossia_report::workbook` (`pdf`, and `run`, which also gives the error values and whether the detail limit was reached).
- **A run.** The workbook is opened as the window opens it: this user's configuration (`GOSSIA_CONFIG_DIR` too), the workbook's settings held down by the user's ceilings, with a warning on stderr for each, and the limits of reports applied. It is trusted when it has no executable content, when this user's trust record trusts it, or when `--trust-digest` equals its digest (case ignored); otherwise nothing runs. The report starts afresh (`Calc::forget_report_run`: no results cache, no remembered values), is shown so its expressions run, and is waited for until idle; then the parameters given are chosen (their lists are known by then; lists do not see `params`) and it is waited for again. The deadline is one, from the start, for the whole run.
- **Parameter values** are read as the design says. My choices: a `choice` or `choices` value is matched against each value written as the command line writes values (`param_text` in `gossia-core`: a number as Python writes it, yes/no, a date as `2026-10-31`); a value given twice for a parameter that is not `choices` is an error; a period may not end before it starts. `--list-params` prints each parameter as `name (Kind, required) "Label"`, then `default:` (or *none*, or why it cannot be used), the presets of a period and the `choices:` with their labels in brackets.
- **Error values** are the controls the layout was given an error for (an expression that failed, or a value that is an error value), noted while it lays the report out, so a control that is not visible is not listed. Each is listed once on stderr as `band, control, column: the first line of the error`, the control named by its kind and what it shows (`Field Orders.total`).
- **Output.** The PDF goes to the absolute path of `--out` (given `.pdf` without an extension), written atomically; an existing file is code 1 without `--overwrite`, as is a folder that does not exist. These are checked before the workbook is opened. `--option=value` reads as `--option value`.
- **Tests** (`crates/gossia-cli/tests/cli.rs`, running the program): each exit code (a trusted report written with its path on stdout and the workbook's bytes unchanged; the trust record and the digest; a wrong digest; the usage; an existing file; a missing report and workbook; each kind of parameter value; a remembered value not used; each parameter error; a preset; `--list-params`; error values listed with code 5; the timeout and the detail limit with code 6, writing nothing). `report_tab_tests.rs` checks *Copy batch command* and the quoting of words.

**Decisions made for packing and CI of the extension** (step 13c-3, 2026-11-06).
- **`cargo xtask vsix`** packs the extension with `packaging/vscode/pack-vsix.py` (until 2026-11-07 with `vsce`) into `target/vsix/gossia-<version>.vsix`: the release build of the wasm version copied into `media/pkg`, the repository's `LICENSE` and the icon at 128 pixels (drawn by the window's code, `xtask icons`) put beside `package.json` for the packing and not kept in the repository. It refuses when `package.json`'s version is not the workspace's. `.vscodeignore` leaves out the tests. The script writes the zip as `vsce` did (the files under `extension/`, `extension.vsixmanifest` from `package.json` with the description from `package.nls.json`, `[Content_Types].xml`, the licence as `LICENSE.txt`); its package held the same files, byte for byte, as `vsce`'s, and installs. The extension has a `README.md` of its own for its page in VS Code's extension view. The first `.vsix` (0.3.0, 41 MB, of which the wasm is 88.8 MB unpacked: the application has grown since 13a's 55.5 MB, which 13g is to cut) installs into a fresh VS Code profile.
- **Published** to the Gitea generic package registry beside the MSIX (package `gossia`, owner `ward`, `…/api/packages/ward/generic/gossia/<version>/gossia-<version>.vsix`), replacing a file of the same version, by `packaging/vscode/publish-vsix.sh` (the upload itself is `packaging/publish-generic.sh`, shared with the MSI): with `PACKAGE_REGISTRY_TOKEN` in CI, or by hand with the login Git's credential helper holds for the server. 0.3.0 was first published from this machine on 2026-11-06.
- **The CI step *VS Code*** (`.gitea/workflows/ci.yml`, on the Gitea mirror's runner, advisory like *Web*) runs `cargo xtask vscode-test` under `xvfb-run` with stable VS Code (`GOSSIA_VSCODE_VERSION=stable`, kept in `target/vscode-test`), then packs the `.vsix` and publishes it with the secret `PACKAGE_REGISTRY_TOKEN` (the server is the job's own Gitea); without it it is packed but not published. *(Until 2026-11-07 this was a job on GitHub's self-hosted runner with Node.js from `actions/setup-node`.)*

**Decisions made for modules and commands in VS Code** (step 13c-2, 2026-11-06).
- **Modules are `gossia:` files** (owner's choice, 2026-11-06, of VS Code's own Python editor over Gossia's code editor in a second panel). Each open workbook is a folder, named after its file in lower case (VS Code writes an address's authority so) with `~2` after it when another open workbook has that name: `gossia://orders.gossia/Tax.py`. The extension's file system answers reading from what the panel last said; **writing, renaming and deleting** a file ask the panel (`writeModule`, `renameModule`, `removeModule`, each with an id), which makes the change in the workbook as one entry in its history (a new name adds a module) and answers (`moduleAnswer`, with the reason when refused, which VS Code shows). A file that is not `NAME.py` is refused, and so are folders.
- **The panel tells the modules.** A host that edits modules says so by having `openModule` (`Host::edits_modules`); the application then tells it the modules, names and sources, at the start and each time they change (`Host::modules_changed`, compared by a hash each frame), and the extension announces the files that changed, came or went, so an open file follows an undo in the workbook. A file just written is known at once, so VS Code finds it as it wrote it. When the panel closes, its modules' files go.
- **Opening a module** in the code editor's explorer, or at a problem *Compile* lists, opens its file in VS Code's editor beside the workbook, at the line (`Host::open_module`), not in a tab of the code editor; the code editor opens no module by itself there. The test page has no `openModule`, so it keeps the code editor's tabs.
- **The Command Palette.** `gossia_app::commands::COMMANDS` names Gossia's commands (New Tab, Import, Export, Export as PDF, Export as HTML (added with 13d), Undo, Redo, Recalculate, Code Editor, Data Sources, Disconnect from Data Sources, Options, and showing or hiding the toolbar, the issues panel and the node inspector); `GossiaApp::run_command` runs one as its menu item does, refused with the reason where the item is greyed (the window's notice says it). `package.json` lists them as `gossia.<name>` under the category *Gossia*, shown when a workbook or one of its modules is active, and a test checks that the two lists agree. The extension runs a command in the workbook whose module is being edited, or else whose panel was active last, and shows that panel. **Gossia: Open Module...** lists the workbook's modules and opens the one chosen. The extension also starts for the `gossia:` file system, so that module files VS Code reopens are found.
- **Tests.** Natively with the fake host (`host_tests.rs`): the modules told once and again after each change, written, added, renamed, removed and refused, an undo told; a module opened in the host's editor and not in a tab; the commands run and refused; and the extension's list against `COMMANDS`. In VS Code (`test/suite.js`, now 19 checks): *Gossia: Code Editor*; a module written as a `gossia:` file and called by a formula; opened from Gossia (`open_module`) in VS Code's editor as Python; edited and saved there, and the formula following; *Gossia: Undo* taking it back, with the open file following; a file with no module name refused.

**Decisions made for the VS Code extension** (step 13c-1, 2026-11-05).
- **Where it is.** `vscode/` in the repository: `package.json`, `extension.js` (the extension host's side), `media/panel.js` (the panel's page and its host object), `media/worker.js` (the worker's text) and `test/`. Plain JavaScript with no build step, as the harness. `media/pkg` holds the wasm module and its glue, copied in by **`cargo xtask vscode`** (with `--test-control`, the test build) and not kept in the repository.
- **A custom editor** (`gossia.workbook`, for `*.gossia`, the default editor), with `retainContextWhenHidden` so that the application keeps its state while its tab is hidden, and one editor per file. A new empty file, or an untitled one, opens as a blank workbook; a backup opens in place of the file.
- **VS Code saves the file.** Save, Save As and backups (hot exit) ask the panel for the workbook's bytes (`request_bytes`, `save` or `backup`), which the page gives once nothing is being calculated, since it cannot wait within a frame: `save` counts the workbook as saved (`saveWorkbook`), `backup` does not (`backupWorkbook`, from `GossiaApp::backup_bytes`), and a failure is given back (`bytesFailed`) so that VS Code reports it. Revert reads the file and gives it to the panel (`open_workbook`). The application's own Save (its menu, Ctrl+S) asks VS Code to save, so it is VS Code's Save. **Undo stays the application's**, so VS Code is only told that the workbook changed (a content change, not an edit with its own undo); it clears the mark when it saves or reverts, and an undo back to the saved state leaves the mark on.
- **The page.** The extension writes the panel's page with a content security policy (scripts by nonce, `blob:` and `wasm-unsafe-eval`; workers from `blob:`; fetches only from the extension's own files), puts `panel.js` in it, and passes the addresses of the module and its glue. The page fetches and compiles the wasm module once, imports the glue from a blob, and makes the worker from the glue followed by `worker.js`, sending it the compiled module as its first message; so both load what was fetched once, from the web view's own origin.
- **Through the extension host:** storage in `globalState` (`gossia:` before each name) and secrets in `SecretStorage` (with the list of their names in `globalState`, since it cannot list them), both given to the page with the workbook before it starts and then answered there at once and written through; VS Code's open and save dialogs, starting in the workbook's folder; opening something outside (`env.openExternal`); and **HTTP requests**, made with Node.js's `fetch` without following a redirect, the body read up to the limit and the time limit applied, so CORS does not apply. The theme is the kind of VS Code's theme, from the classes VS Code puts on the page's body, looked at each frame.
- **Bytes in messages.** A file VS Code reads is a Node.js `Buffer`, a view into a larger buffer, which did not reach the page as bytes (Revert opened a blank workbook); the extension copies bytes into a plain `Uint8Array` before sending them, and the page makes sure of a `Uint8Array` on its side too.
- **Tests** (`test/run.py`, `test/suite.js`; **`cargo xtask vscode-test`** builds the test build into the extension and runs them; `run.py` replaced `run.js` and `@vscode/test-electron` on 2026-11-07, downloading VS Code from its update server and starting it with the suite itself) in a downloaded VS Code Insiders, which runs beside an open VS Code, with a fresh profile and extensions folder; the download is kept in `target/vscode-test` and used again. With `GOSSIA_VSCODE_TEST` set the extension adds test commands: wait for the panel to start, run a command of the test control protocol in it, and read and write its storage. The suite opens a new empty file (a blank workbook), drops the order and calculates a formula in the worker, sees VS Code's unsaved mark, reads an HTTP source from a server on this machine that sends no CORS headers, saves with VS Code's Save (a zip in the file, the mark cleared, the trust record in `globalState`), reverts an edit, and closes and opens the file again (trusted, the formula saved and calculated again): 13 checks. Natively, `backup_bytes` leaves the workbook unsaved (`host_tests.rs`).
- **Not done yet:** dropping files on the panel was not tried in VS Code. *(Gossia's commands and modules in VS Code's editor came with 13c-2; the CI job and the `.vsix` with 13c-3.)*

**Decisions made for the harness page** (step 13b-4, 2026-11-05).
- **The test build's function.** With the `test-control` feature, `gossia-web` exports `control(cmd, args)` (`control.rs`): the command and its `args` as JSON text, answered by a promise that gives the result as JSON text or fails with the error. The page wraps it as `window.gossiaControl(cmd, args)`, which gives and takes plain objects; the page imports the module as a namespace and looks for `control`, so a release build, which has none, still loads. A command waits for the next frame and runs `gossia_app::control::run` on the application, as the desktop's channel does. The page cannot block, so **`wait_idle` is asked again each frame** until the workbook is idle or its time (at most 120 s, as on the desktop) is up. `quit` and `screenshot` are refused in the page: it has no window of its own, and the Playwright script takes the screenshots.
- **Two commands without files.** `drop` also takes `files`, each a `name` and a `text`, and drops them as the browser does (as files picked for `drop`, so a workbook is refused the same way); `save` gives the bytes to the host when there is one, as Ctrl+S does. A new workbook is now offered to the host as `Untitled.gossia`, not `Untitled` (the harness found it), and keeps that name for the next save.
- **The harness** is in `crates/gossia-web/harness/`, apart from the page so that release builds never carry it: `harness.html`, `harness.js` (the helpers, after keyscarf's `tools/web-test-harness`), a file per suite, and `fixture.json`. It opens the application in tabs of its own with `window.open` and clears Gossia's storage for the origin first. **Edit and recalculate** is the desktop's shared scenario (`tests/common/mod.rs`) command for command, the file dropped as text. **The test host** saves through the host (a zip named as a workbook, the unsaved mark set and cleared), keeps the preferences and the trust record in the host's storage, reads an HTTP source answered by the test (run again when the answer comes, and what depends on it), reads `fixture.json` from the page's own server, gets `#SOURCE!` for a 404, and opens the saved bytes in a new tab (a blob URL given to `?open=`), trusted from the record the save wrote and calculated again. `?auto` runs everything on load and leaves the result in `window.harnessDone`.
- **The Playwright script is Python** (`harness/run.py`), not Node.js: no `package.json`, as the harness has no npm. It serves the folder on a loopback port itself (with the type of a `.wasm` file set), opens `harness.html?auto` in headless Chromium with SwiftShader for WebGL, prints the harness's log and errors from the application's tabs, saves screenshots of the open tabs on a failure, and exits with 0 only when everything passed.
- **`cargo xtask web --test-control`** builds the test build in a target folder of its own (`target/web-test-build`, so it does not replace the release build) into `target/web-test`, with the harness beside the page; **`cargo xtask web-test`** builds it and runs the script (`GOSSIA_PYTHON` names the Python). The CI job **Web** runs it, advisory, with Playwright and its Chromium in a virtual environment under `~/.cache/gossia-playwright`, and keeps the screenshots of a failed run.
- **Checked:** both suites pass in headless Chromium on Windows (45 checks), and natively the `drop` with `files` (`ui.rs`) and the name given to the host (`host_tests.rs`).

**Decisions made for HTTP through the host** (step 13b-3, 2026-11-05).
- **The worker builds the request, the host sends it.** The worker has the definitions with their secrets, so it makes the whole request (`http::FetchRequest`: method, URL, headers with the authentication, body, timeout, maximum size) with the same code as the desktop (`http::prepare`), and the host only sends it, without following a redirect, and answers with the status, reason, headers and body, or with why there is none. The host interface gains `fetch(id, method, url, headers, body, timeoutMs, maxBytes)`, answered through `fetch_answered` or `fetch_failed`. The test page uses the browser's `fetch`, so a site has to allow the page's origin there (its own server always does); VS Code's extension host has no such limit. An answer larger than the maximum is refused by the host, and again by the worker.
- **Stopping.** In the worker every call goes through the store of answers (`crate::fetches`). A call without its answer asks for it once and sets the engine's *waiting* flag, and the prelude raises `_WaitForAnswer`, a `BaseException`, so `except Exception` and `IFERROR` do not catch it. Whatever Python then does, the flag decides: the result is not kept.
- **Formulas.** The evaluator asks `Engine::waiting` after each formula. A formula that waits is set aside (`St::Waiting`) without an outcome, and so is a formula whose first out-of-date read is one that waits, and an expression of a control that waits or reads one. They all stay out of date, with the pending mark and their old values. The worker sends the requests (`FromWorker::Fetch`) before the evaluation's `Done`. The page counts the evaluator busy until every answer has come, sends them together (`ToWorker::Answers`), and the next evaluation runs what was set aside. Answers are found by the whole request for every round of a pass, and forgotten when a pass asks for nothing more, so the next pass, F9 included, reads afresh.
- **Procedures and the Immediate window** wait in the worker: the job is kept, nothing is sent back but the requests, and when the answers come it runs again from the start with the same snapshot. Their calls are logged in order with their answers, so each call made before gets its logged answer and a write is sent once. A different call at a logged place fails (*this run called ... where it called something else before it stopped to wait for an answer, so nothing more is sent*). The log is cleared when the run ends. While the worker waits, the page does not count time against the stuck formula, so the time waited is not counted, as on the desktop.
- **The ids.** The worker numbers its requests; the page gives each one an id of its own for the host, and drops answers to a worker that has been started again or configured again (formulas that waited then ask again). `sources.check` in the worker asks the host for the Test's request as well. The panel's Test of an HTTP source goes to the host straight from the page (`host::fetch`, with the time it took). Without a host it still runs on a thread of its own, and in the wasm version the other kinds are not available.
- **Checked** natively: the store of answers (`fetches_tests.rs`: asked once, the rounds of a pass, the size limit, the log in order, another path refused); through the encoding with the real engine (`session_web_tests.rs`: a formula with two calls one after the other, which runs three times, and a formula that reads it waiting with it; a failed request as `#SOURCE!`; F9 reading again; an Immediate expression with a `POST` and a `GET`, the `POST` sent once); and the panel's Test through a fake host (`host_tests.rs`). In headless Chrome on the test page, a `GET` of a file on the page's server from the Immediate window gave its value, and a `POST` and a `GET` answered by the test gave `[201, 'fresh']` with the `POST` asked once.
- **Also:** the YAML writer now quotes a key `<<`, which a reader takes for a merge key. The property test found it (its case is kept in `proptest-regressions/yaml_tests.txt`).

**Decisions made for data sources in a host** (step 13b-2c, 2026-11-05).
- **Where they are kept.** The definitions are in the host's storage under `data-sources.json`, as that file holds them on the desktop but without their secrets; each definition's password and token are a secret of their own, `source:<name>`, as JSON, through two calls more on the host (`read_secret`, `write_secret`, `null` removing one). A host whose secret store answers later, as VS Code's does, reads the secrets before the application starts and answers from what it read. The test page keeps secrets in `localStorage` apart from the rest (`gossia-secret:`); it is no keychain, and needs none.
- **One store in the page, a copy in the worker.** The page's `SourceStore` is the user's, read at the start (`host::load_sources`). The store counts its changes (`SourceStore::version`), and each frame `sync_sources` keeps the definitions in the host when the count has moved, writes or removes the secrets, and gives the worker the new definitions (it is configured again; its modules load again with the next job).
- **Changes made in the worker come back.** `sources.define` in the Immediate window runs in the worker, on its copy. The protocol's worker side is now `wire::Worker` (used by the page's worker and the tests' fake alike): after a job that changed its definitions it sends `FromWorker::Sources` with all of them, before the job's `Done`, and the page puts them in its store, which the host then keeps.
- **Checked** natively: a source defined in the worker reaches the page's store (`session_web_tests.rs`), and a definition with a token is kept with the token only among the secrets, read back with it at the next start, and its secret removed with it (`host_tests.rs`). The Test button and reading a source in the browser come with 13b-3, since HTTP goes through the host.

**Decisions made for files in a host** (step 13b-2b, 2026-11-05).
- **Two calls more on the host**: `pick_file(purpose, extensions)` asks for a file to read with the host's own dialog, and the file comes back later through `host::picked` with the same purpose (nothing comes back on Cancel); `save_file(name, bytes)` asks where to save and writes there, the host saying whether it was written. The application takes what came each frame (`receive_picked`). The purposes are `import`, `drop` and `import-module`.
- **What goes through them**: File > Import (any file, since the format is told from the name and the text, and the host's filter would only hide formats the desktop's filter lists too narrowly), Export (the tab's file and, when asked, the schema's: two saves, so two dialogs), Export as PDF (the save is asked for once the report is calculated and the PDF made), and the code editor's Import module and Export module. Opening a workbook is the host's (13b-2a).
- **Dropped files** are read by the browser later (egui gives no bytes at once on the web), so each comes back as a file picked for `drop`: imported as on the desktop, except a workbook, which says to open it from outside instead of opening it. A copybook's `COPY` members are not looked for, since a file from the host has no folder.
- **The test host** uses the browser's file chooser and downloads; with `testHost.answerPicks` set, it records the requests instead and `testHost.answerPick(name, text)` answers the last one, for the tests.
- **Checked** natively with a fake host (`host_tests.rs`: import, drops, export with its schema, modules both ways, save) and in headless Chrome by clicking File > Import (the CSV given back opens the import dialog) and File > Export (the host is given `Orders.json`).

**Decisions made for the application in the page** (step 13b-2a, 2026-11-05).
- **One wasm module for the page and the worker.** The page needs the formula parser, and with it most of the Python side, so a module of its own for the worker would mostly repeat it. `gossia-worker` became `gossia-web`: `WorkerSide` (the worker), `start_app` and `worker_message` (the page, `page.rs`), and the protocol's probe. With the application in it the release wasm is 92.7 MB (it was 58 MB with the worker alone); size is not a concern in VS Code (see above).
- **The host interface** is the trait `gossia_app::host::Host`: a link to a new worker, saving the workbook, the unsaved-changes mark, storage, opening something outside, and whether the theme is dark. A page shows one application, so the host is set once by the entry point and found through `host::current()`, rather than passed through the application; the desktop has none, and everything it changes is behind `if let Some(host)`. `host::evaluator` and `host::reconfigure` make and change the evaluator either way (a thread on the desktop, the worker in a host).
- **The JavaScript host object** has `startWorker`, `postWorker`, `saveWorkbook(name, bytes)`, `setDirty`, `readStorage`, `writeStorage`, `openOutside` and `dark`; what the worker sends goes to `worker_message`, which also asks for a frame. The test host in `index.html` keeps storage in `localStorage` (`gossia:` before each name), keeps each save for the tests to look at (`window.testHost`), and takes the theme from `prefers-color-scheme`. `?open=<file>` opens a workbook served beside the page and `?sample` the sample workbook (the repository keeps no `.gossia` files).
- **The files of the preferences in the host's storage.** `Config` has a `Storage` (read and write a text by name) in place of its folder, so the preferences, the trust record and the Immediate window's history are the same JSON texts under the same names; the data source definitions stay in memory until 13b-2c.
- **What a host leaves out.** File > New, Open, the recent files, Save As and Quit, the toolbar's New and Open, and Ctrl+O, Ctrl+N and Ctrl+Shift+S: the host opens, makes and names files. Save (the menu, the toolbar and Ctrl+S) gives the bytes to the host. On the Evaluation page the abandoned-threads limit and *Save and restart* are left out. *System* theme follows the host's `dark()`, looked at each frame, since VS Code's theme can change at any time.
- **The clipboard** of the cells in a host is the application's own, in the host's storage (`clipboard`), apart from the system's (owner, 2026-11-05, as in the owner's other web projects): Copy writes it, Paste and Ctrl+V read it. Text boxes keep the system clipboard egui gives them.
