- **Clocks.** `std::time::Instant` and `SystemTime` panic in the browser, so the application and `gossia-user` use `web-time`, as `gossia-calc` does (the first start failed on it). A panic in the page shows as `unreachable`; the page's `window.lastPanic()` gives its message.
- **Checked** in headless Chrome: the sample workbook opens and is calculated in the worker (one worker started), Ctrl+S gives the host a workbook file, the trust record is written to storage, and the dark theme follows the page's colour scheme. The wasm lint covers `gossia-user`, `gossia-app` and `gossia-web` too. Saving when VS Code asks for it (rather than on Ctrl+S) belongs to 13c.

**Decisions made for the worker protocol** (step 13b-1, 2026-11-05).
- **The messages are the evaluation thread's own.** `gossia_calc::wire` has `Job` (evaluate a snapshot within the pass limit, run a procedure, run an Immediate expression) and `Done` (an `Evaluation`, a `ProcedureResult`, an `ImmediateResult`), the same as on the desktop, so the page applies a result with the same code. Around them, `ToWorker` is `Configure` or `Job`, and `FromWorker` is `Started(loc)` or `Done`. `wire::run_reporting` runs a job and reports each formula as it starts; the desktop thread keeps its own loop.
- **A whole snapshot goes with every job**, encoded with serde. That needed serde on the types a snapshot and a result carry, in `gossia-core` (values, paths, schemas with their XML and copybook parts, data formats) and in `gossia-calc`. Two are written by hand: a **node is read back with a fresh id**, since each process numbers its own nodes and ids are never saved, and a **pattern travels as its text** and is compiled again. Sending only what changed since the last job is left until a workbook shows the need.
- **The encoding is MessagePack** (`rmp-serde`) with field names, not a positional format: some form and report types leave out empty fields when they are saved as JSON, which throws a positional reader out of step.
- **The engine's settings** travel as `EngineConfig` (the range limit, the response limits, the formula limit and the data source definitions as a list, kept in memory in the worker). `Configure` is sent to each new worker before its first job, and again when the settings change; the worker then makes a new engine, so the modules load again.
- **The evaluator of the wasm version** (`session_web.rs`) has the methods of the desktop's, so `Session` is shared. It reaches the worker through a `WorkerLink` (start a fresh worker, post bytes, take what came back), which the page's JavaScript provides, so `gossia-calc` needs no JavaScript. A worker is started with the first job. A formula that runs past its limit and the grace, counted from the worker's `Started` message, is given up by **starting a fresh worker**: the formula gets `#TIMEOUT!` saying it *was ended*, a procedure or Immediate expression fails the same way, and the next pass runs on the new worker. Nothing is ever abandoned, so evaluation is never suspended. The desktop's evaluator moved into a module of its own, compiled only on the desktop; the wasm one is compiled everywhere so that its tests run with the others.
- **The page cannot wait**: answers from the worker arrive only between frames, so on wasm `Evaluator::wait` only takes what has come back and `Session::wait_idle` ticks once and says whether the workbook is idle. Saving, which waits for evaluation on the desktop, has to wait across frames in the wasm version (13b-2).
- **Clocks.** `std::time::Instant` panics on wasm, so `gossia-calc` uses `web-time` (the standard one on the desktop, the browser's elsewhere), and `jiff` has its `js` feature on wasm for the current time and time zone.
- **Tests.** Natively: five tests that a job sent through the encoding and run gives what running it directly gives (formulas across tabs with a module, an edit afterwards, a procedure's writes, an Immediate expression, fresh node ids, bytes that are not a message), and four of the wasm evaluator with a fake link that runs each job at once with the real engine (a worker started and configured by the first job, a second job refused while one runs, a stuck formula given up with a fresh worker, new settings reaching an idle worker). In the browser, `gossia-worker` has `WorkerSide` (the worker) and `PageProbe` (a workbook in a real `Session` on the page, with a JavaScript link), and the probe page checks nine things in headless Chrome: a first pass of 0.6 s including starting the worker, an edit recalculated in 11 ms, a formula that never stops ended after 1.27 s (a limit of 1 s and a quarter of it as grace), and the fresh worker loading the modules and calculating in 0.3 s. The page loads the same wasm as the worker for now; whether the application's page and the worker share one module is for 13b-2.

**Decisions made for the engine on wasm** (step 13a, 2026-11-05).
- **The target decides, not a feature.** What the wasm version lacks is left out with `cfg(target_family = "wasm")`, so there is no feature to forget. In `gossia-calc` the drivers (`ureq`, `rusqlite`, `postgres`, `mysql`, the TLS crates, `beancount-parser`) are dependencies for other targets only. On wasm the modules `sqlite`, `database` and `journal` are replaced by stand-ins (`unavailable.rs`) with the same functions, which fail with *Orders: SQLite sources are not available here* and whose Test says the same, as Data sources specifies. HTTP keeps its request building (`http.rs`); only the sending with `ureq` and the Test (`http_send.rs`) are native, and on wasm `send` fails with *HTTP sources are not available here yet* until stop and run again comes with 13b (it came with 13b-3: requests go through the host).
- **No watchdog on wasm.** The watchdog's thread cannot start there (no threads), so on wasm `Watchdog` does nothing, and a formula past its time is the page's to end by terminating the worker. `std::time::Instant` would panic on wasm too; with the watchdog gone, nothing in a recalculation reached it in the probe, but the time budgets (`#TIMEOUT!` on the desktop) still need a clock from the host in 13b.
- **Random numbers** come from the browser: `getrandom` 0.4 with `wasm_js` (RustPython) and 0.2 with `js` (`rand` 0.8 below it), as dependencies of `gossia-calc` on wasm only, as RustPython's own wasm crate does.
- **`gossia-worker`** is a new crate, a `cdylib` with `wasm-bindgen`. In 13a it has only a probe (`Probe::new` starts the interpreter, `Probe::run(schema, data, path, formula)` makes a one-tab workbook, sets the formula, recalculates and answers with the value or the error as JSON). A panic is kept by a panic hook and read with `last_panic()`, because wasm reports it only as `unreachable`. `crates/gossia-worker/web` has the worker script and a probe page that runs eight checks and logs `ok` and `FAIL` lines, as the harness of 13b will.
- **`cargo xtask web`** builds the worker in release, makes the glue with the `wasm-bindgen` command-line tool, and puts it with the probe page in `target/web`, which any static server can serve (`python -m http.server` was used). The tool must be the version in `Cargo.lock`, so the task installs that version into `target/wasm-tools` the first time rather than using whatever is on the machine. `cargo xtask lint` also runs clippy on `gossia-calc` and `gossia-worker` for wasm, and `rust-toolchain.toml` lists the wasm target so that `rustup` installs it on the runners.
- **What the probe measured** (release build, headless Chrome on the development machine, 2026-11-05): loading the wasm 163 ms, starting the interpreter 364 ms, a first formula 16 ms and the next ones 0 to 2 ms; a worker spinning forever was terminated and a fresh one had loaded and answered in about 300 ms. All eight checks passed: a sum, `SUM` over an array, proxy reads, `math` without `import`, text, an error, and the replacement of a runaway worker.
- **Size.** The release wasm is 55.5 MB (28.5 MB with gzip): 31.2 MB of data, mostly the whole Python standard library frozen in by `freeze-stdlib`, 16.7 MB of code, and 7.6 MB of function names. Without the names (`--remove-name-section`) it is 47.9 MB (27.9 MB with gzip). Size is not a concern for an extension installed once (see Web port, 0.3.0), so nothing was cut then; step 13g cuts it (owner, 2026-11-06), to offset the fonts of 13f. The large saving would be to freeze only the modules formulas can reach (the eleven of Names, and what they import) instead of all of `Lib`, which would make the desktop executable smaller as well; `wasm-opt` was not tried. (By 13f-4 it had grown to 98.1 MB, 44.7 MB with gzip; 13g brought it to 45.3 MB, 15.5 MB with gzip.)

**Decisions made for a smaller download** (step 13g, 2026-11-06).
- **Measured** on the release wasm after `wasm-bindgen`, in steps: before, 98.1 MB (44.7 MB with gzip); only the reachable standard library frozen in, 73.4 MB (24.5); only the native modules formulas need, 69.7 MB (23.7); the fonts as statics, 55.8 MB (16.8); without function names, **45.3 MB (15.5)**. Of that, 31 MB is code and 14 MB data. The `.vsix` went from 62.4 MB to 31.5 MB, about half of which is now the Japanese and Chinese fonts. The desktop executable shrinks by the same standard library and native modules; it was not measured.
- **Only what formulas can reach is frozen in.** `gossia-calc/build.rs` starts from the eleven allowed modules of Names, `encodings` (whole: codecs are found by name), and the modules RustPython's own Rust code imports (`locale`, `_pycodecs`, `typing`, `warnings`, `weakref`, `copyreg`, `codecs`, `string.templatelib`). It follows every import a module makes when it is imported, and imports inside functions unless they lead to a tool module (`unittest`, `doctest`, `pydoc`, `argparse` and the like), but nothing under `if __name__ == "__main__"`. Lines are scanned, not parsed, which is enough for `Lib` (including Python 3.15's `lazy import`). Modules that cannot work here (`os`, `posix`, `nt`, `importlib`, and native modules not compiled in, such as `zlib`, `_random`, `pyexpat`) are dead, and so is any module that imports a dead one at import time, until nothing changes. What is left (166 modules, 3.2 MB of source) is copied to `OUT_DIR` and frozen with `py_freeze!`; the build reruns when `Lib` changes. RustPython's own `freeze-stdlib` feature stays on in the vm, because it decides how the interpreter starts, but the `rustpython` crate's frozen `Lib` is no longer used.
- **Only the native modules formulas need are registered**: `formula_module_defs` in RustPython's stdlib crate (binascii, bisect, contextvars, json, math, `_datetime`, `_heapq`, struct, unicodedata, the CJK codecs, suggestions, and `_random` since `statistics` came), added by `vendor-patches/rustpython.patch`, instead of `init_stdlib`'s all.
- **`statistics`** (owner, 2026-11-10: "Python also has some good statistical modules"; numpy cannot run, being C built against CPython). It imports `random`, which imported `os` (missing here) for `urandom` and fork support, so the same patch makes those two imports optional in the vendored `Lib/random.py` (only `SystemRandom` then refuses, with `NotImplementedError`) and registers the native `_random`. `random` itself is not exposed; it is reachable as `statistics.random`, and `NormalDist.samples` without a seed gives other values each run, as any formula calling a random generator would. `tests_more.rs` checks median, mean, stdev, quantiles, linear regression, the normal distribution and the error of an empty list; `tests_frozen.rs` runs a dozen more. `html` was considered with `html.parser` for scraping and dropped (owner, 2026-11-10): the parser is used by subclassing, and classes are not allowed in modules.
- **Internal imports pass the hook.** RustPython's Rust code imports some modules with `vm.import`, which uses the formula's builtins and so the formulas' import hook; those (`INTERNAL_MODULES`: `time`, `locale`, `_pycodecs`, `typing`, `warnings`, `weakref`, `copyreg`, `codecs`, `string.templatelib`) go to Python's own import. This is what made `strftime` work. A formula's `import os` is still refused.
- **Tests:** `tests_frozen.rs` imports every frozen module in an interpreter without the hook, and runs about thirty uses of the allowed modules (formats, codecs by name, `Counter`, `json`, `re`, `textwrap`, ...) through the engine, so a module left out shows as a failing test, not in a workbook. `strptime` is checked to fail with an error.
- **The CJK codecs are kept** (about 1 MB): a file in Shift JIS or GBK is plausible input.
- **The fonts are `static`, not `const`.** A `const` byte string from `include_bytes!` is copied into every codegen unit that uses it, and the wasm linker does not merge the copies: the eight report fonts were in the wasm five or six times (14 MB saved).
- **Function names are left out** of the normal build (`--remove-name-section`); the build for the test harness keeps them, for readable panics.
- **`wasm-opt -Oz` was not adopted**: 40.4 MB, but 15.6 MB with gzip (no better), six minutes, and a 111 MB tool to fetch. **Fat LTO with one codegen unit was not adopted**: 41.8 MB (15.0 MB with gzip) for a release build of eleven minutes instead of four.

**Decisions made for the packages** (step 12i-3, 2026-11-04).
- **The icon as PNG files.** The repository keeps no image files, so `cargo xtask icons <dir>` writes the icon drawn by `app_icon.rs` (included by path, as `build.rs` does) at 16 to 512 pixels, with a PNG writer of its own (stored deflate blocks, nothing but the standard library). The Linux packages take the icon theme's sizes, the MSIX its logos (44, 50 and 150, and the 44-pixel logo's target sizes, plain and unplated).
- **The MSI** builds and installs `bidwillii.exe` beside `gossia.exe`, a component of its own; it is still not put on the `PATH`. Its Start menu shortcut is advertised, so it takes its icon from the MSI's Icon table, not from `gossia.exe`: without one it showed a blank page (found after 0.4.0). `build-msi.sh` writes `gossia.ico` with `cargo xtask ico` (16 to 256 pixels, as PNG images, 280 KB) and the MSI holds that, also as the icon in Add/Remove Programs; holding the executable itself would have added its whole size again.
- **The MSIX** is packed on this Windows machine by `build-msix.ps1`, at the owner's choice, not on Linux CI: it is uploaded to the Store by hand and seldom, MakeAppx is the tool the Store expects, and a packer of our own would be a risk at certification. The version is `major.minor.patch.0`. The package is one application, the window, with the `.gossia` and `.ics` associations; `bidwillii` is reached by the execution alias `bidwillii.exe` on that same application (`desktop4:Subsystem="console"`, which needs `SupportsMultipleInstances`). A second application kept off the Start menu, as first built, is a headless app, which the Store refused at upload (2026-11-07). The package is unsigned: the Store signs it. `-Register` installs the folder it was packed from for this user, with Developer Mode on, to try it before uploading. `-Publish` uploads it to the Gitea generic package registry (package `gossia`, the version, the file name; an earlier file of the same version is removed first), with the login Git Credential Manager holds for `ward` on the Gitea mirror, so the token needs the package write permission (owner, 2026-11-04).
- **The Linux packages** are built by `packaging/linux/build-package.sh deb|rpm|arch` and published by `publish-package.sh` in the workflow `.gitea/workflows/packages.yml`, which only the Gitea mirror runs (GitHub reads `.github/workflows`). One package holds both programs. `cargo-deb` 3.8.0 and `cargo-generate-rpm` 0.21.1 are installed pinned under `~/.cache/gossia-packages`; their settings are in `crates/gossia-app/Cargo.toml`. What is linked (glibc, OpenSSL, fontconfig) the tools find themselves; OpenGL, EGL, xkbcommon, Wayland and X11 are loaded while running, so they are named: by Debian package names, by library names in the `.rpm` (`libGL.so.1()(64bit)`), so that one package suits Fedora and openSUSE, and as `depends` in the `PKGBUILD`, which builds from the checkout and takes the version from the script. The jobs check out with the job's token (the repository is private) into `~/ci/gossia`, keeping `target/` and `vendor/`, and run one after another, since the three runners share one machine. Publishing replaces a package of the same name, version and architecture: the `.deb` under the codename of the Debian that built it and component `main`, the `.rpm` in group `gossia`, the Arch package in repository `gossia`. It needs the repository's Actions secret `PACKAGE_REGISTRY_TOKEN`; without it the packages are built and not published.
- **The window's app id** is `gossia`, so that Linux desktops match the window to `gossia.desktop` for its icon.
- **The MIME type** of a workbook is `application/x-gossia-workbook`, a subclass of `application/zip`, known by the `*.gossia` glob.

**Decisions made for `bidwillii disconnect` and reports in Disconnect from Data Sources** (step 12i-2, 2026-11-04).
- **One report at a time.** A report's expressions run only while it is shown, so the reads are found in rounds (`find_disconnect` in `disconnect.rs`): the formulas and forms first (`Calc::plan_disconnect`), then each report in the order of the tabs, shown and run until idle with the values chosen for its parameters, its own reads planned and probed (`Calc::plan_disconnect_report`). The rounds are merged into one `Disconnect`, and the report shown before is shown again. `apply_and_rerun` makes the changes, then shows each report that changed (and the `--report` of the command line) until idle and takes its run into its results cache, so the cache fits the new design. Both run on a `DisconnectRunner`: the window's and the command line's session with a deadline (`SessionRunner`), or a test's book run directly.
- **What is rewritten in a report**: the expressions of its controls, as a form's; each argument of a chart; and, my choice, the default and the list of each parameter, since a list read from a source would otherwise leave the copy needing it. They are listed as `report Sales, control 2, chart labels` and `report Sales, parameter who, choices`.
- **The dialog** counts the expressions of reports apart from those of forms, names the reports in which a replaced read uses `params` (*Reads that use the parameters of the report Sales are replaced by what they give with the values chosen now.*), and says that the changes to reports, like those to forms, cannot be undone. The results caches are calculated again within the same wait of at most two minutes.
- **The copy** has a new workbook id, as a *Save As* copy has, and keeps the views and the entries this version does not know. `--out` is given `.gossia` when it has no extension; it is compared with the input by resolving both, and the input itself is code 1 even with `--overwrite`. Its trust digest is read back from the file written (`gossia_user::file::saved_digest`, moved there from *Copy batch command*), and goes to stdout on the line after its path. stderr says how many reads were replaced, then lists the uses that stay and the error values of `--report`, each a reason for code 5. The limit of detail rows in `--report` is code 6, as for `report`.
- **Tests.** `python/tests_disconnect.rs`: a report not shown, with a read that uses a parameter, a parameter's list and a chart's arguments, replaced with the value chosen, shown again as before, its cache the run for the new design. `args.rs`: the options of `disconnect` and its errors. `tests/cli.rs`, with a Beancount journal as this user's source: a copy written with `--report` and a parameter (the path and the new digest on stdout, the input unchanged, a new workbook id, the formula and the report's read replaced, the value in the cache), which then runs with `report` once the source is gone; without `--report` the values chosen in the workbook; a read that stays, code 5 and the copy written; the input as `--out`, an existing copy, an untrusted workbook, a missing report and a wrong parameter.

**Decisions made for charts** (step 12e, 2026-11-03).
- **Finding chart types.** A chart type is read from the module's source without running it (`ModuleInfo::charts`, in `PythonSyntax::read_module`): a top-level function with the decorator `@chart("Name")` whose name is a plain string literal (a name computed at run time is not found). So the designer offers chart types in a workbook that is not trusted, and before the module has loaded. Its parameters are those of the function, in order (positional-only, ordinary and keyword-only), less `width` and `height`; one without a default is required. `*args` and `**kwargs` get no box. The decorator itself, in the prelude, only checks that the name is text and gives the function back.
- **The order of chart types** in the designer is the built-in ones, then each module's in module order. The names are compared ignoring case. **A module with a chart type whose name is taken** (by a built-in one, an earlier module's, or one earlier in the same module) is not loaded, as the design says: *Compile* puts the problem at the decorator's line, naming what has the name, and that module's chart types are not offered. The built-in names are reserved like the other names of the engine: `chart`, `bar_chart`, `line_chart`, `pie_chart`, `scatter_chart` and `sankey_chart` cannot name a tab, module or function.
- **The built-in charts** are `bar_chart(labels, values, title=None, series=None, horizontal=False, *, width, height)`, `line_chart(labels, values, title=None, series=None, *, width, height)`, `pie_chart(labels, values, title=None, donut=True, *, width, height)` and `scatter_chart(x, y, title=None, labels=None, *, width, height)` and `sankey_chart(sources, targets, values, title=None, *, width, height)`, named Bar, Line, Pie, Scatter and Sankey (Sankey added at the owner's request, 2026-11-04). For bars and lines `values` is one list of numbers or a list of lists (one per series) and `series` names them for the legend. For a Sankey diagram `sources`, `targets` and `values` are lists of the same length, one flow at each place; a name is a node wherever it appears, placed in the column of its longest way from a start (the ends all in the last column), and the nodes of each column are ordered by where their bands meet the next and the previous column, a few passes each way, so few bands cross. Each name that flows on has a series colour, its bands in it at 38% opacity (more than eight such names: all in the text colour, never cycled); a node is a 6-point bar; a node with room is labelled with its name and total beside it (on the left in the last column), with a ring of the surface so the label reads over bands. A flow that is negative, to itself, or round in a circle is an error with a message; a flow of zero is left out. They are in `charts.py` in calc, run in the prelude's namespace at start-up and put in the workbook namespace like a module's public functions. They follow the rules of a module (no double underscores), so a copy works in a module of the workbook; they read nodes with `PLAIN`. Their marks follow the data visualisation guidance used for the application: series colours in a fixed order, never cycled (a ninth series is an error; a pie with more than eight labels keeps the seven largest slices, in the order given, and folds the rest into one slice, Other, in a grey of the text colour, at the owner's request, 2026-11-04); bars at most 24 points thick with rounded ends and 2 points between them; 2-point lines with round joins and a marker on the last point; 1-point light grid lines and a baseline at zero; a legend for two or more series; text in text colours, never a series colour; one value axis. They are drawn on a light surface, as a printed report is. Values that are not numbers, lists of other lengths and negative slices are errors with a message.
- **The code editor** shows them under *Built in*, as *charts (built in)*, read-only: the text can be selected and copied, typing in it changes nothing, and the status line says to copy a function into a module with a new name in `@chart`.
- **A chart control** (`ControlKind::Chart`, reports only for now; the form palette has no *Add chart* and a chart on a form says it is drawn in reports only) keeps its chart type by name (`chart`) and an expression for each parameter filled in (`chart_args`, by parameter name). Its expression (`ExprKind::Chart`) is one Python call made from them: `_chart_svg(bar_chart(labels=(...), values=(...), width=W, height=H))`, each argument on lines of its own so a comment cannot swallow the rest, the built-in functions by bare name and a module's as `Module.function`. Each argument is checked for syntax first, so the error names the parameter. Nothing filled in, where a parameter is required, is no expression (*Fill in the chart's values*, or *Choose a chart type*); a chart whose parameters are all optional, or that has none but the size, draws at once (fixed 2026-11-12: it waited for a value it did not need); a required parameter left empty, or a chart type that is gone, is an error and nothing runs. Since it is one expression, its reads are the chart's dependencies, `params` is given to it when an argument names it, it runs under the formula budget, and a module change runs it again (as all report expressions are run again when the modules load). It is cached in the results cache like any expression (kind `chart`), so an untrusted workbook draws a cached chart.
- **Size.** `width` and `height` are the control's width and height in points, or 320 by 200 for a control without them (a new chart on the canvas gets that box). A chart that fills its width is given its set width (or 320) and scaled: report pages have a fixed width, and passing the laid width would need layout in calc; this can change when charts come to forms.
- **The result.** `_chart_svg` takes text as it is, decodes bytes as UTF-8 (`#VALUE!` when they are not) and makes anything else `#TYPE!`; an exception in the function is its error value as in formulas (a `TypeError` is `#TYPE!`). The layout checks the SVG with usvg before painting it: larger than the limit, or not parsing, is `#VALUE!` with the reason on hover. A problem is drawn as the error code in the chart's box, with its border.
- **Colours** (owner, 2026-11-03: a chart has to follow its *Fill colour* and *Text colour*). The layout paints the fill behind the chart as for any control, and gives the SVG the colours through a style sheet (usvg's `style_sheet`, CSS overriding the SVG's own attributes), kept with the painted SVG (`Paint::Svg::style`, `chart::style_sheet`): `svg { color: ... }` with the text colour, or black or white on a fill without one as for other controls, so what is drawn in `currentColor` follows it; and with a fill, `.background { fill: none }` and `.surface` / `.surface-stroke` filled and stroked with the fill as it shows on the white page. The built-in charts draw their text, grid and baseline in `currentColor` (lighter by opacity for what matters less, the root having `color="#0b0b0b"` for a chart without colours), their background as class `background`, and the donut hole, slice gaps and marker rings as `surface` and `surface-stroke`; `charts.py` says so for a chart type of your own. The series colours stay as they are. The application keeps a rendered chart by its style sheet too.
- **Parsing and drawing.** usvg 0.47 and resvg 0.47 (the versions krilla-svg uses, for PDF export), without their default features (no raster image decoding, no system fonts), with text. One set of options (`gossia_layout::chart::svg_options`) is used everywhere: the bundled fonts in a font database of their own, Inter for a family the application lacks (sans-serif Inter, serif Source Serif 4, monospace Geist Mono, cursive Dancing Script), 72 dpi so a point is a user unit, and images only from `data:` URIs (a file or URL is never read). The application renders the SVG at the size it has on screen (pixels, up to 4096 on a side), fitted and centred, into a texture kept by the hash of the SVG and the size (up to 64 kept), so a chart is rendered again only when it or its size changes.
- **Licences** (checked 2026-11-03): resvg, usvg, svgtypes, simplecss, kurbo and data-url are MIT or Apache-2.0; fontdb, rustybuzz, rgb, imagesize, float-cmp, strict-num, core_maths, pico-args and xmlwriter are MIT; ttf-parser, roxmltree and the unicode tables are MIT or Apache-2.0; tiny-skia is BSD-3-Clause. All allow distribution in the application and its packages with their notices.
- **Limits.** *Maximum detail rows* (`max_detail_rows`, default 100,000, ceiling 1,000,000) and *Maximum chart SVG size* (`max_chart_svg_bytes`, default 5 MB, ceiling 64 MB) are workbook settings on the *Reports* page of Options under *Limits*, with ceilings on the Trust page like the others. The application gives calc the values in force (`Calc::set_report_limits`), which pages reports and checks SVG with them.
- **The property page** of a chart has *Chart type* (the built-in ones by name, a module's with its module in brackets), then a box for each parameter labelled with its name, ` *` after a required one, a hint saying which are optional, the first error of the chart under them, and a note naming the function called.
- **Tests.** `charts_tests.rs` in calc (reading `@chart`, the order and names of chart types, the call with its size, every built-in chart giving SVG, a missing parameter, a bad result, a name that is taken, a module edit drawing again, the limits); the chart tests in `report_tests.rs` of layout (SVG painted, `#TYPE!`, `#VALUE!`, an error, untrusted, the limit); the chart tests in `tests/reports.rs` (a chart drawn and rendered, untrusted, the palette and the property page, no chart on a form, the code editor); the snapshot `report_charts`.

**Decisions made for report parameters and the results cache** (step 12f, 2026-11-03).
- **Declaring.** `ReportDesign.params`, in the order of the bar: a name, a label (the bar shows the name when there is none), a kind, a default expression (not for a period), a preset (only for a period), a choices expression (only for a choice or choices) and *Required*. The list of choices is always an expression; a fixed list is written as one (`['North', 'South']`), as the choices of a form's field are. A problem of the report names a parameter whose name is not an identifier, two parameters of one name, and a choice or choices with no list. They are declared on the property page with nothing selected, under the page setup: a fold for each with its fields and *Move up*, *Move down* and *Remove parameter*, and *Add parameter* (named `param1`, ..., of kind Date). Like the page setup, they are outside Undo layout.
- **Values.** A value the user chose is kept by report and parameter (`Calc::set_report_param`); one never chosen is the default; *Defaults* on the bar puts them all back. A default that is a date and time, for a date parameter, is its day; a default of the wrong kind is not used, and the bar says why. A choices parameter left empty is `None`, as the design says, not an empty list. A chosen value that is no longer in its list is left out of the value and the bar says it was dropped; the chosen value itself is kept, so it comes back if the list does. While a list has not run yet, nothing is dropped.
- **What runs again.** The defaults and lists are report expressions keyed by the parameter (no place in the design), run while the report is shown, and they do not see `params`. Only the expressions whose text names `params` are given it, so a change to a parameter runs again only those; an expression that does not name it cannot read it, since modules see parameters only as arguments. While a required parameter is empty, only the defaults and lists run, and the report says *Fill in ... to see the report.* A required choices parameter with nothing chosen counts as empty, and so does empty text.
- **The bar.** Each parameter's label (with ` *` when required), then: for a date, a box to type it in and the calendar of forms; for a period, a drop-down of the presets, *Dates* (which shows a start and an end, each a date box) and *(none)* when it is not required; for a choice, a drop-down with *(none)*; for choices, a menu of check boxes with *Select all*, *Clear* and, past 10 items, a search box, its button saying *All*, the one or two chosen, or *n of m*; a box for a number or text, taken as it is typed; a check box for yes/no. A period whose end is before its start is used as it is, and the bar says so. In a workbook that is not trusted the bar is greyed.
- **Chosen values are unsaved changes**, though not edits: the title shows the workbook modified and Save writes them, but Undo does not take them back (`Calc::params_version`).
- **Today** is the user's local date (with `jiff`, as the date picker's), worked out when the parameters are, so a preset follows the date.
- **The results cache** (`reports/<Report>/cache.json`) holds the digest of the design (SHA-256 of its JSON), the time (UTC, to the second), the chosen values, the value of each parameter and each list in the run, and the result of every expression of the design (by control, column, items and kind; an error in words). Values that JSON has no kind for are tagged: `{"$date": ...}`, `{"$datetime": ...}`, `{"$error": {"kind", "message"}}`, `{"$float": "nan"}`, and `{"$object": ...}` for an object with a key that starts with `$`. Results are sorted, so an unchanged cache writes the same bytes.
- **Taking a run.** A run is taken when the report stops being shown and when the workbook is saved, only if the workbook is trusted and every expression of the report has its result (otherwise the cache stays as it was). The time is kept if the digest, parameters, lists and results are all the same.
- **Drawing from it.** In a trusted workbook, an expression that has not run yet (just opened, or the items of a band not yet known) shows its cached result with the pending marker; the report says *Showing the results calculated on ... until the new ones are in* while anything is pending. In an untrusted one the report shows the cache, `#UNTRUSTED!` for what it has not, the parameters as the run had them, and *This workbook is not trusted, so nothing runs: the report is as it was calculated on ...* (or that no results were kept). A cache whose digest differs is not used, nor are its chosen values when the workbook is read; it is kept until a new run replaces it. A cache that cannot be read is dropped with a warning.
- **Keep report results in the file** is a workbook setting in `settings.json` (`"keep_report_results": false`, written only when off), on a new *Reports* page of Options under *Storage*, and changed as the other workbook settings are (one undoable entry). Off, `cache.json` holds the digest and the chosen values only.
- **Renaming and deleting** a report take its chosen values and its cache with it; a deleted report that comes back with Undo has neither.
- **The control channel** has `report_param` (report, name, and a value read by the parameter's kind; no value for the default), and `set_option` takes `keep_report_results`.
- **Tests.** The parameters in `report_tests.rs` of core (presets, choices, storing and problems); `report_params_tests.rs` in calc (`params` and what runs again, dropped choices, a period and a date, a required parameter, a rename); `report_cache_tests.rs` in calc (the values' kinds, taking and saving a run and keeping its time, another design, untrusted parameters, results not kept); `tests/reports.rs` (choosing from the bar and *Defaults*, a required parameter, a period and *Add parameter*, a report opened untrusted drawn from its cache); the snapshot `report_params`.

**Decisions made for multipage reports** (step 12g, 2026-11-03; the owner chose to build it before charts and parameters).
- **The bands are the layout.** A multipage report's controls at the top level are its bands, in the order of the stack: report header, page header, the section headers from the outermost in, detail, the section footers from the innermost out, page footer, report footer (`BandKind::stack`). Each band is a Free group whose height is the band's; `ReportDesign::bands()` gives the number of sections when the layout has that shape (an odd number of Free groups, at least five), and a design that does not shows a problem. So the designer, the property page, Undo layout and the expressions of forms work on bands unchanged. A section's items expression, item name and list name are the `items`, `item_name` and `list_name` of its header band, the detail band's on it. The page-break options of each section are in `ReportDesign.sections`, by depth, outside Undo layout (as the page setup is).
- **Height 0 hides a band.** `builtin:form` now allows a height of 0 (it was at least 16) so that a hidden band reads back. A new multipage report has page header 28, detail 24 and page footer 28 points, and the report header and footer hidden (my defaults); a new section has a header of 24 and a hidden footer, and its item is named `group` (`group2`, ... for the next).
- **Paging** (`gossia_core::report::paginate`) needs no measuring, because every band has a fixed height: only the design, the number of items in each occurrence and which bands are visible. The evaluation uses it for `page.count` and the layout to draw the pages, so both break in the same places. My choices: the report header comes first on page 1, above the page header, and the report footer last in the body, above the page footer, as in Crystal Reports; a band that is not visible (*Visible when* false) takes no room, except the page header and footer, which always keep theirs; a band starts a new page only if something is already in the body of this one, so a band taller than the body starts its own page and is cut at the page footer; *Keep the header with the first band* looks at the first band after the header (the next section's header, the first detail band, or its own footer when it has no items); *Repeat the header* draws it again under the page header of each page the section goes on to, its footer included. At the limit (`DETAIL_LIMIT`, 100,000 detail bands, until the Options page of 0.2.0 sets it) the report stops, without the footers still open, and says so in red under the last band.
- **Where expressions run** (`control_exprs.rs`, `band_seeds`): a section's items expression runs on its header band in each occurrence of the section around it (keyed by that occurrence); the header and footer of a section run for each of its items, keyed by the item of each section around and its own; the detail band for each detail item, the detail item last; the report header and footer once; the page header and footer once for each page, keyed by the page, with `page` bound to an object with `number` (from 1) and `count`. Each item is bound to `this` and to its section's item name; a level's list is bound to its list name in the occurrence around it, so the header and footer of a section can total the detail list inside it (`SUM(r.amount for r in rows)`), and the report header and footer can use the list of the outermost section. A change in the result of an items expression or of a band's *Visible when* syncs the expressions again, since it moves the pages.
- **The page view** shows one page at a time on the paper colour, with a shadow, at the zoom (*Fit width* fits the paper), *Previous page* and *Next page* and *Page n of m* on the strip, and Page Up and Page Down. Bands cut at the end of a page, and the limit, are said above the page in the error colour.
- **The designer** draws the stack of band canvases with 4 points between them, each band's name on its left (*Section header: unit*, with the section's item name; *(hidden)* for a hidden band, drawn as an 8-point grey strip that can still be selected and dropped on). Each band shows the first item of each section, the first detail item and page 1. Dragging the line under a band sets its height, snapped to the grid, not above its lowest control, as one step of Undo layout, with the height shown beside the line; a band has no resize handles and is never deleted or moved (the designer refuses a change that would alter the bands, except *Add section* and *Remove section*). A control dragged over another band or Free group goes into it at the place it is let go, on a single page canvas too. *Add ...* on the strip puts the control on the band of the selection, or the detail band, under its lowest control, and makes the band taller if it has to.
- **The property page.** With nothing selected, a report shows its page setup (paper, orientation, margins) and, if multipage, *Add section*. A band selected shows its name and what it is for, its height with *Hide band* or *Show band*, *Visible when* (not on the page header and footer), for a section header or the detail band its items, item name and list name, for a section's bands the three page-break options and *Remove section*, and on the detail band *Add section*. A section is added inside the others, around the detail band; removing one takes its two bands and what is on them.
- **Starting from an array** (`starting_design`) makes a multipage report: the detail band reads the array (`Orders.items as order`) and has a field for each scalar of the item (flattened as a table's columns; for items that hold arrays, their scalar fields), side by side in columns of at least 60 points, wrapping into more rows if there are many; the page header has a bold heading over each; the page footer has `Page {page.number} of {page.count}` on the right. In *New tab...* choosing an array chooses Multipage and greys Single page.
- **Old multipage designs** (a Flow list, made before 12g) are turned into a stack of bands with their controls in the report header, as a single page design is turned into a canvas.
- **Tests.** `report_tests.rs` in core (the stack, sections, problems, the starting point, paging: filling pages, new page, keeping and repeating headers, hidden and cut bands, report header and footer, the limit); `report_bands_tests.rs` in calc (each band for each place, names and lists, page values over three pages, a change to the data, a hidden band on the pages); `report_tests.rs` in `gossia-layout` (the designer's stack, pages with their places, cut warnings and the limit, a band not visible, an old design turned into bands); `tests/reports.rs` (from an array page by page, turning pages, the stack and a band's line, adding to the detail band and not deleting a band, dragging between bands, the property page and *Add section*, a section with a footer that totals its detail list); `report_tab_tests.rs`; the snapshots `report_pages` and `report_bands`.

**Decisions made for Disconnect from Data Sources** (2026-11-02; see Data sources).
- **Rewriting, not recording** (owner's choice). The workbook keeps no copy of what the sources gave; the text of the formulas and expressions changes, so what was sent is plain to read and needs no new machinery to run.
- **Reads are found by parsing**, with the same parser as the syntax check (`Syntax::source_reads`), not by searching the text, so a read in a string or a comment is never touched. The text found is replaced by its range, from the end, and the new text is checked again; one that does not parse is left and listed.
- **The probes run as expressions** in the next pass (`ControlExprs` probes, each run as a formula on its node or as an expression of its control), so they see the same names and the same per-pass read cache as everything else. A probe is never stored and records no dependencies.
- **One literal for every instance.** A column formula or an expression inside items is replaced only if every row or item gave the same literal; otherwise one text cannot stand for them all.
- **Nested reads**: when a read inside another's arguments and the outer one can both be replaced, the outer one is.
- **Templates are rebuilt from their parts** (`template::rebuild`), doubling braces in the text and putting a space before an expression that starts with `{`; a rebuilt template that does not read back the same is left and listed.
- **Tests.** `python/tests_disconnect.rs` (formulas with dates, bytes and None read back the same, the problems, undo; a column formula; a field value, *Visible when* and a template label; the helper names); `disconnect.rs` (telling a call of `source` from other names); `form/template.rs` (rebuilt parts read back the same); `tests/sources.rs` (the dialog: cancel, replace, F9 no longer reads, undo label; the list when nothing can be replaced).

**Risks that only building and measuring can settle**
- **RustPython.** It is young and not production-ready by its own account, it builds only from a pinned git revision, and the single long builtin call cannot be interrupted. Mitigations: the `PythonEngine` trait, the pinned and vendored build, the abandoned-thread backstop, and the test-suite probes.
- **Proxy layer speed.** The budgets are under Python engine. A miss is addressed by the mitigations listed there.
- **Schema inference and conformance.** Inference over real-world files and the selected conformance cases will turn up cases not yet foreseen. The corpus and the must-reject tests are the way to find them.
- **Report rendering and PDF export** (0.2.0). Charts are SVG (see Chart functions), so they need no second rendering path; text, tables and the page layout do. Layout and pagination must therefore be computed once, independent of egui, and drawn both on screen and into the PDF, with the same fonts so that page breaks match. This is `gossia-layout` (see The report designer); forms stay on egui's own layout (owner, 2026-11-02). The risk is that the page view, drawn by egui, drifts from `gossia-layout`'s line breaks, for example if egui positions glyphs differently from the widths measured; the tests that compare the page view with the PDF catch it.
